c10



PROLOGUE


Josie’s POV


They always said that my sister Morgan could light up a room just by walking into it. I suppose it was true. She was the kind of girl everyone noticed. The one with the bright smile that could easily make everyone fall under her spell. With her golden hair caught in the sun, it shone like she was an angel on earth. She also had the confidence I only wished to have.


Next to her, I was simply the ugly duckling. Morgan’s little sister. The shy one. The awkward younger sister who no one bothered to look at twice.


Despite this, I still possess a heart, and for as long as I can remember, my heart has belonged to Wade Blackwood.


He was my neighbor. The handsome young man with dark hair and warm eyes. His laugh alone could make my stomach flutter.


Morgan used to tease him as a scrawny cowboy. Whenever Wade was in our house, my sister never bothered to hide her disdain for him. For some reason, she hates him.


I didn’t understand her reason for her dislike of Wade. He was the nicest guy I knew. Even if when I tried to speak to him and my words would always get stuck somewhere between my ribs and my throat, he still hung out with an awkward girl like me. I got content with every stolen glance I gave him, every act of small kindness he gave me, as if they were treasures meant only for me.


He was my everything.


I was sixteen when his family packed up and left Oakridge in exchange for a life in the city. I remembered that I stood by my bedroom window that morning they left, watching the dust rise behind their truck. I was certain that something precious slipped from me forever. I told myself it was a silly thought. Boys like Wade didn’t belong to girls like me. Yet I never stopped loving him.


And now, after all these years, I heard the news I was waiting for all my life.


Wade is finally coming home.


Yes, that’s right. They say he’s only visiting, just passing through after his parents had finally sold the last of their land. But my heart doesn’t care about the reasons for his visit. What matters to me is that Wade Blackwood—the boy who once made my teenage heart race faster than a dust devil—is back.


And for the first time in years, I feel like that sixteen-year-old girl looking at my window… waiting. And I was foolishly hoping that one day Wade would come back to this little town of ours.


“Alright, spill.”


My friend, Sierra, leaned across the small café table, her chin propped on her palm and her eyes glinting with mischief. We’d met for our usual Saturday coffee date after work, but she’d been watching me far too closely ever since I walked in.


“Spill what?” I asked, stirring my latte a little too intently.


Her brows shot up. “Don’t play coy on me, Josie Walker. I know you like the back of my hand. You got that quiet smile on your face. Don’t you think I will not notice?”


A deep blush crept up my cheeks. “Sierra—”


“It’s Wade, isn’t it?” she cut in with a grin. She whispered his name like it was my most scandalous secret. “Wade Blackwood. I heard that the prodigal cowboy finally returns.”


I nearly choked on my drink. “Keep your voice down!” I hissed, glancing at our neighboring tables nervously. The café buzzed with the usual chatter of small-town laid-back afternoons, but still, I was aware of how town gossip works.


Sierra smirked. She was clearly enjoying my panic. “I freaking knew it! Josie Walker, the woman who has been hopelessly in love with her neighbor since middle school. She’s still carrying the torch after all these years. I should’ve taken bets.”


I tried to glare at her, but it came out more like a helpless smile. “I’m not—it’s not like that.”


“Oh, please. Don’t even try.” She leaned closer, lowering her voice. “I could imagine the look on your face the moment you heard he was coming back. You’ve been waiting for this moment, Jo. Don’t pretend otherwise.”


I bit my lip as I stared down at the swirl of foam in my cup. She wasn’t wrong. Wade’s return had set my heart fluttering inside me once again. I felt like I was once again sixteen and forever in love with that handsome cowboy.


“I just…” I sighed, choosing my words carefully. I didn’t want to be seen as someone who still hangs on to my teenage love. “It’s been a long time. Part of me wonders if he’ll even remember me. I was just the quiet neighbor kid who loves riding horses. He never really saw me, Sierra. Not the way I saw him.”


Sierra reached across the table, covering my hand with hers. “Then maybe it’s time he does. This is your chance, Jo. Don’t let it slip.”


Her words lingered in the air, both a comfort and a challenge. For the first time in years, I let myself imagine Wade Blackwood looking at me. Not just as his neighbor but something more.


After my coffee date with my best friend, Sierra. I went home, but there were still a few hours before dinner. I decided to ride my horse, Daisy. I didn’t want to stay inside the house and had the chance to bump into my father before dinner.


You could say that I was avoiding my father, mainly because he didn’t approve of my life’s choice of staying in our hometown instead of pursuing a bigger dream in the city like Morgan. To him, I was the failure in his successful life.


The late afternoon sun splattered the fields in gold as Daisy’s hooves carried me down the familiar trail. I always ended up here, at the far edge of our land where the Walker fence met the Blackwood property. It was a habit I’d never seemed to get over, even after Wade’s family left years ago. This stretch of fence felt like the last place his presence lingered, the memories of our countless childhood laughter we shared in the past.


I slowed Daisy near the gate, leaning forward in the saddle as a dust cloud rose in the distance. A truck rattled down the old driveway as the sunlight glinted off the windshield. My heart began to stutter.


And then I saw him.


Wade Blackwood.


He climbed down from the driver’s seat. He was taller now, broader than the boy I remembered. His shoulders carried the weight of years I hadn’t seen him. His jaw was sharper with stubble. He dressed nicely like a bonafide city folk now. But it was his eyes—those deep and steady eyes with the color of a storm just before it broke—that were the same. The same eyes that had once made the sixteen-year-old me believe in forever.


I froze. I forgot how to breathe, let alone move.


As if sensing my presence, Wade looked up. His gaze landed on me across the fence line, his eyes narrowing in recognition before softening into something I couldn’t quite name.


“Josie?” His voice carried across the quiet space a little rougher than I remembered.


My throat tightened. “Hi, Wade.”


A slow smile tugged at his mouth, the kind that had haunted my dreams for years. “Well, I’ll be damned. Josie Walker. You still ride out here, huh?”


I managed a shaky laugh, patting Daisy’s neck to keep her from fidgeting. “Some things don’t change, like our small little town.”


He leaned an arm against the fence. He studied me with that easy confidence I could never imitate. “Looks like some things do change, Josie. For one thing, you’re not the kid anymore. You become a fine young woman.”


The words struck deep in my heart. It gave me hope that he could finally notice me after all these years. My heart began to flutter traitorously and foolishly at the same time. Because for the first time in forever, Wade Blackwood finally saw what I had become in the years that he was away.


And I wasn’t sure I’d ever recover from it.


Wade’s smile lingered as he glanced over the fence, his hand trailing along the weathered wood like he was testing the memories woven into it.


“It feels strange being back here,” he admitted. “I didn’t think I’d ever set foot on this land again.”


I swallowed hard while my fingers curled tighter around Daisy’s reins. “I didn’t think you would either. I thought that you had already forgotten your roots when you relocated to the city with your parents.”


His gaze flicked back to me. “But you’re still here. You haven’t changed a bit.”


The corner of my mouth twitched. “Trust me, I’ve changed.” I hope you’ll finally notice.


That earned a low chuckle from him. “Yeah, I can see that.” His eyes softened before something in his expression made my heart trip over itself. “It’s really good to see you, Josie. Really good.”


I dropped my gaze as heat spread across my cheeks. To him, I was probably nothing more than friendly nostalgia, but to me… he was everything.


“How long are you staying?” I asked, my voice coming out thinner than I intended.


“A couple of days. Just tying up loose ends for my folks for this property and—” He hesitated like he was about to say more but then shook his head as if changing his mind. “—and some news I couldn’t wait to share with you soon.”


My stomach flipped. “Well… welcome home, Wade. I’m sure the town’s folks are happy to see you again.”


He tipped his head. That easy grin of his made my heart flip all over again. “Thanks, Josie. When I saw you, it felt like I never left.”


As he leaned against the fence, he was watching me like the years hadn’t built a canyon between us. I let myself pretend for one reckless moment that maybe he’d come back and there’s hope for us.

c11J



CHAPTER 11


Josie’s POV


I didn’t know what kind of cruel joke the universe was playing on me. But here I was again, standing on Aidan Callahan’s doorstep with a basket in my hands.


Breakfast delivery. Courtesy of my mother. I should’ve refused my mother’s request. I should’ve made up some excuse, like I was deep in my sleep and I didn’t hear her knocking on my door, or I had a sudden headache. But Mom had been insistent on making that man feel at home at Oakridge.


She mentioned the importance of ‘neighborly kindness’ and stated that ‘Mr. Callahan must still be adjusting to his new life here.’ Instead of saying no, I found myself staring at his front door.


My grip tightened on the basket, not from its weight but from the memory it carried.


Last Sunday. The tractor. That look in his eyes. The way he’d leaned closer, lowering his head like he was about to steal something I wasn’t ready to give. And how, at the very last second, was the only time I had pushed him away.


What unsettled me most wasn’t the fact that he’d tried to kiss me. It was that for one heart-pounding moment, I hadn’t wanted him to stop. And the thought had haunted me with a sickening mix of shame and curiosity.


What would’ve happened if I hadn’t shoved him back? Would I have let him kiss me? Would I have kissed him back?


The horror of the thought settled in my stomach as I shifted from one boot to the other while staring at that door, as it might bite at any moment.


I didn’t want to see him. I wasn’t ready to face him again. But Mom’s voice echoed in my ears, firm and impossible to argue with.


I lifted my hand, my knuckles hovering above the door. I told myself to breathe. I could do this. I could act like last Sunday didn’t happen.


“Just drop off the breakfast,” I whispered to myself. “In and out. Don’t let him rattle you again.”


Even as I said it, a traitorous part of me wondered if I was already rattled beyond saving.


I knocked quickly and immediately bent down with the intention of setting the basket on his doorstep before running off like a coward.


He would see the basket once he woke up, right? Furthermore, I already followed my mother’s instructions for delivering his breakfast. She didn’t specifically say that I need to give it to him personally.


But before I could put it down, the door swung open. Once again, he was bare-chested, his dark hair mussed, with that lazy confidence radiating from every inch of him as if walking around shirtless was his natural state.


I gasped as red stains spread through my cheeks so fast I nearly dropped the basket altogether.


“Morning,” he drawled. His voice was thick with sleep and amusement. “Don’t tell me my favorite delivery girl is back again.”


I snapped my gaze away so fast I almost gave myself whiplash. The floorboards of his porch suddenly became the most fascinating thing I’d ever seen. I’d looked anywhere but at him. Anywhere but that broad chest and the way the sunlight caught the lines of muscle across his shoulders.


“Here,” I muttered, shoving the basket toward him without daring to look at his half-naked state. “Breakfast. From my mom. Not me. Her.”


I heard the low chuckle. I felt it more than I heard it. Damn it.


“Why, Josie Walker,” he wondered. “You sound nervous. What’s the matter? Are you afraid to look at me, and you’ll see something you like?”


My head jerked upward, but only far enough to glare at his chin before I forced my eyes to a safer spot. Anywhere but his bare skin and that annoying smirk on his face.


“You’re unbearable,” I hissed. “Just take this. And put on a shirt for heaven’s sake. Some of us don’t appreciate being ambushed by exhibitionists like you early in the morning.”


He leaned against the doorframe with one hand braced high above him. He smiled as he’d just won a battle I hadn’t even realized I was fighting.


“Funny,” he murmured. “You didn’t look away this fast last Sunday.”


The memory of him closing in by the tractor slammed into me like a punch. I nearly stumbled backward. My heart beat wildly, betraying me even as I clenched my jaw and tried to regain control.


“I’m leaving,” I snapped, spinning on my heel before he could say anything more that would completely ruin my day. I was halfway down the porch steps when his voice stopped me in my tracks.


“Josie,” he called lazily. “We really ought to stop this whole enemy thing. We’re neighbors, after all. We might as well try to be friends.”


I turned slowly, narrowing my eyes in his direction. He was still leaning in the doorway, shirtless and so damned smug. While his free hand casually gripped the basket I’d brought like it was some sort of victory prize.


“Friends?” I repeated sharply. “A good friend and neighbor doesn’t try to kiss someone they barely know.”


Aidan’s grin grew wider, as if my outburst amused him so much. “That’s because I’m not the nice next-door neighbor type.” He tilted his head, his blue eyes glittering with that same dangerous mischief that had been undoing me since the moment we met. “I just tried my luck with the most fascinating lady I’ve met in Oakridge. Can you really blame me?”


Fascinating? He was just toying with me. He had to be. There was no way he could be serious.


“You can rest assured,” he went on smoothly, lowering his voice until it carried a weight that made my heart pound faster. “I won’t try to kiss you again… unless you’re the one to initiate it.”


I gasped, horrified at the nerve of him. “That will never happen!”


“We shall see.” He smirked and made my pulse trip over itself. “Do you agree?”


I didn’t answer him right away. My lips pressed tight as my chest rose and fell faster than I wanted him to see.


Aidan didn’t seem bothered by the silence. If anything, he leaned against the doorframe with an infuriating ease, as though he had all morning to wait for my answer. “So, do you want to give this ‘friends’ thing a try? I think we’re better friends than enemies.”


I glanced away. I needed to break free from the magnetic pull of his gaze, and when I spotted the old tractor sitting just outside the barn, my heart gave a little twist. That machine carried too many memories. Some I cherished deeply, while others I couldn’t bear to think about for too long.


“Still trying to make that thing work?”


He gave me a crooked grin. He had this look in his face that said he was about to get what he wanted. “Yeah. She’s stubborn, but I like a challenge.”


His eyes flicked to me. I swear there was a double meaning buried in his words. I was certain of it. I crossed my arms, trying to steady my breathing.


“You still want to give me a hand with it?” His tone was surprisingly sincere, slipping through the usual teasing. “No strings attached. I’ll behave. Like a gentleman, I promise.”


I eyed him skeptically. “You? Behave? Really?”


The dangerous smile was back on his handsome face, though he raised a hand in mock surrender. “Yeah. I swear on a gentleman’s honor.”


I bit my lip, fighting a war inside me. Every instinct screamed that I should turn him down. That I should run as far from his presence as possible. And yet, my gaze slid back to the tractor, and with it came a rush of old feelings I couldn’t ignore.


“Fine,” I said at last. “But only because of the tractor. That thing means a lot to me.”


“Fair enough.” The way he watched me told me he thought he’d just won something much bigger. “So, where do we start with this little project of ours?”


I folded my arms and lifted my chin. “Every afternoon, after I close my shop.”


“Perfect,” he agreed, as if this were a date we were setting instead of work. “How about we start later this afternoon, then?”


I hesitated but finally nodded after a moment of thinking. “Fine.”


“Great.” He lowered his voice in a conspiratorial way. “I’ll swing by your flower shop and pick you up.”


The horror must have been written all over my face because he laughed before I even spoke. “No!” I blurted too quickly and too loudly. “Absolutely not. I’ll… I’ll just meet you here.”


He smiled. It wasn’t the devilish smirk this time. It was worse. Knowing. “What’s the matter, sweetheart? Ashamed to be seen with me in town?”


Pink stains spread through my cheeks. I forced myself to look him dead in the eye. “It’s better if we keep whatever this is between us. At least, for now. I don’t want to be the center of the town’s gossip because of you.”


He studied me with those blue eyes that made me feel both stripped bare and cornered at the same time. “And that’s a bad thing? To be associated with me?”


I didn’t bother to lie. “Exactly.”


Aidan leaned back against the doorframe, crossing his arms over his bare chest like a man who’d just won another round in a game only he knew the rules of. “Secret meetings at the ranch. Every afternoon. Now this is starting to sound very interesting.”


I glared at him, but it only seemed to amuse him more. I took a deep breath, trying to collect myself before I said something reckless I might regret later on. “I’ll see you later this afternoon then.”


Aidan only leaned lazily against the doorframe, that insufferable grin still tugging at his mouth. “Looking forward to it, neighbor.”


I rolled my eyes and spun on my heel. The quicker I got away from his place, the better.


Daisy was tied just beyond the fence, her ears flicking as she waited for me patiently. The moment I saw her, relief washed over me. Something familiar. Something mine. I untied her reins and rubbed her velvety nose. “Come on, girl. Let’s get out of here before he drives me insane.”


I mounted up on Daisy. I cast one last glance over my shoulder. Sure enough, Aidan was still at his door, watching me with that same blasted smile. I refused to give him the satisfaction of reacting. I straightened my back and urged Daisy forward.


As we trotted toward the Walker side of the fence, my pulse slowed, though the echo of his voice lingered stubbornly in my mind.


By the time our house came into view, I forced my thoughts to the back of my head. My work. The shop. The bouquets had to be arranged. The orders that needed to be filled. Anything to stop thinking about Aidan Callahan.


But deep down, I already knew that I’d be thinking about him all day.


I wasn’t wrong.


My morning at the shop should’ve been like any other. But no matter how hard I tried to focus, my mind kept circling back to him and his taunting words.


Later this afternoon. The words played over and over inside my head like a record on a loop.


Every time I tied a ribbon or filled an order, I caught myself imagining what it would be like standing beside him again. The way his eyes narrowed when he teased me. The curve of his grin when he knew he was getting under my skin. The memory made my stomach flutter and twist. I hated myself for behaving like this.


“Josie?” Mrs. Bennett’s voice startled me. I nearly dropped the scissors I was holding. She gave me a knowing look as she adjusted her straw hat. “You seem distracted today, dear. Is everything all right?”


I forced a smile and shook my head quickly. “Oh, of course. Just thinking about something silly. Nothing important.”


Her eyes twinkled with suspicion, but she didn’t press. She accepted her bouquet arrangement and left with a cheerful wave.


She wasn’t the only one to notice my lack of concentration. By noon, two more customers had pointed out how quiet I was. How far away my eyes seemed. Each time, I brushed it off with the same excuse, ‘just thinking.’


If only they knew.


By the time the lunch customers slowed down, I leaned against the counter while staring at the clock. My heart beat harder with every minute that ticked closer to the afternoon. No matter how much I scolded myself, I couldn’t stop waiting for the time I finally closed the shop.


The rest of the day passed in a blur. I didn’t even remember serving the last customer. I only became aware when I flipped the sign to Closed, as my hands were trembling.


By the time I got home, my pulse was racing. I hurried up the stairs, tossed my work dress onto the chair, and pulled on a pair of worn denim jeans and a soft plaid shirt.


The clothes I wore when I wanted to feel like myself, not the timid girl everyone thought I was. I also put my hair in a high ponytail so it wouldn’t be in the way when we started working.


In the stable, Daisy nickered as though she knew I needed her steadiness more than ever. While saddling her calmed my nerves only a little, my heart still beat wildly as we trotted down the familiar trail toward the border.


And then I saw him.


Aidan was already there, leaning against the fence as though he had been there for a long time waiting for me. The late afternoon sun caught in his dark blond hair, casting him in a golden light that made him look almost too perfect. He was too dangerous for a small-town girl like me.


As soon as he spotted me, he pushed off the fence and strode forward. That signature grin tugging at his lips.


“You’re finally here,” he drawled, reaching over the top rail to offer me his hand. His blue eyes glinted with something wicked and knowing. “I’ve been waiting for you, sweetheart.”


And despite everything I told myself. Despite every wall I tried to build to protect myself. My heart skipped a beat.

c14D



CHAPTER 12


Josie’s POV


It had been several days now since I had secretly crossed the boundary between our ranches and spent time fixing the tractor with him.


Every afternoon, after I closed up the shop, I found myself riding Daisy to the Blackwood Ranch. And every evening, just before dinner, I made the quiet journey back home. My hands would smell faintly of grease. My hair was loose from the work. And my heart was in a state I could barely name.


Somehow, it had become my routine.


I told myself it was just about the tractor, and nothing more. That old machine carried too many memories for me to let it rust away. If Aidan Callahan had the nerve to try to fix it, then I wasn’t going to let him do it alone.


Deep down, I knew it wasn’t just about the tractor. Every day, I found myself looking forward to those few hours before dinner. His voice would cut through the silence while we worked on something we weren’t sure we could fix.


From the way he teased me until my cheeks would burn to the strange and dangerous thrill of being just near him, to the way his hand would deliberately brush against mine on occasions, and I could smell the clean soap clinging to his skin under the faint scent of oil.


I hated how I started to notice the way he smells. I hated how I started to notice the way his face tightened when he grew frustrated with the tractor. The way his brows would knit together, and his mouth would press into a firm line.


But most of all, I hated myself for behaving like this. When did I start watching him closely? I told myself I had no business noticing his every move. I kept on telling myself that it would be the last time I would help him fix the tractor.


My peace of mind was more important than Wade’s old tractor.


And yet, I kept going back. Every single day.


By the time my last customer for the day finally left. I was already five minutes past closing.


I still smiled. I still said the polite ‘thank you, come again,’ but deep inside I was practically begging them to walk faster through that door. All I wanted to do was to lock the shop, grab my purse, and head home to change before making my way to Blackwood Ranch.


I’d just ducked behind the counter to get my purse when the bell over the door jingled again. “Josie!”


I froze for half a second before forcing myself to turn. My smile was a little tighter than usual. “Hey.”


Sierra walked in like she owned the place. Her hair tumbled around her shoulders, and her eyes scanned the shop as if she hadn’t already been here a hundred times before.


I loved her. She was my friend. My anchor since high school. But right now, I wish she would leave.


I couldn’t shove her out heartlessly, could I? I couldn’t tell her I had somewhere to be. I couldn’t admit how I’ve spent my afternoons lately.


I smoothed down my apron, hiding my frustration. I prayed she wasn’t planning on staying fat too long.


“What are you doing here this late?” I asked lightly while eyeing the clock on the wall that ticked far too loudly in my head.


Sierra plopped herself onto one of the stools by the counter, propping her chin in her hand like she had all the time in the world. Too bad, I had not. I was already running late.


“So,” she began with her singsong voice. “I was thinking we should grab coffee like right this moment. It’s been forever since our last post-work catch-up. I invited Amber to join us, too. She’ll meet us at the café.”


My smile stiffened. Coffee. Normally, I’d jump at the chance to be with them, but the clock was already screaming at me. And the thought of Aidan waiting by that tractor made my pulse kick hard in my throat.


“I’ll take a rain check,” I said, folding my apron with a little too much precision. “I’ve got stuff to do at home. Sorry.”


Sierra frowned, her eyes narrowing in that way that always made me feel like she was peeling back my layers. “Stuff? What kind of stuff?”


I forced a casual shrug, avoiding her gaze as I busied myself with stacking receipts. “Oh, you know… I’ve been redoing my room. Moving things around. Changing wallpapers. That kind of stuff.”


“Redoing your room?” she repeated slowly. “Since when do you care about rearranging furniture?”


I blushed slightly. She wasn’t wrong. I’d never been the type to obsess over where my dresser sat or if the curtains matched the rug. And I hadn’t had the impulse to do just that. She knew that she would be the first person I would ask for help.


She looked at me with sharpened interest. “So what’s really keeping you busy lately, Josie? You’ve been disappearing right after work all week. You’ve never been this hard to pin down before. There was a time two days ago when I went here to see you, but your shop was already closed. Usually, you linger in your shop even after you’re done for the day.”


I tightened my grip on the folded apron, my heart pounding at her accuracy. I put on my most innocent expression as the walls closed in around me. I could feel that my secret rendezvous was about to be exposed.


I laughed. Even for my own ears, it sounded off. I waved my hand dismissively as if she’d just accused me of something outrageous. “Honestly, Sierra. You’re being silly. Nothing’s going on.”


She didn’t look convinced. Before she could press further, I sighed and threw up my hands. “Fine. You win. Coffee it is. My room can wait.”


She smiled triumphantly as she hooked her arm through mine, dragging me out of the shop. “Finally. I was starting to think you were avoiding me.”


Avoiding her? No. But avoiding the truth? Absolutely.


***


Inside the café, the air was warm and sweet with the smell of roasted beans. Sierra was already chattering away, and Amber soon slipped into the chair with us. Her cheeks were a little flushed from the late afternoon chill.


Normally, this was my safe place, our girly bonding over coffee and gossip. But tonight, I couldn’t focus. My eyes kept darting to the clock over the counter. Each tick of the second hand wound my nerves tighter.


Aidan was probably waiting for me. I had never been late with our work session.


I didn’t even know his number. How was I supposed to tell him I was running late? What if he thought I’d changed my mind?


I tried to sit through it. I nodded when Sierra complained about the latest town gossip. I smiled faintly when Amber rolled her eyes at another well-meaning but nosy neighbor. But the words blurred together, meaningless. My chest tightened with each passing minute.


Finally, I couldn’t take it anymore. I pushed back my chair with a smile that felt thin. “I’m so sorry, you two. I’ll make it up to you soon, I promise. But I really have to get going. That whole redecorating thing… turns out it can’t really wait.”


Sierra groaned. “Seriously, Josie—”


Amber just gave me a knowing look, one that made my stomach flip.


I forced a laugh and waved them off before they could ask any more questions. I slipped out of the café with my heart racing as I hurried down the street.


I had to get to Aidan.


By the time I reached home, I was practically sprinting. I tore up the stairs and went to my room in record time. I tossed my bag onto the bed and hurried into fresh clothes. My trusty denim and a soft flannel shirt that smelled faintly of lavender from my shop.


My fingers fumbled with the buttons as I caught myself in the mirror. My cheeks were flushed from running and not from the anticipation of seeing him again. My hair dropped loose from its braid.


All this just to fix a tractor with the one man I swore I’d avoid.


I was tugging on my boots when I heard my mother’s voice downstairs. My heart sank. What does she want now?


“Josie?” she called as I clattered down the stairs. She frowned when she saw my attire. “Where are you off to? It’s almost dinner.”


I froze at the bottom step, schooling my expression into something calm. “Just a quick ride with Daisy, Mom. I won’t be long.”


Her brows knitted together. “At this hour? You know your father doesn’t like—”


“I’ll be back,” I cut her off quickly, grabbing my jacket from the hook. “You and Dad can eat without me if I’m late. It’s fine.”


“Josie—”


I didn’t wait for her reply. I slipped out the door, and the cool evening air filled my lungs as though I’d escaped something far heavier than dinner. My feet carried me straight to Daisy, who nickered softly as if sensing my urgency.


“Come on, girl,” I whispered, saddling her in record time. My hands shook, but not from the cold, but from anticipation and from nerves I didn’t dare name.


The ride across the pasture felt shorter than ever, my pulse matching Daisy’s steady rhythm. I told myself I was only going because of the tractor, because I hated leaving something unfinished.


I knew that was a lie.


When the outline of the Blackwood barn came into view, my breath caught. There he was. Aidan Callahan, with his sleeves rolled up, was bent over the tractor with stubborn determination.


He must’ve heard Daisy’s approach because he lifted his head. He smiled when he saw me. Just one curve of his mouth that was aimed straight at me.


My heart stumbled so hard I nearly dropped Daisy’s reins. In that single moment, I knew, without wanting to admit it, that I was in far deeper trouble than I’d ever let myself believe.


Aidan straightened as I slid off Daisy, brushing his hands down the front of his designer jeans like he’d been waiting all day just to see me.


Yep, he was wearing denim worth thousands of dollars while fixing the tractor. He didn’t care if they would ruin him after today’s work. He was going to wear a new one tomorrow. He was probably that wealthy.


“Well, well,” he drawled, leaning one hip against the tractor. “Look who finally decided to show up. I was beginning to think you stood me up.”


I rolled my eyes, trying to ignore the warmth creeping up my cheeks. “Don’t flatter yourself. Something came up.”


“Uh-huh,” he said, crossing his arms. “Important things, I’m sure. Something like catching up with your girlfriends, maybe?”


My mouth fell open. “How did you—”


He smirked, that devilish gleam in his blue eyes making my pulse stutter. “What? Surprised that I know? Oakridge isn’t that big, Josie. People talk. Someone sees someone.”


I huffed, walking past him toward the tractor. I refused to give him the satisfaction of seeing how rattled I was. “I didn’t know you had time to keep up with gossip in between breaking tractors and scaring horses.”


“Ah,” he said behind me, his voice dripping with amusement. “So you were late because you were hiding from me, not because you were catching up with your friends?”


I glanced over my shoulder, giving him my best glare. “You’re annoying, do you know that?”


His smile only widened. “Maybe. But you keep coming back. That makes me happy.”


My stomach twisted because he wasn’t wrong. And I hated how much truth sat in that teasing jab.


Aidan leaned closer, lowering his voice like he was sharing a secret. “Admit it, Josie. You still came even if it was already late because you didn’t want to disappoint me.”


I scoffed, turning away so he wouldn’t see how my cheeks flamed. “In your dreams.”


“Careful,” he warned. “You keep showing up in those jeans and plaid shirts. I think I’m starting to like them. I realized that they are sexier than the seductive dresses women usually wear to entice men.”


My jaw dropped, looking at him with utter disbelief. “You—you’re impossible! How can you flirt at a time like this?”


He chuckled. He was clearly pleased with himself that he managed to ruffle my feathers again. “Come on then, Peeping Tom. Let’s see if you’re as good at fixing tractors as you are at spying.”


I narrowed my eyes, ready to snap back, but my heart was thundering so loudly I could barely form words. I just ignored him and bent over the open hood, trying to ignore the way his eyes followed me like I was the most fascinating thing in Oakridge.


“Careful, sweetheart,” he said, leaning against the fence post with his arms folded. “If you glare at that engine any harder, it might fix itself just to spare me.”


I shot him a look. “If it does, it’ll be because it’s terrified of your ‘mechanical skills.’”


He laughed, the sound curling around me like a warm blanket. “Ouch. I’ll have you know, I’m very good with my hands.”


My wrench slipped, nearly nicking my knuckle. I snapped my head up at him. I was horrified at the heat rushing to my face. “You’re shameless. Do you know that?”


“Admit it,” he said. He stepped closer, his shadow stretching over the tractor and me both. “You missed me today. That’s why you still came here even if it was already late.”


“I missed peace,” I muttered, yanking at a stubborn bolt.


He crouched beside me until our shoulders brushed. “Then why are you here, Josie Walker?”


My throat tightened. I wanted to say it was for the tractor. For Wade’s memory. For anything but him.
But the way his blue eyes glittered under the fading sun, like he already knew the truth, made my heart stumble.


I muttered the safest thing I could. “Because I promised to help, that’s why.”


He grinned devilishly. “Good. Then I’ll make sure you keep that promise.”


And just like that, I realized I was already tangled in something far more dangerous than broken bolts and rusty gears.


By the time the last streak of daylight bled across the sky, my hands were raw, my shirt was smudged, and my patience was already stretched thin because of this helpless tractor.


There was no sign that we could fix this. I straightened from the tractor, brushing my palms against my jeans. “That’s enough for today. I should head back. It’s getting late.”


Aidan wiped his brow with the back of his hand. He glanced at me with that infuriating half-grin. “What’s the rush, sweetheart? Don’t want your parents thinking you’ve been rolling around in grease with me?”


I glared at him.


“I’m just saying the truth,” he corrected smoothly. “At least wash up before you go. There’s a bathroom inside. Saves you from explanations you’d rather not want to give.”


Inside the Blackwood Ranch. The house I hadn’t stepped into in years. The one tied to too many memories I still carried in quiet corners of my heart.


“No,” I shook my head. “There’s no need. Thanks for the offer, but I should just go—”


“Josie,” he cut in. His voice was surprisingly gentle without mockery this time. “It’s just a sink. Nothing more. I assure you that I will not take advantage of you, if that’s what’s worrying you.”


I hesitated, but curiosity won over me. I took a steadying breath. I untied Daisy and left her by the fence before following Aidan up the porch steps.


The moment I crossed the threshold, the past rushed at me. The faint creak of the floorboards beneath my boots. The scent of aged wood. The images of Wade’s easy grin and the afternoons we spent baking pies with his mother in the kitchen—all of it echoed through these halls.


For a moment, I almost turned around and fled. But I squared my shoulders. I wasn’t sixteen anymore. I wouldn’t break down just because of a darn house.


“The bathroom is down the hall,” Aidan started, but I was already moving in that direction.


“I know where it is,” I answered.


He raised a brow but didn’t stop me.


Inside the familiar little room, I splashed my face with water. I scrubbed away the grease and the surge of emotions that clawed their way to the surface. When I finally lifted my head to the mirror, my reflection looked steady again. I looked composed.


When I opened the bathroom door, Aidan was waiting in the hall. His expression was different. It was hard to read but undeniably intense.


“You lasted longer in there than I thought,” he murmured. “For a moment, I thought maybe you needed some help.”


“I’m fine,” I said quickly.


He tilted his head, then held up a glass in one hand. “Drink?”


I hesitated but took it anyway. The cool rim brushed my fingers as his lingered just a moment too long.


In the heavy silence that followed, his blue eyes held me captive. I realized leaving this house might prove far harder than stepping inside it.

CHAPTER 13


Josie’s POV


I was standing by the tractor, trying to determine if we would ever make this thing work. The grease smudged across my hands, and the late sun spread through the Blackwood ranch in amber light.


Aidan bent down as he reached past me to adjust a bolt. The space between us suddenly shrank until I could feel the heat of him and the faint smell of the spice of his cologne mixed with sweat.


“See?” he drawled, his breath brushing against my cheek. “We’re getting somewhere. We can make this beast work again.”


Before I could fire back and say not to get his hopes high. He suddenly dipped his head, and his mouth caught lips.


The world became blurred. My protest died on my lips, as it was replaced with a gasp and his lips pressed harder. I should’ve pushed him away. I knew I should.


Instead, I found myself answering him with an equal hunger I didn’t even know I had buried inside me. He framed my face, pulling me closer. I melted instantly into him. My fingers gripping his shirt, terrified he’d stop…


I jerked upright in bed, my heart hammering so violently against my ribcage. My sheets were tangled around me. It was damp with sweat. The familiar outlines of my bedroom walls loomed in the shadows. I was not in the barn, not even on the ranch.


Just a dream.


I pressed both palms to my burning cheeks while trying to slow my breath. No matter how hard I willed it away, his lips felt dangerously real and frighteningly sweet against my lips, and that terrified me more than the dream itself.


I felt annoyed, mostly with myself for having such a dream involving Aidan Callahan. I lay back on my bed, hoping I could catch some sleep before morning. Though I seriously doubt it now. I would surely be wide awake and thinking about our kiss in my dream.


True enough. The following morning, I stumbled down the stairs feeling like I hadn’t slept at all. My head throbbed, and my body felt heavy and sluggish. The real reason wasn’t my lack of sleep. It was my damn dream.


I still felt the touch of his mouth against mine, the way his hands had cradled my face, and the way I’d kissed him back like I wanted it, as if I’d been waiting for his kiss all along. It was only a dream, but the heat of it clung to me like a stain I couldn’t remove.


At the breakfast table, I pushed scrambled eggs and pancakes around my plate while my father discussed cattle prices with satisfaction. My mother fussed with her coffee, her voice carrying too brightly across the table.


“Josie,” she said suddenly, pulling me out of my reverie. “Your sister just called.”


I blinked, dragging myself back into the present time. The last thing I want to discuss this morning is my sister’s wedding. “Morgan? Why?”


“She already has a schedule set for bridesmaids’ dress fittings.” My mother’s face glowed with excitement. “This coming weekend, in New York. She wants you there with the others. Isn’t that wonderful?”


My fork clinked against the plate as my stomach twisted. New York. Bridesmaids’ fitting.


The words fell on me like a weight, heavier than my sleepless night.


I set my fork down, as my appetite was completely gone. “I don’t really see why I have to go all the way to New York for a dress fitting,” I said carefully, keeping my tone light. “We’ve got the shop where Sierra works right here in town. If Morgan sends me the design and the color, I’m sure I could find something close enough in Oakridge.”


My mother’s brows knit together. “Josie! It’s not about finding something close enough. It’s about the experience. Morgan wants all her bridesmaids together. You should enjoy the experience and this bonding with your sister.”


I took a sip of orange juice, mostly to buy time. I wanted to convince them that I didn’t have to go to New York. “Still, it feels unnecessary. I’ll just get Sierra to help me. I promise I’ll find something close enough to the bridesmaid’s dress she wants. You know Sierra has a good eye.”


The scrape of my father’s chair cut through the air as he shifted to look at me. His expression was sharp. “Enough, Josie.” His voice held unmistakable authority. “I already bought your ticket. Your sister is expecting you to be there for her, and the least you can do is be a supportive sister.”


The words sank into me like cold water. I stared at my father. “But—”


“I already bought the tickets yesterday,” he announced, folding his napkin with the kind of precision that left no room for argument. “They are non-refundable. You’re going.”


My stomach dropped. That was it. The decision had been made without even consulting me.


I forced a tight smile, though my hands clenched in my lap under the table. “Well, I guess that settles it then.”


My chest squeezed. New York wasn’t just for fittings and shopping. It was stepping straight into the lion’s den, watching Morgan float around in white lace while Wade stood at her side, exactly where I used to dream he’d stand with me.


***


The bell over my shop door jingled, and I put on my best smile, greeting the customer as if my mind wasn’t miles away. I moved through the motions, ringing up orders, wrapping purchases, and answering small talk.


But the whole time, my thoughts were stuck on one thing: New York.


The city itself was enough to make me anxious. It was too big, too fast, and too polished for a small-town girl like me. I’d always felt like a fish out of water the one time I’d visited the city years ago, trailing behind Morgan as she darted through crowds in her heels like she was ready to conquer the world, while I stumbled with my boots scraping against the pristine sidewalks.


Now I was supposed to show up there and smile through fittings. I had to stand in a room full of Morgan’s friends who’d never had dirt under their nails a day in their lives. I could already imagine their mean smiles and their subtle glances at my hair, my clothes, and everything about me.


My hand slipped on the ribbon I was tying around a package. I huffed out a sigh before redoing it again. Being out of place didn’t even begin to cover it. This wasn’t just about dresses. It was about being dragged into a world that had never belonged to me.


By the time the customer left, I leaned against the counter while staring out at Main Street. I had work to do here. I had customers who knew me by name. I had a rhythm that felt safe around here. New York was loud and sharp-edged for me. I couldn’t shake the feeling that stepping into that world would strip away the little bit of peace I’d finally started to rebuild for myself.


Thanks to my father’s firm resolve and that non-refundable ticket, I had no choice but to suffer a weekend in New York for my dear sister.


The sun had already dipped low as I rode Daisy toward the Blackwood ranch. The familiar path was almost soothing after a day of restless thoughts. Even the steady rhythm of Daisy’s hooves couldn’t chase away the weight pressing down on my chest.


New York.


Morgan.


That glittering world I didn’t belong in.


When I reached the fence, Aidan was already there. His sleeves rolled up, grease smeared across his forearm as he worked on the stubborn tractor. He glanced up when he heard Daisy. His mouth curved into that aggravating smile.


“You’re late,” he drawled, wiping his hands on a rag. “What kept you this time? Got lost on your way here?”


Normally, I’d have some sharp retort to his sardonic comment. But I only managed a weak shake of my head as I dismounted Daisy and tied her to the post. My mind was still on my trip to New York, surrounded by faceless strangers, this coming weekend.


I slipped through the fence and worked with him. Aidan watched me with a crease on his forehead. “You’re awfully quiet tonight, sweetheart. Did I finally manage to annoy you into silence?”


That earned a faint laugh from me, though I kept my eyes on the tractor. “Not everything’s about you, Callahan.”


“Oh, so it’s something else.” He leaned back against the tractor, crossing his arms. He lingered his gaze on me in a way that always made my pulse quicken. “Well, whatever it is, you look like you carry the world on your shoulder. Care to share?”


I tightened the bolt in my hands a little too forcefully. “It’s nothing.”


“Uh-huh,” he said, a smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth. “Funny how nothing has your eyes storming like that. Guess I’ll just have to work extra hard to drag it out of you.”


I shot him a sideways glance, still refusing to answer. Aidan’s smirk deepened like he’d won some invisible game.


I focused on the stubborn bolt, twisting until my wrist ached. Aidan didn’t move. I could feel his eyes still on me as if he was just waiting for me to crack.


He crouched beside me. He was close enough that I caught the faint mix of soap and his signature smell on his skin. “Talk to me, sweetheart. What’s got you chewing nails tonight?”


I bit down on my lip as I remembered my dream last night. I shook my head as I pushed those memories away. “I told you—it’s nothing.”


“Mmm.” His voice carried a teasing lilt again. “You’re a terrible liar, sweetheart. Your shoulders are all tight, your jaw’s locked, and you’ve been glaring at that poor bolt like it’s your worst enemy. Don’t make me keep guessing. I have all night.”


I snapped my gaze to him. “Why do you even care what’s bothering me? Let’s just fix this damn thing!”


A strange smile flickered across his lips. The one that wasn’t a smirk but still managed to rattle me even more. “Because I do.”


My throat tightened. I didn’t want to tell him. I didn’t want to discuss my upcoming trip to New York. I also knew how persistent he was. In the end, I found myself confessing just to keep him from pestering me. “I have to go to New York this weekend.”


Aidan blinked, then arched his brow as if he wasn’t sure he heard me right. “New York?”


I nodded. “For Morgan. She scheduled the bridesmaids’ dress fitting for this weekend. My parents already bought me a ticket. I might not be here to help with the tractor this coming weekend.”


He clicked his tongue. “Well, well. Josie Walker is off to the big city. Are you excited?”


I rolled my eyes. “Do I look excited to you? I don’t even like the city. It’s too crowded and too noisy.”


He chuckled, shaking his head. “Well, I agree. But I thought you might be excited since you’ll be seeing Wade Blackwood again.”


I glared at him insolently. “What—why would you even say that?”


He cocked a brow. “Come on, Josie. Don’t you think I haven’t noticed? The way your face changes every time his name comes up? The way you talk about this tractor like it was part of your happy childhood because Wade once showed you how to drive it.” He tilted his head, his lips curving into a sly grin. “You’ve got it bad for him, don’t you?”


“I do not.” I denied vehemently.


“Mmhmm.” He didn’t believe me. His look lingered as if he could peel away my denial layer by layer. “You’re going to New York for a dress fitting, but what you’re really dreading is seeing him, isn’t that right, Josie? Your precious Wade. Too bad he just happens to be marrying your sister.”


“Shut up, Callahan.”


“Don’t worry,” he murmured, eyes dancing with wicked amusement. He pushed off the tractor, closing our distance just close enough to make my pulse jump. “Your secret’s safe with me. But if you’re planning to keep denying it, you’ll have to do a hell of a better job hiding that expression on your face when his name is being mentioned.”


My hands curled into fists at my side. “I don’t have time for this. I came here to help with the tractor, not to listen to your ridiculous theories.”


“Ridiculous?” he echoed maddeningly. “Maybe. Or maybe I just see things you don’t want to admit.”


I refused to look at him. My heart hammered so hard it made my fingers tremble.


Aidan’s voice dropped. It crawled under my skin and refused to leave. “Don’t worry about New York, sweetheart. If things get complicated with your sister, with Blackwood, or with your sister’s crowd, I’ll come and find you.”


I whipped around to look at him. “What did you say?”


He grinned irritatingly. His smile was laced with something I couldn’t quite name. “I’ll be your knight in shining armor in New York, sweetheart. All you have to do is call, and I’ll come to get you.”


For a heartbeat, all I could do was stare at him. Without a word, I turned back to force myself to focus on our work, while deep inside, my chest felt like there was a raging storm brewing.


I hated more than anything that part of me wondered if he really meant his offer.

CHAPTER 14


Josie’s POV


The moment my boots touched the concrete ground of New York City, I knew it all over again. I didn’t belong here. I was a misplaced country girl in denim and cowgirl boots, dragging around my suitcase that looked as out of place as I felt.


New York City had a way of swallowing you whole, reminding you in every breath and every honk of a cab horn that you were small and unimportant.


It wasn’t my first time here. I’d come here once before, back when my heart was still foolish and hopeful. Back when I thought I could make a life in this city, closer to Wade. I thought maybe if I carved out a place for myself here, I’d prove I was enough to belong in his new world. I would be enough to matter to him too.


Unfortunately, the city didn’t want me. The night I packed my things and begged my father to buy me a plane ticket back to Georgia, I told myself I’d never set foot here again.


Yet here I was.


I didn’t understand why Morgan had been insistent on making me part of her bridal party. As far as I was concerned, we were not that close as sisters. We couldn’t be more poles apart.


“You’re welcome to stay with us, Josie,” Morgan offered the night before my flight to New York.


I knew it was the most logical solution since I would not be staying in the city longer than three days.


But the thought of Wade standing beside her, offering that gentle smile of his. The same smile that never failed to make my heart race. The thought of sleeping under the same roof as them, sharing their space, and witnessing their love again was unbearable to think about.


I’d rather sleep out on the cold streets of New York than witness their happiness all over again. So I’d lied. I smiled politely and told her I had other arrangements.


The truth was, I had none. But I’d figure it out. I am a grown woman. I could arrange my own accommodation.


Before I left Oakridge, something had changed. Aidan Callahan had finally made me put his number into my phone against my better judgment.


I told myself it was just practical, in case something unfortunate happened to me in this big city. I knew that he would tease me mercilessly for it, of course. So I have to make sure that I survive this weekend without a hitch.


“Call me, Josie. Anytime. I’ll come and get you,” he said seriously while looking at me dead in the eye.


That look in his face haunted me as I wheeled my suitcase down the crowded sidewalk, feeling the city pressing in around me. I hated that a small part of me wanted to take him up on his words.


I stood outside the glittering glass storefront. My reflection was warped in the immaculate windows of one of the most expensive bridal shops in New York.


I’d never even touched a dress that cost half of what the mannequins inside wore. My stomach twisted. It was not from nerves about the fitting itself, but from what—or rather, who—was about to arrive.


Sure enough, the sound of a car’s engine turned my head to the street. A sleek car pulled up to the curb, shining like everything else in this city that screamed money.


Morgan got out first. She was radiant as ever, her smile wide, and her happiness was obvious. I could almost believe she was born and raised in this city.


Wade came around to open her door fully, his hand resting lightly on her arm in that way that was second nature to him. My chest squeezed at the sight. He finally became aware of my presence, his expression softening. He greeted with a smile. “It’s good to see you again, Jo.”


I forced a polite smile that didn’t quite reach my eyes. “Hi, Wade.”


That was all. Nothing more. Because anything more and I’d betray myself with my promise.


Morgan leaned into him with a laugh, brushing her lips against his cheek before catching his mouth in a kiss right there on the sidewalk. I turned my gaze away, pretending to fuss with the strap of my bag. The city could’ve swallowed me whole right then, and I would’ve been grateful.


“I’ll see you later, babe,” Morgan sang as she finally pulled away, waving to him like a woman who had everything. She turned to me, oblivious to the way I kept my distance. “Come on, Josie. The others are already inside. You’re late.”


Of course I was. I always felt late where Morgan was concerned. As if I’d shown up to life after she’d already taken all the light.


“I’m sorry. I got stuck in traffic. That’s why I decided to come straight here instead of checking on my hotel first.” I apologized while falling into step beside her. As the glass doors slid open, I caught one last glimpse of Wade through the reflection, his gaze lingering on me a beat too long before he got back into his car.


The boutique smelled like roses. Every corner glittered with chandeliers that looked like they belonged in a palace. I felt out of place the second I stepped inside. My boots squeaked faintly against the glossy floor.


Morgan’s friends were already there. Three impeccably dressed women lounging on velvet chairs, sipping champagne like they did this every day. They turned in unison when Morgan led me in.


“This is Josie,” Morgan announced brightly, her tone saccharine. “My little sister. She came all the way from Oakridge for this dress fitting, girls.”


Their eyes swept over me quickly. I could practically hear the judgment in their silence. The one with sleek black hair tilted her head and murmured, “She’s…adorable. Like what you expect from a small-town sweet girl.”


The blonde with lipstick too red for daylight let out a tinkling laugh and added. “I can’t believe you have a sister like her, Morgan. You’re so well, so New Yorker. And she’s so—” Her gaze drifted over my jeans and plain shirt before landing on my face again. “—different from you.”


I could feel the heat rushing to my cheeks. I pressed my lips together, swallowing the sting of their mean comment.


Morgan didn’t defend me. She laughed along as if her friends’ comments were nothing but jokes. “I know, right? We’re total opposites. But…” she added, looping her arm through mine. “My little country girl sister is my maid of honor.”


I blinked at her. “What?”


Morgan smiled like a cat with cream. “You’re not just a bridesmaid anymore, Josie. I’ve decided you’re going to be my maid of honor.”


The words landed like a stone in my chest. My eyes widened in horror. “Morgan, that’s not what—”


Her smile faltered just enough for me to see the sharpness underneath, her eyes narrowing in warning. “It’s only right. You’re my sister. Of course, it should be you.”


The girls giggled approvingly, sipping more champagne for a toast.


I swallowed hard as the protest died in my throat. It wasn’t a choice, not with that glare. She had me cornered, like she always did. I forced a weak smile, though my insides twisted.


Maid of honor. Does she expect me to plan her bachelorette too? Expect me to stand at her side while watching her marry the only man I’d ever loved.


It felt like a punishment, wrapped in a ribbon and handed to me as an honor.


The boutique attendants swarmed around us with tape measures and silk hangers for sample dresses. Morgan’s friends settled deeper into their chairs, sipping champagne while their eyes followed me the way people watch a clumsy animal at the fair.


“Josie first,” Morgan announced, as though I were some test subject. She wanted to parade before the room. “She needs her maid of honor dress fitted.”


My stomach sank as the attendant returned with a dress draped carefully over her arm. My eyes went wide. It was floor-length but cut low at the front with thin straps and a slit that climbed scandalously high up the thigh.


Surely that was a mistake.


“I think this one is for—” I started.


Morgan cut in sharply. “No mistake, Josie. That’s yours. I specifically chose that for you.”


The attendant ushered me into the changing room. My fingers fumbled with the fabric as I slid it over my body. It clung in all the wrong places, hugging curves I usually hid beneath denim and soft cotton. When I stepped out, I tugged at the slit nervously. I could feel every gaze in the room rake over me.


Morgan’s friends erupted into giggles.


“Oh my god,” the blonde gasped, covering her mouth. “She’s blushing. That’s so cute.”


“She looks uncomfortable,” the sleek-haired one said with a smirk. “But it’s Morgan’s vision, so she’ll adjust. Right, Josie?”


My hands tried to pull the neckline higher, to no avail. “I don’t know, Morgan. This is really—”


“It’s perfect,” Morgan said firmly, sipping her champagne with a satisfied gleam in her eye. “You’ll stand out beautifully next to me. As my sister, that’s only expected, right?”


I shook my head slightly. “But I don’t feel—”


“Josie,” the blonde cut me off with false kindness. “It’s your sister’s day. Don’t be selfish.”


“Yes,” the other chimed in. “It’s not about you. It’s about the bride. You just need to suck it up for her vision. You want her day to be perfect, don’t you?”


My throat tightened. I wanted to disappear into the floor.


Morgan’s eyes pinned me in place, daring me to argue. I couldn’t. How could I? Not when I was outnumbered. The people pleaser in me forced a tiny nod while my cheeks were still burning from embarrassment.


Morgan’s friends clapped like they’d won something, and my sister’s smile only grew wider.


I never felt smaller than ever. I was draped in silk that wasn’t mine. I stood in a city that never wanted me. I played my part in a wedding I wished didn’t exist.


The minutes dragged on like hours. I sat stiffly in the corner of the boutique. The silk of my maid of honor dress itched against my skin while Morgan’s friends twirled and tidied in front of the mirrors. Their laughter rang sharp in my ears. That wasn’t meant for me but cut me anyway.


“Oh, this one hugs just right,” one of them said.


“Can you imagine the wedding pictures? Perfection,” the other one commented.


“Morgan, your vision is flawless. You’re going to have the most stunning wedding.”


Morgan laughed at their flatteries. They didn’t look at me. They didn’t see me. That was fine. I preferred it that way.


I folded my hands tightly in my lap, trying to keep my humiliation from showing on my face. I’d never felt so out of place in my life until today. The boutique lights seemed harsher. The noise seemed louder just beyond the glass, and I wanted nothing more than to vanish and get back to Oakridge.


My phone sat in my purse, a small weight that suddenly felt like my only lifeline. Without thinking, I pulled it out and typed a quick message to Aidan.


Does your offer still stand? I know it’s out of the blue, but it turns out I do need some help.


I stared at the screen for a moment, my thumb hovering before I hit send. My heart pounded hard as I watched the message sent away.


I didn’t really expect a reply. How could I? He was back in Oakridge. He was miles and miles away from here. The idea of him swooping in to save me from this humiliation was a childish fantasy. Something only my heart would dare conjure out of desperation.


By the time Morgan declared the fitting done, my spirit had been wrung dry. My legs felt heavy as I followed her and her friends out of the bridal shop. My head bowed while they chatted breezily about dinner plans. I would just make some excuses about developing a headache or something to skip the dinner they were planning.


Their conversation ceased as we made it outside. They stopped walking and stood frozen in place. I looked up to see what caught their attention, and then I froze too.


A man was leaning casually against an expensive black car parked at the curb. His arms were folded on his chest. I knew that stance far too well.


Aidan.


He was actually here!


For a moment, I couldn’t breathe. He looked like this was his world. He was dressed in an expensive suit, completely unbothered by the glances he drew from passersby, while his eyes were fixed only on me.


My heart stumbled hard against my ribs. All I could think was why he was here. Was it because I asked for his help? How did he get here so fast?


Aidan’s mouth curved into a devastating smile the second his eyes locked on me. The city lights caught the edges of his grin, making it even more dangerous.


“Hello, sweetheart,” he drawled as if we were the only two people on the sidewalk. “Are you done? I’m here to pick you up.”


And just like that, the world seemed to stop. Behind me, Morgan and her friends exchanged startled whispers. I felt their eyes on me, on the plain little sister they’d spent the last hour mocking while trying to figure out who this impossibly handsome man could be and what on earth he wanted with me.


Before, I could think better. Before the voice in my head could remind me that this was reckless, that we were still in public, and that Morgan was right there. I was already moving.


I ran straight into him. I flung myself at Aidan, my arms winding tightly around his neck. His warmth enveloped me instantly, and for the first time all day, I could finally breathe again.


Aidan chuckled. The sound felt delightful against my ear as his arms locked around my waist. He held me without hesitation, without care for who was watching.


“Missed me already?” he teased, his voice eased with laughter.


My throat went thick. It was too thick to speak. Too thick to tell him that yes, I had missed him, admitting that seeing him here felt like salvation I hadn’t dared hope for, so I just held on to him tighter.


Reluctantly, I eased back from Aidan’s embrace, though he didn’t let me go that far. He kept his hand firmly around my waist as if it belonged there.


The warmth of his touch radiated through me, grounding me in a way that I desperately needed after the disaster of the dress fitting.


He shifted slightly, angling us so I was tucked safely against his side while his gaze swept over Morgan and her friends. His expression was all easy charm, but I could feel the steel beneath it and the deliberate way he was staking his claim over me.


“Mind if I steal Josie away?” he asked smoothly. He smiled charmingly. “I’ve missed her all day, and I planned something special for us while she’s in the city.”


The sidewalk seemed to go utterly silent. Morgan blinked, her perfectly glossed lips parting before she caught herself. I didn’t miss the flicker in her eyes, the sharp spark of envy before she forced her smile back into place. It was tight and did not reach her eyes, no matter how hard she tried.


“Of course,” Morgan answered. “Go ahead.”


Morgan’s friends glanced at each other with thinly veiled judgment rippling between them. They weren’t used to seeing a small-town girl, the invisible sister, standing with a man like Aidan.


But in that moment, with his hand at my side and his smile meant only for me. I didn’t care what any of them thought.


Aidan looked down at me with that signature grin on his face. “Shall we go, sweetheart?”


I watched him open the passenger’s door of his luxury car. I slid inside without hesitation. I didn’t care where he would bring me as long as it was as far away from here as possible.
