CHAPTER 15


Aidan’s POV


The old tractor sat half-buried in dust and weeds when I first found it. A relic from another time. It rusted through with one tire deflated, and the engine was cold as stone. It looked like it hadn’t been touched in years, maybe since the day the Blackwoods packed up and left Oakridge.


I didn’t give that junk much thought that night. I’d been walking around the property, getting a feel for the land and the garbage that came with it. The Blackwood ranch had its share of them, no doubt.


Then, I remembered the way Josie kept coming to the border, even if she kept on saying that she didn’t want to have anything to do with me. The quiet reverence in her voice whenever Wade’s name slipped into a conversation she didn’t mean to have.


The idea came to me in a flash with a slow grin attached to the thought.


If I wanted Josie Walker to notice me, I had to let her guard down on me. What better way than through something she couldn’t ignore? Something tied to the boy she loved and the past she still clung to so stubbornly.


I didn’t know much about farm engines, but I knew enough about people. And Josie, the innocent and stubborn Josie, was the kind who wouldn’t resist helping if she thought something could be saved.


I couldn’t just hire someone to fix this garbage for me—or better yet, I could buy a new tractor. But if I wanted to get Josie’s attention, this tractor was the answer.


So I rolled up my sleeves, grabbed the toolbox left at the barn, and made sure that broken tractor was waiting when she finally emerged from that fence again.


What started as a game turned into something else entirely. The more we worked on that tractor together, the more I realized I wasn’t trying to fix a tractor anymore, just to get her attention. I was trying to figure out the girl who couldn’t seem to stop stirring my curiosity.


No woman had ever managed to do such a thing in the past. Not even the most famous socialite or the most famous supermodels with whom I usually hang out back in New York.


Nope. This small-town country girl, who didn’t even bother to hide her distrust of me, had managed to keep me entertained with her unusual nature.


It started with a simple agreement. We would meet every day, late in the afternoon, for a few hours while we try to fix this tractor.


She’d show up just before the sun began to dip. Her hair was pulled back, her cheeks still flushed from riding her horse, and that faint tiredness in her eyes she always tried to hide with a smile.


And every damn time, I’d tell myself this was about the tractor. About using this opportunity to get what I want, like everything else on this ranch.


Somewhere between wanting to burn this rusty tractor and keeping on fixing it for the sake of my plan, I found myself waiting for her outside the house until she showed up at dusk.


I didn’t know when I started looking forward to working on that shitty tractor. It just did.


Josie had this habit of singing under her breath while she worked. There were some days it was soft, barely there, as though she didn’t even realize she was doing it. While there were other days, she’d mutter to herself when something didn’t go right with that little frown pulling between her brows.


And her smell. God, her smell.


I told myself it was because she ran a flower shop. Of course, she’d smell like that. It was like a fresh bloom of flowers after a storm.


But even when she was elbow-deep in grease, with hair sticking out in places and dirt smudged across her cheek. I realized that it wasn’t roses or lilacs I caught on her scent. It was her unique scent.


I’d find myself leaning just a little closer than I should, just close enough to breathe her in. Just close enough to lose the line between teasing her and wanting to pull her closer just to see if she’d smell the same when she wasn’t pretending she didn’t notice me.


She drove me crazy. She wasn’t even aware of it. The scent of flowers stayed long after she had gone home at the end of our work session.


By the third afternoon, I couldn’t pretend anymore. That damned tractor could’ve stayed broken for all I cared. When Josie had walked into my life with grease on her hands, fire in her eyes, and a scent I couldn’t wash off even if I tried. I knew I was in for more serious trouble than I thought.


I wasn’t sure when it happened exactly.
When she managed to slip under my skin like a slow and steady burn, I couldn’t shake it. Maybe it was the way she smiled when she thought I wasn’t looking. Or that one afternoon when grease streaked across her cheek, and I told her about it. She laughed at herself with an embarrassed sound that made my chest tighten for no damn reason.


Whatever it was, none of the sophisticated women I’d met in the city ever managed to do what Josie Walker managed to do just by existing.


Josie didn’t even realize the kind of power she had over me. If she realized it, it would make her hella dangerous. She didn’t try to impress me. She didn’t even try to flirt. She didn’t even look at me the way I was used to being looked at by women.


Most of the time, she barely tolerated me. She was stubborn enough to make me forget why I ever wanted to get close to her to begin with.


And then, just when I thought I had her figured out. She told me she was leaving this weekend.


“New York,” she said.


A bridesmaid dress fitting for her sister’s wedding. It should’ve been a relief. A damn good thing.
A few days without her, without the scent of her hair, without the gleam in her eyes, or the way her laugh got under my skin like whiskey in my veins.


Except it wasn’t. The moment she said she’d be gone for the weekend, something inside me went tight. I told myself it was nothing, just a habit. A routine I’d gotten used to having her around, that’s all.


But then she looked up at me with that nervous little frown, worrying about leaving the tractor halfway done, as it mattered to me.


I realized I didn’t give a damn about the tractor anymore. I just didn’t want her to go and see her sister’s fiancé, the same man she secretly loves. I didn’t like the idea of her looking at that man with obvious love in her eyes.


So I said it. The first stupid thing that came to my head. “Don’t worry about New York, sweetheart. If things get complicated with your sister, with Blackwood, or with your sister’s crowd. I’ll come find you.”


I meant it as a joke. I wanted to see that blush crawl up her cheeks. I wanted her to roll her eyes at me again. And she did. But later that night, when I was alone, I couldn’t stop replaying it.


Because the truth was, if she ever called, I would come and get her. I’d drop everything and go. That was when I knew for certain Josie Walker wasn’t just under my skin anymore. She was in my blood now.


By Saturday morning, I was bound for New York, too. I told myself it wasn’t because of her. It was because of my company.


There were still projects in the city that needed my signature. Meetings I’d been putting off ever since I decided to hole up in Oakridge for my long-awaited revenge, as my lawyer put it.


Truth was, the satisfaction of knowing that I could finally have revenge on the people who wronged my father in the past was not the only thing I got when I arrived in Oakridge. I’d found an interesting woman with chestnut hair, who smelled like wildflowers and avoided me like I was the devil himself.


The sound of the engines moderated as the jet descended toward the private tarmac. The skyline rose into the morning cloud. I hadn’t realized how long it’d been since I’d last seen it.


New York City looked the same. For the first time, I felt like an outsider coming home.


The moment the plane rolled to a stop, the door opened to the brisk New York wind.


“Welcome back, Mr. Callahan,” my secretary, Lena, greeted me with her usual poise. She was efficient and reliable. Exactly how I liked my professional world to be. Predictable.


“Good to see you, Lena,” I said, grabbing my coat from the attendant as I descended the iron steps. A black SUV waited nearby.


“The schedule for your morning meetings is in the car,” she continued, walking briskly beside me. “The board requested a quick update on the acquisition of the SkyLuxe Airlines, and your ten o’clock call with the European partners has been moved to eleven.”


“Perfect.” I nodded, sliding into the backseat. The door closed, sealing me off from the noise outside.


The car glided out of the tarmac and into the blur of the city. Everything was fast and impersonal, just the way I used to like it.


But somewhere between the bridge and the avenue leading into Manhattan, I found myself staring out the tinted window, thinking about the sound of Josie’s laugh echoing in an old barn.


I forced my thoughts back to business.
I was here to work. To check on Callahan Holdings. To reassure the board that their elusive CEO hadn’t completely lost his mind.


Still, as the skyline came into sharper view. A single thought kept circling in my head like a restless current: Josie Walker was somewhere in this city. And for the first time since I’d left New York, it didn’t feel like coming home until I found her.


The view from my office stretched across Manhattan. The glass towers pierced the clouds, and the traffic below moved like veins pulsing through the city. From up here, everything looked small.


Except for the thoughts in my head.


Lena stood across from my desk with a tablet in her hand. She rattled off my schedule for the rest of the day. “At three, you have a conference call with the Tokyo investors. Then the partnership dinner at the Astoria tonight—”


I nodded absently, my attention flicking to the phone on my desk for the tenth time in five minutes.


Nothing.


No messages. No missed calls. No sign of her.


My thumb brushed over the screen again, almost unconsciously. Her contact name glared back at me: Josie Walker.


“Mr. Callahan?” Lena’s voice cut through my thoughts with professional politeness. “Should I move the Astoria dinner to tomorrow? You seem…” Her eyes flicked to my phone before she caught herself. “…preoccupied.”


I leaned back in my chair, exhaling slowly. “No need, Lena. Go on.”


She hesitated for a moment. Lena never hesitated. “Sir, you’ve been staring at that phone for the past ten minutes. Is there something wrong?”


I dragged my gaze away from my device as I straightened my tie just to have something to do with my hands. “No.”


“Are you sure?”


I almost laughed. If she just knew what made me agitated. “Yes. I’m sure,” I said smoothly.


She stared at me, clearly unconvinced but too well-trained to pry. “Very well, sir. I’ll confirm your appointments. Should I call the car for this evening?”


“Not yet.”


I waited until she left the room before leaning forward, my elbows on my desk while my phone was still in my hand.


The screen remained blank. A muscle twitched in my jaw. What the hell was I doing? This wasn’t me. I never waited for any woman’s call before. The audacity of this small-town girl to have me wait for her text as if I were a half-fool.


I had an empire to run. I had deals to close. People who waited on my calls. And yet, I couldn’t stop picturing her in the city, overwhelmed and uncomfortable while trying to hide behind that nervous smile of hers.


The thought made something coil tight in my chest. I glanced at the clock. It was nearly noon. I wondered what she was doing right now.


“Damn it,” I muttered under my breath. The words came out in a low growl as I slammed my phone face down onto my desk. The impact made a smash against the glass. “Fine. Maybe she doesn’t need my help after all. She’s a grown woman.”


I stood from my chair, pacing to the floor-to-ceiling glass window overlooking Manhattan. The city gleamed in the midday light. Everything I used to admire about it now irritated the hell out of me.


“She’s a grown woman,” I repeated out loud. “She can take care of herself.”


I almost believed it until my phone dinged. The sound sliced through the silence like a blade. I froze mid-step, my heart thudding hard before I turned to see my phone on the table.


The screen lit up. I dared to peek at the screen and saw her name along with her message: Does your offer still stand? I know it’s out of the blue, but it turns out I do need some help.


For a second, my world tilted. My throat tightened not from panic but from something sharper.


Before I could take my next breath, I was already moving. My chair spun as I grabbed my suit jacket while snatching my phone off the desk.


“Mr. Callahan—?” I heard Lena’s confused voice as I stormed past her table outside my office.


“Cancel the rest of my appointment today,” I barked without slowing down. “Something came up unexpectedly.”


“But sir, the Tokyo call—”


“Cancel it.”


The elevator doors were sliding shut when she tried calling my name again, but I ignored her. The faint sound of her heels clacking after me echoed just before the elevator’s doors closed.


I leaned against the elevator wall with my pulse hammering. Josie was asking for my help. Something twisted deep inside me with a mixture of adrenaline and anger, maybe even fear. I wasn’t sure which. All I knew was that I needed to see her.


My car was already waiting out front with the engine idling. My driver barely had time to open the door, but I told him I would be driving. He looked confused, but he didn’t say anything.


As I made my way to the boutique where they had a dress fitting. I realized something: for the first time in a long damn while, I wasn’t chasing a business deal.


I was chasing her.


Fuck.


The car engine purred beneath me as I eased into a parking spot across from the boutique.


The neighborhood was alive with noise, but everything around me dampened the second I spotted her through the glass window.


My Josie. She stood there, looking stiff and out of place. She was surrounded by lace and people who’d never know what real work felt like. Her hands were clasped in front of her with a faint frown tugging at her lips. She looked like she wanted to disappear.


I loosened my tie and pulled it off, tossing it onto the passenger seat. I leaned against the car door, folding my arms as I watched her from across the street like a common stalker.


God, she looked so damn beautiful. She looked lost and vulnerable in all the wrong ways for this city.


Something in my chest twisted. I told myself I was just here to make sure she was fine. That I’d see her out safe, then go back to the office.


But the truth was simpler and a hell of a lot more dangerous. I just wanted to see her. To make sure she knew she wasn’t alone here. I waited, leaning against my car for Josie to come out and see that when I said I’d come for her. I meant every damn word.


She stepped out into the fading New York light some time later. She was clutching her bag tightly. Her shoulders were tense as if she’d been holding her breath for hours. Her cheeks were flushed, probably from exhaustion, when her eyes found me leaning against my car. Everything else fell away.


Her lips parted in disbelief. Hell, I almost smiled just at the sight of that expression on her face.


I pushed off the hood, letting an easy grin spread on my face. “Hello, sweetheart,” I drawled more roughly than I intended. “Are you done? I’m here to pick you up.”


Her eyes widened with disbelief. Before I could take another breath, she ran right into me. She wrapped her arms around my neck tightly.


I just stood there. My hands came up on instinct, wrapping them around her waist. I could feel the tremor in her breath against my chest.


A low chuckle escaped me. “Miss me already?”


She didn’t answer. Her fingers only clutched my shirt tighter, as if she was afraid I might vanish if she let go. Behind her, I could hear her sister and her friends whispering with each other.


I paid them no mind.


Right now, all I could think about was Josie. This small-town girl pressed securely against me. When she finally pulled back, her face was flushed, and her eyes were shining with a dozen things she couldn’t say.


I kept an arm around her, turning slightly to face the onlookers. “Mind if I steal her for a bit?” I said, meeting her sister’s gaze squarely. “I’ve missed her all day, and I planned something special for us while she’s in the city.”


Morgan’s polite smile didn’t reach her eyes. “Of course. Go ahead.”


Josie shifted beside me. She was still flustered and still silent.


I glanced down at her at the way her fingers fidgeted at her side and murmured just for her, “Let’s get out of here.”


Then I opened the car door and guided her in, the heat between us thick enough to choke me.


Because I could finally admit to myself that I hadn’t flown across the country just to check on her. I’d come because she was the one thing I couldn’t stay away from anymore.

CHAPTER 16


Josie’s POV


I couldn’t believe he actually came.


One message. One desperate message, and here he was. Aidan Callahan was leaning against an expensive car outside a Fifth Avenue boutique like he owned the entire city.


Now I sat beside him inside his car as the city lights flashed across his face as he drove. One of his hands was on the steering wheel while the other rested casually on the gearshift.


The sleeves of his shirt were rolled up on his arm, his tie was gone, and his hair was slightly tousled. He looked like he’d been running his fingers through his hair the moment he saw my message.


The engine of his car filled the silence between us. I couldn’t believe that he was already in New York before I even texted him. Or did he actually fly straight here from Oakridge just because I said I needed him?


The idea was insane. Absolutely insane. And yet, the thought made my chest feel too tight.


I glanced at him sideways. His jaw was set, and his eyes were fixed on the road ahead, but there was something unreadable in his expression. Something that said he wasn’t in the mood for his usual teasing.


The silence between us wasn’t awkward exactly. It was charged. Every time his hand shifted on the wheel, every time the car slowed at a red light and his gaze flicked toward me, that made my pulse jump.


I tried to look out the window, but all I could see was my own reflection. My cheeks were flushed, and my eyes were wide with a question I didn’t dare say out loud.


What was he doing here, really?


Did I mean that much to him, or was this just another game he was winning far too easily?


“Why are you looking at me like that?” Aidan’s voice cut through the silence.


I blinked and tore my gaze from the window before staring at him. “What?”


He glanced at me briefly, the corner of his mouth twitching. “Your face is all too serious, as if I’ve kidnapped you instead of rescuing you.”


“You didn’t rescue me,” I said, crossing my arms while trying to sound firm.


“You could’ve fooled me,” he murmured with a laugh. “That message you sent didn’t exactly scream independent woman who doesn’t need saving.”


I blushed intensely. “I only want to see if you would actually come.”


“Sure you were.” He flashed me that infuriating half-smile. “And I just happened to be a few blocks away with nothing better to do.”


My eyes narrowed at him. “Were you really in New York before I even texted you, or weren’t you?”


He didn’t answer right away. He drummed his fingers on the steering wheel while a small smile played on his lips. “Does it matter?”


“Yes,” I answered. “It does.”


He finally glanced at me. “Then let’s just say I was around, and when you called, I came immediately.”


That alone shouldn’t have made my pulse skip. It shouldn’t have made my stomach flutter as it did. But Aidan had a way of making everything sound like a promise wrapped in trouble.


I turned my face back to the window, allowing my cheeks to cool down. “You’re incorrigible.”


“Only to you,” he shot back lightly.


I rolled my eyes, but I still could feel his smirk even without looking. The worst part was that a tiny part of me was smiling too.


“Where are we going, exactly?” I asked, breaking the silence when the car turned down a street I didn’t recognize.


Aidan didn’t look at me right away. His eyes stayed on the road, but that smirk of his appeared again. “Didn’t I say I was going to whisk you away?”


I groaned softly, leaning back against my seat. “Were you serious? I thought you just said that lie for my sister and her friends.”


He raised one eyebrow in my direction. “I’m serious when I say that, you know.”


“Oh…”


“Mmm.” His smirk deepened. From the look on his face, it seemed that he was enjoying my reaction far more than necessary. “You think I’m just going to dump you somewhere and head my way back to my office? Is that it?”


I shot him a look that only made him chuckle. “I didn’t say that. Seriously, where are we going?”


“Dinner,” he said finally, his tone shifting just enough to sound normal. “There’s this place I know. It’s quiet and has good food. It’s not far from here.”


“Oh,” I fiddled with the hem of my sleeve. “You know a restaurant in New York?”


“I know a few,” he admitted. “I lived here long enough before I decided small-town life might be better for a change.”


I blinked at him. “Why on earth will you exchange your well-off life in the city for a life in a quaint little town?”


He laughed under his breath. “Even people like me sometimes get tired of skyscrapers and fake smiles.”


I studied him for a long moment. There was something in his profile, in the faint shadow of exhaustion at the corner of his mouth, that made me forget my next snarky comment.


Before I could ask what he meant, he glanced at me once more, and the teasing glint returned in his blue eyes. “Don’t look so worried, sweetheart. I’m not about to sell you on the joys of small-town living again.”


“Good,” I muttered, though the corner of my mouth twitched.


“Relax,” he reassured, with his hands resting loosely on the wheel. “It’s just dinner.”


But the way he said that made it sound like anything but. When the car finally slowed to a stop, I blinked in surprise. This wasn’t what I expected at all.


The car eased to a stop in front of a tall building. I never expected a high-end place like this to have our dinner. This kind of place served food I couldn’t even pronounce and plates too pretty to touch.


Aidan stepped out and rounded to the car to open my door. I immediately realized that I didn’t belong here. The restaurant sat tucked between two old brownstone buildings. The restaurant sign screamed nothing but luxury.


La Rue. It was written with looping gold letters above the door. Through the windows, I could see the soft glow of candlelight that made everything inside scream opulence. There was also a faint sound of jazz piano drifting into the street.


“This is not what I expected,” I admitted, glancing at him as he offered his hand.


Aidan’s smile was faint but smug. “Is it not? I am hoping that you will like it here.”


I hesitated before accepting his offered hand. “You knew this place?”


“I used to come here a lot when I still lived here.”


The hostess greeted him with immediate recognition. “Mr. Callahan! It’s been a while.”


He inclined his head politely. “Yes, it’s been a while.”


She smiled and led us toward one of the best tables near the glass window. As we passed through the dining room, I couldn’t help but notice the way a few heads turned in his direction. I couldn’t help but wonder why. Maybe it was because Aidan Callahan carried that kind of energy that drew attention without even trying.


When we sat at our table, I leaned forward as I lowered my voice so he could be the only one to hear me. “Who really are you?”


He raised an eyebrow, clearly entertained by my confusion. “Seriously? Why are you asking me that now?”


“I think you are not who you seem to be.”


“How’s so?”


“You drive a fancy car. You dressed too nicely. You said you used to live here, and you used to eat in a fancy restaurant like this. It makes me wonder how wealthy you are.”


He chuckled softly, swirling the glass of wine in front of him. “Maybe not as wealthy as your father, sweetheart.”


“I don’t think so.”


Aidan looked up with an intense gaze. “I’m just a man who happens to know what I want.”


Something in the way he said it sent a shiver down my spine. I looked away, suddenly finding the menu very interesting. “And what do you want, exactly?”


He leaned back. There was a mirthless smile playing on his lips. “Tonight? Dinner. Good company. And maybe a smile from you that isn’t forced.”


“You’re asking a lot,” I muttered.


“I think just enough.”


The dinner came in quiet intervals, as the silverware clinked softly, the low jazz filling the space between us. The flickering candlelight painted Aidan’s face in warm shadows, emphasizing the sharp line of his jaw and the lazy confidence in his movements.


He’d ordered for both of us without even asking me what I wanted. And much to my dismay, he’d chosen perfectly. The pasta was rich, buttery, and nothing like the fancy, tiny-portion nonsense I’d expected.


“So,” Aidan said after a while, leaning back in his chair. “Tell me about your day. How was the fitting?”


I made a face, recalling the humiliating scene in the bridal shop. “Awful.”


He grinned. “That bad?”


“Worse.” I stabbed a piece of bread a little too hard. “Morgan’s friends are… well, you’d need to meet them to understand. Let’s just say they made me feel like I should’ve stayed in Oakridge and saved myself the humiliation.”


Aidan tilted his head, watching me closely. “And yet, here you are. You survived.”


“Barely,” I muttered. “You really didn’t have to come all this way just because I sent one stupid message.”


“Didn’t I?” His tone was mild, but his eyes gleamed with something that made my pulse jump.


“You’re in New York,” I commented. “Why? Don’t tell me you just dropped everything and flew here because I texted you.”


He smiled faintly. “You think I’d do that?”


“I don’t know. Would you?”


Aidan didn’t answer right away. He poured himself a bit of wine. He lifted the glass, studying the deep red swirl before him. “Maybe I already had business here. I just happened to be here at the same time as you.”


I frowned. “That’s not an answer.”


“It’s the only one you’re getting tonight.” He set the glass down, leaning against the table a little bit. “Besides, I’m more curious about you. You’ve been thinking about me, haven’t you?”


I almost choked on my water. “What? No! Where the hell did you get the idea?”


He chuckled under his breath. His eyes were full of wicked amusement. “That was a little too fast for an answer, sweetheart.”


I glared at him. “You’re impossible, do you know that?”


“Guilty,” he admitted softly. For a moment the teasing faded from his face. It was replaced by something quieter. Something that made my stomach flutter without a reason.


He dropped his voice. “You looked like you were about to cry when you walked out of that store.”


I was startled at his assumption. “You saw that?”


“I saw everything,” he said simply. “Including the way you held your chin up like you weren’t going to let them see they’d gotten to you.”


For a heartbeat, neither of us spoke, and the noise of the restaurant faded.


I was the one who looked away first. “You shouldn’t notice things like that.”


“Too late,” he murmured. “I see everything about you, Josie.”


I tried to steer the conversation back. I needed space to breathe normally again. “You never answered me earlier. Why are you really here, Aidan?”


He smiled. “Maybe because I just missed the way you look at me when you’re trying not to.”


My heart skipped. I wanted to argue. I wanted to deny it, but he was looking at me like he could see every lie I could ever tell.


And maybe he could.


The waiter had just taken our plates when my phone buzzed against the table. I almost ignored it. Aidan’s gaze was already heavy enough, but the name flashing on the screen made my stomach drop.


Wade.


The lightness that had been bubbling between us deflated in an instant. I swallowed hard, forcing my hand to move and unlocking my phone screen.


Where are you? Morgan said someone picked you up from the boutique. She’s worried.


The lump in my throat tightened. Someone. God, even in text, it sounded wrong.


“What’s wrong?” Aidan’s voice was sharp enough to make me freeze.


“Nothing,” I lied quickly, locking my phone again.


“Josie.” There was a warning in his tone that I couldn’t ignore.


I sighed deeply. “It’s Wade.”


His jaw flexed instantly. “Wade?”


“Yes,” I answered quietly. “He was just asking where I was. Morgan must’ve told him that I left with someone I didn’t even bother introducing.”


“Someone,” Aidan mocked, leaning back in his chair with that dangerous calm I’d come to recognize. “Let me guess, he’s worried.”


I didn’t answer, which apparently was answer enough for him. Aidan’s eyes darkened, and the lazy warmth in his eyes was gone. “Of course he is. The perfect fiancé, always looking out for your sister. And apparently, for you too, huh?”


“That’s not fair.” My voice came out smaller than I wanted. “He’s just… he doesn’t know that you’re the same man who bought their ranch in Oakridge.”


He gave a humorless laugh. “No, I bet he doesn’t.”


The air between us shifted. I tried to hold his gaze, but the anger and something else burning behind his eyes made it hard for me to breathe. “Aidan, it’s not like that. Wade’s just—”


“Wade’s just what?” he cut in sharply. “The boy you used to love? Or the man who’s marrying your sister?”


My pulse thundered painfully in my ears. “Stop it. Let’s not talk about this. Let’s just change the subject.”


I saw the way his hand flexed against the tablecloth, and his jaw became tight. “He shouldn’t get to worry about you anymore, Josie. Not when he gave that right away, the moment he put a ring on your sister’s hand.”


There was a raw edge in his voice that startled me. He wasn’t teasing. He wasn’t playing his usual games.


“I don’t belong to anyone,” I said quietly.


He met my eyes again, the fire in his eyes fading to something softer. Something that almost hurt to look at. “I know,” he murmured. “That’s the problem.”

We drove in silence for a while. Just the sound of his car’s engine and the muted sound of traffic passing by could be heard around us. I could feel the tension still hanging thick in the air, and the weight of what had been said at dinner pressed against my chest.


Aidan exhaled, running a hand through his hair. “I shouldn’t have snapped like that. I’m sorry.”


I turned to look at him. “You were right, though,” I murmured. “I shouldn’t have been so defensive.”


He gave a small, crooked smile. “I guess we’re both terrible at playing nice.”


That made me laugh, and just like that, the sharp edges between us softened. By the time we neared my hotel, the tension had finally thinned. It was replaced by something warmer and more familiar, even.


“Thank you,” I told him. “Thank you for coming to get me today. I was really close to losing it back there.”


His grin was almost boyish. “Ah, so I did save the damsel after all.”


I rolled my eyes. “I’m not a damsel.”


He leaned his elbow on the steering wheel while looking at me. “Well, if I’m the knight in shining armor, then I think I deserve some kind of payment for my gallantry, don’t you think?”


I arched a brow. “What kind of payment are we talking about, Mr. Callahan? A new spare part for the tractor?”


He scowled at me. “What the hell?” He tapped his cheek with his finger while there was a playful glint in his eyes. “A kiss right here should be enough.”


My jaw dropped. “You’re kidding!”


“A kiss will not hurt your pride.”


I swatted his arm as my face burned. He was insufferable, and he knew exactly what he was doing.


“Fine,” I muttered, mostly to shut him up. “If that’s what it takes to keep your ego from exploding. I think a cheek-to-cheek kiss should be enough.”


He looked genuinely surprised, but before he could say another word. I leaned toward him for just a quick kiss on the cheek and nothing more. But at the same time, Aidan turned his head to say something, and our lips met accidentally.


The world went still. The city lights outside blurred into gold and white streaks through the windshield, and all I could feel was the warm press of his mouth against mine. It was brief, but it was enough to steal my breath.


When we pulled back, his blue eyes searched mine. They were wide with something halfway between shock and hunger.


“Josie…”


I couldn’t find my voice. My heart was beating too fast while my face was burning. “That was an accident.”


“Sure,” he agreed, his lips curving slowly into that dangerous smile that always undid me. “Let’s call it that way.”


I turned to open the car door and fled away inside the hotel lobby before he could see the smile tugging at my own lips.

CHAPTER 17


Josie’s POV


I was finally back in Oakridge.


But even I was surrounded by the familiar silence of the town I’d always known. My mind refused to settle.


Every time I closed my eyes, I was back in Aidan’s car in New York. Where his hand was on the steering wheel, and the city lights were flashing across his jaw. The moment his lips met mine, unexpectedly, I felt the electric and unforgettable spark that lit inside me.


It had been days since I came home, and that kiss still haunted me. I’d dreamed about it twice now. Both times, I woke up breathless. My heart was pounding so loud it filled the silence of my room.


And that was the problem.


Because the kiss wasn’t supposed to mean anything. It was an accident, just a mistimed movement between two people who spent far too much time teasing each other. At least, that’s what I kept telling myself.


The more I think about it, the more I feel that it didn’t feel like an accident at all.


I shook my head, dragging myself out of my thoughts as I sat behind the counter of my shop. The vases gleamed in the morning light, the flowers bursting in soft pinks and whites. I should’ve been focused on work, on deliveries and arrangements.


My phone buzzed again, pulling me right back into the confusion I’d been trying to ignore.


It was from Wade: You made it home okay?


My chest tightened. This was his third message this week.


Before my recent trip to New York, Wade rarely texted me unless it was my birthday or a special occasion, such as Thanksgiving or wishing me a Merry Christmas. But lately, he kept finding excuses to message me. He would ask random questions about the flowers I think they should use for the wedding. The weather in Oakridge right now. How did the trip go?


And I didn’t know how to deal with it.


I barely replied anymore, and yet, the messages kept coming. Part of me wanted to ask him why. Why now, after everything? Why, when I was finally trying to move on?


But another part of me, the one that still remembered the sting of what could never be, didn’t dare open that door again.


I locked my phone and took a deep breath. I pushed those thoughts out of my head.

Too bad, I think he was still in New York. When I went to ride at the border, his house was still dark, and there was no sign of his ostentatious truck. It made me wonder when he would be home.

“Look who is back from the big city!” Sierra’s cheerful voice carried through the air before I even looked up.


I blinked. I was startled to see both her and Amber walking in. Sierra, with her ever-present sunglasses perched on top of her head, and Amber cradling her little four-month bump like it was already her entire world.


“Surprise!” Amber said with a grin. “We figured you’d be here drowning yourself with work again.”


“I was not,” I answered, though Sierra’s knowing smirk said otherwise.


“Mm-hmm,” she drawled, eyeing me as she plopped down on one of the stools near the counter. “You’ve got that mentally somewhere else, look. Let me guess, New York didn’t suit you?”


Amber laughed softly, easing herself into the chair beside her. “Or was it Morgan and her mean girl attitude again? Come on, tell us. How bad was it?”


I groaned, leaning against the counter. “Let’s just say my sister was in full bridezilla mode.”


Sierra snorted. “As expected.”


“She made me maid of honor,” I added.


Both their eyes widened.


Amber’s jaw dropped. “No way!”


Sierra let out a low whistle. “Wow. What is she planning?”


“I am not sure,” I muttered. “Her friends were there too. Perfect hair, perfect dresses, and perfect attitudes. I felt like a misplaced extra in one of those glossy bridal magazines.”


Amber chuckled. “Oh, honey, I bet you still looked better than all of them combined.”


“Hardly,” I said, though the corners of my mouth lifted. It felt good to laugh again until Amber’s next words made my stomach twist for an entirely different reason.


“You know,” she said, “thanks to your handsome neighbor, the town finally stopped talking about me.”

Amber nodded, her eyes sparkling mischievously. “Yup. Word spreads fast in our dear little town. People can’t stop wondering about the mysterious new Blackwood ranch owner.”


Sierra turned to me with a wicked smile. “How is he lately, Jo?”


I rolled my eyes, feeling my cheeks warm for some reason. I was reminded again of the kiss we shared back in New York. “How should I know? We’re not even that close.”


Amber grinned knowingly. “Right. And I’m not craving pickles dipped in chocolate.”


“Amber!”


She laughed. “Come on, Josie. We’re your friends, you know. We can tell something happened.”

Amber arched a brow. “Uh-huh. Sure. If you bother to explain the blush that spread through your cheeks when we mention him?”

I blinked, forcing a laugh. “Oh, please,” I touched my cheek consciously. “This is just because of the weather and nothing more.”


Amber smirked. “Sure, sweetheart. Keep telling yourself that.”

And if I wasn’t careful, he’d become mine too.

“Careful with those, boys!” My mother’s voice rang out from across the yard. She stood near the barn, overseeing everything.


“Mom?” I called, walking toward her. “What’s going on?”


She turned to me with a smile. “Your sister, Morgan. She decided to move the wedding here! Isn’t that exciting, Josie?”


I stopped dead in my tracks. “What? Here? Why? I thought she’d have her wedding in Napa Valley?”


“She changed her plan,” she said, clasping her hands together. “She wants a more authentic Southern feel, as she put it. She’s scrapping the Napa Valley plans entirely. Your father’s thrilled. He says it’ll be good for the town and for us.”


“For us?” My voice came out faintly.


Mom gestured toward the barn, where a couple of workers were already unloading beams. “He’s planning to renovate the barn. Clean it up and add new lights. Maybe even refinish the floors. You know how your father gets once he’s got a project. He wants everything perfect for Morgan’s big day.”


My stomach dropped.


“Isn’t that wonderful?” Mom asked again. Her face was bright with pride. “Your sister is getting married in our home.”


I forced a smile, though my heart was a tangled mess. “Yeah. That’s wonderful.”


I made my way inside to change into my riding attire. I told myself I was just going to check if he was back. Nothing more. Just curiosity, then I would go home.


I saddled Daisy and made my way toward the border. My heart had other plans. Every thud of her hooves matched the restless beat in my chest.

There he was.

The sight made something inside me loosen. Something I hadn’t realized until I saw him again.


When he finally turned and spotted me at the fence, he froze before his whole face broke into a grin. He dropped his bag right there in the dirt and spread his arms wide.


“Well, are you going to sit up there all day, or are you planning to say hello?” He said with that easy drawl that never failed to do terrible things to my heartbeat.


Without thinking twice, I swung off Daisy’s back and ran toward him.


Aidan chuckled as he caught me. He closed his arms around my waist effortlessly, spinning me just enough to make me laugh. The solid warmth of him. It was all too familiar.


“Miss me that much, huh?” he teased, still holding me close.


I pulled back just enough to glare at him, though my cheeks were burning. “Don’t flatter yourself.”


He laughed, that deep sound rumbling in his chest. “If this is the kind of welcome I get after my return. Maybe I should disappear to the city more often.”


“Don’t you dare,” I said before I could stop myself. 
Then added quickly, to hide my embarrassment. “I mean, we have a tractor to fix. Your presence is needed here.”


His smile softened. And for a moment, he just looked at me. The teasing faded into something that made the world around us stand still.


He brushed a loose strand of hair from my face and said, “I guess I’ll have to stick around, then. For the tractor, I mean.”


Aidan didn’t let me go right away, not until I squirmed a little in his hold. Even then, his hands slid down slowly before settling on his hips. His eyes were tracing my face like he was making sure I was really there.


“How are you, sweetheart?” he asked gently. The teasing was edged with something that almost sounded like concern.


I cleared my throat, trying to step back and reclaim some space between us. “I’m fine. I’m glad to be back. The city isn’t exactly my kind of place. Too much traffic. Too many people. Too much of everything.”


“I could’ve told you that,” he smirked. “Too loud, too crowded. Not enough fresh air or horses.”


“Or peace,” I added with a smile.


His expression warmed, and then his tone shifted. “Speaking of peace, what’s all that ruckus over at your house? I saw trucks unloading wood and steel, like someone’s building a new barn.”


I let out an exasperated sigh. “Not a new barn. A wedding.”


That made his brow lift. “A wedding?”


“Yeah. Morgan’s wedding. She decided Napa Valley wasn’t authentic enough. Now she wants to have a rustic Southern wedding right here at our ranch.”


Aidan blinked. “You’re kidding.”


“I wish I were.”


He chuckled under his breath, shaking his head. “So let me get this straight, you mean the same sister who thinks dirt is a biohazard wants a country wedding on her family’s ranch?”


“Apparently, yes.”


He grinned, as he was downright amused now. “Well, I have to see this. Bet your folks are thrilled.”


“Mom’s over the moon. She’s been fluttering around like a wedding planner on caffeine. Dad’s willing to spend an unlimited fortune to make Morgan’s dream wedding a reality.”


Aidan leaned against the fence. “And you?”


I hesitated, biting my lower lip. “I’m fine.”


He laughed softly. “You don’t look fine?”


“She’s bringing the entire New York crowd down. They’ll probably just complain about the dirt and make me wish I was the one getting married just so I could escape.”


Aidan chuckled, shaking his head. “I can’t believe it. You Walker girls sure know how to stir up the quiet.”


“I didn’t do anything.”


“Didn’t have to,” he said with that knowing smile. “You’re part of the storm, sweetheart, whether you like it or not.”


I rolled my eyes. “You’re wrong.”


He tilted his head while his eyes glinted. “But admit that you missed me.”


I rolled my eyes, shaking my head at him. “You should get some rest. You’re probably jet-lagged since you’re spouting nonsense.”


He grinned, that lopsided smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth. “Nonsense, huh?”


I crossed my arms, fighting the smile threatening to escape. “Yes. I’ll let you rest from all your nonsense.”


I started to turn on my heel as I headed toward Daisy but felt far too aware of him behind me. The air seemed thicker somehow, charged with something I didn’t dare name. I was almost at Daisy’s reins when his voice caught me.


“Josie.”


I slowly looked back at him. He was standing by the fence, the fading sunlight casting long shadows across his face. His expression was unreadable at first, then his mouth curved in a lazy smile.


“I missed you.”


The words landed like a stone thrown into still water, rippling through me while scattering every thought. My heart tripped and stumbled against my ribs. For a second, I forgot how to breathe.


I wanted to say something, but the words wouldn’t come out. Instead, I swallowed hard and forced a shaky smile. “Goodnight, Aidan. See you tomorrow.”


I turned before I could see his reaction, before I could make a fool of myself.  I swung onto Daisy’s back, and I could still feel his eyes on me.


Even as I rode away, that single sentence echoed in my head, over and over, until it was all I could hear.


I missed you.


And maybe, just maybe, that was the most dangerous thing he’d ever said to me.

CHAPTER 18


Josie’s POV


I perched on the top rail of the fence that separated the Walker ranch from Blackwood. One boot hooked on the lower rung while my chin rested on my knees.


From here, I had the perfect view of the chaos unfolding on our side of the property.


The hammering of nails, the buzz of saws, and the constant barking of instructions filled the air. The dust swirled in lazy spirals that were carried by the late-afternoon breeze.


Apparently, my father had changed his mind about renovating the old barn. He was now building an entirely new barn because Morgan’s dream of a rustic southern wedding couldn’t possibly be achieved in a structure that still had some character left in it.


Beside me, Aidan balanced easily on the fence. His elbows were resting on the top rail as if he’d done this a hundred times before. He followed the movement of the construction crew with an unreadable expression.


A faint smirk tugged at the corner of his mouth. “Your family doesn’t do things halfway, do they?”


I sighed, tucking a loose strand of hair behind my ear. “Apparently not. When Morgan wants something, she gets it, no matter how impractical or expensive it is.”


Aidan chuckled humorlessly. “A new barn for a single wedding. That’s dedication.”


“More like pride for his golden daughter,” I muttered as a reluctant smile tugged at my lips.


We’d meant to work on the tractor this afternoon, same as always. The plan was to check the fuel line and maybe repaint a few parts. But somehow, neither of us had made it that far. The noise from the construction crew had caught our attention, and now here we were.


Two supposed adults sitting like kids on a fence, watching other people work.


“I can’t decide if the current situation is entertainment or torture,” Aidan mused, nudging me lightly with his elbow.


“Both,” I admitted. “Mostly torture. At least, on my part.”


He turned his head toward me then, that blue-eyed gaze catching me off guard. “You look different when you laugh.”


The air seemed to be still between us. My heart gave a stupid little lurch, and I tore my eyes away before I could forget how to breathe.


“Don’t start with your nonsense again, Callahan,” I muttered, jumping down from the fence to busy myself with brushing off imaginary dust from my jeans.


He just laughed softly behind me, like he knew exactly what kind of effect he was having on me and was enjoying every second of it.


Aidan landed beside me with the ease of someone who’d spent half his life climbing fences. “Are we back to work, boss?”


“Yes,” I said, ducking under the hood of the old tractor. “You tighten the bolts, and I’ll check the fuel line again. It’s still sputtering.”


He grunted his approval and moved around to the other side. For a while, the only sounds were the clinking of metal, the scrape of tools, and the low conversation from the nearby crew. The kind of companionable silence we’d fallen into easily over the past weeks.


My phone beeped. I ignored it, and then a minute later, it sounded again. I grimaced, muttering a soft curse as I reached for the wrench.


Aidan’s voice came from the other side of the tractor. It was calm but edged with curiosity. “Someone’s eager to talk to you.”


“It’s nothing,” I replied dismissively, tightening the bolt harder than necessary.


“It doesn’t sound like anything.”


I exhaled deeply. I knew he wouldn’t stop until I told him.  Besides, there was no reason to hide it. “It’s just Wade.”


Aidan’s head appeared from behind the tractor, his brows pulling together. “Wade? As in your sister’s fiancé?”


I grimaced. “Unless there’s another person you know with that name.”


He straightened, his arms folding over his chest. “And why is your sister’s fiancé texting you? Is it about the wedding?”


I shrugged, pretending not to notice the irritation in his voice. “He’s been… well, persistent lately. Asking about who picked me up in New York, making small talk about ranch work. He is telling me how he is missing Oakridge lately. Nothing important.”


“Persistent,” Aidan repeated flatly, his jaw flexed. “Does your sister know about this?”


“I don’t know. And honestly, I don’t care.” I grabbed a rag to wipe my hands, hoping he’d drop it. “He’s probably just bored. I’m not answering his texts, though.”


Aidan’s silence was telling. He crouched beside the tractor again. His movements were sharper and more deliberate. The sound of the wrench hitting metal had a little too much force behind it.


“A man doesn’t text his fiancée’s sister just to make small talk, Josie,” he commented.


I frowned at him. “I know that. As I said, I am not responding to his texts. I don’t want trouble when Morgan learns about this. I’m sure she’ll get the wrong idea.”


He looked up. His blue eyes were steady and unreadable. “You don’t want trouble. What about him?”


Before I could find something smart to say. He stood and went back to work, the muscle in his jaw still ticking. Our easy afternoon had somehow shifted, and I couldn’t help wondering what exactly Wade wants from me.


We worked in silence after that. The afternoon sun had begun to dip lower, spilling warm orange light through the open barn doors.


Aidan was crouched near the back of the tractor. He was focused on loosening a rusted bolt that had probably been welded there by time itself. He muttered under his breath while twisting harder.


“Careful,” I warned. “That part is sharp.”


“Relax, I’ve got it,” he grunted, giving the wrench another powerful tug.


The sound that followed was a quick scrape and a distinctive curse.


I straightened immediately. “Aidan?”


He hissed through his teeth, pulling his hand back. Blood welled bright against his skin where the jagged edge of metal had caught him across the palm.


“Oh my God,” I gasped, rushing to his side. “You’re bleeding!”


He tried to wave me off, already reaching for a rag. “It’s nothing. Just a scratch.”


“That’s not nothing,” I snapped, grabbing his wrist before he could hide it. “You sliced it open! That needs to be cleaned before it gets infected.”


“I’ve had worse.”


“I bet.” I tugged him toward the old workbench where the little first-aid kit sat. It was a good thing that I bought this when we started working on the tractor. He followed, muttering something about me being bossier than a foreman, but he didn’t pull away.


“Sit,” I ordered.


He obliged. I tore open an antiseptic wipe, my hands trembling only slightly, and the moment I touched the wound. He flinched.


“Sorry,” I murmured.


“It’s fine.”


I kept my focus on the wound. I cleaned it carefully, though my heart hammered too fast. His hand was big and warm under my hand. The blood smeared against his skin. He smelled faintly of sun and that heady scent that was just uniquely him.


“This could use a few stitches,” I muttered under my breath.


“There’s no need,” he said immediately. “You patch me up, and we’ll call it even.”


I gave him a sharp look. “I’m serious, Aidan. This could lead to infection if not properly addressed.”


He grinned, despite the blood and the sting of antiseptic. There was mischief in his eyes. “You’re adorable when you’re bossy, you know that?”


I rolled my eyes, but I could feel the flush crawling up my neck. “You really can’t take anything seriously.”


“Oh, I can take some things seriously,” he said softly, his gaze dropping briefly to my mouth before flicking away.


The air between us tightened. I finished wrapping his hand, doing my best to ignore the heat curling low in my stomach. When I finally let go, he flexed his fingers, testing the bandage.


“See? Good as new.”


“Try not to injure yourself again,” I said, turning away to clean up the supplies.


Behind me, I heard him chuckle. “I guess that means I’ll have to be extra careful then. I wouldn’t want to make my nurse mad.”


“You’re hopeless.” I pretended not to smile, even though I definitely did.


When the tractor was finally tucked away, the sun slipped behind the treeline. I figured that was the end of it. Aidan would head back to his house, and I could pretend my heart hadn’t been tripping all afternoon. But of course, Aidan Callahan never did what I expected.


“Care to have a drink with me before you go?” he asked, looking at me expectantly, “A cold beer sounds good after a long day at work.”


I narrowed my eyes. “You’re not planning anything, are you?”


“Don’t you trust me? Didn’t I prove that I could be trusted and that I am a gentleman?”


I sighed, shaking my head. A few minutes later, we were on the porch. The air was heavy with the scent of honeysuckle and the soft sound of crickets.


He handed me my second bottle of beer while he took a slow sip from his third bottle as he leaned back in his chair.


Neither of us spoke while enjoying our cold beers. The silence between us wasn’t uncomfortable, but it buzzed with something that made my chest ache.


He broke the silence first. “So are we ever going to talk about it?”


I went still while my fingers tightened around my bottle. “Talk about what?”


His mouth curved into that infuriating half-smile. “You know what I mean, Josie. The kiss.”


I looked away quickly. “There’s nothing to talk about. I thought we already established that was an accident.”


“Was it?” His tone was maddeningly calm, but his eyes were sharp on me.


“Yes,” I said quickly. “You leaned in. I leaned in. It just happened. An accident.”


He took another sip, the silence stretching between us. “The funny thing is, I’ve had a lot of things just happen in my life. None of them felt like that.”


My heart stuttered. “Aidan…”


He set his bottle down and leaned forward slightly, elbows on his knees. “You can pretend it meant nothing, Josie. If that’s what you need to do. But I was there, and I know what I felt.”


I swallowed hard, staring out at the fading light. The warmth of the evening pressed against my skin, but I couldn’t seem to breathe. “It was a mistake. It didn’t mean anything.”


“Are you sure?” he asked quietly. “But it didn’t feel like one.”


His words hung dangerously in the air. I didn’t answer. I couldn’t. Instead, I stood abruptly. “I should get going. It’s late.”


He studied me for a long moment before rising too. Just before I stepped off the porch. He spoke again. “You can keep telling yourself it was a mistake, but I don’t think either of us believes that.” I was halfway to the porch steps when Aidan’s voice stopped me again. “Wait.”


I frowned at him. “What now?”


He stood there in the dim porch light, one hand tucked into his pocket while the other held his half-finished beer. The smirk on his face didn’t quite mask the concern in his eyes. “I’ll walk you home.”


I blinked. “You’ll what?”


“It’s late,” he said with that maddening calm of his. “And you’ve had a few drinks.”


I scoffed. “Two beers hardly count as a few. I can handle my alcohol, thank you very much.”


He tilted his head, the corner of his mouth twitching. “Sure, you can, sweetheart. But humor me. I’d rather not have to ride out in the dark to scrape you off the dirt road if you decide to take a nap on Daisy’s back.”


I grimaced. “I’m not going to fall off my horse, Aidan.”


“Maybe not.” He pushed off the porch post and came closer to me. “But I’d sleep better knowing you got home safe.”


Something about the way he said it made my chest tighten. I could’ve argued more. But the warmth in his tone, mixed with that teasing glint in his eyes, undid me.


Five minutes later, I found myself perched on Daisy’s back, muttering under my breath while Aidan held the reins and walked beside us.


“This is ridiculous,” I complained. “You look like my babysitter.”


He chuckled. “Trust me, Josie. Babysitting you would be the hardest job I’ve ever had.”


I rolled my eyes, but the heat crept up my cheeks anyway. The road was quiet. The only sound was the pace of Daisy’s hooves and the crickets. The moonlight silvered the edges of Aidan’s hair, catching the faint smirk on his face as he glanced up at me.


“See?” he said after a moment. “This isn’t so bad. You get a free escort home, and I get to make sure my favorite troublemaker doesn’t fall off her horse.”


I tried not to smile. “You don’t know what you’re saying, Callahan.”


“I’ve been told that before,” he agreed playfully. “They say that usually right before someone decides they like me anyway.”


I shook my head, biting back a laugh. When we reached the edge of the Walker property, I finally said. “You can stop here. I can take it from here.”


But he didn’t stop right away. He walked my horse a few more steps before I could protest. He slipped his hands around my waist and lifted me clean off the saddle.


The move stole my breath. His hands didn’t fall away right after my boots hit the ground. For a suspended heartbeat, we just stood there. He rested his palms lightly at my waist while my hands braced against his chest.


“Thank you.”


He didn’t answer. His clear, blue eyes searched mine with their usual teasing glint as they were replaced by something that reached straight under my skin.


“Josie,” he said roughly.


I didn’t think. I just moved. Before I could stop myself, I rose onto my toes, slipped my arms around his neck, and kissed him.


It was supposed to be quick. A soft brush of lips for all his trouble. But the moment our mouths met, the world tilted again.


Aidan inhaled sharply. His hands tightened at my waist, pulling me closer until my body was flat against his. He deepened the kiss deliberately, like he’d been waiting for this far longer than either of us wanted to admit.


He tasted faintly of beer and something wholly Aidan that made my knees go weak. He brushed his thumb against my lower back, coaxing another shiver out of me before he barely pulled back. He rested his forehead against mine.


“Josie,” he murmured again, his breath still uneven. “You’re going to drive me insane. Do you know that?”


I tried to say something clever, but my voice had vanished. Instead, I just whispered. “Goodnight, Aidan.”


He smiled faintly. “Goodnight, sweetheart.”


And as I turned toward the house, my heart was thundering. I knew I wouldn’t get a wink of sleep tonight.

I woke up smiling.


It was ridiculous, really. I haven’t liked this since I was sixteen. For a blissful second, I just lay in my bed while staring at the ceiling.


My lips still tingled with the memory of his kiss that had followed me into the morning.


You’re going to drive me insane.


I could still hear the rasp of Aidan’s voice. I still could feel the rough warmth of his hands at my waist. The way the night air seemed to buzz between us.


Every time I blinked, my mind replayed the way he’d looked at me right before it happened.


By the time I managed to drag myself out of bed and pull on a clean blouse, my heart had already started its steady thrum again.


I told myself I needed to get a grip before heading downstairs. I had a small business to run. And mooning over a man who kissed like that wasn’t exactly productive. The second I walked into the dining room, the smell of fried eggs and toast greeted me.


So did the sound of my parents’ voices floating from the breakfast table.


“…the tent company said they’ll deliver everything by Friday,” my mom was chirping. “And Morgan wants the flowers ordered early this week. Oh, and she told me last night that she’s sending an invitation for Mr. Callahan, too.”


I froze in the doorway.


“Really?” my dad asked casually, flipping through the morning paper.


“Yes, she said it’s only a polite thing to do,” Mom replied. “You know, he’s our new neighbor and the guy who bought Wade’s family ranch.”


“Morning,” I greeted politely when I joined them at the table.


Both of them looked up. Mom smiled in that way only mothers do when they’ve already seen too much. “Well, don’t you look bright and sunny this morning,” she said, voice filled with curiosity. “Did something good happen, sweetheart?”


I poured myself coffee. “Nope. Just slept well last night.”


Dad grunted something that might have been a greeting, but Mom wasn’t letting up. “Really? Because you’re glowing, Josie.”


I almost choked on my sip. “Mom!”


“What? I’m just saying. You’ve been dragging yourself out of bed all week, and now suddenly you’re smiling at the eggs like they’ve told you a love story.”


I ducked my head, pretending to focus on buttering my toast while my cheeks felt like they’d caught fire. “Maybe I’m just in a good mood. Is that a crime?”


Mom laughed softly. “Not at all, dear. I’m just glad about that. Oh, before I forget, Josie. Your sister called this morning. She tells me to remind you about her bachelorette party.”


I blinked. “Her what?”


“The bachelorette,” Mom repeated cheerfully. “She said you haven’t gotten back to her yet about the plans.”


My fork paused halfway to my mouth. “Plans?”


Mom turned to look at me, brow arching as if this should be obvious. “Josie, you’re the maid of honor. It’s your job to organize it. She wants it to be a surprise, of course, but she said to make sure it’s something classy.”


My stomach dropped like I’d swallowed a stone. “Oh, no,” I whispered under my breath. “No, no, no.”


Mom chuckled. “What’s wrong?”


“I can’t. I don’t even know what to—” I stammered, setting down my fork. “Mom, I’ve never even been to a bachelorette party before. I have no idea what people even do at those things besides drink too much and wear matching shirts.”


“Well,” she said, stirring her coffee like this was the most casual conversation in the world, “then I suppose you’d better start figuring it out. Maybe Sierra or Amber could help you plan.”


I stared at her with disbelief. “How can I impose on my friends?”


She gave me one of those knowing smiles. “Just a suggestion, dear.”


I slumped back in my chair, staring at the pancakes I no longer had an appetite for.


A bachelorette party for Morgan. Where? In Oakridge?


I could already picture it: my perfectly polished sister surrounded by her perfect friends, judging every decoration, every playlist choice, and every hors d’oeuvre I dared to serve.


I dropped my head into my hands and groaned. “This is going to be a disaster.”


Mom smiled faintly. “Not if you put your heart into it, sweetheart. Your sister’s wedding is a big moment. Make it special.”


Easy for her to say when all I could think of was how to survive it.


By early afternoon, I’d buried myself with work. There were peonies, roses, and eucalyptus stems spread across the counter like a battlefield. The scent of greenery clung to the air, and the rhythmic snip of my scissors was the only thing keeping my mind from spinning back to Aidan’s kiss.


The last thing I needed was another distraction until the bell above the door chimed.


I looked up, half expecting one of my regulars needing a quick bouquet for an anniversary or apology. But instead, Aidan Callahan stood there, his hands in his pockets with an easy smile, tugging at his mouth.


He looked nothing like a man who had any business in a flower shop.


“Well,” I said, trying to compose myself at his unexpected arrival. “This is a surprise.”


He glanced around the shop, his blue eyes catching the light through the window. “I thought I’d drop by.”


I arched a brow. “Don’t tell me you’re here to buy flowers again, or do you have another flimsy excuse?”


“Actually…” He leaned on the counter, pretending to examine a pot of lavender. “That’s exactly why I’m here. I thought my house could use something bright.”


I gave him a pointed look. “Your house? Or your excuse?”


Aidan grinned unashamedly. “Maybe both.”


I turned back to the roses. I snipped stems a little too aggressively. “We have ferns if you’re looking for something low maintenance.”


“Hmm.” He tilted his head, pretending to think. “What do you recommend for someone who has no idea how to keep a plant alive but could use something that smells good?”


I bit the inside of my cheek to keep from smiling. “That depends. Are you planning to actually water it, or just admire it when you walk by?”


His eyes sparkled. “You make it sound like commitment.”


“It is commitment,” I answered, meeting his gaze. “Even plants need effort.”


“Well,” he said softly. “I’ve been working on that lately.”


My pulse stuttered. “With plants?”


“With people,” he corrected.


I dropped my gaze, pretending to rearrange a vase of tulips that didn’t need rearranging. “You’re awfully talkative for someone buying flowers, sir.”


“Maybe it’s the company,” he murmured.


The air between us thickened, the same kind of tension that had sparked in the dark outside the ranch. He was too close now, leaning over the counter as if he might reach for a stem or maybe for me.


“Stop staring at me like that,” I told him. “You’re starting to make me uncomfortable.”


Aidan’s grin widened. “You keep saying that, yet you never tell me to leave.”


“I could.”


“Could you?”


My smile slipped before I could catch it.


Aidan noticed. Of course he did. His attention was always sharp. He always caught the small things. “What’s wrong?”


I shook my head, but he didn’t buy it.


He moved his hand deliberately until his fingers brushed against mine, where they rested on the counter. It was barely a touch, the kind of accidental contact you could ignore if you wanted to. Except I didn’t want to.


His fingertips traced the edge of my hand, just a light brush that sent a shiver up my arm.


“Josie,” he murmured, forcing me to look at him.


I sighed and gave in. “It’s nothing serious. Just Morgan, that’s all.”


Aidan leaned a little closer. “Your sister again?”


I nodded, my throat tightened. “Well, she reminded Mom that I’m supposed to organize her bachelorette party. Apparently, since I’m the maid of honor, it’s my job to plan the whole thing. Surprise her and all that.”


His brows rose, but there was amusement glinting in his eyes. “And that’s bad because…?”


“Because I have no idea what to do!” I exclaimed. “Morgan’s friends are all city girls who think champagne showers and rooftop clubs are ‘low effort.’ I run a flower shop and go to bed at ten. How am I supposed to compete with that?”


Aidan snickered. “So that’s what’s got you frowning.”


“I’m not frowning,” I muttered.


“Sure,” he teased, the corner of his mouth lifting. He turned his hand over and gently caught mine. His palm was warm, the kind of touch that grounded me even as it made my pulse skip.


“You don’t have to worry about that.”


I blinked as I was caught off guard. “What?”


“The bachelorette thing.” He smiled with that self-assured curve that always seemed to mean trouble. “I’ll take care of it.”


I stared at him naïvely. “You’ll what?”


“I’ll handle it,” he repeated calmly.


I almost laughed. “What do you know about organizing a bachelorette party? For my sister, of all the people.”


He brushed his thumb over the side of my hand, a slow motion that made my words falter. “I know someone who can help us.”


“Aidan—”


He gave my hand a light squeeze. “Just trust me, okay?”


I found myself nodding. “Okay,” I answered, taking a deep breath. “I trust you.”


Aidan leaned closer. “Josie…”


Before his words turned into something else. Before I had to decide whether I’d actually let him close that last inch between us, the bell above the door chimed.


We both froze. Aidan jerked back so fast he nearly knocked over the small vase behind him. I turned toward the door, my heart racing as Mrs. Whittaker, one of my oldest customers, came in.


She clutched her handbag and smiled as if she hadn’t just interrupted something borderline scandalous. “Well, if it isn’t my favorite florist. I need my usual bouquet for Sunday service.”


“Of course, Mrs. Whittaker,” I said, plastering on the most innocent smile I could muster while pretending my pulse wasn’t still pounding from almost being kissed by my neighbor.


Aidan had turned toward the flower fridge, his hands clasped behind his back while whistling quietly as if he were suddenly deeply invested in tulips.


Mrs. Whittaker’s eyes flicked between us. Thankfully, she didn’t seem to suspect anything. I arranged her flowers, wrapped them neatly, and walked her to the counter while she chattered about the upcoming church fundraiser.


“Tell your mother I said hello,” she said, waving as she headed out the door.


The bell chimed again as she left. I rounded on Aidan the moment it shut behind her.


“I will kill you,” I hissed. “If rumors start about us kissing in my shop, I swear—”


Aidan turned around. He was a picture of pure innocence, though the smug twist of his mouth betrayed him. “Relax, sweetheart. You think the town gossip mill needs us to give it something new to chew on? They’re already busy with your sister’s wedding.”


“That’s not the point—” But I didn’t get to finish my sentence when my heart began to race again.


Aidan locked the door and stepped around the counter with that lazy confidence that always seemed to make the air change.


“What do you think you’re doing?” I demanded.


He reached past me with one swift tug and pulled the curtain over the shop’s glass door.


“Aidan,” I warned again, but my voice didn’t sound nearly as firm as I wanted it to.


He didn’t answer. Instead, he looked at me, and the teasing vanished from his face. His blue eyes darkened, and something inside me stuttered.


“You were saying, sweetheart?” he murmured.


Before I could find a word. Before I could decide whether to push him away or pull him closer. He kissed me already.


It wasn’t hesitant this time. It was deliberate, as it stole the breath right out of my chest. His hand found the back of my neck while the other settled on my waist as if he already knew exactly where I fit.


By the time I remembered to breathe, it was too late. I was already kissing him back.


The moment his mouth left mine, I could still feel the shape of it, and the warmth lingered like it refused to let go.


I opened my eyes, and I found Aidan was staring at me. His chest rose and fell hard, his hair was slightly mussed, and his lips were still parted. We just stood there, both pretending not to be trembling from the kiss.


I swallowed hard, trying to find my voice. “You—you shouldn’t have done that.”


His mouth curved slightly, though his eyes remained dark. “Yeah. But I wanted to.”


My heart lurched. “That’s not an excuse. What if someone sees you?”


“I know, but it’s worth the risk.”


I should’ve pushed him away. I should’ve said something sharp. Something that reminded us both that this was wrong, that I was supposed to be helping him fix the tractor, not getting kissed senseless behind drawn curtains.


Instead, I just stood there, clutching the edge of the counter as if it could anchor me.


Aidan stepped closer again. My breath hitched when he reached up and brushed his thumb across my bottom lip, the touch almost reverent.


“Josie,” he murmured. “You have no idea what you do to me.”


“Aidan…”


“I’m serious.” His gaze met mine. “I told myself I should stay away. Keep it easy. But every time I kiss you…” He exhaled shakily. “It’s like I can’t stop. I’m getting addicted.”


The word addicted sent a shiver down my spine. I didn’t know what to say. Because if I were honest, I would let myself admit I felt it too. That same pull. That same terrifying craving for more.


He must’ve seen it in my eyes, because his expression softened, almost tender. “Tell me to stop. And I will stop.”


I didn’t tell him. I couldn’t. My hands found his shirt instead, curling into the fabric as if they already knew what I wanted before I did.


Then I kissed him again. It wasn’t hesitant this time. It was hungry. Desperate. As though we were no longer able to control whatever this was.


Aidan groaned softly against my mouth, one hand threading into my hair while the other anchored me against him.


Somewhere in the back of my mind, I registered the faint rustle of flower petals and the vibration of the refrigerator case, but all of it blurred into nothing.


There was only us. Only the way his lips moved against mine as if he’d memorized them already. By the time we finally broke apart, we were both breathless again. My heart was racing so fast I thought it might give out.


Aidan’s forehead pressed against mine. “You’re trouble, sweetheart.”


I managed a shaky whisper, “You started it.”


He laughed softly, which made something inside me ache. Before I could say another word, he kissed me again.
