CHAPTER 20


Josie’s POV


By the time the first hint of sunlight crept over Oakridge, I was already wide awake.


And panicking.


The bachelorette party. Morgan’s bachelorette party. The one I was supposed to plan. The one I’d stupidly let Aidan Callahan take over with nothing but two words: Trust me.


It was Saturday. The party was tonight, and I still had no idea what he’d planned or if he had planned anything at all.


By the time the clock on my bedside table struck six, I’d convinced myself that he’d probably forgotten about it entirely. He’d spent the week fixing that tractor or charming the entire town instead of figuring out what on earth one does at a fabulous bachelorette party.


Before I could talk myself out of it, I threw on faded jeans, a plaid shirt, and my trusty old boots. I saddled Daisy and rode straight for the Blackwood ranch.


The morning was cold, and the air was still misty. The world was still asleep, except for my heart, probably, which was beating like I was riding into a battlefield.


When I reached his porch, I didn’t even hesitate to march up to the front door and knock.


No answer.


I knocked again, louder this time. Finally, the door swung open, revealing a very groggy and bare-chested Aidan Callahan. He stood there in nothing but sweatpants and bed-mussed hair. For a full second, my brain completely short-circuited.


“Josie?” He squinted, rubbing his neck. “Do you have any idea what time it is?”


“Uh—” I cleared my throat, focusing very hard on the space just above his shoulder. “I’m aware. Which is why you should be up. We need to talk.”


He leaned against the doorframe, fighting a yawn. That infuriating smirk was already forming on his lips. “And here I thought you came because you missed me already, as I do.”


I rolled my eyes, though I could feel my cheeks heating up. “Don’t flatter yourself. I have no time for your flirtation this morning. Do you know what day it is today? The bachelorette party is tonight.”


“Ah.” He ran a hand through his hair, muscles shifting under his skin like he knew exactly what he was doing to my focus. “The party. Right.”


“Yes, the party,” I said, crossing my arms. “The one you said you’d handle but never told me a thing about. I need to know what’s happening before my sister decides to murder me in front of everyone she knows.”


Aidan chuckled softly, stepping aside as if to let me in. “You’re awfully feisty this early, sweetheart. Come on in before you decide to murder me first. I’ll make coffee, and you can scold me then.”


“I’m not—”


But he was already walking toward the kitchen, yawning. I glared at him as he had already left me no choice but to follow him inside.


I shut the door behind me. I couldn’t help but think this was a terrible idea. Because the only thing that was more dangerous than an unplanned party 
was when I was alone with Aidan Callahan before sunrise, while he was still half-naked and smiling like that.


The kitchen was warm. The first light of the morning sun spilled through the wide windows. It smelled faintly like coffee, leather, and him. It was very distracting in every possible way.


He moved around the space as if he owned it, which, of course, he did. There was something disarming about watching him walk around half-awake and barefoot in his kitchen. He reached up into a cabinet for mugs, the edge of his sweatpants hanging dangerously low on his hips.


Focus, Josie. The party. You came here for answers, not for abs appreciation.


“So,” I started, crossing my arms on the kitchen island like a shield. “Before you start flirting your way out of this conversation, I want specifics. What exactly have you planned for tonight? Or have you planned anything at all?”


He shot me a sleepy grin over his shoulder. “Good morning to you, sweetheart.”


“Aidan.”


He poured water into the coffeemaker leisurely. “Relax, sweetheart. I’ve got it handled.”


“You said that last week,” I reminded him sharply. “But you’ve told me nothing. No location, no schedule, not even a theme. My sister’s wedding is the most important day of her life, and if her bachelorette goes wrong, guess who she’ll blame?”


He turned, leaning against the counter. The coffee was already dripping behind him. “You worry too much.”


“Because you don’t worry enough! Do you know how stressful it is when my mother asks me who the bachelorette party is when I have no idea myself?”


He chuckled, running a hand through his tousled hair. “You’re so adorable when you’re stressed.”


I glared at him. “Aidan!”


“Alright, alright.” He held up his hands in mock surrender. “If you must know, everything’s arranged. The venue, music, food, and drinks. Even the decorations.”


I stared at him suspiciously. “You’re serious?”


“Would I joke about your sister’s big night? Would I let my woman get into trouble?”


I pointedly ignored the way my heart skipped a beat when he casually referred to me as his woman. I was not sure if I liked the label. But for now, that didn’t matter. There were far more important matters that we needed to discuss.  


“Yes,” I answered.


He smirked, unbothered by my lack of trust. “Fair enough. But not this time.”


I studied his face, searching for any hint of mischief. “So what did you prepare?”


“You’ll find out tonight.”


I groaned, letting out my frustration. “That’s not an answer! Do you want me to have a heart attack right now?”


He stepped closer, close enough so that I could feel the faint heat from his body. “It’s the only answer you’re getting.”


“Aidan Callahan—”


He reached past me, grabbing a spoon from the drawer. His arm brushed mine, and the touch sent a pulse of warmth straight through me. His voice dropped to a teasing drawl. “You really don’t trust me, do you?”


“I’d trust you more if you kept a shirt on when answering your door,” I muttered before I could stop myself.


He laughed. “I’ll take that as a compliment.”


“It wasn’t.”


“Mmm.” He poured the coffee and slid a mug in my direction. “Drink up. You’ll need the caffeine tonight.”


I frowned at the mug, then at him. “Why? What did you plan for us?”


“Sweetheart,” he said, there was trouble brewing in his blue eyes. “I promised you I’d handle it. What kind of man would I be if I didn’t make it a night you’d never forget?”


I tried to think of a sharp comeback, but my mind had gone blank. Mostly because he was still standing too close, and his eyes were too knowing.


I did the only thing I could do. I took the mug and muttered. “Fine.”


I sipped my coffee, pretending not to notice his amused smile. The one thought that kept looping through my head was: Whatever Aidan Callahan has planned for tonight, I’m not sure my heart or my composure will survive it.


By the time I got back home, I was more confused than ever. Aidan had been infuriatingly vague, just telling me to dress for travel, pack an overnight bag, and wait for a car that would pick me up that afternoon.


No details. No clues. Nothing.


I did what any rational woman under duress would do: I overpacked.


When the sleek black SUV pulled up in front of our house, my heart was already hammering. The driver smiled politely, confirming my name before taking my bag.


“Destination?” I asked curiously.


He smiled at me. “You’ll see, ma’am.”


Oh, for heaven’s sake. He was in on it too, was he not? The ride felt endless. We wound past the outskirts of town until the scenery changed. When we finally stopped, I blinked at the sight before me.


A private airstrip. And right in the middle of it was a sleek Gulfstream gleaming under the afternoon sun.


I blinked again. “This is a joke, right?”


The driver didn’t answer. He just opened the door, smiling like this happened every day.


I stepped out slowly, the wind tugging at my hair. My stomach dropped when I spotted the pilot walking toward me with a clipboard in hand.


“Miss Walker?” he said with a crisp nod. “Mr. Callahan sends his regards. We’ll be taking off shortly.”


I stood there, jaw slack. “He—he what?”


He gestured toward the stairs leading up to the jet. “If you’ll please board, we’re on a tight schedule.”


“Oh,” I muttered under my breath, still stunned. “But I’m going to kill him when I land.”


Inside, the jet was absurdly luxurious. Cream leather seats, soft lighting, and a faint hint of something citrus and expensive in the air.


I think I was dreaming or something. A champagne bottle was already chilling on the counter with a note in Aidan’s handwriting: Sweetheart, I told you I’d handle it.


I groaned. Half in disbelief and half in admiration.


By the time we landed again, my nerves had frayed down to threads. I recognized the skyline the moment the jet window framed it. New York City.


I was back in New York.


The same city where everything had started with Aidan. The same town I’d sworn I wouldn’t set foot in again anytime soon.


When the pilot helped me down the stairs, the disbelief on Morgan’s face was absolutely priceless.


“Josie?!” she gasped, heels clicking on the tarmac as she and her friends approached. “What on earth—what is this?!”


I tried to smile, clutching my purse like it could explain anything. “Uh… surprise?”


One of her friends’ jaws dropped. “Please tell me you didn’t rent a jet for a bachelorette party!”


“Y-Yeah,” I said quickly. “I hope you won’t mind?”


The collective gasp from the group nearly echoed across the tarmac.


“You planned this? Do you rent a private jet? Did you ask for money from Dad for this?” Morgan asked, eyebrows arched.


“No!”


Her eyes narrowed at me. “Are you sure? How did you even afford to rent a private jet?”


“Someone help me,” I answered. “But it was not Dad.”


Before anyone could interrogate me even further on how on earth I could afford to rent a jet. The pilot cleared his throat politely and gestured to the jet. “Ladies, if you’ll please board, we’re ready for departure.”


Morgan turned to me. She still had this suspicious look on her face. “Josie, where are we even going?”


I opened my mouth, but no words came out. I, myself, didn’t even know where we were heading.


Thankfully, the stewardess handed Morgan a glass of chilled vintage champagne and said with a grin, “It’s a surprise, ma’am. Congratulations on your wedding.”


Morgan raised an eyebrow while looking at me meaningfully. “Is that so?”


One of the bridesmaids clapped her hands like a child at Christmas. The others broke into excited chatter, already rushing toward the stairs.


And me? I just stood there, staring at the jet with equal parts mortification and amazement.


Leave it to Aidan Callahan to turn my sensible bachelorette plan into an international escapade.


When I finally boarded, the plane began to whirr beneath us. The stewardess passed around more champagne. Laughter filled the cabin, the girls already snapping pictures, and for the first time, I allowed myself to exhale.


I glanced at the note still in my hand. The one in Aidan’s familiar scrawl. I smiled despite myself while shaking my head. “You’re amazing, Callahan.”


“I’m excited for my bachelorette party,” Morgan shouted over the noise, and her grin widened.


“To our dear Morgan,” Morgan’s best friend added, raising her champagne glass too. “And her last few days as a single lady.”


I rolled my eyes. I couldn’t help the flutter in my chest as I reluctantly clinked my glass against theirs. Because maybe, just maybe, he was right. Maybe all I had to do was trust him.


If I thought the private jet was over the top, then landing in Las Vegas made me realize that Aidan Callahan had gone mad.


The moment we stepped out of the airport, a sleek white limousine waited at the curb. Its driver was holding a sign that said: Walker Bachelorette Party.


“Oh my God!” One of Morgan's squad squealed, practically bouncing on her heels. “That’s ours, right?”


Morgan was still speechless. Her designer sunglasses failed to hide the wide-eyed disbelief on her face. “Where the hell did you get the money for all of this, Josie?”


I winced. “I told you. I had someone help me plan your bachelorette party.”


“Well,” she muttered, sliding into the limo. “Is that someone who is also the one who picked you up after our dress fitting?”


“Well—” I started to say, but the words were drowned out by another excited cheer from the girls as the limo pulled away from the terminal and merged onto the bright stretch of Las Vegas Boulevard.


The city flashed past like a fever dream. Billboards, neon lights, fountains, and palm trees glittering in the desert sun. And just when I thought we’d reached our stop, the limo slowed in front of a hotel that made my jaw drop.


The Bellagio.


We were met at the doors by hotel staff, who already knew our names, our itinerary, and even our drink preferences. The concierge personally escorted us to the elevator, swiping a special gold card that brought us to the very top floor.


“Welcome to the Presidential Suite,” he said with a smile while holding the doors open for us.


Just like that, every single one of us stopped dead in our tracks. It wasn’t a suite. It was a palace!


Marble floors gleamed beneath chandeliers that probably cost more than my entire shop. Floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the Strip below. A sparkling maze of lights stretching endlessly into the desert. There was a private bar, a grand piano, and a hot tub on the balcony, and, of course, there was a team of staff waiting with trays of chilled champagne and chocolate-dipped strawberries.


Morgan turned in a slow circle, her hand pressed to her chest. “This can’t be real! This is insane.”


“I know.”


One of the attendants stepped forward. “Compliments from the hotel. The management wanted everything to be perfect for the bride-to-be and her guests. Dinner reservations have been made for tonight, followed by your private show experience. There’s also a spa package scheduled for tomorrow morning.”


“Private show?” one of the bridesmaids asked, her eyes glinting with glee. “What kind of private show?”


The attendant only smiled. “It’s a surprise, ma’am. But I’m certain that you’ll like it.”


Morgan whirled on me, her expression hovering somewhere between disbelief and mistrust. “Josie, this bachelorette party you planned for me is the most extravagant bachelorette party in the history of weddings.”


I smiled faintly. “I’m glad you think so.”


She looked smug, tossing her hair as she took another look around the room. “Oh, please. This is perfect. People will envy my bachelorette. Thanks to you, my dear sister. You’re the best.”


My stomach did an uncomfortable flip. I felt like a fraud while Morgan’s friends kept on praising me for organizing everything and how luxurious this bachelorette weekend was.


I didn’t plan any of this. I was as clueless as they were with the rest of our activities.


I trailed toward the balcony. The cold desert wind brushed my face as I stepped out into the fading light. Below us, the Strip had begun to glow and was alive with noise and promise.


I could still feel Aidan’s presence in every detail in the folded note cards. The perfect timing. The champagne label I recognized from that night in New York. It was so him: thoughtful, reckless, and infuriatingly confident.


And somehow, it made me miss him.


“Josie!” One of the bridesmaids voiced a call from inside. “Come on, we’re getting ready for dinner! You’re sitting next to your sister, and I’m not letting you wear beige!”


I turned back toward the suite as I tried to look forward at the rest of our schedule tonight. “Fine, fine, I’m coming.”


But as I walked back inside, my heart whispered an impossible question I couldn’t quite silence. If Aidan could pull this off just for my sister’s party.  Why did he take the extra mile to make this possible?

CHAPTER 21


Josie’s POV


If someone had told me a month ago that I’d end up flying to Las Vegas on a private jet for my sister’s bachelorette party. I would’ve laughed in their face.


Yet that was exactly what I did.


I stood in front of my mirror days later, curling my hair for the rehearsal dinner and still replaying every absurd, jaw-dropping moment of that weekend like it was a fever dream.


The spa day. The five-star dinner overlooking the Strip. The private concert where Morgan got serenaded by an actual celebrity she used to gush about in high school. And the way every single one of my sister’s friends looked at me like I’d just pulled off the impossible.


Except I hadn’t done a thing. It was all Aidan’s effort. Even now, I could barely wrap my head around it. I mean, who does that? Who books a jet, a penthouse suite, and a private show for a woman he barely even knows just to help her sister?


Only Aidan Callahan was crazy enough to dare.


I could still remember Morgan’s reaction that night. The tipsy laugh she’d let out as she hugged me, her voice thick with champagne and disbelief.  


“Josie,” she’d whispered, “you really did a great job for this bachelorette weekend. Thank you, my dear sister!”


I’d brushed it off, pretending it was because of my effort. Because the truth was, I didn’t even know how to thank Aidan once I got back to Oakridge. I didn’t even know if I could ever repay him for this.


I sighed deeply, setting my curling iron down and reaching for the necklace I always wore. A tiny gold charm shaped like a daisy, the same name as my horse.


The mirror reflected a version of me who almost looked calm and collected. But underneath, my stomach was in knots.


The rehearsal dinner tonight was going to be packed with our family, old neighbors, Wade’s parents, and the entire town’s social circle. My father had spared no expense to make sure the Walkers looked like the picture-perfect family before the big day.


Aidan was officially invited…


My chest tightened. He hadn’t said whether he’d come. But somehow, I hope he would. Aidan Callahan didn’t do maybes.


“Josie!” My mother’s voice floated up the stairs. “Are you ready? The guests are arriving!”


“Yes. I’m coming!” I took one last look at myself in the mirror, smoothing down the soft blue dress I’d chosen.


Vegas had been chaos wrapped in glitter and champagne. Tonight, though… tonight felt different.


Tonight, I’d have to face Aidan again after my Vegas weekend and pretend that I wasn’t still dreaming about his kiss almost every night.


By the time I made it downstairs, the Walker mansion was already buzzing with the soft clatter of cutlery and laughter. The hired help glided through the room carrying trays of champagne.


The scent of roasted lamb drifted from the dining hall. Everything sparkled, from the silverware to the lights to even the smiles that looked just a little too polished even for Oakridge.


I barely had time to take a breath before the grand doors opened, and Morgan and Wade walked in.


They looked like they stepped out from a magazine cover. Morgan was in a champagne dress that shimmered every time she moved, while Wade was in a tailored suit that probably cost more than my car. Everyone turned to greet them with applause and good-natured teasing.


Wade’s gaze found me almost immediately. For a second, he just stared longer than he should have. It was long enough for me to feel the burn of it on my skin. His mouth curved into a slow smile, the same boyish grin that used to make my heart tumble years ago when I was young and naïve.


“Josie,” he said warmly as he crossed the room. His voice carried that familiar lazy charm, the one that used to make me forget how to breathe. “It’s been too long. How is it going?”


I forced a smile and nodded. “Hey, Wade. Good to see you again.”


He looked me over in a way that made my skin prickle. There was something different in his eyes.


I wasn’t sure if it was the wine already flowing through the room or the soft lighting, but it wasn’t the friendly and brotherly warmth I remembered from him.


“Josie, you look—” he hesitated, his gaze lingering far too long on my face, which was making me uneasy with each passing second. “—different. The city must’ve treated you well.”


I gave a polite laugh. “The city and I don’t exactly get along, Wade. I tried it once, and it failed miserably.”


Before Wade could say anything else, Morgan swept in and looped her arm through his. She beamed at me with that perfect smile of hers. “Josie! There you are. Doesn’t Wade look handsome tonight?”


He did. But the only thing I could think of was how wrong it felt. How uncomfortable it was to stand here and feel his eyes flicker toward me even as Morgan leaned into him like she always did.


Once upon a time, I would’ve given anything for Wade Blackwood to look at me that way.


Now, I just wanted him to stop.


“Excuse me,” I murmured, slipping away before something would go wrong at this rehearsal dinner. My heart was hammering a little too hard as I reached for a glass of champagne. I pretended to admire the flower arrangements instead of acknowledging what I’d just seen in Wade’s eyes.


And all the while, all I could think of was if Aidan would ever come tonight.


***


The air outside was colder than I expected. The porch lights cast an amber glow over the yard, and I let out a long breath as soon as the door shut behind me.


Inside, laughter still floated through the walls while glasses clinked and music muffled into something distant and dull.


I just needed five minutes of silence. But of course, fate didn’t care much about what I needed.


“Couldn’t resist sneaking away too, huh?”


I froze at the sound of Wade’s voice behind me. He was leaning against the porch rail with a half-empty glass of bourbon in hand, his tie loosened, and that easy smile still fixed on his face.


I forced a thin smile. “I guess we both needed a break from the chaos.”


He chuckled and stepped closer. “It’s been a while since we’ve talked. Just the two of us.”


Every instinct told me to move. To go back inside and not give him the chance to start whatever this was. But people would probably notice, and they’d probably whisper that I was avoiding my future brother-in-law. I couldn’t stomach another rumor staining this family, not before Morgan’s wedding.


So I stayed in my place while Wade stood beside me. He was silent for a moment before he spoke again. “You’ve been ignoring my messages, Jo.”


My fingers tightened around my glass. “I’ve been busy. Between the shop, the bachelorette party, and helping around the ranch. I barely have time to sleep.”


He laughed quietly, shaking his head. “That’s a shame. I was starting to think you were avoiding me.”


I turned to him, trying to keep my expression neutral. “You’re marrying my sister. Why will I avoid you? Besides, what could we possibly have to talk about, Wade?”


He didn’t answer right away. He was swirling the bourbon in his glass as his eyes focused on the yard. “I miss the old times, that’s all. You put together one hell of a bachelorette weekend. Vegas, Josie? I didn’t know you had that kind of pull.”


My stomach knotted. “I admit I couldn’t pull that on my own. Someone help me.”


“Who?” he asked casually, though there was an edge buried underneath the question.


“Just someone,” I answered vaguely. “A friend.”


Wade’s jaw shifted. “Would this friend happen to be the same man who picked you up after your dress fitting in New York?”


I stiffened, finally looking at him fully. “What exactly are you implying?”


He met my gaze. His eyes were unreadable. “Nothing. Just asking questions.”


“Then stop asking.” My voice came out sharper than I intended. “Because I don’t have to explain myself or my actions to anyone.”


He blinked, as if I’d slapped him. The silence that followed was taut and uncomfortable. I could hear the cicadas buzzing in the distance and the faint murmur of voices inside the house. I was seconds away from walking away when I heard the familiar low sound of an engine pulling into the driveway.


Headlights swept across the porch.


Wade turned first, his brow furrowed. But my heart already knew who it was.


Aidan.


He stepped out of his truck confidently. The sight of him under the porch light made my pulse trip. He looked unfairly good with his expensive tailored suit and his sleek hair. This polished city guy looked out of place in our little town.


Wade’s expression shifted. The easy charm slipped from his face as Aidan’s gaze landed on us.


“Good evening,” Aidan greeted. His tone was smooth, but it was edged with something unmistakable as his eyes flicked briefly to Wade before he settled his gaze on me. “I hope I’m not interrupting.”


I exhaled, the tension draining from my shoulders all at once. “No. You’re right on time.”


For a moment, no one said a word. The air was thick, so thick I could almost hear the tension vibrating.


Wade straightened first, his hand tightening on his glass. Aidan’s expression didn’t change.


I cleared my throat, desperate to cut through the taut silence. “Um… Wade, this is Aidan Callahan. Aidan, this is Wade. Morgan’s fiancé.”


Aidan’s lips curved faintly, but his eyes never left Wade’s. “Nice to meet you.”


Wade blinked, his easy charm faltering for the first time. “Wait—Callahan? As in…” He trailed off as disbelief spread over his face.


I nodded before he could finish. “Yes. Aidan’s the new owner of the Blackwood Ranch.”


Wade just stared, his brow furrowing as if the words didn’t make sense. Then he let out a short and incredulous laugh. “You’re kidding.”


“I don’t usually joke about property titles,” Aidan replied smoothly, his tone polite but layered with something. “I bought it a few months back.”


Wade’s eyes widened. “Huh. I—I didn’t mean to, I just assumed…” He hesitated. He glanced at me, then back at Aidan. “The broker mentioned his client wanted some quiet and peaceful place. I thought the buyer was an older guy. Some retirees want to settle down in the countryside.”


Aidan’s smirk deepened. “I guess you were half-right about the settling part.”


Wade cleared his throat, forcing a chuckle that didn’t quite reach his eyes. “Well, that’s surprising. You’re a bit young to be running a property that size.”


“I get that a lot,” Aidan said evenly, sliding his hands into his pockets. “But I want to try my hand at ranching.”


I could practically feel the weight of testosterone thickening in the air. Both men stood just a little taller while each assessed the other. Wade was the small-town guy turned polished city boy with charm sharpened by ambition. Aidan was the kind of man who didn’t need to prove anything but somehow did with every word.


“So,” Wade said finally, his tone carefully neutral. “You and Josie know each other?”


Aidan’s gaze flicked to me before returning his attention to Wade. “I think that’s a given since we’re neighbors. Josie used to ride between our fence every morning, and her mom usually sent Josie to my house to give me a freshly made breakfast.”


Something flashed in Wade’s eyes, but it was gone too quickly to name. “Ah. I see.”


“You should come by sometime,” Aidan added, his voice deceptively casual. “You can look around the Blackwood Ranch and reminisce about your childhood.”


Wade’s jaw tightened, the smile returning to his face but not to his eyes. “Maybe I will.”


“Good.” Aidan’s tone was smooth as honey. “Just make sure to call ahead. You wouldn’t want you to interrupt something.”


The air between them felt electric. A standoff in polite words. I stepped in quickly, my nerves fraying. “Well, thank you, Aidan, for stopping by. I’m sure you’ve had a long day, and Wade probably needs to get back to Morgan.”


Wade glanced at me reluctantly and finally stepped back. “Right. Of course. See you around, Jo.”


He went inside, the door clicking softly behind him. Only then did I realize I’d been holding my breath.


When I turned to Aidan, he was watching the door with a faintly amused expression, though the tension still lingered in his posture.


“Enjoying yourself, Mister?” I asked, crossing my arms.


He looked down at me, that infamous smirk returning to his face. “Immensely.”


We slipped back into the dining hall. The music had softened into something classy. The kind of melody that filled the space without stealing attention.


Aidan and I fell into step beside each other. We were like two people who had definitely not just exchanged silent death glares and possibly saved me from an awkward confrontation with my sister’s fiancé.


We moved like strangers who happened to know each other’s rhythm too well.


He guided me subtly with a hand at the small of my back as we entered. It was a fleeting touch that made my spine straighten. I shot him a warning look over my shoulder, and his only reply was that infuriating curve of his mouth.


“Josie!” Morgan called from across the room, waving me over. “Come sit next to me!”


I started toward her, grateful for the distraction, until she tilted her head and narrowed her eyes. “Wait a second, you’re the guy who picked up Josie after the dress fitting, aren’t you?”


Aidan didn’t even blink. “Guilty as charged,” he said smoothly, flashing his polite smile. “I’m just returning the favor. Josie’s been helping me with some local business.”


“Oh, really?” Morgan asked, curiosity sparkling. “You two have been spending time together, then?”


“Purely professional,” I cut in quickly.


Aidan made a quiet sound beside me that could have been between agreement and laughter disguised as a cough.


Morgan thankfully didn’t notice. “Well, that’s sweet. You really outdid yourself with the bachelorette weekend, sis.”


I smiled, trying not to glance at the man responsible for the entire Vegas extravaganza sitting right next to me. “I had help.”


“Still,” Morgan said, lifting her glass, “it was unforgettable.”


Across the table, Wade’s gaze flickered between Aidan and me. There was a shadow of something unreadable passing through his eyes. I quickly focused on my plate, pretending not to feel it.


For the next hour, the evening rolled on in a blur of champagne and small talk. And through it all, Aidan played his part perfectly as the charming and polite neighbor.


Beneath the tablecloth, where no one could see, his hand grabbed mine. He didn’t look at me. He just kept his attention fixed on whatever story my father was telling at the head of the table, but that single touch was enough to make my pulse quicken.


I turned my head slightly, giving him a sharp glare that was meant to kill the smirk I knew he was suppressing.


He reached for his wine glass, his fingers grazing mine again. So subtle it could’ve been accidental, but I knew it wasn’t.


I bit back my frustration. My face was hot. My stomach was doing somersaults. And my smile kept slipping, no matter how hard I tried to stay composed.


If anyone had looked closely. They’d see a woman sitting perfectly still beside a man who looked far too pleased with himself.


“Stop it,” I hissed under my breath, leaning ever so slightly toward him.


“Stop what?” he murmured innocently.


I glared at him again, though the corners of my mouth betrayed me. He smiled just enough for me to see the wicked satisfaction in his eyes.


And God help me, I smiled back.


By the time the dessert was served, Aidan leaned closer and whispered something to my father that made him laugh before he rose quietly from his chair.


I frowned when I noticed. He was leaving. No one else seemed to notice as he slipped away through the side doors. The light from the chandeliers caught in his hair one last time before he disappeared. And just like that, the room felt emptier.


I told myself it didn’t matter. He’d probably had a long day. Maybe he was being polite enough to leave early. Still, the unmistakable feel of disappointment settled in my chest.


I stayed a little longer, but my mind wasn’t in it for dinner rehearsal anymore. Eventually, I mumbled an excuse about getting some fresh air.


The night outside was cooler than I expected. The garden lights glowed softly. For a few blissful seconds, I breathed in silence. The laughter and music from the dining hall faded behind me. I had just started down the path toward the stables when someone grabbed my hand. I gasped as instinct kicked in, ready to shout for help and—


“Josie.”


My heart flipped. “Aidan! You scared me half to death! I thought you had already gone home. What are you doing here?”


He didn’t stop moving. He just tugged me gently along the shadowed path. “Where are we going?” I demanded, trying to sound annoyed. But my pulse thought the opposite. “You could’ve just called my name like a normal person! Are you listening?”


He didn’t answer. He just glanced back at me with a mischievous glint in his blue eyes. “Would you have come if I did?”


I opened my mouth, then closed it again.


We crossed the back path toward the new barn, the very barn my father had built for Morgan’s big day. The air inside still smelled of sawdust and fresh paint.


“Aidan, what are we—”


Before I could finish, he pulled me close. And then he was kissing me. All the air rushed out of me.


My hands instinctively went to his chest. I planned on pushing him away, but my fingers curled into his shirt, holding on to him. His mouth was warm and demanding, the kind of kiss that made every thought inside my head scatter like dandelions in the wind.


By the time he pulled back, my breath came out in a shaky exhale.


“I didn’t like leaving you back there,” he said quietly, his forehead still resting against mine. “But if I stay a little longer, I don’t think I can control myself any longer. I longed to taste your lips the moment I arrived, sweetheart.”


I wanted to tell him he was crazy. This was crazy. That someone could see that we were playing with fire.


But I found myself whispering. “You’re an idiot. What if I didn’t decide to come out of the house to get some fresh air? What will you do then? Will you wait in vain?”


He smiled, brushing his thumb over my cheek. “I’ll still wait for you. Tell me you don’t want this and I’ll leave right now.”


I thought of saying exactly that, but I didn’t. Maybe I was being reckless here. Someone might catch us kissing in here and start a maelstrom of rumors, but I didn’t care.  When his lips found mine again, in a slow dance of our mouths. There wasn’t a single part of me that wanted him to leave.

CHAPTER 21


Josie’s POV


When I first learned about Morgan and Wade’s engagement, I imagined a hundred different versions of this day.


First, I would storm out of the ceremony like a tragic heroine in a soap opera. Second, I would just stay home altogether with my curtains drawn, pretending it wasn’t happening. And lastly, I stood right where I am now, watching the man I once loved promise forever to someone else.


I thought I’d completely break apart when it happened, but I didn’t. I was surprisingly calm.


Maybe it was the way the sunlight hit the fields. Perhaps the guests’ laughter made the world feel steady again. Maybe I’d just simply run out of tears.


As Morgan walked down the aisle. I didn’t feel jealousy or bitterness. I just felt peace.


Wade’s eyes never left her, and for once, that didn’t sting. He looked at her like she was the only woman in his eyes. I realized, deep down, that maybe this was always how it was meant to be.


It wasn’t my story to have. I would always be the extra in someone else’s love story. That was the fate of a pitiful side character. Morgan would always be the main character, no matter how I resent the idea.


I stood there with a bouquet in my hand with a smile fixed on my face, but it was genuine enough. The wind caught my hair as vows were exchanged and rings slipped onto their fingers.


For years, I dreamt this moment would be between Wade and me. We would be standing before the wedding officiant, exchanging wedding vows.  


But that would never have happened. Sometimes dreams were far from harsh reality.


I stood beside Morgan with my bouquet clutched loosely in my hands, as the officiant’s voice carried through the barn.


The place looked beautiful. There were strings of lights draped along the wooden beams, expensive flowers spilling from vases, and the sunlight streaming through the open doors in golden streaks.


It smelled like roses and cedar. I should’ve been paying attention to the ceremony. My sister was glowing in her custom-made white lace gown. And Wade looked dashing in his tux. He was the epitome of every small-town girl’s dream.


My gaze drifted toward the crowd. Through the rows of familiar faces blurred together until one caught me completely off guard.


Aidan Callahan.


He was leaning casually at the end of the last row, dressed in a dark suit that somehow looked both reckless and refined. The afternoon light hit him just right, turning his hair to burnished gold and his eyes… oh, those devilish blue eyes glinted wickedly.


He was looking straight at me with that knowing half-smile that tugged at the corner of his mouth. The sight made it impossible for me to breathe properly.


He didn’t have to say a word. That look alone said enough.  He saw me. He’d always see me, even in a crowd full of people who barely thought of my existence. Before I could stop myself, my lips curved upward.


Aidan’s smile deepened in response. His smile had the power to make my knees weak from across the aisle. My heart fluttered helplessly in my chest, completely at odds with the serenity of the ceremony.


Morgan and Wade were promising each other forever, and all I could think about was the man sitting in the last row. One smile from him could undo me completely.


Seriously, Josie!


The reception was already in full swing before I realized what was happening. I was running around like a headless chicken. The maid of honor, they called me, which apparently translates to the woman who fixes veils, wrangles tipsy bridesmaids, and politely smiles while people keep asking if I’m the next one to walk down the aisle.


I’d caught glimpses of Aidan earlier, talking to my father, flashing that devastating smile that could charm the wings off angels. Every time I thought I could sneak away for even a minute, someone else needed me.


When I saw him again, he was across the room near the open bar. I started to approach him as my pulse picked up despite myself. But the moment I got close enough to see who he was talking to, I stopped in my tracks.


Jessica Monroe.


She was laughing a little too loudly, tilting her head just so. The way she always did when she had an audience.


Aidan was standing there looking far too amused. His blue eyes were bright as he listened to whatever nonsense Jessica was spilling from her lip-glossed mouth.


A strange feeling coiled in my chest. I told myself it wasn’t jealousy. I didn’t have the right to feel this way. But when I saw Jessica touch his arm lightly, tracing a circle on the sleeve of his suit, something inside me snapped like a taut string.


He laughed at something she said. A deep sound that once made my knees weak, and it felt like someone had wrung the air out of me.


I slowed to a stop, still watching them. My eyes narrowed before I could help it. So this was what it felt like to want to march across a crowded room and throw a drink in someone’s face.


Before I could decide whether to do something stupid or just turn around and pretend I didn’t care. Wade’s voice cut through the noise.


“Josie,” he called, sharp enough to make me flinch.


I tore my gaze away from Aidan and turned toward my new brother-in-law, forcing a smile onto my face that didn’t quite reach my eyes.


“Wade,” I smiled, trying to sound composed.


My heartbeat was still hammering. When I looked back once more, Aidan was still standing with Jessica. He had an unreadable expression on his face now. He was looking in my direction, watching my interaction with Wade.


Wade was already halfway across the room when I turned back from watching Aidan and Jessica.


He smiled as he approached, that familiar grin that used to make my teenage heart flutter. Now, it just made me uneasy.


“Hey,” he said, adjusting his tie. “I haven’t really had a chance to talk to you all night.”


My pulse stuttered. The last thing I needed was to be seen chatting too long with the groom at his own wedding. The whispers would start before the cake was even cut. I forced a polite smile. “You should probably save that attention for your bride.”


“She’s busy,” he said with a shrug. “She went to change into her dress for our first dance.”


I nodded, glancing toward the hallway that led to the bridal suite. Good. Let her be busy.


The last thing I wanted was Morgan walking back in and seeing me standing here with her husband.


“That’s nice,” I said softly, taking a half step back. “You must be excited.”


“I am,” Wade replied, though his voice carried a faint note I couldn’t quite place. “But you know, Josie, it feels like we haven’t really talked since you came back. I didn’t mean to make you uncomfortable before. Are you avoiding me?”


I blinked. “Excuse me?”


“You stopped replying to my messages,” he continued, his tone mild but unmistakably probing. “Then, suddenly, there’s talk of some mystery man picking you up in New York. You can’t blame me for being curious.”


So that’s what this is about.


I opened my mouth to reply. To brush it off with some half-hearted joke, but my eyes drifted almost against my will to the guests on the far side of the room.


Aidan was still there, but he was no longer laughing at Jessica’s flirtatious talk.


His gaze was locked on Wade. It was sharp and simmering with something that twisted my insides. He tightened his jaw when Wade leaned slightly closer to say something, and I could feel that storm building from across the room.


Our eyes met for the briefest second. And then, just like that, Aidan pushed off from the bar. He muttered something to the bartender and strode toward the exit. His broad shoulders cut through the crowd easily, his steps were deliberate, and his expression was dark.


My heart dropped. “Aidan.”


“What?” Wade asked in confusion, but I barely heard him.


“Sorry, Wade,” I said as I was already getting away. “I’ll talk to you again soon. I have to go.”


I wanted to follow Aidan. Every instinct in me screamed to run after him. To find out why he looked like that. He was furious.


The weight of the maid of honor's sash around my shoulders pulled me back down to reality. My duty. My family. The perfect wedding I had promised not to ruin. So I stayed at the party, I smiled, I nodded, and I played the role everyone expected of me.


But all through the rest of the evening, while the newlyweds danced and laughter echoed in the barn, my heart wasn’t in the party anymore. It was outside, walking away under the stars with Aidan Callahan.


***


By the time I managed to slip away from the reception, the music had already turned rowdy, and half the guests were too drunk to notice I was gone.


I ducked into the house and slipped off the satin maid-of-honor gown. I pulled on a pair of old jeans and a shirt. My heels were replaced with my boots, and before I could change my mind, I was already saddling Daisy.


The night air was cold against my flushed skin as I rode out across the fields. The rhythm of Daisy’s hooves was muffled by the grass. The lights from the Walker ranch faded behind me until the only thing that guided me was the soft glow coming from the Blackwood porch.


Aidan Callahan was sitting on the steps with a half-empty beer bottle dangling from his hand, his head tilted slightly toward the night sky like he’d been deep in thought or probably trying not to think at all.


I tied Daisy to the fence and hesitated for a moment. I watched him. He didn’t move. He didn’t even glance my way when I walked up the path.


“Couldn’t sleep?” I queried casually.


He didn’t answer. He just took another long sip of his beer. Stubborn lout!


I let out a deep breath and sat down beside him anyway. I was close enough to feel the warmth coming off his arm. The silence between us stretched tight and was only filled by the sound of crickets.


“You should probably go back,” he said finally, still not looking at me. “They’ll notice you’re missing.”


I smiled faintly. “I don’t think so.”


He took another swig of his beer. “You shouldn’t be here, Josie.”


“Why not?” I tilted my head, trying to catch his eyes. “Are you mad at me or something?”


“Did you make something to make me mad?” he asked. “If yes, then I’m mad. If not, then I’m not mad.”


I laughed softly under my breath. “You’re sulking. You do realize that, right?”


“I don’t sulk.”


“Yes, you do.”


He turned his head, and even in the dim porch light, I could see the flicker of irritation in his eyes. The kind that came not from anger.


“Then what’s this?” I asked quietly, smoothing the creases on his brow with my finger. “You’ve been ignoring me since I got here.”


He looked away again, his thumb tracing the neck of the beer bottle. “You should’ve stayed at the party and not come here. It’s already late.”


“Maybe I wanted to be here.”


His shoulders went rigid, and his breath caught for a second. Then he set the bottle down with a thud and finally turned toward me. “Josie…”


And just like that, my pulse went wild. The silence stretched so long I could hear the faint sound of the cicadas and the soft clink of his bottle against the porch.


I hated that look on his face. The tightness in his jaw and the shadow in his eyes, like he was locking himself behind some invisible wall. Without thinking, I leaned in and brushed my lips against his cheek.


He didn’t move. He didn’t even flinch. So I did it again. A little slower this time, closer to the corner of his mouth. Still nothing, no reaction from him.


“Aidan,” I whispered. “Don’t be mad, please?”


His hand flexed against his thigh, the only sign that he’d heard me.


I tried one more time for another kiss. A light sigh came out from his lips. This time I lingered my lips just long enough to feel the warmth of his skin, the rough scrape of stubble under my lips.


That was when he moved. His head turned sharply. His eyes caught mine with those stormy blues that could melt steel if they wanted to. “Josie.”


My breath hitched. “What?”


“Don’t.” His voice was rougher and darker, as his gaze dropped briefly to my lips before meeting my eyes again. “Don’t play with fire if you’re not ready to get burned.”


For a moment, I forgot how to breathe. The porch light cast a halo around him. He looked every bit the danger he warned me about, and I hated how much I wanted to test him anyway.


“I wasn’t playing.”


A muscle in his jaw twitched. He reached for his beer again, as if that small act could undo everything that just passed between us.


I watched him tip the bottle back again. Something inside me snapped. If he wanted to pretend nothing was happening, fine. Two could play that game. Before he could set the bottle down, I leaned forward and snatched it from his hand. His brows shot up as I took a long swig, the bitter taste of beer burning down my throat.


“Hey—” he started.


I cut him off with a defiant grin. “What? You weren’t talking to me anyway.”


For a heartbeat, we just stared at each other. I could almost hear the words he wasn’t saying. Then I did the thing I’d been dying to do since the moment I saw him tonight.


I kissed him. I pressed my mouth against his mouth hard, my hands fisting the front of his shirt.


He froze for half a second, just long enough for me to think if I’d pushed too far before the bottle hit the porch floor with a thud. Then he kissed me back, which made the world tilt and the air disappear.


I felt him melt against me. I felt the tension drain out of his body as the heat between us took over. A small sound escaped him roughly, and it sent a thrill through me.


So this was what it felt like when Aidan Callahan lost control. For once, I was the one who lit the fire, and I didn’t want it to stop burning.


When we finally broke apart, I was breathless. My lips were tingling. My heart raced so hard I could barely think straight.


Aidan’s forehead rested against mine, his breathing just as ragged as mine. His hands still gripped my hips, as if he couldn’t quite bring himself to let go. Then he drew back. His eyes were so dark and intense that they made every nerve in my body come alive.


“Josie,” he warned. “We should stop.”


He exhaled shakily, brushing his thumb across my lower lip. He followed the motion with his eyes like he was fighting himself. “If we don’t, I might not be able to let you go home tonight.”


I felt my mouth curve in a daring smile. Maybe it was the way he looked at me like I was the only person left in the world. Because the next words left my lips before I could stop them. “Not a bad idea.”


His eyes flicked up to mine dangerously. For a moment, I thought he’d give in, that he’d pull me close again and finish what we’d started.


He gave a strained laugh, and he stood abruptly, running a hand through his hair like he needed distance to breathe.


“Josie,” he warned again. “You have no idea what you’re doing to me.”


I tilted my head up at him, still seated on the porch step. I still could feel the heat of his mouth on mine. “Maybe I do.”


He stared at me with that unreadable look that flickered between restraint and desire before he shook his head with a half-smile. “You’re going to be the death of me.”


And I couldn’t help but grin. “You started this. You should finish it, Callahan.”

CHAPTER 23


Josie’s POV

I sighed softly. I was careful not to wake Aidan as I slipped out of bed. The floor was cold under my feet. I grabbed his shirt, and it hung loosely on me.


I padded toward the kitchen. The familiar creak of his wooden floor follows each step. The coffee was waiting. I poured myself a cup, leaning against the counter as I looked out the window. The horses were grazing by the fence. The morning mist still clung to the fields. Everything looked so normal.


If anyone saw me like this, I was barefoot and only wearing Aidan Callahan’s shirt while I was standing in his kitchen. I also smelled like his soap. They’d instantly know everything. And yet, a part of me didn’t care.


Aidan appeared behind me, his voice still husky with sleep as he wrapped his arms around my waist tightly. “What are you thinking?”


I smiled faintly without turning. “Good morning. You’re awake early.”


He playfully nibbled the tender skin on my neck. “I couldn’t sleep without you beside me.”


I laughed at him. “You were soundly asleep when I slipped off the bed.”


He rested his chin on my shoulder. “You’re tense, sweetheart. What’s wrong?”


“I’m just thinking about home.”


“Blackwood?” he guessed immediately.


I didn’t answer, which was answer enough for him apparently.


He heaved a sigh, pressing a kiss just below my ear. “Don’t let him get in your head again, sweetheart. You’re over him, right?”


I wanted to believe that. I wanted to believe that what Aidan and I had was strong enough to withstand anything, even the scrutiny that would come if people found out. Even Wade’s unexpected return.


***


The morning air was still chilly when I rode Daisy back toward the Walker ranch. I could already see the outline of the house. The white fences were gleaming in the light, and I thought I might actually slip inside unnoticed.


But luck had never been on my side.


Wade was there. He stood near the pasture with a steaming mug of coffee in his hand. He was wearing a casual shirt and jeans that looked too at home here, as if he’d never left. He found me instantly as I slowed Daisy to a stop.


“Morning,” he greeted, lifting the cup slightly. “You’re up early.”


I forced a small smile, patting Daisy’s neck. “I always am. I took her out for a ride.”


“Right,” he said, drawing out the word like he didn’t quite buy it. “Just a morning ride, huh?”


My fingers tightened on the reins as I kept my tone light. “Yes. What else would it be, Wade?”


He didn’t answer right away. He just watched me too closely before setting his cup on the fence post beside him. “The funny thing is, I was up late last night. I couldn’t sleep. I looked out the window and saw someone sneaking out of the stable around midnight.”


My heart tripped over.


He gave a knowing smile. “I didn’t want to assume anything, but you just got back from that direction.” He nodded toward Blackwood Ranch. “So I’m guessing you weren’t exactly out stargazing last night.”


My pulse was so loud in my ears. “You do a lot of guessing lately, Wade.”


He tilted his head, pretending to be amused. “Am I wrong, Josie?”


I swung my leg off Daisy and got down from her back. I brushed the dust off my jeans. I kept my face composed even as my stomach twisted. “Even if you were right, that has nothing to do with you.”


“Josie—”


“No.” I cut him off, meeting his gaze squarely. “You don’t get to ask questions like that, Wade. You don’t get to imply things or act like you have any say in what I do.”


He became tense.


“You’re my sister’s husband, Wade. Nothing more. So if you’ve got time to watch me leave at midnight, maybe you should also keep in mind to mind your own business.”


The words landed sharply. I thought he might snap back. But instead, he just stared at me. There was something dark flickering behind his expression before he smiled slowly.


“I guess I hit a nerve,” he murmured.


“I guess you did,” I said, brushing past him and heading toward the mansion. I didn’t look back, even when I felt his gaze burning between my shoulder blades all the way to the entrance door of the house.

CHAPTER 27

By the time I got to the diner, I was already running on nerves and too little sleep. Sierra and Amber were tucked into our usual booth by the window with two iced coffees already served on the table.


Sierra looked up first. Her smile was immediate. “Well, well. Look who finally decided to grace us with her presence.”


Amber giggled. “She looks like she’s been through a storm. What’s up, girlfriend?”


“Ha-ha,” I muttered, sliding into the seat beside them. “Nice to see you too.”


Sierra leaned forward. “Are you okay? You look stressed.”


“Stressed?”


“Yes, you got this, something’s wrong, but I’m pretending it is fine, look,” Sierra commented while stirring her drink. “You always get that when your dad’s on your case or when you’re bothered by something.”


I gave her a deadpan glare. “What makes you think that I have a problem?”


Amber raised a brow. “Then what’s with that long face, Jo?”


I sighed, picking at the edge of my napkin. There was no use in hiding it from them. They would eventually learn about it through town gossip anyway. “Wade is back in town.”


That got their attention. Sierra’s straw froze mid-sip, and Amber blinked.


“Wait. Did you say Wade?” Sierra asked. “As in your new brother-in-law slash ex-crush Wade?”


“Unless you know any other Wade besides him, then, yes, that same Wade,” I said flatly.


Amber frowned. “I thought he and Morgan were still in Europe. Their European honeymoon was the favorite subject of the single ladies in our town.”


“They were. Apparently, Morgan had to go back to New York early for work, so Wade decided to come back here. He said he’s got some unfinished business in town.” I made air quotes around the words. “And now he’s staying at the Walker mansion until he’s done.”


Sierra whistled. “So your ex-crush-slash-sister’s husband is sleeping under the same roof as you. You’re practically living your teenage dream.”


“Don’t start with me, Sullivan,” I warned.


Sierra's grin only grew wider. “Oh, come on, Josie. The teenager in you would’ve died for this chance. Dream boy Wade Walker, right back where you can see him every day.”


“Yeah,” I said dryly. “Except now he’s married to my sister and apparently has nothing better to do than make me uncomfortable at breakfast.”


Amber appeared to be very interested. “He’s been bothering you? How?”


I hesitated on how to tell them without sounding conceited. “Well, he was not exactly bothering me. I think he's paying too much attention to me lately. The way he looks at me sometimes feels weird. Like he’s trying to figure out something. I don’t like it. It makes me uncomfortable.”


Sierra’s teasing ceased immediately. “Hey, are you okay?”


“I’m fine,” I answered with a forced smile. “Really, I can’t wait until he’s gone and things go back to normal.”


Sierra reached across the table and squeezed my hand affectionately. “Then we’ll just keep you distracted until he leaves.”


“With extra dessert,” Amber said.


I let myself laugh with them, trying to forget the weight sitting in my stomach. The way Wade’s knowing eyes had followed me all the way up the front steps this morning.


Sierra was already halfway through her sandwich when she said. “So, other than Wade Blackwood reappearing from town, how’s life treating you? Anything new and exciting you’d like to confess?”


“Not really,” I told them, reaching for my lemonade. “Just the usual work, deliveries, flowers.”


Amber gave me a skeptical look over her straw. “Please. You’ve been glowing lately. Don’t even try to tell me it’s from trimming flower stems all day.”


I laughed, though my pulse skipped a beat. “It’s the new moisturizer I’m using.”


Sierra wasn’t buying it either. “Uh-huh. Sure. You’ve got that kind of smile on your face, Josie. The kind of smile that says someone’s been keeping me up at night, kind.”


I choked on my drink. “Sierra!”


Amber burst out laughing. “Oh, I missed this when I left town.”


I tried to act unbothered, but my cheeks were definitely warm. I focused on my salad instead, stabbing at a tomato as if it had personally offended me.


“Alright, fine,” Sierra finally gave up. “We’ll drop it. But—” She paused, her eyes flicking toward the diner’s window. “I just remembered I did see Aidan Callahan at the grocery store the other day. He looked so handsome at close distance.”


My fork froze halfway to my mouth while Amber made a thoughtful noise. “Oh, I’ve seen him too. At the coffee shop last week. He’s so enigmatic, isn’t he? I swear, he barely talks to anyone, but that didn’t stop the ladies from trying to flirt with him.”


“Maybe he just doesn’t like small talk,” Sierra said, smirking. “Or maybe he’s got a secret girlfriend he’s trying to keep hidden.”


My stomach tightened. “You two read too many romance novels, I swear.”


Sierra grinned. “Says the florist who names her roses after Greek goddesses.”


“Touché,” I played along, though my palms started to sweat under the table.


“I wonder how many ladies had been knocking on his doorstep every morning with breakfast,” Amber mused out loud.


Only one, I thought silently. But of course, I wouldn’t say that out loud.


Amber leaned in, her eyes narrowing with suspicion. “Wait. You know him, don’t you, Jo?”


I looked at my friend blankly, just a split second too long before answering. “Who?”


“Aidan Callahan,” Amber said. “He went to your flower shop once, didn’t he? I remember you saying something about him being rude.”


“Oh. That.” I forced a shrug. “Yeah, that was ages ago.”


Sierra looked at me like she didn’t quite believe a word I was saying. “Mmm. Mysterious, handsome, and living alone on a big ranch sounds like every girl’s mistake waiting to happen.”


I gave her a pointed look. “What are you implying?”


“You know,” she said brightly. She stirred the last of her iced coffee when she said it. It was so casual, as if she were talking about the weather. “I am thinking, maybe I should take my shot with the infamous Aidan Callahan.”


My head snapped up so fast I nearly spilled my drink. “You are going to… what?”


Amber snorted. “Oh, here we go.”


“I’m serious!” Sierra insisted. “The man’s gorgeous. Brooding, even. He was built like he could lift a tractor with one arm. If nobody’s claimed him yet, maybe it’s a sign.”


I stared at her with a horrified expression on my face. My heart gave a painful lurch in my chest, and before I could think twice, the words flew out of my mouth. “No. You can’t!”


The table went quiet. Sierra blinked while her brows shot up. “I can’t? Why not?”


Amber’s head tilted. “Yes, why not, Jo?”


Because he’s mine. The words clawed at the back of my throat, but I couldn’t say them. I forced a shaky laugh as I waved a hand as if to brush it off. “I mean, he’s not your type. You’d hate him within a week.”


Sierra smirked. “You think so? I can handle a little attitude. I might even like the challenge.”


I swallowed hard. “No, you wouldn’t. He’s a complicated man from what I hear.”


Amber was watching me too closely now. “Why do you sound like you know that based on experience?”


“I don’t,” I said quickly, as a blush spread through my cheeks like wildfire. “I just mean everyone in town’s got some kind of opinion about him, right? Maybe don’t make yourself another one of those stories.”


Sierra leaned back. She was studying me with narrowed eyes and that little grin she got when she was two steps from catching a lie. “You’re awfully defensive about a man you barely know.”


I forced myself to meet her gaze. “I’m not defensive. I’m just saying maybe he’s better left alone.”


Amber exchanged a look with Sierra. There was a silent conversation passing between them before Sierra finally shrugged. “Alright, alright. No need to panic, Jo. I was only kidding. Besides, I am not interested in dating. You know about my bad experience with it, right?”


I exhaled slowly, though my heart was still pounding, as I’d just run ten miles. I wasn’t sure if Sierra was serious about her intention of asking Aidan out. I knew she had been wary of dating since her high school boyfriend cheated on her. From then on, she swore off love. I wonder if she was still scarred from that incident.


Zachary Colton left town after our high school graduation, and it became a cardinal rule never to mention his name in front of Sierra again.


I realized that they had already moved on to another topic. Something about the upcoming fall festival, but my mind was spinning too fast to follow as my mind drifted back to my secret relationship with Aidan.


For the first time since this whole secret thing with him began, it hit me just how fragile it all was.


One slip. One careless word and everything could come crashing down.


By the time I made it to the Blackwood Ranch that late afternoon, the sun was low enough. I spotted Aidan near the paddock, fixing a broken fence post. His shirt was damp with sweat, and his sleeves were rolled up. His forearms were flexing as he worked.


I just stood there, watching this infuriating, impossible man, and wondered what it was about him that made me forget how to breathe.


He didn’t look up when he spoke. “Are you planning to stare all day, or are you going to come over here and give me a kiss?”


I scoffed, but I still walked closer to where he works. “You’re awfully cocky for someone who didn’t even hear me arrive. Besides, I’m not your dog.”


“I heard Daisy’s hooves a mile away,” he replied dryly. “And I could feel you staring. You’ve got that prickly energy when you’re keeping something from me.”


I folded my arms, pretending to be unfazed. “I’m not keeping anything.”


He finally glanced up, those blue eyes locking on mine with a knowing heat. “So what’s got you all worked up, sweetheart?”


I sighed. “Sierra.”


His brow lifted. “Your friend? What about her?”


I glared at him as I kicked at the dirt beside him. Suddenly, I felt ridiculous. “Well, she might’ve said she wanted to take her shot at you.”


That got his attention. His mouth quirked with half amusement, and the other half was something darker. “Did she now?”


“She was joking,” I said quickly. “Mostly, I think.”


He leaned against the fence post, his eyes glinting in the sunset. “And what did you say?”


I looked away, as I could feel the heat spreading through my cheeks. “I told her she couldn’t.”


I could feel his gaze burning into me before he spoke. His voice was dangerously low. “You told her she can’t? Why?”


“Why? Are you sad?” I snapped, crossing my arms on my chest. “Maybe I should have said, ‘Go ahead and take your shot—’”


He cut me off with a smug grin. “Sounds a little like jealousy to me.”


“I’m not jealous!”


“Sure you’re not.” He pushed off the fence, stepping closer until I could feel the heat coming off his skin. “You just couldn’t stand the thought of your friend flirting with me, huh?”

He chuckled meanly. “I’m not teasing. I just like it when you get all territorial. It’s cute.”


“I am not territorial,” I shot back.

“I’m sunburned.”

I rolled my eyes, even as warmth pooled low in my stomach. “You’re so obnoxious.”


“Maybe.” He brushed his lips on me again. It was unhurried this time. “But you like me that way.”


I did, damn it.


Aidan’s lips were still lingering over me when he pulled back just enough to look at me. He still had that infuriating smirk tugging at his mouth. “Still jealous?”


“I told you I’m not,” I muttered, pushing at his chest half-heartedly.


He raised an eyebrow. “Really?”


I sighed exasperatedly. “Yes! Why do you have to be so insanely handsome? You walk into our town, and every woman between sixteen and sixty suddenly forgets how to breathe. You can’t even buy coffee without making someone blush.”


Aidan chuckled. He seemed to be amused at the thought. He ran his thumb over the corner of my mouth. “So now it’s my face’s fault?”


“Yes!” I huffed, trying not to smile. “Do you know how annoying it is to have people talk about you constantly? If I hear one more person say how mysterious and attractive you are, I’m going to lose it.”


He laughed deeply. “You know, I never thought there’d come a day when my girlfriend would complain about my looks. Thought girls liked it when their boyfriend was a very handsome man.”


I narrowed my eyes in his direction. “You’re enjoying this way too much.”


“Maybe a little.”


“I’d prefer it if you weren’t that handsome,” I said with seriousness. “Then no one would bother staring at you.”


“Oh, sweetheart,” he drawled, stepping closer until our noses nearly touched. “You know I can’t do anything about that.”


“Maybe you should,” I muttered under my breath, trying to hide my grin. “Maybe you should do something with your handsomeness. Make your face a bit ugly or something.


He tilted his head, feigning thoughtfulness. Before he leaned down, and his voice dropped to a wicked whisper. “If you’re that worried about other girls, maybe you should do something about it, sweetheart.”


“Like what?” I asked suspiciously.


He tapped his neck, his eyes gleaming with mischief. “Maybe you should give me a mark. You know, a warning sign for everyone else that I’m already taken.”


I stared at him with a horrified expression before laughing all at once at his outrageousness. “Are you kidding me? Are you out of your mind?!”


“Am I?” he teased, angling his throat closer. “Go ahead, Josie Walker. Stake your claim.”


My cheeks burned. “You’re impossible. How can you suggest such a scandalous thing?”


“I’m just giving you options,” he stated, his grin widening when he saw my blush deepen. “We wouldn’t want your friends thinking I’m available, would we?”


I glared at him. But the heat between us was already sparking undeniably. “You’re the worst.”


“That’s how I am, sweetheart,” Aidan smirked.
That lazy grin of his did something terrible to my resolve.


“Stop looking at me like that,” I demanded.


“Like what?” His voice was pure sin.


“You know exactly what you’re doing.”


He grinned grew wider. “I do know exactly what I’m doing.”


Before I could stop myself, I grabbed him by the collar and pulled him down hard enough to wipe that smug smile right off his face. His soft breath brushed against my lips just before I pressed my mouth to his neck, right where his pulse beat strongly beneath his skin.


He went completely still.


I sucked gently on the spot just above his collarbone. I heard his sharp intake of breath and felt the shiver that went through him. My lips curved against his skin.


“J-Josie…” he warned dangerously.


I pulled back just slightly, just enough to whisper against his skin. “There. Now everyone will know you’re taken.”


He laughed huskily. “Sweetheart, if you think that’ll stop anyone from looking, you underestimate yourself.”


Before I could blink, his hand was on the back of my neck. He pulled me in until our mouths met again. He kissed me deeply, and I could faintly taste beer and something darker.


When he finally let me breathe again, his forehead rested against mine. “You just marked me, Josie Walker.”


“I know. Now maybe you’ll behave.”


He chuckled softly, brushing a stray hair from my cheek. “You realize this means I’ll have to return the favor, right?”


I swatted my hand at him, but he caught my hand easily. He pressed a kiss to my knuckles.


“Don’t push your luck.”


His smile softened, and it was less wicked now. “I don’t have to. You already give me everything I want just by showing up.”


We stayed like that for a while, and when he finally kissed me again, I tasted the faintest trace of victory and something terrifyingly close to love in his lips.
