CHAPTER 1


Josie’s POV


By the time I guided Daisy back toward the stable, the sun had already set for the day, and the shadows began to stretch long across the pasture. My heart was still rattled from seeing Wade again unexpectedly. My mind was still replaying the way his smile lingered when he said my name.


It had been years, but to me it felt like only yesterday when we were still together almost every day after school. And now… now he was finally back. Well, not permanently, but only for a few days. I was more than content with it. I was not greedy. A few days were better than nothing at all. Maybe this was my chance, as Sierra suggested.


I slipped from the saddle, brushing Daisy’s mane with steady strokes, trying to calm the storm that raged inside me from seeing that man I had loved all along.


Way to go, Josie…


I shook my head and decided to go to the house. It was almost dinner, and my father would not appreciate it if I were late. I didn’t want to test my fragile relationship with him tonight, but the moment I stepped into the yard, I froze.


The house was alive with noise.


Through the open kitchen window, I heard my mom’s voice sharp with instructions to the maids. On the porch, the gardener was busy sweeping the fallen dry leaves on the lawn even when the sun was already out. The front steps were meticulously clean too, with a bouquet of fresh flowers waiting in a mason jar.


I frowned, climbing the steps with a hand still clutching my riding gloves. “What on earth is going on?” Do we have visitors I am not aware of?


Once inside, the house was in full chaos. A maid was dusting every piece of furniture while another was changing the curtains. The third one was filling vases with fresh flowers as if the president of the United States were coming to our home.


“Josie! Thank goodness you’re back,” Mom called. She was dressed a little extra for this evening. “Go wash up. Dinner is almost ready.”


“Do we have company tonight?” I asked. I knew that my father was already one of the wealthiest men in town, but despite that, our dinner as a family wasn’t always like this.


Mom gave me a look like I’d grown two heads. “You mean you forgot?”


“Forgot what?”


Mom grimaced, her hands on her hips. She looked at me exasperatedly. “Your sister’s coming home tonight, remember? Josie, where’s your head been lately? I already mentioned it to you the other day.”


My heart slumped. Morgan is coming home? Of course. She’d mentioned in her last call that she was visiting, but somehow the thought had been buried under the excitement of Wade’s return.


Now the truth sank in my heart with a heaviness I couldn’t explain. Morgan, the perfect daughter, the daughter who could do nothing wrong in our father’s eyes, was coming home.


I couldn’t help the dread that filled inside me upon hearing the reason why our household was extra busy tonight. My dad’s favorite child is coming home. God knows Dad wouldn’t hesitate to compare me with my perfect sister again at the dinner table with my life’s choices.


I used to tell myself I didn’t mind being the overlooked one. Not everyone was like my sister, right? Not everyone was outgoing and could easily be the center of attention at parties.


But when your older sister is Morgan Walker, you are inevitably bound to be compared to her all the time.


I can still remember school dances, the way boys lined up for her with their eyes following every sway of her dress and every toss of her golden hair. If I stood nearby, it was only to hold her purse or fetch her a drink. Teachers praised her confidence, the neighbors adored her charm, and even strangers on Main Street seemed to draw their attention to her when she passed.


Meanwhile, I lingered in the background as if I were part of the wall. My words were swallowed before they reached my lips, and my presence was barely noticed by everyone when she was around.


Mom used to say, “Josie, you’re just more reserved, that’s all. Morgan is outgoing and the life of the party. That’s why she got all the attention.”


I smiled when she said it, but deep down I wondered: did that mean I was meant to be nothing more than a shadow for my picture-perfect sister?


I remembered the way Morgan used to wrinkle her nose whenever Wade Blackwood was around.


Back then, he was nothing more than the ‘sickly-looking cowboy’ who tracked mud into our kitchen and smelled like hay after a long day. I can still hear her sharp little sighs when he teased her about breaking her polished nails if she tried climbing the fence or when he called her ‘princess’ just to see her scowl on her face.


“Ugh, Josie, why do you follow him around?” she’d whisper to me once. She flipped her shiny hair over her shoulder in irritation. “There’s nothing special about him. Just a cowboy who thinks he’s funny. There are a lot of guys you can give your attention to other than him.”


But I never agreed with her. To me, Wade was special. He was the boy who gave me a steady hand when I slipped in the creek. The one who carried extra apples in his pocket for my horse. The one who sat with me under the sycamore tree when Dad scolded me again.


Morgan only saw the dirt under his boots and the way he laughed too loudly or when he was criticizing her princess persona in our little town.


Even now, years later, the thought curled tight in my chest. Wade might have changed, but Morgan hadn’t. She still had the kind of beauty that made people stop and stare. She was still the woman whose confidence drew every gaze in the room. I knew this because she came to visit once a year despite seemingly favoring her city life now.


While I was still just Josie. The unnoticed sister. The sister no one remembered until Morgan wasn’t around anymore.


I removed all those dark thoughts from my head as I finished showering. There was no reason to be insecure about Morgan’s homecoming. For all I know, she will be here for only a good two days since she thinks that our hometown is too boring.


Steam still clung to my skin as I pulled on a simple cotton dress, the soft blue one I always favored when I didn’t know what else to wear. My hair was still damp, curling at the ends as I combed it back with my fingers. The smell of roast chicken drifted up the staircase, which made my stomach give a low growl. I was halfway down the stairs when the front door opened.


The laughter that was brighter than the sun was familiar and unmistakable as it floated through the hall.


Morgan.


I slowed my pace, my hand tightening on the banister as our parents’ voices rose in a chorus of greetings for their favorite daughter.


“There’s my girl! I miss you, sweetpea,” Dad boomed, the sound of his boots crossing the hardwood floor to greet his firstborn.


“Oh, sweetheart, let me look at you,” Mom cooed, her tone bursting with delight. “You look so beautiful! Is this truly my daughter?”


“Oh, Mom, please!” I could hear the laughter in Morgan’s voice. “You do know how to make me smile.”


From the landing, I could see their interaction. A picture-perfect family.


Morgan stood in the middle of the living room, her face framed by the golden locks. She wore a sleek, wine-colored dress that hugged her figure just so, the kind of dress I’d never dare to wear. Her hair was styled and caught the light like a halo, while her smile—oh, don’t get me there—lit up the whole room, as everyone says.


She looked like she belonged on the cover of one of those glossy magazines I sometimes peeked at in the grocery store.


With my hair still damp and my plain cotton dress, I looked out of place to my overly dressed family. I suddenly felt small, like the little sister who was always second best next to her.


Mom fussed over her, brushing imaginary lint from her shoulder while Dad lifted her suitcase as though it weighed nothing at all. The air vibrated with their excitement and their joy at having her finally at home again.


While I lingered halfway down the stairs, invisible in the shadows. I clutched the banister as though it might steady the strange twist in my chest.


Morgan was home.


And just like that, the house belonged to her again. I hadn’t realized how tightly I’d been gripping the banister until my fingers ached. The room buzzed with Morgan’s laughter, her voice carrying like music in a crowded hall. For a moment, I considered slipping back upstairs and forgetting about dinner. I’d rather starve tonight than suffer from a dinner that I didn’t even want, but my foot creaked against the last step.


Mom turned towards the staircase. “Josie! There you are.”


All at once, three pairs of eyes landed on me. I felt my cheeks creep with heat. I stood there in my simple blue dress, and my hair was still damp from my shower. I couldn’t have looked plainer even if I tried.


“Josie,” Morgan said, her lips curving into a smile. She crossed the room with the kind of grace I’d never quite managed. Up close, the scent of her expensive perfume lingered in the air sharply. She tilted her head, her eyes sweeping over me all at once. “Well, little sister. It’s been a while.”


I swallowed hard. “Welcome home, Morgan.” My voice sounded too small even to my own ears.


Her smile widened, the kind that could charm anyone. Though to me, it always felt like a reminder that we were playing roles we’d never chosen. She was the golden girl, while I was the ugly sister.


A low rumble of an engine sounded from the driveway that distracted me from my depressing thoughts. A pair of headlights washed across the lawn as a truck crunched to a stop outside.


Dad straightened as he looked surprised. “Now who could that be?”


Mom looked confused, too. “We weren’t expecting anyone else tonight.”


Curiosity sparked in me. I went closer to the window, peeking through the heavy curtain. My heart tripped at the sight of the dark pickup parked outside, dust still settling around its tires. The driver’s side door swung open, and Wade Blackwood stepped out.


My breath was caught. After all these years, his effect on me was still as strong as it was when I was still a teenager. Why was he here? Why tonight, of all nights, when Morgan had just returned?


The front door creaked open before anyone could react, and there he was. The man who could make my heart wild just at the sight of him.


He might be older now, more mature. He was broader through the shoulders with muscles in his body, but just enough to make him more manly. But he was still Wade—the boy whose smiles once filled my teenage dreams. His eyes swept over the room, and for a moment, they landed on me. There was a flicker of recognition before his face broke into a soft smile. My heart stumbled so hard it almost hurt.


“Evening, folks,” he said with that same easy drawl curling around the words like a true Southern gentleman. “Sorry for barging in unannounced.”


“Wade!” Dad’s voice boomed with genuine surprise. He appeared to be delighted to see him. He crossed the room to clasp his hand for a handshake. “What a surprise. Good to see you again, young man!”


Mom approached next, fussing at him like he’d never left town at all. “Oh, look at you! Such a fine young man you have become. When did you come back? How are your parents these days?”


Morgan stepped forward, her heels clicking against the newly renovated floor of our house. Her expression was blank at first but then broke into something else entirely. Something like triumphant.


“Well,” she said, her smile glittering under the chandelier lights. “I was hoping to surprise you all, but I guess Wade had other plans.”


I blinked. Surprise? What kind of surprise?


Before anyone could react, Wade slipped an arm around Morgan’s waist and pulled her close to his body. My breath caught at the sight of them striking me like a blow.


No. No, this wasn’t—


Morgan’s grin widened, and it felt like it was aimed squarely at me, though she spoke to everyone. “Well, we have been keeping this for a while now, and we thought it was time you guys knew our little secret. Wade and I are engaged!” She made a show of displaying her enormous engagement ring for everyone to see.


Mom gasped in delight while Dad allowed himself to laugh proudly. Then my mom was already asking about dates and details without even recovering from her initial shock.


But all I heard was the ringing in my ears.


Engaged.


The word pressed down on my chest mercilessly. My fingers curled against my skirt while trying to hide how they trembled. Wade, the boy I’d secretly loved all these years, had chosen her. My sister once dismissed him as an irritating boy who never saw what I saw. Now he belonged to her.


Morgan’s triumphant smile burned into me brighter than any spotlight. For the first time in years, I desperately wished I was anywhere but here.


The room suddenly blurred around me. My parents were so happy with the news. They were eager to hear every detail, to soak in the glow of the happy couple.


As for me, I couldn’t look away from them. I was too stunned to speak. I was still having a hard time accepting their news.


Wade stood tall and proud at Morgan’s side. His hand was resting easily on her back, as though it had always belonged there. He looked dignified, the way I always remembered him, but his smile now belonged to Morgan.


Not me.


Never me.


Morgan looked at him lovingly. She rested her head against his shoulder, her eyes sparkling as she told the story of how he’d proposed in one of the skyscrapers of New York City. Every word was eased with the kind of charm only Morgan could wield. My parents couldn’t be happier for them.


I just stood there. My smile was frozen on my face as my chest tightened with a pain I didn’t dare to show. I wanted to be happy for them. I truly do. But how can I do that when it felt like my heart was breaking? I had dreamed of this moment too, for years in the quiet corners of my heart. I’d imagined Wade looking at me with that same warmth he was giving Morgan. I’d imagine him slipping a ring on my finger and promising forever.


But that was not going to happen. Not in this lifetime, at least.


I pressed my nails into my palms that were hidden in the folds of my dress. I was desperate for something to keep myself from crying. My throat started to burn, but I forced myself to swallow it down. I couldn’t ruin this night. Not when everyone was so happy.


So I just watched. I stood on the sidelines like I always do, watching the golden girl shine with the boy I always loved. He was holding her hand as if she were his whole world.


And with every smile and every loving glance they shared, a little piece of me began to shatter and fall away. I had almost managed to disappear and hide in my room when Morgan’s voice cut through the noise.


“Josie.”


Her gaze found me across the room expectantly. She tugged Wade a little closer to where I had been standing. Her arm looped through his as if to remind me exactly where he stood. “Aren’t you going to say something to your big sister?”


The room fell quiet as their eyes shifted toward me. They were expecting me to share their happiness.


My heart thudded painfully, every beat echoing in my ears. The words lodged in my throat like stones, but I forced myself to smile. The kind of smile that felt like brittle, thin glass that was about to crack at the slightest pressure.


“Congratulations,” I managed to say. My voice remained steady enough to make them believe I was nothing but genuinely happy for them. I nodded toward them both. “I—I-I’m… really happy for you guys.”


Morgan’s lips curved into a satisfied grin, her eyes glinting like she’d won something. She leaned into Wade’s shoulder, basking in the moment.


Wade’s eyes softened as he looked at me. A wistful smile touched his mouth. The kind of smile I couldn’t quite read.


Gratitude? Relief? Or maybe just kindness, the kind he’d always shown me back in the day.


That same smile that made my chest ache even more. Because a part of me still wanted to believe this was just a bad nightmare.


But it was not a nightmare.


It was very much the reality.


He was hers now.


And I had just given them my blessing, even if it shattered me inside.


Way to go, Josie…

CHAPTER 28


Josie’s POV


I came down the stairs the following morning in a bright mood. The sun had barely crept over the ridge, but I’d already packed a small basket of biscuits, bacon, and hot coffee.


I would surprise Aidan with breakfast and stay for a bit so we could actually eat breakfast together before I went to the shop.


I was just about to cut through the hallway when the echo of voices stopped me in my tracks.


It was Morgan. Her voice was furious. What was she doing here? I thought she was in New York for her work? “…Why did you come here, huh? You should’ve followed me to New York instead of playing the wounded husband hiding in Oakridge!”


I gasped behind the half-closed front door as my heart was pounding.


Did I hear it correctly? Are they fighting?


Wade’s reply came dangerously low. “You don’t get to talk about hiding when you’re the one who ruined our honeymoon, Morgan.”


My older sister’s voice cracked. It was sharp enough to slice through the morning stillness. “Stop acting like a bastard, Wade. You think I don’t know what’s going on in that head of yours?”


I hadn’t meant to listen. Goodness, I didn’t want to, but my feet wouldn’t move as I was stuck on the other side of the door. I didn’t know what the good time to announce my presence was.


But I didn’t have all day waiting for them to finish their conversation when I should be on my way to the Blackwood Ranch. I had decided to make myself known by coming out of the mansion with loud footsteps.


“Josie,” Morgan said suddenly, her tone snapping from rage to polite sweetness.


I smiled back. Her expression was perfectly composed as if she hadn’t just been spitting fire at her husband seconds ago. Wade appeared behind her with a tight jaw and his eyes flicking to me for a split second too long.


I forced a smile and lifted the basket between us. “Good morning. What a surprise. I thought you were in New York for work?”


Morgan smiled tightly while discreetly shooting a glance at Wade. “Yes. My work ended unexpectedly early. That’s why I decided to head back to town, too. What about you? Where are you going this early?”


“Oh, I was just heading out to the stable. I thought I’d bring breakfast with me.”


Morgan nodded. “Still love having breakfast in the woods, do you?”


I shrugged casually. “You could say that.”


Wade’s lips curved in something that wasn’t quite a smile. “Enjoy your ride, Josie.”


“Thanks,” I murmured, already inching toward the stable. As I got away from them, I drew a long breath I didn’t know I was holding. The cold morning air was scraping against my skin, and I still could feel Wade’s stare on my back.


Whatever that fight had been about, one thing was clear: Something was breaking between them. And somehow, I had the awful feeling I was standing right in the middle of the fault line.


I pushed that thought away as I headed for Daisy’s stall. I couldn’t wait to see Aidan.


The gravel road crunched under Daisy’s hooves as I rode up the familiar trail. The closer I got to Aidan’s house, the lighter I felt. I was leaving everything heavy behind at the Walker Ranch.


By the time I reached his porch, I could hardly keep the smile off my face. The basket was still warm in my hands. The scent of fresh biscuits still lingered in the air. I knocked eagerly, my heart fluttering faster than I cared to admit.


The door hadn’t even fully opened when a strong arm caught me around the waist and pulled me inside.


“Aidan—” I barely managed to say before his mouth claimed my lips in a kiss. I squeaked a laugh against his lips as he kissed me again, until my back hit the wall and my fingers curled into his shirt.


“Good morning to you, too,” I murmured breathlessly when he finally let me catch air. “You’re up early today.”


He grinned against my cheek and planted another trail of kisses down my jaw. “Morning, sweetheart. You taste better than coffee.”


I laughed, squirming as he kept kissing every bit of skin he could find. “Aidan, I brought you breakfast—”


“I’d rather eat you for breakfast instead. God, you’re so delectable,” he murmured, nipping lightly at my throat.


My face went hot. I pushed at his chest with a laugh. “I’m serious. I’m here for breakfast, not a quick tumble in bed.”


He paused, leaning back just enough to look at me. There was a teasing smile tugging at his lips. “Is that so? What a shame then.”


“Yes,” I answered sternly while trying to sound firm even though my pulse was still racing. “You already had your way last night, Mister.”


“Still not enough.”


“It is for now.” I slipped out of his arms and headed toward the kitchen. I could feel his eyes on me. I set the basket down on the table and started unpacking the food. The biscuits were still warm, the strips of bacon wrapped in cloth, and there was a small jar of jam.


I heard him move closer. “Are you arranging breakfast for two?”


I glanced at him over my shoulder. “If you don’t mind company for breakfast. I thought of staying for a bit before heading to the shop.”


He didn’t answer at first. He then crossed the space between us with a crooked smile appearing on his handsome face. “Yes, I’d like that a lot.”


Aidan had insisted on making the coffee, even though I’d brought enough for both of us. Now we sat across from each other at the wooden table. The sunlight slanted in through the kitchen window, catching on his messy hair and the lazy smile that never left his face when he looked at me.


He reached over, swiping a crumb from the corner of my mouth. “You’ve got jam.”


I batted his hand away, laughing nervously. “Is that so?”


“Yes,” he said easily. “Is it really that good? The sandwich, I mean?”


“You want to try it?” I offered him a bite of my sandwich, and without hesitation, he obliged.


We ate in silence for a while, the kind of silence that felt calm and unhurried. I hadn’t realized how much I’d missed this, the ordinary peace of being with him without watching the clock or worrying about who might see us.


Then, I remembered what had happened this morning, and the warmth in my chest dimmed a little.


“By the way,” I said casually, tracing the rim of my mug. “Morgan’s back in town, too.”


Aidan’s fork paused midair. “Really?”


I nodded grimly. “I kind of overheard them arguing outside about something when I came downstairs this morning.”


His brow furrowed slightly, interest flickering in his eyes. “Arguing? About what?”


I hesitated, unsure how much to say. I sighed deeply. “I don’t know exactly. I didn’t catch everything. But I think it wasn’t about Morgan’s work, that’s for sure.”


He leaned back in his chair, and the easy grin on his face quickly faded. “What makes you say that?”


I toyed with my biscuit. “Remember when Wade told us that their honeymoon ended early because Morgan had some project in New York? But I heard her this morning. She said something about him acting like a bastard and that he didn’t follow her to the city because he wanted to come here instead.”


Aidan’s jaw flexed slightly. “So they fought during the honeymoon.”


“That’s what it sounded like,” I admitted. “I think he lied about the reason why he came here. I don’t know what’s going on, but I think there’s trouble in paradise already. I’ve never heard Morgan sound that angry before.”


Aidan exhaled. He settled his mug down with a soft clink. “And now he’s staying under the same roof as you because he had some hidden intentions.”


“Just for a few days,” I said quickly, though I wasn’t sure who I was trying to convince, him or myself. “He said he’s just here on some business in town.”


He gave a humorless laugh. “Yeah, I bet he does.”


“Hey, now that his wife is here, maybe they were heading back to the city. I think the reason Morgan came to town was to end their rift and fetch Wade.” The silence that followed wasn’t uncomfortable, but it carried weight with an unspoken awareness that Wade’s presence wasn’t just a coincidence.


Aidan reached across the table again, brushing his fingers over mine. “Don’t let your guard down when he’s around, all right? I don’t trust your new brother-in-law. I don’t care if he was your friend in the past.”


I looked up at him, a small smile tugging at my lips despite the twist in my chest. “Don’t you trust me?”


“I do trust you, sweetheart.” His gaze softened, but the protective edge in his voice didn’t fade. “It was him that I didn’t trust. I know trouble when I see it, Josie. And Wade Blackwood looks a lot like trouble.”


I could feel the heaviness in the air between us, that lingering tension from talking about Wade and Morgan. Aidan’s thumb was still stroking the back of my hand, but his jaw was tight again. That stubborn muscle twitched whenever he was thinking too much.


I decided to steer them both back to safer waters. “Do you have any plans for the fall festival?”


He blinked. He clearly had no idea about the most anticipated festival in Oakridge. “The what?”


I laughed at the utterly blank look on his face. “You’ve got to be kidding me! You’ve been in Oakridge for months now, and no one’s told you about the fall festival? Not even the ladies who were always trying to flirt with you at the diner?”


“I can’t say they have,” he smirked. “I don’t really pay attention to them. I only have my eyes on a certain Southern belle.”


“Is that so?” I asked dubiously. Though I couldn’t help the grin slipping on my face. “Anyway, there’s going to be a parade, a pie contest, music, and a little dance in the square. Everyone in town goes.”


“Even you?”


“Even me. Usually, I help my mom with the floral booths, but…” I hesitated for a moment, then decided not to overthink it. “Maybe this year, I’ll actually just enjoy the festival.”


Aidan tilted his head, studying me. “And you’re asking if I’ve got plans because…?”


I met his eyes. There was an unmistakable glint in my eyes. “Because I was thinking maybe you’d want to go to the festival. With me.”


The silence that followed was short but heavy. His expression softened in a careful way that always undid me. He looked at me as if he wasn’t sure if he’d heard me right. “With you?”


“Yeah, as my… uhm, neighbor, of course. You know, since the whole town will be there. You wouldn’t want to look unfriendly with your new neighbor.”


That earned a low chuckle from him. “Ah, I see. So we’re friendly neighbors now.”


“We’ve been friendly,” I said primly, though my cheeks were already warm.


He leaned forward, his elbows on the table, and his eyes were glinting with amusement. “You sure about that, sweetheart? Because I could’ve sworn what we were doing lately was a little more than friendly.”


I shot him a sharp look.  He was smiling wickedly, the one who always made my heart trip.


When I didn’t answer, he asked quietly, “You’d really want to be seen with me, Josie? With everyone watching?”


For a moment, the air between us shifted. I met his gaze steadily this time. “Yes. Because we’re neighbors, remember?”


He chuckled, but there was something warm in his eyes. “Right. Just neighbors.”


“Exactly,” I agreed, trying not to grin.


“Then I’ll be there,” he said simply, the corner of his mouth lifting. “With my pretty neighbor.”


“Good,” I fought a smile I couldn’t quite hide.


Aidan leaned back, finishing the rest of his coffee. “You know, Josie Walker, for someone who claims she doesn’t want anyone to suspect anything about us. You’re getting awfully bold.”


I shrugged nonchalantly. “Maybe I just want to see what the fuss is all about.”


He smirked. “Oh, sweetheart. You have no idea what kind of fuss you’ll be starting.”


The morning sunlight had grown warmer. The coffee was gone. The plates were nearly empty, and neither of us made a move to clear them. Also, I had stayed longer than I intended to for breakfast.


Aidan was watching me again. Not in that hungry way he sometimes did, but thoughtfully. His elbows rested on the table, and his fingers were idly tracing the rim of his mug.


“What?” I asked. My lips curved in a teasing way. “You look like you’re trying to memorize my face.”


“Maybe I am. You look different this morning.”


My brows drew together. “Different how?”


He tilted his head. “You look peaceful, like you belong here in this house and in this kitchen with me.”


Something fluttered deep inside me when he said those words. I wanted to tease him. Instead, I found myself asking him carefully. “You think so?”


He turned his palm upward, catching my fingers and drawing me closer until I had to stand. He rose from his seat, too. He closed the space between us as his hands settled on my hips like it was the most natural thing in the world.


“I could get used to this,” he murmured against my hair. “Waking up and having breakfast with you.”


I laughed, pressing my cheek to his chest. “Careful, Callahan. You’re starting to sound dangerously domestic.”


He chuckled. I could feel his heartbeat quicken beneath my ear. “I guess that’s what happens when a man falls for a certain woman.”


My heart skipped a beat. He hadn’t said 'I love you,' not exactly, but it was close enough to make my knees weak. I tipped my head back to look at him. “You’re not playing fair.”


He brushed a stray strand of hair from my face while looking down at me. “I’m not trying to.”


The world outside seemed very far away.  It felt like it was just this kitchen, this morning, and the man who somehow made me forget I was supposed to be careful.


I sighed deeply while stepping back reluctantly. I didn’t want to be reckless. I was not even sure if what I felt about him was just infatuation or something else.


We have just known each other for a few months, and I didn’t think that it was enough to say if I had fallen for him already. I was not even sure if I had already gone over to Wade completely.


“I should go,” I said reluctantly, pulling away from his embrace. “It’s already late. If I don’t show up at the shop soon, my customers might send a search party.”


Aidan smiled faintly, though I saw the flicker of disappointment in his eyes. “Yeah. Go before I decide to keep you here all day.”


I rose on my tiptoes and kissed him lingeringly. “You’d like that too much, huh?”


He grinned against my mouth. “You have no idea.”


I laughed before I gathered the basket and was about to leave. “I’ll see you later?”


“I’ll be counting the hours until then,” he said simply.


I left his house and mounted Daisy. My heart was still warm from the way he looked at me. Somewhere deep down, I knew I was in far deeper than I’d ever meant to be.

CHAPTER 29


Josie’s POV


There was something about the first morning of the fall festival that always made Oakridge feel like a picture-perfect community.


The air was cold and smelled like sweet cinnamon and roasted nuts. The streets were lined with stalls draped in orange and gold. The kids darted between hay bales, their laughter mixing with the crowd as the music spilled from the bandstand at the corner of Main Street.


I’d barely had a chance to catch my breath since dawn as I ran around with Sierra and Amber. We helped string the banners, wrangle volunteers, and make sure the pumpkin display didn’t topple over for the third time.


By midmorning, I was standing by the flower booth we’d decorated. My fingers were smudged with dirt, and my hair slipped from its braid. But I was grinning like a fool.


“You’re suspiciously happy today, Miss Walker,” Sierra commented as she handed me a paper cup of cider. “Don’t tell me it’s because of the festival. You love this event every year, sure, but this year? This is another level.”


Amber smirked, brushing hay off her jeans. “Yeah, you’re practically glowing. Did you win the lottery or something?”


I rolled my eyes, trying to hide my smile behind the cup. “Maybe I just love seeing our little town all dressed up for our favorite time of the year. The fall festival makes everything look alive.”


Sierra wasn’t buying it. “Alive, huh? Is that code for a certain someone that makes your heart gallop like a thoroughbred run?”


I choked on my cider. “What? Seriously, Sierra!”


Amber laughed. “Relax, she’s just teasing you, Jo. Though, you do have to admit that there’s this look about you lately…”


I waved them off. My cheeks burned as I pretended to focus on rearranging the flower garland around our stall. “I’m just happy it’s fall, okay? The weather’s perfect. The cider’s good. And nobody’s crying over bad flower arrangements. Let me have this.”


They exchanged a knowing look, but thankfully, Sierra only grinned. “Fine, fine. We’ll let you enjoy your mysterious happiness. But you can trust us, you know. I think you’re being secretive lately.”


I threw a handful of petals at her. “Sierra!”


She only laughed harder while ducking away. Their laughter echoed over the music and the chatter of Main Street.


I tried to keep my smile casual, but my pulse was racing. What my friends didn’t know was that Aidan Callahan had promised he’d come today. And if he did, I wasn’t sure how I’d manage to hide what he really meant to me.


“Oh, look who just arrived. The newlyweds have just arrived,” Amber murmured.


I followed her gaze toward the entrance of Main Street, where I saw Morgan and Wade.


They looked like something out of a magazine spread. Morgan wore a cream sundress that caught the breeze. She had her blonde hair swept into soft curls. While Wade stood beside her in a crisp white shirt rolled at the sleeves, his hand resting casually on the small of her back.


They smiled for the crowd and waved at familiar faces. They were the picture of small-town perfection.


Standing beside me, Sierra let out a low whistle. “Damn. They really came here to make an entrance, did they?”


Amber nodded, adjusting the sunhat that rested on her hair. “They look good together. Like, insanely good. Sorry, Jo. You’d think they just walked out of a bridal catalog.”


She looked at me apologetically, but I just shook my head silently, telling her that she didn’t offend me.


Sierra turned to me cautiously. “Are you okay?”


“Yeah. I’m okay. Really. It doesn’t hurt anymore. Not like it used to.” I blinked at her unexpected question, and I was almost surprised at how easily my answer came out.


Sierra studied my face. She appeared to be trying to read between the lines before she gave a small smile. “Good. You deserve that kind of peace.”


I returned it with a faint nod, though my eyes stayed on the couple who just arrived.


Amber sighed dreamily. “They look perfect, don’t they? They were like one of those couples who just have everything figured out.”


“Yeah,” I agreed with Amber. “They do.”


Even if I said that, something inside me twisted. I couldn’t stop hearing Morgan’s angry voice from yesterday morning echoing in my head.


You acted like a bastard, Wade!


I remember the way they’d frozen when they saw me standing in the doorway. They looked perfect now, yes. They were smiling, glowing, and in sync with the festival lights.


But I’d seen the cracks in their marriage already. I couldn’t help but wonder what it meant that Wade was here in Oakridge when Morgan clearly hadn’t wanted him to be.


“Josie!” Morgan called out. Her voice was as bright as ever, as if there hadn’t been a single crack in her perfect world. She walked toward us with Wade, a steady shadow behind her.


Both of them were wearing that easy smile that said everything was fine.


I matched it and gave my best smile. “Hey, I didn’t know you’d be coming by the festival.”


“Oh, we wouldn’t miss it. Dad mentioned you’d be helping organize some of the booths. It looks amazing, Josie. You really outdid yourself this year,” Morgan said smoothly.


“Thanks,” I replied, shifting my weight. I tried to ignore how Wade’s eyes flicked over me with too much familiarity.


Sierra and Amber jumped into the conversation before I could, as Amber gushed. “Morgan, you look stunning. Europe must have been incredible. We’ve been dreaming of going to Europe someday.”


Morgan laughed. “It was wonderful. The honeymoon was short, but we made the most of it.”


Her eyes darted briefly to Wade’s. Some unspoken conversation passed between them, a flicker too fast for anyone else to notice, but not for me.


Wade cleared his throat and looked at me. “It’s good to see you, Josie. We rarely see you at home. You’ve been busy at the festival?”


“Yeah, you could say that,” I smiled politely, even as something inside me bristled.


The conversation turned safely back to travel and town gossip, where Sierra and Amber carried most of it while I nodded at the right times. Everything about the moment felt choreographed, as if we were all actors performing our roles. The dutiful sister. The perfect wife. The polite ex-secret love.


And then, I heard the familiar sound of an engine over the chatter and laughter. I turned my head just as the black truck rolled to a stop near the main street curb.


Aidan climbed out. He looked devastatingly out of place and yet somehow looked perfect in his dark jeans. He rolled up his sleeves in that self-assured way he carried himself. The sun caught in his hair, and his blue eyes scanned the crowd before landing right on me.


I couldn’t stop the smile that spread across my face, something my body decided before my mind could catch up.


Sierra noticed immediately, her grin widening like a cat with a secret. “Well, well. Someone’s in a good mood all of a sudden.”


I tried to school my features, tucking a strand of hair behind my ear as if that could hide the warmth blooming across my cheeks. “I don’t know what you’re talking about.”


“Uh-huh,” Sierra answered.


“I didn’t expect him to come.” I didn’t know who I was convincing more with my statement.


Either way, the moment Aidan walked into the festival, everything else blurred into the background. When Aidan’s eyes found mine across the crowd, the look we shared said everything we couldn’t in public.


Holy hell. I couldn’t stop the smile slipping from my face. Aidan started walking toward us. His confident stride was impossible to ignore. I could feel my heart rate pick up with every step he took.


Morgan was the first to notice him approaching. “Isn’t that the new owner of the Blackwood Ranch?”


I swallowed quickly before answering, trying to keep my voice light. “Yeah. Aidan Callahan. He’s our neighbor.”


Morgan smiled, adjusting the hem of her dress. “Ah, the man who outbid half the ranchers eyeing that property.”


“Good afternoon, ladies,” he said smoothly, his deep voice somehow managing to sound polite and intimate at once. He flicked his gaze over each of us politely before lingering on me for a long moment.


“Mr. Callahan,” Morgan said brightly, extending a hand. “I’m Morgan Walker. Well, Morgan Blackwood now. My husband is the son of the previous owner of your new ranch. We have already met on a few occasions, and yet, we have not formally introduced ourselves.”


Aidan shook her hand briefly, the corner of his mouth quirking up in that knowing way that made my stomach flutter. “That’s true. Congratulations on your wedding. It was beautiful.”


Morgan preened a little before Wade stepped forward. He offered his hand as well. “Wade Blackwood. Nice to finally meet you. The Blackwood place looks better than it has in years.”


“I appreciate that. Your family’s property has always had the prettiest view from my porch,” Aidan replied, shaking his hand firmly.


That earned a laugh from Morgan, but I caught the slight twitch of Wade’s jaw. Meanwhile, Aidan turned to me with the faintest trace of amusement sparking in those blue eyes of his.


“Josie,” he said with mock formality, dipping his head just a little. “Always good to see my favorite neighbor out supporting the town.”


I nearly choked on air. “You too, Mr. Callahan.”


“Mr. Callahan?” he repeated softly as amusement tugged at his lips. “We’re being formal today, huh? I thought we already established that we’re already friends?”


My glare promised a murder scene if he pushed his luck even further, but the wicked man merely smirked. He was clearly enjoying my discomfort far too much.


Amber and Sierra were openly staring now. Their expressions were unreadable, but they were definitely entertaining. Morgan continued with the small talk while I tried to keep my expression neutral.


Aidan stood there as if he belonged. He was perfectly relaxed with his hands tucked into his pockets while I silently warned him not to say something that would give us away.


And just when I thought I might combust under the effort of pretending, an elderly couple called out from the next booth. “Aidan! You must come and say hello!”


He looked over at the elderly couple, then back at me. His eyes glinted with quiet mischief.


“Excuse me, will you?” he murmured, his voice low enough for only me to hear his next words. “Try not to miss me too much, neighbor.”


I could only shoot him another warning look as he strolled away toward the couple, leaving me to deal with the very obvious curiosity in my friends’ and sister’s eyes. And the worst part was, I knew he’d done that on purpose just to see me squirm.


Sierra and Amber had been hovering by the judges’ table while I went over the last batch of pies for the contest. The air was filled with laughter and that easy, small-town chatter that always filled Main Street during the Fall Festival.


I was just about to announce the winner when Sierra leaned closer, whispering behind her cup. “Hey, Josie. Look, but don’t be obvious, but your neighbor looks like he’s enjoying himself.”


My brows drew together. “What?”


Amber smiled and nodded toward the end of the street, where Aidan stood near the caramel apple stand.


He was talking to a blonde in a sundress. I knew that woman. She was a schoolmate from high school, Lauren. She was laughing, and her hand was brushing his arm like she’d been waiting for this moment her entire life.


An irrational twist hit my stomach.


“What about it? I think he’s just being friendly,” I said quickly, adjusting the clipboard in my hand.


Sierra smirked devilishly. “Friendly? She’s about two seconds away from climbing him like a tree.”


Amber giggled into her cup. “I mean, I can’t blame her. He’s Aidan Callahan. The wealthy city guy who decides to grace our boring little town with his beauty.”


I rolled my eyes, focusing very hard on the pie slices. “You’re both ridiculous.”


But my gaze betrayed me. I couldn’t help but steal another glance, just a quick one. He was leaning in slightly, smiling at whatever Lauren had been saying to him. The sight hit me like a pin to the chest.


Don’t be ridiculous, Josie.


He wasn’t mine. Not here, not publicly. I had no right to feel that burning sting in my chest.


By the time I’d finished judging, I’d told myself sternly that I didn’t care. When Sierra handed me a cup of apple cider sangria and pulled me into the crowd. I was laughing again, trying to shake off that strange ache I had felt seeing Aidan getting friendly with another woman.


We stood near one of the hay bales. We joked about the terrible karaoke contest when I heard that familiar drawl behind me. “Good afternoon again, ladies.”


I turned around. Aidan was here with his hands in his pockets. The afternoon light caught in his hair, and my heart did a stupid little leap.


“Oh, hey, Callahan,” Sierra said with a grin that was far too innocent. “Enjoying the festival so far?”


“Very much. Nice to see familiar faces,” he said, his eyes flicking to me for a beat.


“I bet. You can’t stop smiling at Lauren,” I muttered under my breath as I sipped my apple cider sangria.


Amber tilted her head, looking confused at my snappy reaction. “Do you drink, Mr. Callahan? We’ve got cider and sangria. Josie’s been hoarding the good stuff since she knew I couldn’t drink.”


“Yes, I can tell,” he said, amusement glinting in his tone while he shot me a look. “What’s in that one?”


Sierra turned to grab him a cup. “It’s sangria. Hold on, I’ll get—”


“You want to try it? This is Oakridge's specialty. Here. Try mine,” I interrupted before I could stop myself. I thrust my own glass toward him.


Aidan’s eyes held my gaze for a heartbeat longer than necessary. Without hesitation, he took the glass from my hand and drank from it slowly without breaking our eye contact. He looked like he knew exactly what he was doing.


Sierra and Amber’s jaws dropped in unison. I could practically feel the heat creeping up my neck.


“Not bad,” Aidan said finally, setting the glass back on the table with a satisfied smirk tugging at his lips. “Are you mad at me?”


“Why would I be mad at you?” I murmured, trying to sound unfazed even though my pulse was roaring in my ears.


Sierra was staring between us as if she’d just uncovered the plot twist of the century. Amber looked like she was trying to cover her astonishment.


“Well, that’s very neighborly of you two,” Sierra drawled, her eyes dancing with mischief.


Aidan’s mouth curved dangerously. I felt that smirk before I saw it. Before I could even react, his hand slid to the small of my back. He anchored me in place next to his body. “I think the cat is out of the bag already. To tell you the truth, we’re more than neighbors, actually. Right, sweetheart?”


The world seemed still. Sierra’s jaw dropped again, and Amber’s eyes went comically wide.


My heart leapt straight into my throat.


“Aidan—” I started, but he only squeezed my waist gently while his eyes were still fixed on my friends.


“We’ve been seeing each other for a while now. We didn’t mean to keep it from you. I guess now’s as good a time as any to share it with you guys.” He added, like he wasn’t detonating a bomb in the middle of Main Street.


Sierra blinked. Twice. “You two are what?!”


Amber let out a tiny gasp that quickly turned into a delighted squeal. “Josie Walker, you sneaky—”


I buried my face in my hands as Sierra laughed incredulously. That didn’t go unnoticed. Even Morgan and Wade looked in our direction curiously.


“Oh my God! So this is why you freaked out when I said I’d flirt with him! I thought you were just being weird!” Sierra concluded.


I peeked through my fingers to glare at him, but Aidan was grinning as he’d just won something. He leaned in and brushed his lip on the edge of my ear.


“Relax. They were bound to find out eventually, weren't they? Besides, there’s no reason to keep our relationship secret, especially when your brother-in-law kept on shooting you glances. I hate that I am with you and yet, I can’t touch you.”


Maybe so, but not like this. Not in the middle of the Fall Festival, surrounded by half the town and my two friends, who were clearly about to combust with glee.


Amber clasped her hands merrily. “Oh, this is so much better than I imagined.”


I groaned softly, wishing the ground would swallow me whole. But when Aidan’s thumb brushed a slow circle against my back steadily, my mortification melted into something else entirely.


Despite everything. Despite Wade’s gaze. Despite the whisper I knew would spread by morning. I couldn’t stop smiling.

CHAPTER 30


Josie’s POV


I wasn’t expecting to see him too soon.


The little bell above my flower shop door chimed. I looked up from the arrangement I was doing with the bouquet of sunflowers when I nearly dropped the bundle on the floor.


Aidan’s presence filled the doorway. He was wearing dark jeans, a blue shirt, and that lazy confidence that made the women on Main Street lose their train of thought just at the sight of him. He had this smirk that could undo every ounce of calm I’d managed to build since the Fall Festival.


“What are you doing here?” I asked more sharply than I intended to. I was not mad at him. I was just unprepared for the expected encounter.


“Good morning, too, sunshine. You didn’t show up for breakfast today. So I came to check on you,” he drawled, leaning his shoulder against the doorframe casually.


I blinked as I completely forgot how to breathe. “I—uh… I—I decided to open early today. The festival orders left the shop a mess.”


Aidan’s eyes softened as they swept over me. He was probably taking in the stray petals in my hair and the faint circles under my eyes. “So you thought avoiding me was the best way to start the day?”


I straightened, pretending to fuss with the flowers even as my cheeks burned. I laughed humorlessly. “Avoiding you? Don’t flatter yourself.”


He chuckled knowingly. He pushed off the doorframe to come closer to where I’d been standing.


The shop smelled faintly of roses and morning dew, but the air shifted entirely once he came near. The air suddenly went thick, as it always did when he was around.


“Well, it’s a little late to regret your decision now, don’t you think?” he stated, as he stopped right in front of me with glinting eyes.


I blinked. “Decision? What decision?”


Aidan tilted his head slightly, his smile widening. “You know, the one where you offered me your drink in front of your friends. The one that made it very clear we’re more than friendly neighbors.”


I groaned softly and buried my face in my hands. “Please don’t remind me.”


He laughed. The sound was warm and rough at the same time. “You should’ve seen their faces, Josie. Especially the one with the big eyes, Amber, right? I thought she was going to faint.”


“She almost did,” I muttered through my palms. “And Sierra’s been texting me nonstop since last night. I’m officially the talk of the town.”


Aidan leaned forward, his voice dipping to a whisper. “I guess we’re the news scandal now, huh?”


I looked up at him with an expression of half-annoyance and half-smitten. “You’re enjoying this way too much.”


His grin softened. “Only because I finally get to see you in daylight without you pretending I’m just the guy next door.”


And just like that, every bit of embarrassment in me melted into something tender. I was just about to lean over the counter for just a quick kiss, nothing scandalous, when the door burst open so hard, and the bell nearly flew off its hook.


“Josie!” Sierra’s voice hit like a gunshot. Amber was right behind her, all wide-eyed as the two of them charged into the shop like a pair of detectives on a mission.


I froze halfway over the counter, my lips barely inches from Aidan’s lips.


He grinned. He was completely unbothered by the interruption while I nearly toppled into the vase of tulips.


Sierra blinked, as the smirk on her face belied her innocent expression. “Oh, dear. We’re interrupting something, aren’t we?”


“Nope!” I said way too quickly, straightening so fast my head spun. “You are not! I was just, uh… checking for dust. On the counter.”


Aidan folded his arms, a smirk tugging at his mouth like he was enjoying every second of my humiliation. “Good morning, ladies. Pleasure to see you again.”


Amber’s jaw dropped slightly. “So it’s true.”


“What’s true?” I demanded, pretending to rearrange a bouquet that was already perfect.


“Mr. Mystery Rancher is often seen buying flowers,” Amber informed us, her eyes sparkling like she’d been waiting all morning for this confirmation.


“Don’t be ashamed, Jo? Our prim Josie is having a spicy relationship with the newest man in town.” Sierra added evilly.


Aidan rested lazily against the counter beside me. He looked at home amid the flowers. “Believe me. I didn’t mean to shock anyone, but Josie didn’t seem too keen on hiding anymore. I was under strict instructions the day before the festival that we shouldn’t let people know about our relationship, including her friend.”


I shot him a daggered look in his direction. “You love stirring trouble, don’t you?”


He chuckled low in his throat. “Only when I get to see you blush this much, sweetheart.”


Amber gasped dramatically. “Oh my god! He’s perfect for you, Jo.”


Sierra crossed her arms, studying Aidan like she was trying to decide whether to like him or interrogate him. “So, Aidan Callahan. You’re the reason our Josie’s been walking around like she’s floating on air lately. You didn’t know how many times I caught her smiling to herself that I almost thought that there was something wrong with her.”


“Sierra!” I protested.


Aidan tilted his head, chuckling. “Really? I guess I’ll take the blame for that.”


My cheeks burned even hotter. I clapped my hands together, herding them toward the door. “Okay, interrogation over! The shop opens in ten minutes, and some of us actually have to work.”


Sierra's grin just grew wider. “Oh, we’re not going anywhere yet. We’ve got so many questions.”


Aidan’s laughter rumbled beside me. “I don’t mind answering them, but maybe Josie should be the one to tell you the details.”


I glared at him. “You’re incorrigible.”


“You like me that way, right? Come. If you have more questions about me, by all means, I am happy to answer them all. Shall we head to the diner as you interrogate me fully?” Aidan invited gamely as his mouth quirked into that dangerously charming grin that made my heart misbehave.


Amber gasped. “You’re actually volunteering yourself for questioning? Bold move, Mr. Callahan.”


“I’ve got nothing to hide,” Aidan replied smoothly, offering a hand toward the door as if escorting royalty. “Ladies first.”


Sierra was already halfway to the door, linking her arm with Amber’s. “Come on, Josie, let’s go to the diner before he changes his mind.”


I laughed while shaking my head. I found myself falling into step beside them. It felt absurdly normal with this man who’d once driven me crazy with irritation now walking casually at my side as though he’d always been part of my little Oakridge bubble.


As we reached the diner’s entrance, Sierra tugged on my wrist lightly. “Hey, wait a sec, Jo.”


Amber and Aidan walked ahead. They chatted easily as if they had known each other for years, leaving the two of us in front of the shop.


Sierra’s grin was pure mischief. “You know, I like him already.”


I blinked. “You do?”


“I like him for you,” she whispered, her eyes glinting. “He’s confident without being cocky. And the way he looks at you, yeah, that’s the good kind of trouble.”


I rolled my eyes, though my chest swelled with warmth. “You’re ridiculous.”


She nudged me lightly. “Uh-huh. Don’t tell him I said that. Let him work for my approval a little longer. You know, just to keep him humble.”


I laughed softly. “Your approval means the world to me, Sierra.”


“Damn right it should,” she said with a wink before looping her arm through mine again. “Now come on, before Amber sells your entire love story to the diner regulars.”


We walked toward the diner together. Aidan was already holding a booth by the window, leaning back with an easy confidence that somehow made every woman in the place glance his way.


But his eyes, those piercing blue eyes, only softened when they landed on me.


The diner was already half full when we slipped inside. The smell of coffee and pancakes mixing with the buzz of low chatter from the folks made the place alive.


Sierra muttered under her breath. “No wonder half the town’s been talking about him. He walks like a model on some high fashion catwalk.”


I glared at her lightly. “Behave.”


Amber and Sierra sat together while Aidan was sitting beside me, his thigh brushing mine under the table. He handed me a menu even though I knew exactly what I was ordering.


Sierra cleared her throat. She had this grin that spelled trouble. “Mr. Callahan, tell us, how does a man like you end up in Oakridge of all places?”


Aidan’s lips curved. “A man like me?”


“You know,” Amber chimed in, “the mysterious, city-type stranger who suddenly buys one of the largest ranches in town. Theories are circulating, you know.”


“Oh, I’ve heard them. What’s your favorite theory so far?” He took a slow sip of his coffee, blue eyes glinting with amusement.


“The one that you’re secretly a retired movie star hiding from fame,” Sierra said with a deadpan face.


“Or a cowboy millionaire in witness protection,” Amber added.


I groaned, hiding my face behind my menu. “Where the hell did you get those insane rumors?”


Aidan chuckled. “Disappointing, I know. But sad to say, I’m just a businessman who wanted peace.”


Amber raised a brow. “Oakridge isn’t exactly a hermit’s paradise.”


He flicked his gaze briefly to me before he replied to my friend. “It became paradise once I found the right reason to stay.”


“Oh, he’s smooth. I would give him that credit.” Sierra’s brows shot up so high I thought they might fly off her face.  She whispered, pretending to fan herself.


I kicked her under the table, but the woman merely grinned at me.


Aidan only looked more amused. “What? Am I too honest?”


The waitress arrived with plates before I could die from embarrassment. Pancakes. Bacon. More coffee.


I couldn’t miss the way the waitress's eyes stayed a bit long on him before she smiled dazzlingly, asking if he still wanted anything. He politely smiled at her before looking at me, asking if I wanted anything more from the menu. I shook my head, and the waitress finally left us.


Amber drizzled syrup over her plate. Her eyes were sharp with curiosity. “Okay, your turn to answer something less poetic. What’s your story before Oakridge? Family? Work?”


He sat back unhurriedly, as if he had all morning to indulge their curiosity. “I used to run operations for a family business in New York. My father and I had different ideas about what mattered. So, I left. I bought the ranch and never looked back.”


There was something guarded in his tone, something that made my chest tighten.


Amber nodded slowly. “So Oakridge is your fresh start.”


He smiled faintly. “You could say that.” Then, turning to me, he added, “Best decision I’ve made yet.”


Sierra groaned dramatically. “Okay, if you keep looking at her like that, the pancakes are going to spontaneously combust.”


The table burst into laughter. I sank lower in my seat, torn between wanting to vanish and wanting to freeze this moment forever. This impossible ease between the people I loved and the man who’d quietly become my whole world.


When the laughter died down, Aidan reached for my hand under the table, hidden from view. His thumb brushed gently over my palm.


Despite my mortification, I couldn’t help but smile.


When breakfast was finally over, Sierra and Amber had declared Aidan officially passed the interrogation, though Sierra added that she’d be conducting further tests in the future.


Aidan only smiled and told her to bring her toughest questions next time.


We all stepped outside the diner, and the midmorning sun was just starting to warm the air. The others lingered by the door, but I used the excuse of walking Aidan to his truck to steal a few quiet seconds.


We stopped by his pickup. The noise from Main Street drifted behind us, and it all felt strangely far away from us.


“Well, you survived breakfast with my friends,” I said, tucking a strand of hair behind my ear.


He gave a low chuckle. His eyes were bright with amusement. “Survived? I’d say I passed with flying colors. Though I think Sierra’s secretly planning to interrogate me about my intentions later.”


“She probably is. Thank you for being patient with them. I know they can be difficult sometimes.” I smiled up at him.


He stepped closer with his one hand resting on the open door of his truck while the other brushed lightly against my waist. “You have no idea how hard it is to sit there pretending I wasn’t thinking about kissing you the entire time, sweetheart.”


“Aidan—”


He lowered his head. His voice was low enough for only me to hear. “You looked beautiful today. And I liked watching you laugh with your friends. You looked happy.”


Something in my chest fluttered at the warmth in his tone. “I am. You made sure of that.”


He brushed his thumb along my jaw gently. “Good. Because I’m not planning on going anywhere.”


Before I could reply, he bent down and pressed a soft kiss to my forehead. A quiet promise, not meant to be seen by anyone else. “I’ll see you later at the ranch.”


I stood there, watching him climb into his truck as the sunlight glinted off the windshield as he pulled away.


For the first time in a long while, I realized how dangerous it was. How easy it had become to picture a life where moments like this weren’t stolen but real.

CHAPTER 31


Aidan’s POV

I just wanted a few extra minutes with her before she went back inside her parents’ house. Then maybe we can have one last kiss before we pretend to be neighbors again.

“See you later,” she murmured, rising on her toes.


I didn’t need much convincing. My head dipped, the world narrowing to just her face and her breath until a voice thundered from behind us.


“Well, look who we have here.”


We broke apart faster than guilty teenagers. Josie’s father stood there, his expression unreadable. He was looking at us with an expression that was halfway between curiosity and amusement.


“Mr. Walker,” I said, straightening immediately and forcing a polite smile. “Good afternoon, sir.”


He stepped out, wiping his hands with his handkerchief. “Good afternoon to you, too, young man. Are you dropping Josie off?”


“Yes, sir. She—uh, she offered to help me get a discount at the general store in town earlier.”


Josie coughed beside me. “Yes, discount.”


Mr. Walker’s eyes twinkled as if he wasn’t buying it for a second. “I see. Well, since you’re already here, why don’t you stay for dinner? We’re having a roast. Your name’s come up around town often enough, and it’s about time you joined us properly.”


Josie froze beside me. I could feel the panic radiating off her, which only made me want to laugh. I, on the other hand, was suddenly very aware that this might be the universe’s idea of a test. “I wouldn’t want to impose—”


“Nonsense, Morgan and Wade are still here, too, you know. There’s plenty of food to go around,” Mr. Walker cut in warmly.


I reached for Josie’s hand behind my back, giving it the smallest squeeze. A silent promise that I’d behave. “Dinner sounds great. Thank you, sir.”


Her father grinned. “There’s no need to be formal. Come on in.”


I followed him into the Walker mansion, and Josie shot me a look that could’ve set fire to the curtains.


Behave, she mouthed silently.


I only smiled in return. No promises.


Inside, the faint sound of laughter drifted from the dining room. I caught a glimpse of Wade and Morgan seated together, her polished smile hiding something tight in her jaw while Wade was already halfway through a drink. And just like that, I knew tonight wasn’t going to be as simple as a roast dinner.


The dinner at the Walker table was every bit as lively as I’d imagined. Good food, warm chatter, and that small-town intimacy that made a man like me feel about three inches too tall for the room.


Josie sat next to me, her nervous energy almost palpable. She kept fidgeting with her napkin, avoiding my gaze as if pretending I didn’t exist might somehow make me less noticeable.


It didn’t work.


“So, Aidan,” Mrs. Walker began, her tone deceptively casual as she spooned mashed potatoes onto her plate. “You’ve been in town for a while now, haven’t you?”


“Yes, ma’am. A few months. I’ve been working on the Blackwood ranch since spring,” I replied politely.


“Oh, yes, I heard of it through town gossip at Sunday service. You’ve certainly made a name for yourself here in Oakridge. Everyone says you’ve whipped that place into shape again, just like when the Blackwoods still resided there.”


I smiled faintly. “I’m trying my best, ma’am. It’s such a nice place. I know it has the potential to be thriving again with livestock.”


Across the table, Wade gave a small grin over the rim of his glass. “Mr. Callahan’s being modest. Word is going around that he’s done more than just try. The Blackwood ranch is turning a profit again. That’s impressive work, I might say, for a man born and raised in the city.”


Josie caught the exchange and shot me a warning look that practically screamed: Don’t start.


Mrs. Walker, who was blissfully unaware of the undercurrents between their new neighbor and her new son-in-law, smiled approvingly. “That’s wonderful to hear. I didn’t realize that you are close to Josie. My daughter here isn’t saying anything.”


Josie nearly choked on her wine. “Mom!”


“What?” her mother said, feigning innocence. “I’m just asking. When I sent you to deliver breakfast for him, you seemed reluctant to do so. I thought you didn’t like our new neighbor.”


Wade chuckled, and even Morgan looked up from her plate with mild interest. The air suddenly thickened.


Josie’s father leaned back in his chair with a look of amusement on his face. “That’s right. You two have seen each other closely. I’ve seen you around together quite a bit lately.”


Josie looked like she wanted the earth to open and swallow her whole. Maybe I should’ve let her off the hook. Maybe I should’ve smiled and deflected like a gentleman. But then I caught Wade’s glance again. That was when I decided, no. I wasn’t hiding this anymore.


I set my fork down and looked Mr. Walker straight in the eye and said clearly. “Actually, I’m dating your daughter, sir.”


The entire table went silent. Josie froze mid-motion, her fork halfway to her mouth. Morgan blinked. Mrs. Walker’s eyebrows shot up so fast I thought they might never come down. And Wade, he sat back in his chair, studying me with an expression that didn’t quite reach his eyes.


“You’re… what?” Mrs. Walker asked after a long beat, her voice full of disbelief and just a touch of curiosity.


Josie groaned softly beside me. “Aidan!”


“Dating her,” I repeated calmly. “It’s recent, but it’s real.”


A small smile tugged at the corner of Mr. Walker’s mouth. “Well, I’ll be damned. Josie’s been keeping secrets, surely. If I had known, I would have invited you sooner for dinner, young man.”


Wade’s jaw tightened almost imperceptibly. “Well, that’s unexpected. Josie isn’t the type of woman who will date someone she barely knows.”


I met his gaze head-on meaningfully. “You could say that, but then again, it’s inevitable when you’re neighbors, don’t you think? We kept on bumping into each other at the border.”


Josie’s hand found my knee under the table and pinched me hard.


I ignored the pain and looked at her with a smirk. Her cheeks were flushed, and her eyes were wide. All I could think was how beautiful she looked, even when she was mad as hell.


Mrs. Walker finally broke the tension with a laugh. “Well, I suppose congratulations are in order. I just hope you treat our girl right, Aidan.”


“Yes, ma’am. You don’t have to worry about that. She’s the most precious thing in my life.”


Wade’s glass clinked softly against his plate. “You’d better, or you’ll have to answer to me.”


I smirked. “I wouldn’t dream of it.”


Josie exhaled like she’d been holding her breath for an hour before muttering under her breath. “I told you to behave.”


“I did,” I murmured devilishly. “You should’ve seen what I wanted to say.”


Her glare promised I’d pay for this later. When her hand stayed on my knee instead of pulling away, I figured it was a price I was more than happy to pay.

I politely told the Walkers that I was thankful for the lovely dinner, but I should get going before I lost my temper with Wade Blackwood. “It is when you ambush me like that!” she hissed. “I nearly choked on my food.”

“I’d rather you didn’t turn dinner into a…” She stopped, gesturing helplessly, “…a declaration of war.”


“War? Sweetheart, all I did was tell them the truth. Where’s the lie in there?” I echoed while grinning.

I caught the flicker of hesitation in her eyes when she said his name.


I queried quietly. “Wade, what?”


She darted her gaze away. “He already looks at you like you’re some competition or something, which is crazy, I know.”


“Because I probably am,” I said simply.

She lifted her eyes to mine. “You shouldn’t linger. It’s getting late, Mr. Callahan.”

The moon sat low like a pale coin resting on the horizon. Neither of us said much. Every brush of her fingers against mine was enough. Every glance felt like something unsaid building between us.


When we stopped near the old oak fence line, Josie turned to face me. Her breath came out in a nervous puff that misted in the cool night air.


“This is reckless,” she whispered.


“Everything about us is,” I murmured back. “Are you scared?”


She started to protest, but I leaned in, and the words died on her lips. The next breath between us became a kiss.


The kiss deepened without a thought, heat curling beneath the calm night as our hands found their way along our racing hearts.


The rest of the world fell away, just the soft rush of wind in the trees, the rasp of fabric, and the sound of her name against my mouth.


The moment turned fierce and inevitable. When she looked at me with dark eyes and trembling mouth, I knew I had lost the battle.


Under that vast sky, everything else disappeared. I made love to her here in the wild with nothing but the starry night sky as our witness.

CHAPTER 32
