By the time the sun finally dragged itself over the hills, I was already halfway to the Blackwood Ranch.


I’d saddled Daisy so fast I don’t even remember buckling the last strap. I just had the overwhelming urge to get out of the Walker house before Mom asked me for the hundredth time about Aidan.


Before Morgan flashed that knowing newlywed smirk again, or before Wade tried to accidentally corner me to get the full story on how I ended up dating the man who bought the Blackwood Ranch.


Nope. 


Absolutely not.


I’d had enough emotional chaos in one evening.


Daisy’s steady canter was the only thing that settled the knot in my chest. The chilly morning air bit at my cheeks, but it was better than choking on awkward breakfast small talk.


As we galloped, the Blackwood ranch property came into view. I slowed Daisy because I spotted a familiar figure leaning against the fence with his arms crossed.


He lifted his gaze as soon as I approached. There was a smile spreading across his handsome face. It was not his usual half-smirk and not the teasing grin he wore when he knew I was flustered.


“Good morning,” he called, pushing off the fence as Daisy slowed to a stop.


I tried not to stare, but the man looked unfairly good for someone who’d had barely any sleep. His hair was mussed, the collar of his shirt undone, and he looked like someone who had very thoroughly ruined me the night before.


I swallowed, fighting back the flush that wanted to spread to my cheeks as I recalled our adventurous night before. “You’re up early.”


He reached for Daisy’s reins with one hand and offered the other to help me down. “I could say the same for you, sweetheart.”


He curled his arm around my waist as he steadied me to the ground. A gentle but firm touch, warm enough to make my pulse jump. As soon as my boots hit the dirt, he didn’t let go right away. He brushed his hand on my hip like he remembered every single thing we did last night.


And I remembered too.


Oh, I did.


Every single moment of it.


Before I could say something stupid, my attention snagged on something behind him.


A shiny tractor sat in the driveway, gleaming in the sun like it had rolled out of a dealership lot five minutes ago.


My eyes widened with disbelief. “Aidan! Please tell me you didn’t buy a new tractor.”


He snorted, shaking his head as he finally released me slowly, like he didn’t want to. “As if. Look closer, sweetheart. Don’t you recognize her? The one we’ve been fighting with for weeks.” Then he crossed his arms, pride tugging at his mouth. “Finally got it running this morning with some help, of course.”


I blinked at him. “No way! You’re kidding!”


“I wouldn’t lie about machinery, Josie.”


The reality hit me all at once. How much he’d been trying to handle this. How hard he worked. How stubborn that tractor had been. And how he still woke up early, even after everything.


My heart kicked against my ribs. Before I could think better of it, I threw my arms around him and let out a delightful squeal. “You’re the best! Do you know that?”


He caught me instantly, one arm around my back, and the other hand sliding to the back of my head as he let out a surprised laugh.


“Well, if I knew fixing the tractor would get me that kind of welcome, I’d have done it sooner,” he murmured against my hair, sounding thoroughly pleased with himself.


I didn’t let go. Because for the first time in a long time, it felt like the morning wasn’t something I needed to run from. It felt like something I wanted to run to.


Aidan didn’t let go right away. His arms were solid around me. He was grounding me in a way that made the morning finally feel real. The world had narrowed to just the two of us and the faint buzz of the newly revived tractor behind him.


“What?” I asked as heat rose to my cheeks.


He brushed a loose strand of hair from my face, his fingertips dragging lightly across my cheek in a deliberate sweep that made my stomach twist.


“Nothing,” he murmured. “I just like seeing you happy. It makes me happy, too.”


“Well, then, keep fixing tractors. You know, I have a soft spot for tractors,” I tried to joke.


That earned a smile from him, one of his crooked smiles that always made my knees feel like wet noodles. He dipped his head closer. “I can’t promise I won’t start breaking things on purpose if it means you’ll look at me like that.”


My laugh died in my throat when I realized how close he’d suddenly become. He rested his forehead against mine. His breath warmed my lips. He slid his hands down to my waist, holding me not in a way to cage me, but in a way that said he didn’t want the moment to end.


“Aidan…” I whispered nervously.


He tilted his head just enough that his nose brushed mine. My fingers curled into the front of his shirt instinctively, tugging him closer.


Aidan froze when he realized that we were not all alone. I followed his gaze and nearly stumbled when I saw Wade on the top of one of my father’s prized thoroughbreds. Aidan caught me instantly, tugging me against his side in a protective motion as he faced him.


Wade was riding toward us, slowing as he reached the border fence. His expression said everything: confusion first, then something sharper when he saw how close Aidan had me tucked against him.


“Josie,” Wade called out, though the tightness in his voice didn’t match the casual word. “I didn’t realize I’d be interrupting something when I decided to ride on this path to where my family used to own this place.”


Aidan’s arm tightened around me, subtle but unmistakable. He settled his hand at my hip like he didn’t trust Wade’s arrival one bit.


He didn’t step away. He didn’t let go. He didn’t hide a damn thing. He didn’t welcome this intrusion.


Well, I couldn’t blame him. This was his property now. Wade had no business trespassing on his land.


I swallowed hard, my pulse thudding in my ears. Because Aidan wasn’t just standing beside me. He was staking his ground.


Wade’s gaze flicked between us, his jaw flexing before he managed a tight smile. “I guess you two are up early.”


Aidan didn’t even blink. “I guess we are.”


I felt Aidan’s silent message, unshakable beneath his calm tone. She’s with me.


Wade slowed his horse to a stop at the fence line, his eyes flicking toward the tractor behind us. Recognition sparked immediately.


“Well,” he drawled, “I’ll be damned. That’s the old tractor from the south lot, isn’t it, Jo?” His brows shot up. “I didn’t think anyone could bring that thing back from the grave.”


Aidan didn’t shift. He didn’t break his steady stance beside me. “Yeah. She runs like new again.”


Wade nodded. “Must’ve cost a fortune to bring her back to her former glory.”


Aidan shrugged. “Worth every cent. And that’s all because of Josie.”


I nudged him with my elbow, but he didn’t look away from Wade.


Wade’s mouth curved into a smirk I knew too well. The one he used when he wanted to get under someone’s skin. “You know, Josie and I used to ride that tractor when we were teenagers. Practically lived on that thing every harvest season.”


He wasn’t being nostalgic. He was trying to poke the bear.


Thankfully, Aidan didn’t take the bait, at least not in the way Wade expected. Instead, he let out a low sound of acknowledgment and tipped his head, almost thoughtfully. “Well, it’s got new memories now.”


Wade’s smirk faltered.


Aidan continued, his fingers brushing deliberately along my hip where his hand rested. His stance was a possessive gesture that he absolutely knew Wade would notice.


“Josie and I spent weeks working on it, most afternoons, actually.” He shot me a sideways glance with a private smile that was kind and warm. “We learned a lot together.”


My breath hitched.


Wade’s jaw tightened.


And for once, he didn’t have a comeback.


Wade cleared his throat. “Right. Well, Morgan’s waiting for me back at the main house.” He tugged lightly on his reins, visibly recalibrating. “I just wanted to check in on the place I once owned.”


“Consider it checked,” Aidan said easily. “Also, it’s not yours anymore. It belonged to someone else now.”


Wade gave me one last look. He conflicted before he turned his horse and rode off the way he came.


Silence settled behind after he was gone. Aidan let out a slow exhale and looked down at me. “That was something.”


I groaned, burying my face in my hands. “My entire life is something.”


He laughed under his breath and brushed his thumb along my hip. “He can say whatever he wants, Josie. But you’re with me now.”


Wade was barely a speck in the distance, and that was the only time I let out the breath I’d been holding. I looked up at Aidan. His jaw was still tight, which made something wicked spark inside me.


I bumped my shoulder into his playfully. “I think someone is jealous.”


Aidan cut me a warning look that only made me grin wider. “Josie.”


I folded my arms and raised a brow. “You practically glued me to your side. That’s quite possessive and territorial, Mr. Callahan.”


“I was not—”


“And the hand on the hip?” I asked. “Can you explain it?”


His nostrils flared. “It was just being protective.”


“Mmhmm,” I agreed, rocking back on my heels. “Protectively jealous.”


Aidan dipped his head until his mouth hovered right beside my ear.  “You keep teasing me, sweetheart. I’ll remind you exactly why I don’t like other men looking at you.”


I coughed. “O-okay… fine, you were a tiny bit jealous.”


He straightened, his expression smoothing into something thoughtful and almost troubled. “I’m serious, Josie. I think Wade is starting to realize he married the wrong sister.”


I blinked at him, then burst out laughing so hard I nearly doubled over. “Are you kidding me? Oh, come on. Aidan. Are you for real?”


“I’m not kidding,” he insisted, though the corner of his mouth twitched. “He kept staring at you like he wanted to say something.”


“Yeah,” I said, wiping a tear from my eye, “like ‘I hope Josie didn’t hear Morgan and me arguing yesterday,’ not ‘I married the wrong Walker.’”


Aidan didn’t laugh. Which only made it funnier.


“Aidan,” I said, still giggling. “You are out of your mind, baby. Wade is already married, to my sister. And whatever I felt for him is already firmly in the past.”


He didn’t appear to be convinced. “What about him? Do you think he feels the same way?”


“It doesn’t matter. I’m with you now.”


That finally got him. His arms slid around my waist, tugging me against him. Aidan’s voice dropped. “Good. Just making sure we’re clear on that matter.”


I pretended to gasp. “Oh my God! So you were jealous?”


“Josie…”


I laughed again. He kissed the top of my head while he couldn’t decide whether to scold me or kiss me senseless. Well, he probably wants to do it at the same time.


Aidan hadn’t even finished brooding over Wade’s departure when I clapped my hands together. “Come on. We didn’t even try the tractor yet.”


Five minutes later, we were climbing onto the freshly restored machine like two kids sneaking onto a carnival ride. Up close, the old tractor looked beautiful. Glossy paint, clean lines, and the engine purring beneath us like it had been waiting decades to run again.


I ran my fingers over the wheel. “It’s perfect.”


Aidan sat behind me on the seat smugly. “Only because you kept believing it could be.”


Heat curled in my stomach at the way he said it, like the tractor wasn’t the only thing I’d brought back to life. He reached around me to turn the ignition, his chest brushing my back as the engine rumbled to life. I let out a delighted squeal.


“Ready?” he asked, his lips brushing my ear.


“Let’s go!”


He shifted the gear, or at least he tried to. The tractor lurched forward, then stalled. It jerked sideways as Aidan swore under his breath.


I burst out laughing. “Oh my god! You have no idea what you’re doing, do you?”


“I helped rebuild the entire engine,” he said through his teeth. “Driving it is different.”


I wiggled back into his lap just to torture him. “Need help, Mr. Callahan?”


His hands tightened around my waist. “You’re not helping.”


I leaned back until my head rested on his shoulder, my laughter shaking us both. “I know that’s the fun part.”


He tried again. The tractor jerked, sputtered, and then rolled forward in a crooked line that made me dissolve into giggles. Aidan groaned. “I swear, if you weren’t sitting on me right now—”


“You’d still be terrible at this?”


He nipped my shoulder lightly. “I’d be able to focus.”


He didn’t tell me to move. He didn’t even pretend to. I settled deeper into his lap, my hands covering his on the wheel, guiding him gently as the tractor finally smoothed into a steady rumble.


We drove along the fence line, the wind flipping at my hair and the sun warm on our backs. Every few seconds, he’d kiss my neck or squeeze my waist like he couldn’t believe I was here.


“This,” I murmured, “is my new favorite way to travel.”


Aidan’s voice dropped to that low rumble. “Mine too. Though I’d like to try it again when I’m not so distracted.”


I giggled helplessly. “You mean when I’m not sitting on your lap?”


“I never said that.”


And God help me, I never wanted to move.

CHAPTER 33


Josie’s POV


I woke up the next morning to the single sweetest news I’d heard in weeks: Morgan and Wade were heading back to New York tomorrow.


I didn’t even try to hide my relief. I think I was actually in high spirits throughout breakfast, which earned me a raised eyebrow from my mom and a suspicious look from Morgan.


I didn't care. Tomorrow, the ranch would be quiet again. Tomorrow, I could breathe again. Tomorrow, I wouldn’t have Wade’s eyes tracking me every time I walked into a room.


When I finished the last bite of my toast, I carried my mug to the sink and grabbed my bag. I was already planning the flowers I needed to order for next week.


I was two steps from freedom.


“Josie.” Wade’s voice cut across the foyer.


I stopped. Of course. He wouldn’t leave without making things complicated. I turned slowly, finding him leaning against the doorway like he had every right in the world to stop me. Morgan had gone upstairs to take a call, and my parents were out back, which meant there was no buffer.


“What is it?” I asked distantly.


Wade stepped closer. He was not too close, but close enough to make my stomach tighten in warning. “I want to talk to you before I leave.”


I slung my bag over my shoulder, ready to bolt. “I’m actually running late for work—”


“It won’t take long.” Something in his voice made me pause. I hated that he sounded like he had something important he needed to say.


I crossed my arms and faced him. “Fine. Say it. Just make sure it’s important since I’m already running late.”


His jaw flexed before he spoke. He was trying to decide how honest he wanted to be, and that alone made me nervous. Wade’s eyes held mine for a long moment before he exhaled a frustrated sound. “I shouldn’t have come back to Oakridge, not like this. Not with things the way they are.”


My stomach dipped. I wasn’t sure if I wanted this conversation to happen at all. “Wade, if this is about you and Morgan—”


“It is,” he cut in. “But it’s also about you, too.”


Every muscle in my body locked. I didn’t want this conversation, after all. I didn’t want the direction I already knew he was steering toward. I didn’t want anything from him except distance.


But he kept going. “When I saw you the first day I got back, it hit me all at once. I didn’t see this before, and I don’t know how the hell I missed it, but—”


“Wade,” I warned.


“I married the wrong sister.”


My breath left my lungs. I couldn’t breathe. I couldn’t speak. I just stayed rooted in my place, staring at him with utter disbelief. How many times in the past have I wished I could hear those words from him? But now, when my dream finally happened, I felt nothing but a cold shock.


He took a step closer, his eyes softening in a way that used to make my heart twist when I was sixteen. Now it only made me furious.


“I know Morgan thinks I came home because I was having second thoughts about our marriage,” he said. “She was right. I needed space. I needed to clear my head. And then I saw you again. Dammit, Josie, everything clicked. I should’ve realized it years ago. I should’ve seen what was right in front of me.”


I stared at him. I was unable to formulate a single word. I was half scared that Morgan or someone might hear our conversation and take it the wrong way. “You’re… what? Are you confessing to me? Now? After you married my sister?”


He winced but didn’t look away. “I’m saying I made a mistake. And that I can’t stop thinking about you.”


“No. Absolutely not. You don’t get to say things like that. You just mistook kindness for affection, that’s all. It’s Morgan that you love, not me.” I shook my head in disbelief.


“I’m certain of my feelings for you, Jo. I know I figured it out too late. But—” he insisted.


My voice shook with anger. I didn’t bother hiding it. “But nothing. You’re already married. To my sister. And even if you weren’t—”


I stopped myself before I blurted out the words I had known all along.  I’m with Aidan now. I love him.


Wade’s eyes searched mine hopefully. “If the timing were different—”


I snapped. “It isn’t. And it won’t ever be.”


He flinched at that. Good.


“This conversation shouldn’t even be happening. Whatever’s going on in your marriage, fix it. With your wife. Don’t drag me into it,” I said, forcing the tremor out of my voice.


Wade swallowed hard, regret flickering across his face. “I’m sorry, Josie. I just needed you to know.”


I shook my head and turned away. “I didn’t need to,” I whispered, about to leave before I said things I would regret later on.


“I have one more question,” Wade stated, stopping me mid-stride.


I turned around to face him again. I was already exhausted before my day had barely begun. “What now?”


His eyes sharpened slightly. “Are you serious about him? About Aidan Callahan?”


For a heartbeat, I stared at him speechlessly. Then I lifted my chin defiantly. “Yes. I am.”


Something in Wade’s expression broke, just a tiny fracture. “Josie—”


“I’ve never been this happy. Not once in my life. Not until Aidan came along,” I said before he could twist anything. I said it before he could twist anything.


Wade’s jaw tensed. His hands curled at his sides. “I need to tell you something.”


I laughed humorlessly. “No, Wade. You’ve said enough. More than enough. I have to go before Morgan catches us talking and misinterprets this. I don’t want trouble.”


He didn’t stop. As far as he was concerned, this conversation was far from over. “I’m not saying this because of whatever you think happened between us,” he insisted. “I’m saying it because you need to be careful.”


My stomach tightened. “Careful of what?”


“With him, Aidan Callahan isn’t who you think he is,” he cautioned.


I stared at him, too stunned to catch up before anger surged hot through my veins. “Unbelievable! You’re trying to smear him now?”


“This isn’t about jealousy. It’s about facts,” Wade said, his eyes fixed on mine.


“You don’t have any facts,” I bit out. “You’re making things up because you can’t stand that I moved on, and—”


He interrupted, voice hardening. “I’m not making anything up. I’ve heard things. From people we both know.”


I scoffed. “Rumors? Gossip? Oh, you’re reaching, Wade.”


But there was something in his eyes, something grim and certain, that made my pulse skip.


“Wade,” I warned, “don’t do this. Please.”


He exhaled slowly, bracing himself. “There’s a reason why a man from the city suddenly bought the Blackwood Ranch. A reason he picked Oakridge instead of any other town in the state.”


“Because he wanted land. Because he fell in love with the property,” I answered flatly.


“No,” Wade said. “Because he wants revenge.”


Ice raced down my spine. Wade was being absurd with his lies. I forced a shaky laugh. “Revenge? On whom? Who could have crossed him from our little town?”


Wade swallowed, eyes pinning mine with a mix of regret and urgency. “I’m not completely sure yet. I don’t have all the proof I need, but—”


My voice trembled with anger, or maybe fear. I knew he was lying about Aidan. “Stop before you say something insane.”


But Wade wasn’t done yet. He stepped closer, lowering his voice. “They say his father died because of a man who ruined him. Drove him to the edge. And Aidan came here to settle that score. And from what I’ve gathered, the man he’s after is your father, Josie.”


Everything inside me went still. I felt the world shift beneath my feet for a fraction of a second, but it was enough to send my pulse slamming through my chest. I whispered, shaking my head. “No. You—you’re lying.”


“I wish I were. But if any of this is true, then Aidan Callahan didn’t come here to build a new life.” He paused for a moment, letting his accusation sink in. “He came here to destroy the man whom he believed was the reason for his father’s death.”


My breath caught. Aidan’s smile flashed through my mind. His arms are around me. His voice at dawn. His touch, his warmth, his whispered promises.


My Aidan.


Wade’s voice cut through the panic rising in my chest. His expression softened with a kind of pity that made me sick. “You’re in love with a man who might be playing you. Or worse yet, just using you. And if I were you, I’d start asking questions.”


My fingers trembled where they curled at my sides as I forced myself to meet his eyes, even as my heart thundered painfully. “You’re wrong. You don’t know him, not like I do.”


Wade shook his head. “I know enough to worry for you.”


I turned away, because if I didn’t, he’d see everything cracking inside me. I whispered angrily. “Don’t say another word.”


But the damage was already done. Because now there was a seed of doubt lodged deep inside my chest. I hated that it came from Wade. I hated that it shook me more than it should have.


I spent the entire morning at the flower shop pretending everything was normal. I arranged bouquet orders, wrapped stems in twine, smiled at customers, and restocked baby’s breath like my hands weren’t slightly shaking every time Wade’s words replayed in my mind.


Aidan didn’t come here to start over. He came here for revenge. Against your father.


It was ridiculous. Absolutely impossible. I repeated words in a loop inside my head until they became something to keep me upright and something to drown out the panic creeping up my spine.


Aidan wasn’t capable of that. He wasn’t that kind of man. He couldn’t be. Right? He didn’t approach me with some calculated plan. He hadn’t used me for his game of revenge. He hadn’t—


But the doubts kept scratching with what-ifs, like tiny claws against my ribs. Every time I tried to shove the thoughts away, Wade’s voice slithered back in: He’s lying to you, Josie. You’re being played.


I made a mistake at one point as I wrapped a bouquet of lilies with the wrong ribbon, the ugly neon one I reserved for last-minute birthday orders. I had to redo the entire thing. Twice.


By the time afternoon crawled in, my nerves felt scraped raw. I kept checking the clock because I wanted the day to end, and I needed to see him.


I wanted to look at his face and hear his voice just to prove to myself that Wade was wrong. That my heart hadn’t been stupid enough to fall into something twisted.


When the time finally came, I closed up shop early, blaming low foot traffic. I rode Daisy out toward the Blackwood Ranch, the familiar path doing little to soothe the knot in my stomach.


As the ranch came into view, so did Aidan. He was standing near the front porch, his sleeves rolled up, sweat beading at his temples as the sun caught the edge of his jaw like it always did when he looked unfairly handsome. He smiled when he spotted me instantly.


My heart tried to leap toward him, but the crack inside me made it stumble.


“Hey,” he called lightly as I dismounted. “I didn’t expect you this early.”


I wished I felt normal, too. I tied Daisy to the post with stiff fingers, forcing a smile. “Slow day at the shop.”


He walked toward me, his brow lowering with quiet focus. “Everything okay?”


There it was. He sensed it immediately. Of course he did. I swallowed, trying not to show the way my pulse jumped. “Yeah. I’m just tired.”


Aidan’s steps slowed right in front of me. He braced one hand on the fence beside my hip as he leaned just close enough to steal my breath the way he always managed to do without even trying. “No, you’re not okay. Something’s off.”


The gentleness in his voice nearly undid me. I looked up at him, and the worry in his eyes twisted something deep inside me.


Guilt. Fear. Doubt. All tangled into a painful knot. I tried to smile again, but I could feel it wobble.


He reached out and brushed his knuckles along my cheek. “Talk to me, Josie. What’s wrong, sweetheart?”


My throat tightened. Because for the first time since I met him, I didn’t know if I could. Not yet. Not until I figured out what I even believed. So, I managed a whisper that felt brittle and small. “I’m fine. Really.”

CHAPTER 34


Josie’s POV


Aidan’s hand stilled against my cheek. He was reading me the way only he could. He was not angry, but he was probably sensing a storm.  


I didn't know how to tell him that I wasn't sure if the storm was around us or between us.


“You’re lying to me,” Aidan stated, the quiet certainty in his voice making the hair on my arms stand up. He pulled his hand away, the loss of his warmth making me feel suddenly exposed. “Don’t lie to me, Josie. I know your ‘tired’ look, and this isn’t it. This is your something-is-wrong-and-I’m-trying-to-hide-it face, Jo.”


I took a shaky breath. I couldn’t stand here and lie to him. It felt like a betrayal of everything we had built. But speaking the truth felt like detonating a bomb.


“I had an unsettling conversation this morning,” I finally managed, the words catching in my dry throat.


Aidan crossed his arms over his chest, his expression hardening with impatience and a thread of something that looked like dawning dread. “Okay. With whom?”


I swallowed hard. “Wade.”


The immediate change in Aidan was shocking. His body language shifted instantly from concerned boyfriend to something alert and dangerous. His eyes narrowed, and the muscle in his jaw jumped. The casual comfort we shared minutes ago evaporated.


“What did Wade want?” Aidan spat out the name like it tasted foul.


“He—” I hesitated, trying to find the least damaging way to frame Wade’s confession. “He confessed something to me. Something ridiculous. He said he regretted marrying Morgan and that he should have realized his feelings for me years ago.”


A volatile flicker of contempt flashed in Aidan’s eyes. “That predictable idiot. Typical. He marries the one who shines, and then, when the novelty wears off, he wants the one who was always quietly waiting. Did you tell him to take a long walk off a short pier?”


“I told him to leave me alone and fix his marriage. I made it clear I’m with you, and I’m happy.” I confirmed quickly.


Aidan relaxed fractionally, taking a step closer. The possessive smile returned. “Good girl. I knew you would. So why the long face? His pathetic attempt to sabotage our relationship shouldn’t bother you.”


“It’s not that.” My voice was barely a whisper. I had to get this out. The truth was burning a hole in my heart. “He had another question. He asked if I was serious about you. And when I said yes, he… he said something else.”


Aidan’s composure snapped. He moved swiftly, closing the remaining distance between us, his hands grasping my arms in a non-negotiable grip. His eyes were usually so warm and inviting. Now they were suddenly dark and agitated.


“What did he say, Josie?” His voice was demanding. The warmth was gone, and it was replaced by an underlying nervousness that seemed to vibrate through his fingers and into my skin.


I couldn’t look at him. I stared down at the ground, forcing the terrible words out. “He told me you weren’t who I thought you were. He told me you didn’t come here for the ranch or a new life.”


I finally lifted my eyes, meeting his with a desperate plea for denial. “He said… he said you came here for revenge. And that the man you were after, the one who ruined your father, was my father.”


The silence that followed was heavy and absolute. The ranch seemed to hold its breath. I watched as every trace of color drained from Aidan’s face. He gripped my arms tightly as he slumped his shoulders almost imperceptibly, as if struck by a physical blow.


The charming mask he usually wore was shattered that moment. For a second, I saw a flash of exposed terror in his expression.


He darted his gaze away from mine, scanning the open fields and the distant woods, as though searching for an escape route or a threat. He looked like his world was suddenly closing in. The thought hit me with an awful certainty. Wade hadn't been lying.


“That’s… that’s absurd,” Aidan finally stammered, his voice rough and uneven. He was lying.


“Is it?” I countered, the small seed of doubt Wade planted blooming into a suffocating vine of dread. “A man from the city, buying a defunct ranch in a tiny town like Oakridge, falling for the quiet sister of the local golden girl who happens to be connected to the family you supposedly hate. Don’t you think that’s a coincidence, Aidan? Because it sounds a lot like a plan.”


He dropped my arms, stepping back as if I had burned him. He ran both hands roughly through his dark hair. He was pacing now, a movement that screamed panic.


“Wade is trying to manipulate you, Josie! He just confessed he wants you, and now he’s trying to poison your mind against the man you’re actually with,” Aidan insisted, his voice rising with indignation, but his eyes told a different story. They were agitated. “He got it wrong.”


“Did he?” I asked, my voice breaking. The tears were close, stinging my eyes. “Tell me, Aidan. Look at me and tell me he got it wrong.”


He stopped pacing, his chest heaving. He looked at me, and his eyes were scared and full of a profound regret that had nothing to do with Wade.


“He doesn’t know what he’s talking about, sweetheart,” Aidan pleaded, his voice softening, attempting the familiar comforting tone that no longer worked. He took a hesitant step toward me. “I came here to settle something, yes. An old family business with the Walkers. But it has nothing to do with your father driving my father to ruin. Nothing like that. Wade has been grasping at rumors and twisting them into a cheap, dramatic narrative to scare you away from me.”


He was denying the specifics, but he hadn't denied the core truth. He admitted he came here to ‘settle something’ with the Walkers. He admitted the plan. He just denied the details of the motive.


“And me?” I whispered, the pain finally cracking my voice. “Where do I fit into this settling of yours, Aidan? Was I just a convenient way to get close to them? To get information? Was I just part of the plan?”


Aidan’s breath hitched. He closed his eyes tightly, shaking his head. “No! Never! You were never part of any plan, Josie. You were… the complication. The best complication I’ve ever had. I deny that I used you. I deny that I don’t love you.”


He opened his eyes, and his gaze was searching mine for forgiveness, for belief. The charm was back, layered over with a terrifying mixture of sincerity and deception.


Aidan stepped forward, his hands reaching out to plead his case. “He got it wrong. Wade got the truth twisted. I love you, Josie. And I was going to tell you the real reason I was here. I promise. Soon. I was just waiting for the right time, when I could explain everything, and nothing could hurt you.”


I couldn't move toward him. I was frozen by the chilling realization that my golden light, the man who had made me feel such happiness, might have just been using me as a tool for his revenge.


“What is the real reason, then?” I challenged, my voice shaking with tears, demanding the full truth right now, before the storm completely overtook us.


Aidan took a breath that shuddered in his lungs, his eyes still wide and searching. He looked trapped, and I was the one holding the key. “Josie, please. I… I can’t tell you. Not yet, just know that it’s complicated. It’s a mess I inherited, and it has nothing to do with you. I need more time. Just a few weeks. I need to—”


I cut him off, the raw pain in my voice turning cold. “Need to what, Aidan? Need to tie up the loose ends of your revenge plot before you tell your naïve girlfriend that she was just the convenient neighbor? That everything we built was just a lie built on your secret?”


He took a desperate step, reaching for me again, but I instinctively backed away. “That is not what this is. I swear it. When I met you, the plan changed. You changed everything. This isn’t a game, and you are not some piece of collateral. You are the only good thing that has happened to me since I got here, and that’s why I have to protect you from this. Telling you now will just put you right in the middle of it.”


I stared at him, desperately searching for the man I thought I knew. The sweet, attentive Aidan, who helped me with my problems and taught me how to be confident. But all I saw now was a calculating stranger who was desperately holding onto a secret that was destroying us both.


“You’re asking me to trust you blindly,” I stated flatly. The irony was bitter enough to choke me. “You say you love me, but you won't give me the only thing that proves it: the truth.”


He ran his hand over his jaw, his expression a tortured mix of frustration and fear. “It’s not a trust issue, sweetheart. Once you know, you can’t unknow it. Just trust my judgment for a little longer.”


My heart was pounding against my ribs in a frantic rhythm and finally settled into a dull ache. I saw his hesitation, his absolute refusal to share the real reason, and I knew no amount of persuasion on my part would change his mind.


I inhaled slowly, letting the air cool the heat of my anger. He was not going to break. And if he wouldn't tell me, I had to stop asking. I had to regain control of myself.


“Fine,” I said, the word barely a whisper, yet it felt as final as a slamming door.


Aidan’s shoulders relaxed visibly, and a huge sigh of relief escaped him. He looked like a man who had narrowly survived a car crash. “Thank God. Thank you, Josie. I promise, I’ll—”


I cut him off with a cold gaze that stopped him mid-sentence.


Aidan finally looked closely at my face. The genuine delight was replaced by a fresh surge of dread. He recognized the shift. The complete lack of warmth and the icy expression that now masked my hurt.


I continued, lifting my chin slightly. “But until you are ready to share it with me, maybe it will be best if we don’t see each other.”


He stumbled back a step. “What? No… Josie, you can’t mean that, sweetheart.”


I announced simply. “I can’t be with a man I don’t trust. And I certainly can’t be with a man who might be using me to hurt my family. You need to decide who you are—the man who loves me or the man here for revenge. When you decide, and when you can look me in the eye and tell me the whole truth, then we can talk.”


“But… but Josie…” he pleaded miserably. He looked desperate. He was no longer trying to hide his need for me. “Don’t do this, sweetheart. Please. This is exactly what Blackwood wants. Don’t let him win.”


“Wade has nothing to do with this. This is about us, Aidan. It’s about trust. And you just failed the test.”


I turned quickly, untying Daisy’s leash with stiff fingers. I didn't let myself look back. I had to get away before the sight of his pain broke my newfound resolve.


“Josie, wait! Please… let’s talk. Come back!”


I swung myself onto Daisy’s back and dug my heels in gently. We broke into a sharp canter, leaving the Blackwood Ranch and the man who was both my salvation and my torment behind in the rising dust.


I rode away as tears finally started to fall, knowing I had just traded the man I loved for the tiny peace of mind I desperately needed.


I didn't stop riding until I reached the edge of our property. My legs were shaking when I finally slid off Daisy's back near the barn, and the sudden silence of the Walker Ranch was a harsh contrast to the desperate shouting I’d just left behind.


I quickly unsaddled Daisy. My movements were stiff. I focused only on the task of caring for her. I didn't want to think about the man I’d just abandoned or the terrible possibilities his secret raised. I focused on the familiar scent of hay and manure, the comfortable sounds of the animals, and anything to keep the truth from sinking in.


I was throwing the blanket over Daisy’s back when I heard my mother’s voice. “Josie? Is that you? I thought you were going to the shop.”


I didn't turn around. I couldn't face her or the inevitable questions about why I was home early, why my face was damp, or why my shoulders were shaking. I needed to be invisible again, the shadow she always overlooked.


“I’m not feeling well, Mom,” I managed to say with my voice sounding distant even to my own ears. “I’m going straight to my room.”


“But the flower shop—” she started, stepping closer. I heard the concern in her tone, the rare attention that usually came when something was wrong. But I just wanted her to leave me alone.


“I will take a day off,” I cut her off sharply before she could press me further. I gave Daisy a final pat, and then I was moving. I practically ran toward the house without looking back.


I bypassed the kitchen, where I could hear Morgan and Wade talking in serious voices. A conversation I was now certain was about their own broken marriage, but which I no longer had the emotional capacity to care about.


I reached the stairs and took them two at a time. I didn’t stop until the door to my bedroom slammed shut behind me. I turned the lock with a desperate click.


The room was exactly as I had left it, a quiet sanctuary. I stumbled to the bed and collapsed face-first onto the quilt.


The carefully constructed wall of composure I had held up for Aidan, and then for my mother, finally crumbled. I didn't just cry. I shattered.


I buried my face in the pillow and let the grief consume me. It wasn't just the betrayal that hurt. It was the realization that I had finally allowed myself to be happy, and it had all been built on a time bomb of lies.


The secret Wade had warned me about was real, and it meant the man I loved was nothing more than a cruel person sent to destroy my world.


I cried for the dream of us. I cried for the joy I had felt at Blackwood Ranch. I cried for the agonizing fear that Aidan was right, that he truly did love me, but that his secret was too big, too dark, to ever let us be.


I cried until my throat was raw, my eyes were swollen, and the pillow was damp with all the beautiful, painful hope I had just lost.

CHAPTER 35


Josie’s POV


The quiet that settled over the Walker Ranch after Morgan and Wade finally left felt less like peace and more like an echoing emptiness. They had packed up their designer luggage and their relationship drama and flown back to New York, leaving behind a silence that was supposed to be a relief.


Now I didn't have to dodge Wade's regretful looks, and I didn't have to listen to Morgan’s carefully curated updates on her blissful plan on how to start their married life together.


But the relief was brittle. It did nothing to soothe the persistent, aching pain in my chest. The real tension hadn't left with my sister and her husband. It was still firmly lodged here, right over my heart, in the shape of Aidan Callahan.


Days had bled into each other. I spent days submerged in the refreshing scent of my flower shop. It was my only safe space. I arranged peonies, clipped roses, and filled vases with an almost manic energy. I was desperate to exhaust myself enough to stop thinking about him.


The one thing I was ruthlessly successful at was avoiding him.


I hadn’t answered a single one of his calls. I deleted his texts, the ones that just said, “Please, Josie. Talk to me.”


I even changed my riding route. Daisy and I stuck to the far east fields of our property now. It was miles away from the familiar fence line where I used to meet him at dawn.


I missed him. God, how I missed him. It was a physical ache that sat in my stomach every morning. I missed his voice, the way his hand felt on my cheek, and the effortless way he made me feel seen and cherished.


Every beautiful memory we shared was now poisoned by the awful truth: everything, or at least the foundation of everything, had been built on a lie.


My logical side was furious and resolute. I was upset, I was hurt, and I was determined not to be played the fool. He’d confessed he came to Oakridge to ‘settle something’ with the Walkers. That was the only admission I needed to know the whole thing was a calculated risk on his part.


The stupid and romantic part of me couldn't let go. It whispered traitorously every night, clinging to the faint hope that maybe Wade had been wrong about the specifics. Maybe Aidan’s initial intention had been dark, but the way he looked at me, the way he spoke to me, and the way he fixed the old tractor just because I loved the ranch.


Maybe that was real.


Maybe that wasn't part of his plan.


A small voice prayed that Aidan had approached me with honest intention and that the feeling between us was genuine, not some twisted mechanism of revenge. I just needed to hear him deny the worst of it convincingly.


But until he was ready to drop the protective wall around his secrets, I was staying right here behind the locked door of my heart. The Blackwood Ranch might as well have been a world away.


The fourth night after my confrontation with Aidan, I was finally starting to believe I might survive this. I’d established a routine: work until exhaustion, come home, and hide. I was lying in bed, staring at the ceiling and trying to convince myself that I was better off alone than caught in a lie.


A light knock came on my bedroom door. “Josie? Honey, are you awake?”


I groaned softly. “Yeah, Mom. What is it?”


She opened the door a crack, her voice lowered to a conspiratorial whisper. “It’s Aidan. He’s outside.”


My heart, which had been resting in its dull ache, gave a painful wobble. Aidan.


After four days of silence, he was finally here.


“I’m not talking to him,” I whispered back immediately, pulling the quilt higher. “Tell him I’m asleep.”


My mother hesitated, stepping fully into the room. “He said it’s important, honey. He said it’s about your last conversation. He looks… distraught, Josie. He’s standing out on the lawn.”


A cold thread of fear wrapped around my chest. About our last conversation. That meant the secret. The truth.


I felt the relief and terror warring inside me. He was ready to talk. Was I ready to listen? The thought of knowing the worst made my stomach flip. But the thought of sending him away, of denying myself the chance to hear his side, felt like torture.


With a heavy sigh, I pushed myself up from the bed. I threw on the thickest cardigan I could find, not caring that it hid my shapeless pajamas. “Tell him I’m coming.”


I descended the stairs slowly, my palms sweating, and the anxiety tightening my throat.


I found him standing near the edge of the porch light, a dark silhouette against the deepening night. When I stepped outside and the porch light illuminated him, my heart lurched painfully.


It had been four days. Four agonizing days since I’d last seen him. And the change was undeniable and stark. His usually impeccable dark hair was messy, as if he’d run his hands through it a hundred times. His face looked thin, pulled taut across his cheekbones, and there were prominent, bruised-looking dark circles under his eyes, betraying every hour of sleep he’d lost.


He looked like a man who hadn’t eaten, slept, or known peace since I’d walked away.


The sight of his visible torment chipped away at my anger, replacing it with overwhelming pity and residual love. He looked utterly broken.


“Josie,” he breathed, stepping forward immediately. His voice was strained and filled with relief just at seeing me. He stopped abruptly when I stayed planted on the porch, putting a distance of six feet between us.


“What do you want, Aidan?” I asked, keeping my voice low and devoid of warmth. It was the only armor I had left.


He clasped his hands together in front of him, his eyes desperately searching mine. He swallowed hard, his gaze flickering down for a moment before snapping back up. He looked like he was bracing himself to jump off a cliff.


“I… I can’t do this anymore.” He met my eyes. His expression was utterly defeated. “I’m ready to tell you the truth.”


I stood motionless, the night air suddenly feeling too cold. All the blood seemed to drain from my face, leaving me feeling weightless. This was it, the moment the floor would fall out from under me.


“Say it, then,” I managed, my voice a dry rasp. “Don’t leave anything out.”


Aidan nodded slowly, his gaze locked on mine, as if forcing himself to witness my pain.


He began with the painful confession. “It’s true, Josie. Wade was right about one thing. I did come to Oakridge for revenge. That was the only reason I bought the Blackwood Ranch. It was all a means to an end.”


The words were a physical blow, yet they only confirmed the terrifying whisper I’d been fighting. I didn't flinch. I just stared and waited for the rest of the execution.


“The man I planned to take everything from was your father. Your family’s fortune, the one that’s built on this ranch, was—is—stained. It’s what ruined mine.”


My breath caught. “What are you talking about?”


Aidan’s eyes were filled with a profound sorrow. “Years ago, your father, Frank Walker, scammed my father with a fraudulent investment. My father risked everything, our entire family fortune, all the equity we had. Frank Walker took it all.”


He paused, running a trembling hand over his face. “When the investment proved fraudulent, my father couldn't accept the shame. The loss. He took his own life, Josie. He left a note, saying he couldn’t face the consequences of ruin. After he was gone, my mother… she fell into a deep depression. She never recovered. Soon, the light faded from her eyes, too. She died six months later.”


I was beyond shocked. I was paralyzed. My father, the hardworking rancher who spent his life working on building our family fortune? A scammer? A murderer?


The sheer magnitude of the accusation and the horrific depth of Aidan’s pain stole my voice. I could only stare at him with utter disbelief.


Aidan didn’t wait for my reaction, perhaps knowing it would be a denial. He pressed on, the momentum of his truth carrying him forward. “I spent every waking hour after they were gone struggling to save what remained of my father’s business. I swore an oath on their graves that I would avenge them. I swore I would make the man who destroyed them feel the same kind of devastation.”


His eyes hardened with a cold resolve. “That’s why I came here. I meticulously planned everything. Buying the neighboring ranch was the perfect way to execute my plan. I planned to get close to your family, find the weak links in your father’s business, and use every asset I had to strip the man bare of everything he possessed. I was going to leave him with nothing but the dust beneath his boots.”


He stopped, his confession hanging in the space between us. He looked at me, and the avenging look in his eyes softened. It was replaced by a devastating regret. “The one thing I didn’t factor into the plan, Josie. The one thing I never counted on was falling for his youngest daughter.”


Aidan looked at me. “You have to believe me. Everything I just told you about my past, about the revenge, is true. But what I feel for you... That’s true and honest, too. It was the only thing I didn't plan.”


He took a desperate step forward. “The moment I realized I was falling for you, I couldn't stop thinking about you. I abandoned my plan. The revenge stopped mattering.”


I scoffed. “If that was the truth, Aidan. If you had truly abandoned your plan and if what we had was real, you would have confessed this to me the moment you realized it. Not now after Wade forced your hand.”


He hung his head, an admission of guilt in the slump of his shoulders. “You're right. I should have. I wanted to. Every day, I wanted to tell you everything. But I was terrified, Josie. I was terrified of this exact moment. I was afraid of losing you. I thought if I just waited, if I finished the final arrangements to liquidate the ranch and quietly walked away from the whole mess, you'd never have to know. I thought I could protect you from this ugly truth.”


“So, you had no plans of telling me this, did you? You were just going to let me live happily ever after with a ghost of a lie?"


Aidan's silence was the only answer I needed. He couldn't meet my gaze, and the agonizing truth hit me with full force.


A hysterical laugh bubbled up from my throat. I felt a stinging shame wash over me, making my whole body tremble. “God, I feel so stupid. I fell for such an elaborate plan of revenge. I thought I was finally worth something. I should have known better. No man would ever give me a second glance, let alone a man of your caliber, Aidan.”


He recoiled slightly. “Don’t say that, sweetheart. Don't you dare talk about yourself like that. You are the most beautiful and honest woman I have ever met. I love you, Josie. I truly love you.”


“How?” I choked out, tears finally streaming down my face. “How could you be with the daughter of the man who ruined your life? The man who took your parents from you?”


I took a shaky step back, pulling myself further away from him, needing space to breathe. “The resentment is still there, Aidan. It has to be. I know how this ends. Sooner or later, you'll look at me, and you won't see Josie. You'll see Frank Walker's daughter. And you'll start to resent me too.”


I lifted my gaze, fixing it on his devastated face. The painful truth of our situation crystallized into one unavoidable decision.


“It would be better if we ended things tonight,” I stated, the words slicing through the fragile silence. They were the hardest words I had ever spoken, yet they were utterly necessary.


Aidan recoiled as if I’d struck him. Anguish flashed in his eyes. “No… Josie, please… Don't do this. Don't throw away what we have because of a lie that started before you even knew me. I love you. I will prove it to you. I will leave Oakridge, I will sell the ranch, and I will do anything to show you I've abandoned this whole terrible revenge plot.”


He pleaded, his hands reaching out tentatively, but I held my ground. My heart was breaking and being torn into pieces. But my resolve was solid. This wasn't about the past anymore. It was about our impossible future.


“It doesn't matter, Aidan. The truth is, you're here because of my father. You swore to destroy him. That will always be between us. Every kiss, every word, every plan for the future, will always be tainted by the reason you first came to my fence. I didn't want the girl in the shadows, Aidan. And I certainly don't want to be the girl who is the convenient target of revenge. I want to be someone's first, honest choice.”


His eyes were shining with unshed tears. “You are my honest choice. I chose you over everything, over my parents' memory, over my purpose, over years of planning.”


“Then prove it by respecting this decision,” I said. I looked straight into the face of the man who had given me back my self-worth, only to tear it away so cruelly. “I don't want to see you again. I don't want you showing your face in front of me, in this town, ever again.”


I watched as the pain finally broke him. A deep sound escaped his throat, and the tears that had been threatening spilled over.


Aidan Callahan, the mysterious, powerful man who had sworn revenge. He was openly crying on my family's lawn.


My own heart was weeping in an agonizing mess inside my chest, but I hardened it. I focused on the dark circles under his eyes, the haunted look of a man capable of years-long deception. I stood tall, offering him no comfort and no glance back.


He stumbled backward, shaking his head. He was incapable of forming another plea. He just stood there broken.


I turned sharply and walked back inside the house, leaving him alone in the darkness. I didn't slow down, I didn't look back, and I didn't hesitate until I was back inside my locked room.


The sound of my own trembling breaths was the only thing I could hear. Outside our house, the silence was absolute.


He was finally gone.

CHAPTER 36


Josie’s POV


Days turned into a hollow week, then two. Time didn’t heal. It just moved, dragging me along with it.


I forced myself back into the rhythm of my life, a life I now recognized as painstakingly ordinary. I went to the flower shop, I managed inventory, and I created beautiful arrangements, though deep inside, I felt like dying. Every stem I cut felt like a severed tie, and the sweetness of the blossoms was a cruel contrast to the bitterness in my heart.


Sierra and Amber were predictably relentless. They knew something was terribly wrong. My laughter had vanished. It was replaced by strained smiles. I ate dinner with the energy of someone doing chores.


“Okay, Jo, what's going on?” Sierra demanded one afternoon, leaning across the counter at the shop. “You haven't been yourself since Morgan and Wade left. Did they say something to you?”


Amber, who was five months pregnant, gently added, “Honey, you look exhausted. Just tell us. Did Aidan do something?”


I shook my head, avoiding their eyes as I meticulously stripped thorns from a long-stemmed rose. “It's nothing. We broke up. It was mutual. It's fine.”


I was tight-lipped. There was no way I could explain the reality of our situation. The mysterious man who lived next door was actually here to destroy my family. The idea of Aidan’s revenge, of my father’s supposed crime, was too monumental and too ugly to share with anyone.


Ten days after that agonizing night on the porch, the town gossip broke. It started with a casual question from Mrs. Henderson at the market, a whisper exchanged between customers over a bouquet.


Soon, the rumor had solidified into a confirmed story that spread through Oakridge like wildfire. The new owner of the Blackwood Ranch, Aidan Callahan, had left town.


The pervasive whisper was delivered with a mix of excitement and disappointment. Rumor had it that he wasn't coming back.


The Blackwood Ranch was dark. The gates were locked. There was even a talk that he'd put the property back on the market and make it ready for a quick sale.


I was hit with a paralyzing wave of conflicting emotions. The news brought a surge of relief. He had done exactly what I asked.


He was gone.


The thought of him leaving Oakridge for good made my heart hurt even more than the breakup itself. He’d chosen to leave, honoring my request for distance, which confirmed both his respect for me and the impossibility of our future. He had walked away, and I was left alone with the pieces.


I tried to throw myself into work, volunteering extra hours at the community garden and even taking on a difficult order for a wedding bouquet that required painstaking precision. I was trying to put my life back on track, as if fixing the routine would fix the person living it.


It wasn’t working. The shop that had once been my haven now felt like a suffocating place. The silence at home was now deafening. I looked fine on the surface. My clothes were clean, my hair was pinned up, and I knew how to smile politely at customers, but I felt like my heart had been dying.


Every time I caught my reflection, I saw a stranger with dull eyes. The worst part was the sinister uncertainty about the depth of my own emotions.


I thought what I shared with Aidan was a profound connection. But the ease with which he seemed to have walked away made me question everything.


Had it really been that easy for him to abandon me? Had our connection truly been so shallow? Had he sworn his love was real?


Apparently, I was wrong about the depth of our bond, or perhaps I was wrong about my own worth.


The breaking point came late one afternoon in the backroom of the flower shop.


Sierra was processing a shipment of greens, and Amber was sitting on a stool sorting ribbons, her bump prominent beneath her apron. They said they were here to help me, but I think they were here to make sure that I wouldn’t do something stupid.


I was trying to arrange a dozen white roses, but my hands kept trembling when one of the rose stems snapped with a sickening crunch. It was a stupid accident, but the sound was the trigger.


I dropped the entire bundle of roses onto the wooden floor. I didn't try to pick them up. I just stared at the scattered blooms, and the dam finally burst.


I collapsed onto my knees amid the debris of petals and snapped stems. I covered my face with my hands, letting out a wrenching sob that shook my entire body. It was a sound of pure bottled-up grief of weeks finally escaping.


Sierra was at my side instantly, dropping the box she was holding. “Oh, my God, Josie! What is it? What happened?”


Amber struggled off her stool, her eyes wide with concern while rubbing my back gently. “Honey, talk to us. Tell us what’s wrong.”


I couldn't stop the tears, my anguish filling the small room. Once my tears started, the need to unburden myself was overwhelming.


“It's him. It's Aidan,” I choked out, unable to articulate the full horror right away. “It was all... It was all a lie, Sierra. H-He wasn't here for me. He was here for revenge against my father. H-He admitted it.”


Their reaction was a stunned silence.


I wailed as the shame and pain poured out in my chest. “He just used me. He used me to get close to the family, and I stupidly let him. I was so blind, so stupid.”


“Oh, Josie…”


I buried my face in Sierra's shoulder. “But the worst part... The worst part is he's gone. He left just like I asked him to, and it confirms everything. I was just a mistake. But I still... I still miss him. I still love him despite everything. I don't know how to stop.”


I held nothing back with my confession, from the shocking revelation about my father and the elaborate revenge plan to the impossible choice I'd made to push him away.


As the terrible secrets finally spilled out, my friends held me tighter while their shocked faces gradually hardened into expressions of protective loyalty.


***


By the third week since Aidan's departure, a new distraction finally managed to nudge my sorrow off center stage.


My two best friends and I threw ourselves into a project of blissful domesticity. Amber had recently moved into a new and slightly bigger apartment. The small spare room was designated as the nursery. The room was empty and waiting for life.


Sierra took charge. “Right, Josie, we're doing this early. Amber needs the morale boost, and frankly, so do you. We're planning a surprise baby shower and turning this beige box into a proper nursery.”


I latched onto the plan with desperate focus. It felt good to be useful. I applied my eye for color and arrangement to something that wasn't a funeral bouquet for my own heart.


We spent hours measuring, painting the walls a cheerful color, and assembling flat-pack furniture that tested both my patience and Sierra’s vocabulary.


We sneakily gathered the gifts for an early baby shower, inviting just a few close friends under the guise of an apartment warming party.


Amber was genuinely surprised when we led her blindfolded into the decorated room, revealing a crib nestled under a painted mobile and stacks of tiny clothes.


I watched Amber's peace settle over me. I told myself that I was slowly getting over Aidan. I was functioning. I was celebrating the future. This was proof I was healing, right?


But deep inside, I knew the truth. My participation was fueled by a fierce gratitude for the diversion and a determination to distract my thoughts, not by genuine emotional recovery.


Sierra and Amber were my anchors. They made sure I wasn't lonely. They forced me out of my room, dragging me to movie nights, and coming over to the shop just to sit with me during the quiet hours. They didn't pry anymore after my confession, just offered a protective presence.


“We love you, Jo,” Amber murmured, resting her head on my shoulder one evening as we watched a silly movie.


“We've got you,” Sierra confirmed.


“I know,” I said ruefully. I was grateful for them. Their kindness and unwavering loyalty were the only things keeping me from sinking entirely.


In their presence, I felt a little less cold, and the silence from the Blackwood Ranch felt a little less like an endless chasm.


I was broken, but I wasn't alone.


When I finally left Amber’s apartment late that night, the residual warmth of the baby shower and the comfort of my friends faded the moment I stepped into the silent night air.


I drove back to the Walker Ranch with my fragile composure beginning to crack again. The imposing house felt less like a home and more like a museum of secrets.


Since Aidan’s devastating confession about my father's dirty deeds, I had been avoiding my parents with an intensity.


I’d stopped joining them for meals. I told them I had extra work at the shop. I was essentially living a parallel life, slipping out before breakfast and returning late. I simply couldn’t look at my father without seeing the ghost of Aidan’s parents. A man driven to suicide and a woman consumed by grief.


This night, however, my routine was broken.


I stepped through the massive front door, ready to silently climb the grand staircase to my sanctuary, when the sound of voices stopped me cold. Both my parents were in the living room.


My father was seated in his favorite armchair. He looked serious while my mother was perched nervously on the edge of the sofa.


“Josie,” my father said, standing up immediately. His voice was firm and laced with an unfamiliar tension. “We need to talk.”


My stomach churned. I already knew what this was about. They had finally noticed my deliberate absence. “I’m really tired, Dad. I had a long day. Can it wait until the morning?”


He took a step toward me. “It can’t wait. You’ve been avoiding us for weeks. You come home late every night, and you won’t even sit down for dinner. What is wrong, Josie? This isn’t like you.”


The gentle reprimand was the final straw. All the hurt, the betrayal, and the furious exhaustion I'd been carrying exploded in a blinding rush of heat. I turned back to face them, letting my bag drop with a heavy thud onto the polished marble floor.


I snapped. My voice shook with raw emotion. I couldn’t recognize the harsh sound of it. “Fine. Let’s talk about it.”


I walked a few steps closer. I focused solely on my father, my eyes blazing with the anguish I’d been hiding. “I stopped sleeping well when I found out you’re not the man I thought you were, Dad,” I accused, letting the truth fly like shrapnel. “I stopped wanting to come home, and I stopped joining you for meals because I stopped respecting you after what you did to Aidan Callahan’s father.”


The effect of my words was immediate and devastating. My mother gasped, her hand flying to her mouth while her eyes were wide with terror and shock. My father, Frank Walker, who rarely showed fear or surprise, went completely still. The color drained from his face, leaving him pale.


“Aidan Callahan? What… what are you talking about, Josie?”


“Don’t pretend you don’t know,” I spat out. “I know he came here for revenge. I know you scammed his father, drove him to ruin, and drove him to take his own life. And then his mother died from grief. You didn’t just ruin a business, Dad. You destroyed a family. And you think I can just sit here and eat steak with you as if nothing happened?”


The accusation hung heavy in the opulent living room, shattering the peaceful facade of the Walker family forever.


“That is absolutely not true! You have to listen to me, Josie. I don’t know what kind of monstrous lies that boy fed you, but it’s a fabrication. I swear, I have never met Aidan Callahan’s father. I have no idea who he is.”  He thundered when he finally recovered from his shock.


My mother began to weep silently, but I ignored her. My focus was solely on my father's wide eyes.


“It is true,” I insisted, tears of frustration replacing the anger. “Aidan said it himself! He came here to avenge his parents! His father killed himself because you ruined him with a scam investment. His mother died of grief! He told me the whole story, Dad! Why would he lie about something like that?”


“He is lying because he has some distorted version of the truth, or he is simply trying to hurt you, Josie! There has to be some sort of misunderstanding. I have not had business dealings that match that description. I have had dealings, yes, but not with a man whose family was destroyed like that. I did not know the man. I did not ruin the man.”


He took a calming breath, his voice lowering but retaining its desperate urgency. “Think about this rationally, honey. Why would I lie to you now? Aidan Callahan tells you a story of revenge and destruction, and then he immediately leaves town. Doesn’t that sound convenient? He must have some reason to paint me as a villain.”


I pleaded. “He knew the details of the scam, the ruin. He admitted his motive for being here.”


“And I am telling you, I have not met him, and I have not met his father in the past. I have no idea where Aidan got that information, but I have not had any business dealings in the past with his father. He is either genuinely misinformed, or he manipulated those facts to fuel a vendetta against this family.”


The confrontation had shifted. I expected a confession and an apology. I was met with an absolute denial that left me utterly confused. Both men—the one I loved and the one who raised me—were swearing on the absolute truth, and their stories were mutually exclusive. The ground felt like it was shifting all over again.

CHAPTER 37


Josie’s POV


The room felt less like a sanctuary and more like a cage. It was now filled with two versions of the truth that couldn't both be right.


My father was still standing there, swearing with every ounce of his authority that he was innocent. He didn't just deny scamming Aidan's father. He denied knowing him. My mother was sobbing quietly beside him, confirming nothing but her own distress.


“Dad,” I whispered, the fight draining out of me. It was replaced by a terrifying uncertainty. “I watched Aidan cry when he told me. He was broken. He sold his ranch and left town because of what he believes. How can you say he's wrong?”


“He's wrong because I am telling you the truth. Your faith in that man is clouding your judgment. He gave you a revenge story, and he targeted me. He had a reason to lie or to manipulate a half-truth to suit his purpose. He used an emotional devastation story to gain access and sympathy. Think about it. Why would he leave everything behind if he hadn't accomplished what he came for? He left because his plan was foiled, either by his feelings for you or because his information was faulty.”


I stumbled away from them, clutching my head. The fear in my father’s voice had sounded genuine. The desperate grief in Aidan’s eyes had also sounded genuine. I didn't know what to believe anymore. If Dad was telling the truth, then Aidan had been deeply misinformed. If Aidan was telling the truth, then my father was a cold-blooded monster.

“Let me get this straight, Aidan said your dad ruined his dad with a scam. And your dad swears he's never even met the man?” Amber summarized thoughtfully while rubbing her hand over her baby bump.


“Exactly,” I replied grimly, running a tired hand through my hair. “Aidan's entire life was built on avenging that loss. He didn't just hate Dad. He hated the concept of my family, but Dad was genuinely shocked at his accusation.”


Sierra tapped a finger thoughtfully against a potted fern. “That's why Aidan left, Jo. Because he realized he couldn't go through with the revenge once he fell for you. He chose you over the plan.”


“What if Dad is innocent?” I argued, “Aidan's entire life purpose is based on a lie. He destroyed his chance at happiness with me over a vendetta against the wrong man.”


Sierra looked me straight in the eye. “Listen to me, Josie. You have two men telling you two versions of the truth: your father, who has a strong motive to deny a twenty-year-old crime, and Aidan, who has a strong motive to believe the painful story he was raised on. You are caught in the middle, and it is tearing you apart.”


“That’s right.”


She leaned forward, her voice dropping. “The truth isn't here, Jo. It’s not in the Walker Ranch. The only person who can solve this problem is Aidan.”


“He's gone, Sierra. I told him never to show his face again,” I told her regretfully.

“I need to see Mr. Callahan,” I managed to say evenly while trying to sound confident.

“No. No, I don't,” I admitted, my shoulders slumping slightly. I knew what she was probably thinking. This small-town girl wearing dust on her shoes wants to see the CEO?


“I’m afraid Mr. Callahan’s calendar is fully booked for the foreseeable future,” she replied smoothly as she was already looking past me.

She picked up her phone, pressing a button discreetly. “Mr. Callahan's office, please. This is the lobby desk. I have a walk-in, a Ms. Josie Walker, claiming the matter is highly personal and wanting to talk to Mr. Callahan.”

“Ms. Walker?” she asked, her voice calm and efficient. “I'm Mr. Callahan's personal aide, Lena. He's waiting for you in his office.”

“Thank you,” I managed to say, my voice small against the magnitude of the office floor.

“He's been expecting you,” Lena murmured. She gave a respectful knock before opening the door wide.

My entire focus was pulled toward the man seated behind a massive mahogany desk in the center of the room. Aidan Callahan.


He wasn't pleading as he had been the last time I saw him. He was simply sitting, his presence filling the immense space with an undeniable authority that was far more intimidating than his earlier desperation.


He was dressed in a charcoal-gray suit that was obviously tailored, and his dark hair was impeccably styled. He looked every bit the billionaire CEO, utterly detached from the worn jeans and dirt of the Blackwood Ranch.


I couldn't quite meet his gaze. My eyes tracked the expensive art on the walls, the leather of the chairs, anything but his face. Yet, I could feel the intense weight of his eyes on mine, following every subtle movement I made.

His tone was devoid of the anguish and panic I'd last witnessed in the Walker Ranch. The man who was speaking to me was the CEO and not the broken man I’d left crying on the lawn.

I watched him closely for any change in his demeanor. “He denied all of it. He insisted he never met your father. He swore that the allegations you threw at him were not true, and he has no idea where you got that information.”

“My information is not wrong. I have proof. I have documents my father kept. And I know your father, Josie. I learned everything about him. He is a predator, and I was going to be the justice he never faced."


We sparred back and forth, the accusations flying fast. I defended my father's present innocence fiercely while Aidan defended his past pain and his absolute conviction of my father's guilt. The air crackled with anger. The conversation was going nowhere, just a painful loop of denial and affirmation.


Finally, I had had enough. My strength was spent. My heart felt bruised and exhausted. I hadn't flown three thousand miles just to fight the same pointless battle.


“I can't do this anymore,” I whispered with trembling defeat in my voice. “I don't care. I don't know who is lying anymore. I am done fighting with you, Aidan. I'm done.”


I turned abruptly, heading straight for the massive door. I intend on finding the elevator and disappearing back into the anonymity of the city. I reached for the door handle, but it didn't turn. I tried again and yanked it hard. The door was locked.


My blood ran cold. I spun around, glaring across the vast office space at Aidan, who was slowly rising from behind his desk.


He didn't move quickly, but the way he eased himself up was unnerving. There was a chilling smirk curved on his lips, a look I’d never seen before that was purely calculating. “You were highly mistaken, Josie Walker. If you thought you could just walk all the way across the country into my office, drop your allegations, and then walk away when I'm not yet done talking to you.”


Aidan slowly approached me like a hungry predator. The immense office suddenly felt impossibly small, every path of escape cut off by the man advancing on me.


I backed up until my spine hit the wood of the locked door. The rapid beat of my heart was so loud, I was certain he could hear it over the silence of his office.


He didn't stop until he was right in front of me, forcing a proximity that stole the air from my lungs. We were standing toe-to-toe as the heat radiating off his body overwhelmed the conditioned cool of the room.


He smelled faintly of expensive cologne and something dangerously familiar that my body instinctively recognized and responded to.


“What are you doing?” I choked out, pushing against the door with my shoulder as I was desperate to create space between us. “Step back, Aidan, and open this door.”

He leaned in closer, his voice dropping to a seductive murmur that was far more potent than his anger. “Don't you dare think you can come here, ignite this again, and walk away on your own terms. You think I'm going to let you push me again?”


Instinctively, I placed my hand against his chest, intending to push him away, to break the suffocating proximity. It proved to be a fatal mistake because once my palm landed flat against the hard muscle beneath his tailored suit jacket, my brain went short-circuited.


Aidan’s eyes flashed with triumph. He didn't move, just covered my hand with his own large one. He pinned it against his heart. “I let go of you once,” he stated. “I chose to respect your pain, and it nearly broke me. It was naïve of you to think I would make the same decision twice.”


Before I could react, he was already lowering his head. His lips came crashing down on mine.
