CHAPTER 38


Aidan’s POV


The four days leading up to the confrontation had been an agonizing torture. Every morning, I woke up watching the sun rise over the Blackwood, knowing my time here was running out.


Morgan and Wade were finally gone. The official threat to my revenge plot had been removed, yet the true complication remained.


I was supposed to be relieved. The whole point of buying this ranch and playing the mysterious city man was to isolate Frank Walker. I want to find his weakness and to finally deliver the justice my father was denied.


But all of my meticulous planning had dissolved the moment Josie Walker threw her arms around me after I fixed that damn tractor.


She wasn't part of the plan. She was the surprise that made me realize what I was about to do was wrong, not because Frank Walker didn't deserve it. But because destroying her family would ruin her.


I’d made the decision that morning. I would sell the ranch discreetly and find a way to make a future with Josie while keeping the truth buried forever. I knew it was a selfish lie, but losing her felt like losing the last part of my soul.


I was outside when I saw her riding Daisy along the fence line. Relief flooded me, followed instantly by a gut punch of guilt. I greeted her with a warm tone while trying to mask the churning fear inside. I asked her what was wrong, because I could read her better than any document my father had ever left behind.


She kept stalling. She talked about Wade’s pathetic confession, and I dismissed it instantly.


That was the easy lie.


Then she said, “He said something else.”


The temperature dropped about ten degrees. I didn't need her to finish the sentence. I could see the implications in her eyes. My secret was no longer mine alone.


She choked out. “He said you came here for revenge, and that the man you were after was my father.”


I felt the air leave my lungs entirely, and the mask I had worn flawlessly for months cracked. I felt a surge of absolute panic.


Wade. That motherfucker. How the hell did he know about my plan? It was impossible. My action was airtight. But the specifics didn't matter. The only thing that mattered was the devastation on Josie's face.


I saw the love there, yes, but it was being choked by betrayal, and I knew I had failed. I had failed to protect her from my past.


My heart slammed against my ribs. I denied it, of course. The practiced denials came easily. He’s trying to manipulate you. He twisted the facts.


I clung to the flimsy hope that I could convince her I was just here for some business, just not the patricide-avenging kind.


Josie was sharp. She saw the tremor in my voice and the way my eyes darted away. The nervousness overwhelmed the CEO’s control.


When she demanded, “Tell me the real reason, then?”


I halted. I couldn't tell her because I didn't want her to know. After all, telling her the graphic truth about my father’s suicide and about my mother’s demise would irrevocably change the way she looked at her own father.


It would force a choice she couldn't come back from. I was still protecting her, even as I was being cornered.


When she saw my hesitation, her face changed entirely. The pain in her eyes didn't vanish. It morphed into a hard and unforgiving resolve.


“It would be best if we didn't see each other,” she said.


The words were like an axe splitting me in two. No. This wasn't how the plan was supposed to fail.


The plan was supposed to fade away, leaving me with her. I panicked, knowing I was losing the single most important thing that had happened to me in years. I offered to sell the ranch, to leave Oakridge, anything just to erase the connection to my former life.


But she was determined.


“I don't want to see you again,” she whispered. Her eyes were harder than any diamond. “If you truly loved me, you would respect my decision.”


And then she was gone.  Taking the color, the light, and the future right out of the Blackwood Ranch.


I stood there with broken remnants of our happiness. She had given me an impossible ultimatum: respect her decision and lose her forever.


I had fought for so long to get justice for my family. But in that moment, all I could do was cry over the justice I was denying myself.


The right to fight for the woman I loved.


I stayed on that lawn until the sun dipped below the horizon. I felt every ounce of my purpose drain away. The revenge was meaningless. The fortune was grim. I had my justice, and it felt like absolute ruin. I would give her what she wanted, knowing it was the most painful proof of my love.


I called my pilot that night and had the plane fueled. Oakridge and Josie were already in the rearview mirror.


My private jet felt like a silent prison. I didn't sleep on the flight back to New York. I just stared out the window at the endless black expanse below. The silence amplified the ringing failure in my ears.


I left the Blackwood Ranch exactly as Josie demanded. I honored her desperate need for distance, but the agonizing emptiness I left behind was a testament to the enormous mistake I had made.


I landed in New York before dawn and walked straight back into the skyscraper that was the headquarters of my company. The city was my home, the place where I wielded unmeasurable power, yet it felt cold and alien.


I resumed my duties instantly. I attended board meetings and dictated strategy with the cold logic I was known for. I worked like an autopilot, relying purely on instinct and learned behavior.


Still, my mind was back in Oakridge, replaying the look of devastation on Josie’s face when I failed to give her the truth.


I put in an eighteen-hour-a-day workload as a form of self-flagellation. I ate lunch at my desk, usually nothing more than black coffee and a protein bar. I left long after the cleaners had finished their rounds.


It didn't take long for my exhaustion and inattention to be noticed, even in the highly demanding world of corporate finance.


Lena, my executive secretary for five years, was the first to notice. Lena was a fortress of efficiency. She also had a radar for anything that threatened the company, and right now, that was me.


I was staring blankly at a complex merger proposal, my pen hovering over the signature line, when her voice cut through the silence of my office intercom.


“Mr. Callahan, forgive me for the intrusion. But are you certain about the terms of the Shenzhen acquisition?” she asked, her tone carrying a hint of steel.


“Yes, Lena. Finalize the paperwork,” I responded curtly, forcing myself to focus on the numbers.


“With respect, sir,” she persisted, “you usually demand a third-party audit on anything involving that kind of capital fluctuation. You just skimmed the summary. Are you sure you shouldn't—“


I slammed my pen down on the desk. “I'm sure, Lena. I said finalize it.”


The line went silent for a moment. Then, she replied. “Understood, sir. But you've signed off on three major deals this week without any of your usual due diligence. You haven't taken a meeting in person that wasn't legally required, and you haven't slept more than four hours a night. Everything okay?”


I closed my eyes briefly, rubbing the bridge of my nose. Her concern was unwelcome, and her observation was frighteningly accurate. I was running on fumes, making reckless decisions because I simply didn't care about the outcome. The only outcome that mattered was the one I had just abandoned in Oakridge.


“Everything is fine, Lena,” I lied easily. “Just a complicated transition at the Blackwood Ranch. I need you to focus on your work, not my sleep schedule.”


I dismissed her, but the seed of doubt was planted. My relentless drive had always been rooted in my mission of revenge. Now that the mission was abandoned, the prize I wanted was gone.


I realized I had nothing left to power the machine. I was a multi-billion-dollar entity, operating without a purpose. And it was all because of a fiercely loyal florist who had dared to look past my facade and demand the devastating truth.


The subsequent days blurred into a monotonous cycle of work and torment. I was meticulously destroying my own company through neglect, and the financial damage felt irrelevant compared to the gaping wound in my chest.


I was losing my sanity.


Every meeting felt like a performance I was too exhausted to sustain. I would catch myself staring out the seventy-eighth-floor window, imagining the muddy landscape of Oakridge.


I wanted to leave everything behind and go straight back to the Blackwood Ranch. I wanted to beg for Josie’s forgiveness. I wanted to prove that my love was stronger than my hate.


The three agonizing weeks of separation were slowly killing me. I had kept my word. I had vanished. But the silence I’d bought was a lie. It was a constant scream of regret.


I was sitting in my cold office, supposedly reviewing Q4 projections, but I was actually staring at my mahogany desk while debating whether I should finally lose my mind and call the pilot to fly me back to Oakridge immediately.


I needed to formulate a plan. I needed a strategic CEO plan to win her back. However, all I could do was stagger in the miserable reality that I had been given the one perfect thing, and I had destroyed it with my secrets.


Suddenly, the private line on my desk phone buzzed. It was Lena.


“Mr. Callahan,” she said with surprise, which she couldn’t hide in her voice. “There is a—a walk-in in the lobby. A certain Ms. Josie Walker. She insists on seeing you. She says it's personal.”


My whole body locked up. I suddenly couldn’t breathe. I was certain that I had misheard her. The combination of exhaustion and grief had finally induced a hallucination.


“Repeat that, Lena,” I ordered in a dangerously low tone.


“There’s a woman named Ms. Josie Walker, sir. She mentioned she wants to talk to you personally. She doesn't have an appointment—”


My heart slammed against my ribs with the force of a battering ram.


She was here.


“Cancel everything,” I barked into the phone. I slammed my hand down on the desk. “Cancel the afternoon board meeting. Cancel the dinner commitment. Cancel the entire rest of the week if you have to. I don't care.”


I surged to my feet, already heading for the door. “You send her up immediately, Lena. Use the private executive lift. Do not let her wait, and do not let her bolt. Get her in this office now!”


My assistant, who had seen me handle market crashes and hostile takeovers with icy calm, sounded utterly shocked. “Understood, sir. Right away.”


I didn't wait for her to hang up. I paced the length of the office, running a hand over my hair. My professional facade was completely abandoned. The only thing that mattered was seeing if it was indeed Josie and if the sight of her could finally bring me back to life.


I paced the length of the office as my adrenaline surged through me after weeks of numbness. My eyes were fixed on the door every second, an eternity.


Had she changed her mind?


Had she seen the lobby and run?


The door finally opened, and my secretary ushered in a familiar figure. I immediately sat on the chair and pretended that I was calm and unbothered by this unexpected visit.


Josie. She looked exactly as I remembered, and yet devastatingly different. She wore a wool coat and jeans, which were perfectly normal in Oakridge. It made her look small and fragile against the backdrop of my office.


My gaze immediately fixed on her face. She looked so thin. The vibrant color was gone from her cheeks, and her eyes looked exhausted. The unmistakable sign of shared suffering.


A surge of agonizing relief mixed with regret hit me. She had been hurting, too. The separation hadn't been easy for her either.


She wouldn't meet my eyes. She was scanning the room desperately while trying to maintain that wall of distance she had so fiercely constructed when she told me to leave.


I fought the urge to cross the room and simply pull her into my arms. I forced myself to remain rooted behind my desk, adopting the posture of the CEO she now clearly saw me as. My cold persona was my only defense against completely breaking down.


The conversation that followed was painful and swift. I commanded her to look at me, and she reluctantly obeyed.


The argument was the same: my accusation, her father's denial, and the insurmountable wall of conflicting truths between us. I pushed back, my voice edged with frustration.


“I can't do this anymore,” she whispered, trembling with defeat. “I don't know who is lying, and I am done fighting with you, Aidan. I'm done with your revenge, your secrets, and your company.”


She turned abruptly and headed straight for the door. I watched her hand reach for the cold metal handle, the sight of her trying to walk out of my life again bringing me back to that moment on the ranch lawn. I wouldn't allow it. Not this time.


My hand darted under the desk. With a barely perceptible movement, my finger found the small button I used to secure my office during sensitive calls. There was an internal click as the magnetic lock engaged.


She pulled the door handle, confusion turning to alarm as she realized she was trapped. She spun back, and her eyes were wide with a fearful glare. The vulnerability in her face was gone, and it was replaced by furious indignation.


I eased myself out of the chair. The controlled calm I’d been wearing evaporated and was replaced by a calculating intent.


“You were highly mistaken, Josie,” I told her in a dangerous growl. “If you thought you could walk all the way across the country into my office, drop your allegations, and then walk away when I'm not yet done talking to you.”


I began my slow approach. This wasn't about talking anymore. This was about reclaiming what was mine. Every step was deliberate, closing the immense distance between the powerful CEO and the furious florist trapped against the door. I was the predator, and she was my beautiful target.


I didn't stop until I was right in front of her. She instinctively tried to push me away, her small hand meeting the unforgiving muscle of my chest. I caught her wrist, holding her hand pinned against my heart.


My eyes were burning with the memory of the past three weeks. “I let go of you once. It was naïve of you to think I would make the same mistake twice.”


The realization hit her. I watched her eyes widen as her breath hitched. I gave her no more time to think or to pull away. I closed the final inch, capturing her lips, and my control snapped.


The kiss was not gentle. It was the taste of three weeks of agonizing separation that I had lost her forever. I deepened the kiss, crushing her mouth beneath mine. I pulled her against the hardness of my body to prove that she was finally back where she belonged.


The moment my lips crashed down on hers, the world narrowed to the fierce contact. It was everything I had starved for. The sweetness of her mouth and the perfect fit of her body against mine.


I kissed her with all the desperation and regret that had fueled three weeks of living hell. I demanded an answer that only her body could give.


She was rigid at first, but then I felt the inevitable. The subtle softening of her lips yielded for a fraction of a second with a small response that sent a scorching wave of heat through me.


The moment was fleeting. I felt the sharp prick of her fingers digging into my shoulders, and then she was putting genuine force behind the push.


She found the strength to push me away, and though my body screamed in protest, I let her go.


I stumbled back a step, breathing hard as the taste of her was still on my mouth. The sudden void where her body had been was a brutal shock.


Josie stood pinned against the door, her eyes wide, but now they were filled with a renewed anger. She quickly wiped the back of her hand across her mouth, a gesture that sliced through my victory like a knife.


“Don't you dare do that again.”


My immediate elation curdled into bitter disappointment. “You can't deny that, Josie. You felt it too. Everything we had is still there.”


“I didn't come here to rekindle our relationship,” she spat out with blazing eyes. “I came here because I am losing my mind over the idea that my father is a criminal and you are a calculating avenger. I came here for answers, not to be manhandled into a kiss.”


“And you have your answer,” I argued, running a hand roughly through my hair. “My answer is, I love you. My answer is I'm leaving my revenge, I'm leaving my past, and I'm leaving everything for you. I came here because I couldn't be near you, and yet I can't live without you. Look at me, Josie. Look at what three weeks of separation have done to us both.”


I gestured to my tired face, then to her own thin features. “We deserve a second chance, sweetheart. We deserve the chance to figure out what's true and what's not, together. I'll tell you everything. I'll show you the documents. We will go through every single piece of information my father left, and if we find out I'm wrong, I will apologize to Frank Walker. But we do it together. We fight this together.”


She shook her head, her expression resolute. “No, Aidan. That's not how this works. How can you say you love the daughter of the man you think is responsible for your father’s death?”


“I know what I feel, sweetheart. Please, don’t give up on us. Give us another chance. I told you that I can forget about this revenge plan. I just want to be with you. I love you, sweetheart. Please, give us a chance.”

CHAPTER 39


Josie’s POV


I stood pressed against the cold wood of the locked door, my lips still burning from Aidan’s desperate kiss. My mind was reeling, completely unable to process the swiftness of events.


One moment, I was demanding answers about my father, and the next, I was pinned in the CEO’s office while fighting off a reckless embrace.


I pushed away from the door, clutching my arms to my chest, trying to regulate my breathing. “Open the door. If you truly want to prove that you respect me, open the door and let me walk out without a fight.”


Aidan stood facing me, his blue eyes locked on mine like a tempest of anguish and fury. I expected him to fight, to argue the point, and to kiss me again. But then, he turned and walked back toward his desk.


He leaned over the mahogany surface, his hand disappearing beneath the edge. I heard a faint internal click. He had unlocked the door.


A cautious flicker of hope ignited in my chest. Had he really listened?


I didn't wait to find out. I wasn't going to risk another moment of charged silence. I turned and headed for the exit. My entire focus was fixed on leaving this luxurious cage. I wanted to forget this stupid idea of a confrontation altogether. My only plan now was to find a taxi and get on the next flight back to Oakridge, back to my safe misery.


I made it only two steps outside his office door when I felt an iron grip clamp around my wrist.


“Don't you dare,” Aidan's voice came from directly behind me. He didn't pull me back into the office. Instead, he simply dragged me toward the executive elevator.


I was speechless, stumbling to keep up with his long strides. The entire executive floor was dead silent, save for my shoes trying to keep pace with his expensive leather shoes.


I risked a glance at the few people we passed. Aidan’s impeccable secretary and a man in a headset were utterly speechless. Their powerful CEO was manhandling a woman down the hall.


We reached the elevator, and he stabbed the ground floor button with ruthless force. He pulled me inside before the doors could close.


“Aidan, what are you doing?” I finally managed, trying to shake his hold by twisting my wrist to get free.


He tightened his grip firmly. He held me pressed against the cool elevator wall, his body acting as a shield against the closing doors.


“You want the truth, Josie?” he asked grimly. “You want to know if I'm a liar or if your father is? Then we're going to find it. We're going to end this once and for all. Come with me.”


I wasn't just scared of the confrontation anymore. I was scared of him. Maybe Aidan had truly reached his limit. Maybe he wasn't dragging me off to find answers but to deliver the ultimate final blow to our relationship. For a chilling moment, I thought this was truly the end.


I was still reeling when the elevator doors opened onto the lobby floor. Aidan was ignoring the wide-eyed stares of the receptionist and security guards while he was dragging me straight toward the exit.


I assumed we were heading back to his luxury apartment to finish our shouting match. Instead, he pulled me out of the skyscraper and straight into a waiting matte-black car idling at the curb.


“Where are we going?” I demanded as he shoved me gently into the back seat.


“To get answers,” he stated simply. He slid in right after me and instructed the driver to bypass traffic.


My surprise intensified when the car didn’t merge into the heavy city traffic but instead sped directly toward the freeway. We drove through the skyscrapers of Manhattan, and I realized we weren’t heading to any neighborhood in the city. Soon, we were pulling off the main road and through a restricted gate.


I gasped. We were at the airport, but not the massive commercial terminals I knew. We were on a private tarmac.


A familiar silver jet was parked on the polished concrete. It was the same plane we used for Morgan's bachelorette trip. I thought Aidan had rented it for us.


Aidan didn't pause. He pulled me out of the car, his hand tight around my wrist. He marched me directly up into the cabin of the aircraft.


A flight attendant greeted him with a warm smile. Before I could form the question, the door was sealed, and the engines whined to life. We were already taxiing onto the runway and headed for an unknown location.


The sudden silence and the reality of the situation finally penetrated my shock.


I sank into one of the plush leather seats, pulling my hand out of his slackened grip now that we were safely imprisoned together. I needed to fill the silence, to break the tense intimacy of the confined space.


“Yours?” I asked Aidan, nodding toward the interior of the plane. The question was unnecessary, but I needed to hear him confirm the unimaginable.


He was looking out the window as we climbed higher, his grim expression unwavering. “Yes.”


I felt a dizzying surge of disbelief. I had known he was rich, but not this rich. I had thought that when this plane was used for Morgan’s bachelorette, I assumed Aidan had just rented it to impress Morgan and her entourage.


It honestly didn't occur to me that it was his to begin with. The immense scale of his wealth and the secrets he kept hit me again, cementing how far apart our worlds truly were.


“Where are we going, Aidan?” I asked, demanding a destination instead of a fight.


“Oakridge,” he answered, the name of our small town sounding foreign and heavy in the luxurious cabin.


I blinked. “Oakridge? Why? You already left. I told you not to come back.”


Aidan leaned forward, resting his forearms on his knees casually. “You came to my office and told me that the man I based my life's vengeance on swore he's innocent. You want the truth, Josie? There is only one way to get it, and it's not going to happen in a luxury jet 30,000 feet in the air.”


“Aidan!”


He met my gaze with a terrifying intensity. “We are going back to the source. I'm not going to keep fighting this ridiculous, circular argument with you. I'm going to talk to your father.”


My heart began to pound faster than the plane's engines. “What? No! Aidan, you can't. He’ll—he’ll be furious. He’ll call the police. You can’t just show up and confront him about a twenty-year-old crime!”


“I have to,” Aidan insisted. “I can’t live in this limbo, Josie, and neither can you. If Frank Walker is telling the truth, I need to know why my father gave me documents pointing straight at him. If he’s lying, you need to know what kind of man your father truly is.”


He sat back, his demeanor signaling the end of the discussion. “I am done running. I'm done hiding. This entire ordeal ends tonight. One way or another, we are going to face him.”


***


The flight back to Oakridge was the longest three hours of my life. I sat in a state of suspended terror, watching the grim resolve on Aidan’s face. He was determined and entirely prepared for a war I desperately wanted to avoid.


We landed at the small regional airport, and we were met by another car. It was called by Aidan during the flight. Within minutes, we were speeding toward the Walker Ranch. It was nearing midnight.


Aidan didn't knock. He simply used his immense height and power to shoulder the heavy oak front door open while pulling me in behind him.


The house was dark, save for a light spilling from the study room of my father. We found him sitting at his large mahogany desk.


He was likely reviewing accounts or trying to find solace in his routine. He looked up, startled by the sudden intrusion. His eyes widened in instant disbelief and fury when he saw Aidan.


“You!” My father roared, pushing his chair back. “Get out of my house right now, Callahan! I’ll call the sheriff—”


“No, Frank, you won’t. We are going to settle this. Right now,” Aidan retorted. It was overriding the noise inside my father’s office.


I stepped forward, placing a nervous and pleading hand on Aidan's arm. The two men I loved, the one who raised me and the other who saw me for who I really was, were at opposite poles of an awful conflict. I prayed with everything I had left that this could still be resolved without destruction.


Aidan ignored my touch, his eyes locked on my father. He moved to the center of the room and started talking. His tone was factual. He wasn’t pleading his case anymore. He was presenting the evidence.


“The year was 2008. The investment was called Pacific Rim Futures. You pushed it on my father, and you guaranteed it. You told him it was a sure thing that you were heavily invested yourself. It was a scam that cost him everything. It led to his ruin, Frank. And then his suicide.”


My father’s face hardened. “I told you, Callahan, this is a lie! I wasn’t involved in any futures scam fifteen years ago. I never met your father, and I never pushed that investment.”


“Then explain this.” Aidan reached into the inner pocket of his suit jacket and pulled out a thin, folded document. He walked quickly to the desk and slapped the paper down in front of my father.


It was an old, faded letterhead signed at the bottom. The document detailed the investment and the guarantees.


My father’s hands were trembling as he picked up the paper. He scanned the archaic typeface, and then he froze, his breathing quickening.


He recognized it, didn’t he?


My heart sank. So it was true. Aidan was right.


“I… I recognize this document,” my father whispered, his conviction crumbling instantly. “It’s been decades. But… but I didn’t push this on your father. I swear on Josie’s life, this wasn’t my idea!”


He looked up at Aidan frantically. “It was Wade’s father. David Walker. He was the one who told me about this investment. He brought the documents to me. He insisted it was the next big thing, and he used my name to try to get other investors involved.”


The room went silent. I felt like I had been bathed in cold water. The shock was so intense it was physical.


Aidan’s eyes went wide. They were no longer filled with vengeance but with profound and shattering disbelief. “David? Wade’s father?”


My father, who desperately tried to clear his name, nodded quickly. “Yes! David. He approached me. He wanted my name on the prospectus to make it look legitimate, but I pulled out before the final signatures, before it became official. I thought it seemed dubious. I thought the whole thing had fallen through. He must have taken those documents, those prospectuses with my name, and used them himself. He probably used my name to scam your father.”


The world tilted again. Not my father, but Wade’s father. The man whose son was the supposed love of my life. The golden boy. The one who had just warned me about Aidan. His father was the true culprit.


The years of revenge, the hatred, and the destruction of two families had all been meticulously pointed at the wrong man.


The silence was deafening. It was heavier than the midnight fog outside. The revelation that Wade's father was the actual architect of the scam slammed into the room like a physical shockwave.


I stared at my father, who was still holding the faded document, but his relief at being exonerated was battling the horror of the truth. He was innocent of the crime, but the Walker name was still tied to the devastation.


I looked at Aidan. The man who had spent his adult life driven by a single burning purpose was frozen in his place.


The grim resolve that had brought us across the country instantly evaporated. His jaw hung slightly slack while his eyes fixed on my father with a bewildered look. The fifteen years of hate, the meticulous planning, the sleepless nights, and the sacrifices he had made had all been aimed at the wrong man.


I watched the realization sink in savagely on his face. It wasn't just that he had been misinformed. It was that his entire identity, the righteous rage that had defined him, was a lie built on a name fraudulently attached to a document.


The man who stood to gain the most from Aidan's failure was Wade. The son of the true culprit had been the one to warn me, twisting the half-truth just enough to send Aidan packing and break us apart.


“Wade,” I whispered, the name tasting like bile. “He must have known. He knew the document existed. He knew you were close to finding the truth. He was trying to protect his father.”


Aidan didn't react to my words immediately. He looked down at his own trembling hands. Then he slowly looked up at the ceiling, as if seeking an answer from an indifferent sky. “All of it, from my father's pain... my mother's death... my years of work... it was all for nothing.”


He had sacrificed his happiness, his peace, and almost his love for me all for an error. The crushing weight of that error seemed to buckle his knees. The powerful CEO, the avenging angel. Suddenly, he looked like a lost boy.


I realized then that his desperation in his tears and his commitment to revenge had all been true. His devotion to his parents’ memory was real. His belief that he was fighting the Walkers was absolute. He hadn't lied to me about his pain. He had simply been fed the wrong enemy.


The anger I held against him vanished. It was replaced by an overwhelming surge of compassion and sorrow. His mission was his purpose, and now his purpose was gone. It was destroyed not by me but by a lie that had survived a decade.


The truth was devastating. But it also offered a startling clarity. Aidan hadn't lied to me about his feelings. He had truly fallen for the enemy's daughter, because the man he swore to destroy wasn't my father at all.

CHAPTER 40


Josie’s POV


The drive from the Walker Ranch to the Blackwood felt unreal. The tension was gone. It was replaced by a suffocating and disbelieving silence.


My father had been left in his study, reeling from the realization that his name had been used to commit a terrible crime, while my mother rushed to comfort him.


Aidan didn't speak. He drove silently while his face was a mask of weary devastation. The revenge he’d carried like a torch for years was extinguished. The crushing knowledge that he had been fighting a ghost and, in the process, he nearly ruined the only person who had ever truly mattered.


We arrived at the Blackwood Ranch well after midnight. It felt emptier than ever. The windows were dark, and the silence was absolute. We didn't go inside right away.


Instead, we sank onto the porch steps, sitting side-by-side but separated by the weight of everything that had happened. The air was cold, but I barely noticed.


I watched the weak moonlight illuminate the outline of the vast fields. I wanted to reach for him, to offer comfort, but I didn't dare.


After what felt like an hour of staring into the darkness, I broke the silence. I wrapped my arms around my knees. I didn't know how to start. I turned my head to look at him. “What is your next plan, Aidan?”


He remained perfectly still, his eyes focused on some unseen point on the horizon. He was the most beautiful man I’d ever known, and now he was basically lost. He didn't answer right away. I let the silence stretch.


I was patient now, knowing he needed time to formulate an answer from the wreckage of his life.


“I’m not sure,” he finally admitted. It was the first time I had ever heard uncertainty from him. The decisive CEO was gone.


I pressed on gently. I needed to know what this meant for us. “Are you planning to sell the ranch? The gossip in town… it was circulating after you left. They said you put the Blackwood back on the market.”


He finally looked at me, his blue eyes tired and distant. “Maybe.”


That single word was like a physical punch. It told me everything.


“I suppose,” he continued, his voice void of emotion, “nothing is left for me here.”


My heart, which had been tentatively stitched back together by the revelation that he hadn't deliberately sought to hurt me. It broke me upon hearing that. He might have proved that his feelings for me were genuine and that they were strong enough to abandon a false vendetta. But now that his purpose was gone. He was prepared to abandon me, too. The truth had saved my family, but it hadn't saved us.


He was right. The ranch was an asset, a tool for his vengeance. Now that it was useless, the billionaire CEO was ready to move on.


I fought to keep the devastation out of my voice. He was right. The ranch was tainted. It was a symbol of his pain. I had to be logical, even if it meant confirming my loneliness.


I nodded slowly. “It’s the only logical thing to do. Oakridge holds such bitter memories for you, Aidan. It’s the place where you discovered the extent of your family’s ruin and where your entire mission turned out to be a mistake. If I were in your position, I would probably do the same. Get rid of it and move on.”


I waited for the final crushing agreement. For him to seal the fate of the Blackwood Ranch and our brief history.


Aidan didn't answer immediately. He stared out into the dark fields for a long moment. The silence was broken only by the chirping of crickets. Then, he turned his head slowly, his blue eyes finally focusing entirely on me.


“You’re right,” he said huskily. “But this little town didn't always have bad memories for me. In fact, most of them were beautiful, Josie. Because of you.”


His admission that the memories he valued most from Oakridge were of me ignited a sudden, renewed hope in my chest. He wasn't just talking about a failed venture. He was admitting he valued our time together.


But the hope was a dangerous and fragile thing. I crushed it immediately. I couldn't allow myself to be drawn in by emotion when our whole situation was still so precarious.


“Aidan,” I said, my voice sharp with forced practicality. “It's not nice to joke like that at times like this. We just discovered your whole life was a lie based on my father's name. We shouldn't be nostalgic right now.”


His expression didn't waver. Instead, the cold CEO’s face softened. He turned profoundly tender as he looked at me. It was the Aidan from the porch swings and the fence line. The man who truly saw me for who I was.


“I'm not joking, Josie,” he insisted softly. “I know how much the Blackwood Ranch meant to you, even before I bought it. It held so many beautiful memories for you, too. And I remember how much you hated the idea of a new owner. The cold welcome you showed me when I first arrived in town.”


A nervous laugh escaped me. It felt foreign but good. “I was not cold to you! I was polite. I was just territorial because I thought you were going to turn the place into a resort.”


Aidan let out a low chuckle. The first sign of genuine humor I’d heard from him in weeks. “Polite, territorial, and absolutely stunning while sitting proudly on Daisy’s back, I remember.”


He grew serious again, but the tenderness in his face remained. He looked out at the dark silhouette of the ranch house. “If I sell this place now, it goes to some investment group. They will probably tear it down and put up condos. I can’t stand the thought of that. You shouldn’t have to see that happen, not after all this.”


“W-what are you saying?” I dared to hope.


He turned back to me. The look in his eyes was intense, with a flicker of the decisive CEO returning. “I might keep the ranch if you agree to certain conditions, of course.”


The words hit me like a sudden burst of energy. The quiet despair that had been settling over me was instantly chased away by a spark of hope and was mixed with a rush of intense anxiety.


My head was already racing with the possible conditions he wanted. What could a man who owns a private jet and commands a corporate empire possibly want from me?


Did he want me to manage the ranch? Did he want a cut of the flower shop's profits?


The terrifying thought that I would have to buy the place back surfaced immediately. I mentally tallied my life savings, my business equity, and the value of the Walker Ranch, all of it combined. It still wouldn't even cover the interest on the Blackwoods' purchase price.


“I don't have the money to buy it from you, Aidan,” I stated honestly. “I'm just a small-town flower shop owner. I also don't have the means to barter for a wealthy man like you.”


The reality of our drastically unequal financial standing was a harsh barrier. I felt suddenly inadequate.


Aidan let out a low sigh and shook his head. A mirthless smile touched his lips. “It's not about money, Josie. And it's not about business.”


He reached out and gently brushed a strand of hair back from my cheek, his touch sending a nervous shiver down my spine. The contact was intimate, a reminder of whatever he wanted. It was going to be personal.


Despite the fear of what he might demand, the curiosity and the intense desire to keep the Blackwood Ranch safe won out. I had to know.


I lifted my chin, forcing myself to look him directly in the eye. I was ready for whatever impossible request he was about to make. “Then tell me, what are those conditions?”


Aidan didn't look away. His blue eyes, which had held the cold fury of vengeance and the deep sorrow of betrayal, were now filled with a focused and unmistakable resolve. The kind of look a man gets when he's making the biggest and riskiest investment of his life. “The Blackwood Ranch will stay exactly as it is,” he stated firmly, “if you agree to one thing.  The condition is this, Josie: you have to marry me.”


I simply stared at him. The sound of crickets suddenly seemed too loud, and the rush of blood in my ears deafening. My mind raced through logistics. The ranch and finances had completely stalled.


Marry him?


“I... I think I have misheard you,” I stammered, pulling back slightly. “What did you just say?”


He didn't smirk. He didn't laugh. He didn't even backtrack. His expression remained utterly serious and devastatingly sincere. “I said, I will not sell the Blackwood Ranch, but the price of this land, the price of my stability, and the price of keeping this place safe is that you become my wife.”


I felt I could faint. This wasn't a business proposal. It was a demand wrapped in a bizarre and desperate package.


Aidan reached into his suit pocket. The same pocket that had held the document that upended his life, and pulled out a small velvet box. “I know this is a lousy proposal. I've just hauled you across the country, locked you in my office, and demanded you marry me on a cold porch step after we just found out my entire identity was based on a lie.”


He looked genuinely regretful about the circumstance but not about the request itself. “But if you say yes, I promise to make it better. I'll make the real proposal something you deserve. A thousand times better than this. But I need to know now, Josie. I need this to stay. And I need you.”


I stared at the velvet box in his hand, then up at his earnest face. The raw honesty in his eyes, coupled with the impossible audacity of his proposal, was overwhelming. The tears that I thought I had exhausted weeks ago suddenly welled up, blurring his image.


My heart wanted to scream ‘yes’ right away. It was the only word my soul recognized. This man had seen my worst and my best. The one who had just faced down the destruction of his life's mission was choosing me and choosing to stay.


But the logical part of my brain, the part that had been sharpened by three weeks of separation, kicked in. This was not a romance novel. This was real life. A life built on massive complications.


I sniffled, wiping the tears from my cheeks with the back of my hand. “Aidan, I... I can't just say yes. Not like this. There are other things to consider.”


“I know,” he replied, his voice instantly softening in a reassuring way. “Whatever it is, we'll solve it.”


I took a shaky breath, trying to articulate the massive chasm that separated our two lives. “Where would we live? I don't think I would like the city at all, Aidan. I hate crowds, and I hate being trapped. I need the space here. I need the ranch.”


Then came the biggest and most terrifying hurdle. His entire identity and career.


“And your work?” I continued, gesturing vaguely toward the east where his massive empire sat. “You can't just throw away your life in the city for a quaint life in this small town with me. You're a CEO. You run an enormous company. You have responsibilities that go far beyond what my flower shop brings in.”


I looked at the ground, then back at him. “Before I can even begin to give you an answer, I need these things to be absolutely clear. This can't be another temporary solution, Aidan. I can't do another temporary lie.”


I waited. I held my breath tight as I waited for him to falter. For him to admit the logic was impossible. For him to take the box back and say, 'I'm sorry, you're right.'


Instead, Aidan shifted closer. The velvet box rested carefully on his knee. He didn't argue or defend his empire. He looked at me with a steady tenderness that made my own eyes burn with fresh tears.


“Josie, look at me,” he urged softly, pulling my attention entirely away from the fear of the future. “The most important thing I realized over these last agonizing weeks, and especially in the last few hours, is that the only real thing I have is you. I realized that everything else except you is replaceable.”


“Aidan…”


He shook his head and focused on my first concern. “My work requires a connection to the world, yes. But I’m the CEO, sweetheart. I don't punch a clock. I can move back and forth. I've been doing it, quietly, for a while now. From juggling the demands of my company with the necessity of being near you. I've learned to manage the distance because you are worth the inconvenience.”


He addressed the heart of my fear. “I know you hate the city. I saw your face in that lobby, and I would never be cruel enough to impose that life on you.”


He moved closer still until his shoulder was brushing mine. “We will live right here, Josie. We will live at the Blackwood Ranch. We will transform this place into our home, not a place of vengeance but a place of new beginnings. I'll commute when I have to, but my home will be with you right here in Oakridge.”


His voice grew thick. The tears finally welled in his own beautiful blue eyes while reflecting the moonlight. “I spent my life chasing a lie that led me to this town, and that lie brought me to the only truth I've ever known. I found you, Josie. I love you, not because you're a convenient neighbor but because you are the kindest and most honest person I have ever met. You taught me how to feel again.”


He finally opened the velvet box. Inside, resting on a cushion of satin, was an exquisite diamond. “I need to stay here. I don't want to sell this ranch because it's the only place I can look out and see a reminder of when I started to choose love over hate. Marry me, Josie. Let me make this ranch our life. I want a home with you.”


The dam broke completely. I didn't care about the logistics of his commute. I could always accompany him for a few days when he would be in the city. I only cared that he was choosing us.


I could feel tears streaming down my face, but they were no longer tears of sorrow. “Oh, Aidan,” I choked out, reaching up to cup his cheek. His skin was rough from stress but felt wonderfully warm beneath my palm. “Yes. Yes, I will marry you, my love.”


A shaky breath escaped him, a monumental sound of relief, as if a weight heavier than his entire corporation had been lifted from his chest.


He didn't speak another word. He simply closed the final distance between us, leaning in and capturing my mouth in a kiss. This kiss was sweet with profound tenderness and heartfelt promise.


It was soft enough to soothe the wounds of our separation but deep enough to seal the commitment we had just made.


When he finally pulled back, he was smiling through his own unshed tears. His eyes were radiant with a light I hadn't seen since that first day he realized he was falling for me.


He gently took my left hand, his thumb stroking my trembling fingers. He took the ring carefully and slipped it onto my finger. It fit perfectly, a dazzling symbol of an impossible love.


“Forever,” he vowed huskily. He brought my hand to his lips and pressed a kiss to the diamond. “No more secrets, Josie. No more running. I promise you.”


I leaned my forehead against his, my entire world settling into place. “I love you, Aidan,” I whispered, the words coming from the deepest part of my soul. “I love you.”


“And I love you, my brave, beautiful girl,” he whispered back, pulling me into a fierce embrace. “I’m finally home.”


We sat there, wrapped in the protective bubble of our newfound future. We watched the first faint hint of dawn touch the sky over the Blackwood Ranch.


Our ranch.


Our home.


Our beautiful, messy, and permanent beginning.

EPILOGUE


Josie’s POV


The scent of fresh-cut cedar and wild jasmine hung heavy in the air. A perfume far sweeter than any I had ever sold in my shop. I stood in the center of the master bedroom while smoothing the delicate lace of my gown, still unable to believe how perfectly it fit.


It wasn't some designer dress from a high-end Manhattan boutique like the one Morgan had for her wedding. It was from the local shop back in town where Sierra works.


She had spent weeks hunting for the right piece, and when she finally pulled this one out, I knew it was the one. It was a simple but elegant wedding gown that was a perfect fit for a girl who preferred dirt paths to runways.


Sierra had outdone herself with this one, and seeing her tear up during the final fitting made it mean that much more.


Outside my window, the rolling hills of Oakridge were bathed in the golden light of a perfect June afternoon. The view had changed in the best possible way.


True to his word, Aidan hadn't just kept the ranch. He had brought it back to life. The most magnificent change stood just a few hundred yards from the house.


In place of the old, sagging structure that had once been a symbol of decay, Aidan had built a brand-new barn specifically for our wedding day.


It was massive with high vaulted ceilings and wide doors that opened to the valley. He told me it was a symbol that our marriage wasn’t being built on the ruins of the past, but on a fresh foundation we made ourselves.


As I stepped onto the porch, my heart swelled at the sight of the sign hanging over the main gate. He had officially renamed the property. It was no longer the Blackwood Ranch, a name that carried the weight of a cold vendetta.


Our ranch was now Daisy’s Ranch, named after my beloved horse who had been my only comfort through the hardest years.


The ranch was operating again with vibrant and loud sounds of a working life. I could hear the distant lowing of cattle and the steady rhythm of a place that had finally found its soul. We had livestock in the pastures, horses in the stalls, and a future that felt as wide as the horizon.


I began the walk toward the new barn, my heels clicking softly on the path. The sound of a fiddle started to play a joyful melody that echoed off the hills. I saw the familiar faces of Oakridge. My parents look younger and more at peace than they have in decades.


Aidan stood at the end of the aisle, framed by the massive cedar beams of the barn he’d built for us. He looked every bit the powerful man I’d met months ago, but the hardness in his eyes had been replaced by a steady warmth.


When his gaze locked onto mine, I felt that familiar rush. He wasn't just a CEO or a man who had escaped his past. He was my husband-to-be. The man who had chosen a quiet life in a small town because a world without me simply wasn't enough.


I reached his side, and he took my hands. “You look breathtaking, sweetheart.”


“And you look like you're finally home,” I replied, a tear of pure joy finally breaking free.


I stood at the barn he built, wearing the dress my best friend found for me. I knew the shadows were finally gone. The land was healed, the truth was out, and I looked at the man who had given up an empire to be my partner. I couldn't be happier.


The reception was everything a small-town wedding should be. The new barn was filled with the smell of slow-roasted brisket and the sound of laughter echoing against the rafters. Warm string lights draped from the cedar beams, casting a soft glow over our friends and neighbors as they danced on the fresh wood floor.


For the first time in my life, Oakridge felt like a place of celebration rather than a place of secrets.


Aidan kept me close, his hand resting firmly on the small of my back as we moved through the crowd. He looked more relaxed than I’d ever seen him. He already removed his tie, and the top button of his shirt came undone. He looked finally at peace.


As the music slowed to an acoustic melody. He leaned down, his lips brushing against my ear. “There’s something I should tell you. In case you were looking for her... Morgan didn’t attend today.”


I pulled back just an inch, searching his face. I’d noticed her absence, of course. My sister loved a spectacle. A wedding at Daisy’s Ranch, especially one involving a billionaire, was exactly the kind of event she usually lived for.


“I wondered,” I admitted softly. “I thought maybe she was just being... well, Morgan.”


Aidan shook his head, a grim smile touching his lips. “It’s more than that. The news about Wade’s father on how he framed your father hit her hard. She couldn't look at Wade the same way, knowing his family was the reason for all this pain. She filed for divorce last week.”


I gasped, my hand flying to my chest. “A divorce? Already? Their wedding was the event of the century.”


“It was,” Aidan agreed. “But Morgan has a lot of pride. Once the truth came out in the legal circles, she realized she’d married into the very family that tried to ruin the Walkers. She couldn’t handle the irony or the betrayal. She couldn't face the folks in town after such a public failure.”


I looked out at the happy crowd, feeling a pang of sadness for my sister. She was hiding away, unable to face the "I told you so's" of a small town that watched her every move.


“I hope she finds what she's looking for,” I murmured, leaning my head against Aidan's shoulder.


“She might,” Aidan said, tightening his hold on me. “But I think we're the lucky ones, sweetheart. We didn't need the extravagance. We just needed the truth.”


He turned me in his arms, spinning me slowly as the fiddle player started a new song. The drama of the Walkers and the Blackwoods and the lies of the past and the sting of Morgan’s departure all faded into the background.


Here, in the heart of the ranch that was finally ours. Under the roof of a barn built with love, I had everything I ever wanted.


I looked up into Aidan’s eyes, the man who had traded a corporate throne for a piece of Oakridge dirt, and I knew that whatever challenges came our way, we’d face them right here.


Together.


-The End-

CHAPTER 2


Josie’s POV


The long oak table was filled with platters of roast chicken, mashed potatoes, fresh bread, and other dishes that made it feel like it was already Thanksgiving, but I barely tasted a thing that was on my plate.


My fork moved unconsciously, and the food felt like dry sand in my mouth, while around me the air buzzed with laughter.


Wade’s unexpected arrival threw the whole house into chaos, and the housekeepers made last-minute arrangements for his place at the dinner table.


Dad was sitting at the head of the long table while Mom was on his right. Morgan was sitting on the left next to Wade, while I sat next to Mom, which made me sit across from Wade at the table.


Mom leaned forward, her eyes shining with happiness. “So when did this all begin? You never told us you were even seeing each other! We had no idea that our neighbor’s son would end up with our daughter!”


Morgan’s laugh filled the dinner table. “Oh, you know me, Mom, I like surprises. We met in New York again early last year. We ran into each other by chance, and… well, it just clicked. Let’s just say the feeling blossomed instantly as if it had been destiny all along.”


“You guys have only been dating for a year, and now you're getting married?” Mom exclaimed. “Don't you think that's a bit rushed?”


“What's so surprising about that?” Dad inserted. “They have known each other their whole lives. Hell, the Blackwoods used to be our neighbors and good friends.”


“Dad is right, Mom,” Morgan inserted, holding Wade’s hand that was resting on the table. “I’ve known Wade all my life.”


Dad chuckled, shaking his head as though he’d known it all along. “I’ll be darned. Wade Blackwood is marrying into the Walker family. Who would’ve thought that the son of our neighbor would be my future son-in-law?”


“Where will the wedding be?” Mom piped up next. “Here our town or something fancy in the city?”


Morgan’s eyes glittered while looking at Wade. “We haven’t decided yet, but I’m leaning toward the vineyard in Napa Valley. It’s elegant, picturesque, and perfect. It’s memorable for us since that’s where we had our first date, right, babe?”


Wade beamed at my sister affectionately, which shot me straight to the heart. “That’s right.”


Mom clasped her hands under her chin. “Oh, Morgan, it sounds like a dream. You’ll be such a beautiful bride. I can almost picture you in your wedding gown. Oh! I’m getting teary-eyed just thinking about it.”


“I will pay for the wedding!” Dad announced at the table proudly.


Morgan smiled gratefully at him. “Really?! Thanks, Dad. You’re the best!”


“Anything for my daughter and her special day.”


Everyone laughed and agreed. Their voices overlapped in excitement, with each question spilling into the next about dresses, their guests, and how Wade proposed.


I remained silent with their happiness about the upcoming wedding blurred in my ears. My hands stayed folded in my lap as my plate was barely touched. Each mention of the wedding was like another knife pressed into my chest.


Wade squeezed Morgan’s hand. He was smiling at her the way I had always dreamed he’d look at me one day. His gaze never wavered from her, as though she were the only person in the room worth seeing.


And maybe she was.


I lowered my eyes to my untouched plate, fighting the tears behind my eyelids to stay hidden. No one noticed my silence. No one ever did.


I was just the sister in the background, listening to the sound of my heart breaking while the world toasted to Morgan’s happiness. I kept my eyes on my plate, letting the talk wash over me like a storm I couldn’t escape. With Morgan’s happiness, Mom’s endless questions, and Dad’s pride for his eldest daughter all swirled together, filling every corner of the room.


Just when I thought that they had already forgotten that I was still at the dinner table with them. That no one noticed I hadn’t spoken a single word since offering my reluctant congratulations.


But then Wade’s voice broke through.


“Josie?”


My head snapped up before I could stop myself. His warm and familiar eyes were fixed on me across the table. He smiled the same smile he’d given me when we were kids as he handed me an apple for my horse after we went for a ride and let our horses rest.


“How have you been? It’s been a long time.”


The room stilled. Suddenly, all eyes were on me. Morgan gave me a sharp look for taking the spotlight from her even for a moment.


My cheeks flushed. “Oh… I’ve been fine. Just busy with stuff.”


Morgan’s face softened, and her voice slipped smoothly. “Busy with her flowers as usual,” she said with a light laugh, threading her words. “You must not know this, babe, but Josie runs that little flower shop on Main Street. I swear, she spends more time with roses and horses than with people.”


A chuckle rippled around the table, not cruelly, but a dismissive laugh, like my life’s work was nothing more than a quaint hobby.


My stomach knotted.


Wade’s smile. He nodded as though he meant it. “That sounds nice. Peaceful. Do you like your job?”


“I do.”


“That’s all that matters, right?”


I nodded and met his eyes for a fleeting second. My breath caught at the kindness. But Morgan’s hand tightened around his, pulling his attention back, and the moment slipped away.


I lowered my gaze again, my fork trembling in my hand. I didn’t know how long I sat at the dinner table torturing myself while fighting back my tears, but I made it through dinner. By the time the last plates were cleared and the coffee poured, my chest felt so tight I could hardly breathe. The living room glowed with warm lights, my parents perched on the couch, and I hung on to Morgan’s every word as she recounted the proposal again. This time it was with more detail about the ring and about how Wade had made her feel like the only woman in the world.


I couldn’t take another second. I had had enough of this torture.


“Excuse me,” I murmured, pushing back from the table before anyone could stop me. “I need some fresh air.”


No one asked why. No one noticed the way my voice cracked except Wade. He was looking at me worriedly as I made my exit.


The door creaked as I slipped outside, the night air rushing cool and sharp against my flushed cheeks. Crickets hummed in the pasture, and the faint smell of hay drifted on the breeze. I wrapped my arms around myself while trying to hold everything in place when it felt like I was coming apart at the seams.


I had known seeing Wade again would stir up old feelings, but nothing could have prepared me for this, for Morgan to come back with the news that the very man I’d loved quietly all my life was standing at her side now.


My sister has always had everything in life. Beauty, confidence, and the admiration of the town. I’d made peace with being the quiet one, the overlooked sister. But this? This felt downright cruel.


I sank onto the porch steps, pressing my palms against my knees to stop their trembling. The house behind me was filled with laughter. Inside, Morgan was probably flashing that perfect smile while Wade leaned close to her, and my parents were glowing with pride.


Out here, under the night sky, I let the mask slip. My throat burned as hot tears welled in my eyes. They spilled faster than I could swipe them away. I pressed a hand to my chest, as if I could cradle the broken pieces of my heart, as if I could make sense of what just happened.


I had never felt smaller.


And for the first time in years, I wondered if maybe I really was meant to be nothing more than the shadow to Morgan’s light.


The porch boards creaked behind me. I hurried to wipe at my face, praying the night hid the redness in my eyes as I suddenly had unwanted company.


“Josie?” His voice was warm and familiar. The very sound that once made my heart flutter crazily.


I stiffened as Wade stepped outside, the front door clicking shut behind him. He crossed the porch slowly, his boots scraping against the wood before lowering himself onto the step beside me. He was not too close, but close enough that I could feel the heat of him in the cool night air.


For a moment, neither of us spoke. The crickets filled the deafening silence around us.


“I’m sorry,” Wade said at last with a low voice. “I wanted to tell you myself. Before all of this… before tonight. You deserved to hear it from me, but Morgan wants—”


I swallowed hard, staring at my hands twisted in my lap. “Tell me what exactly?” I whispered, though we both knew the answer.


“That Morgan and I…” He paused, as if even he couldn’t quite believe it himself. “That we’ve been seeing each other. That your sister and I are engaged. I didn’t want you to feel blindsided about this news. After everything, how it used to be between us.”


A humorless laugh caught in my throat. Used to be. Morgan had once mocked him, dismissed him, and rolled her eyes at the boy I adored. And now, she had his heart, his future, and his name.


Wade shifted, leaning forward. His elbows were on his knees. “You were my best friend, Josie. Back then. You mattered to me. You still do. That’s why I wanted you to know first.”


My heart twisted at his words. Best friend.


That was all I’d ever been to him, wasn’t it? All I would ever be. His best friend.


He turned his head, his eyes searching on my face. “Are you genuinely happy for us?”


The question sliced clean through me. I forced a smile that felt like it might shatter me at any second. “Of course,” I said softly. “I’m happy for you.”


Inside, the truth burned: I wasn’t. I wasn’t happy at all. I was miserable.


But when Wade gave me a relieved smile, I let him believe the lie. Because that’s what best friends do. They try to be happy for their friend’s happiness.


“Thank you, Josie,” he said with that signature gorgeous smile on his face. “You don’t know how much it means to me that you approved my relationship with your sister.”


Do I have a choice?


From the start, I knew that I didn’t stand a chance with my beautiful sister. I was always second best when it came to her. I always knew that life’s unfair.


The crickets filled the silence between us once again. His elbows were resting loosely on his knees. He’d said his piece. His apology. His explanation. And I’d given him the answer he wanted, even if it was a lie. That was all that mattered, right?


He studied me for a moment, then a small grin tugged at his mouth. “So, is there someone in your life now? Some fellow I should be shaking hands with?”


My heart lurched. For a split second, I almost wished I could say yes, that there was someone. But there wasn’t. There had only ever been him.


I forced a laugh. “No. I’m afraid there’s no one.”


Wade chuckled, shaking his head. “That’s hard to believe. You were always the kind of girl someone would be lucky to find.”


The words hit me like a cruel kindness, a balm and a wound all at once. He said them so easily, so sincerely, without the faintest idea that he’d always been the one I wished would see me that way.


I turned my gaze toward the pasture, blinking hard against the sting in my eyes. “I guess I’ve just been busy with the shop.”


“Yeah,” Wade agreed gently. “A flower shop, right? That sounds like you.”


He gave my shoulder a gentle squeeze. Then, he stood. He stretched as if to shake the heaviness from the air. “I’m heading back inside. Let’s go.”


I shook my head. “I’d like to stay a bit more here.”


He nodded. “All right. Don’t stay out here too long, Josie. It’s getting chilly.”


I nodded. My voice caught somewhere deep in my throat. I didn’t answer him. I was afraid that if I did, my voice would break and I might say something stupid.


When he was gone, the front door closed behind him as the laughter from the living room spilled out before the night absorbed me in silence again.


Only then did I let my mask slip. Tears blurred the stars in the night sky, my chest hollow with a grief I couldn’t name out loud. He was Morgan’s now. While I was still just the girl he thought of as his best friend.


***


The bell over the shop door jingled just as I was arranging a cluster of hydrangea in a vase. After a sleepless night, my hands moved on autopilot. But my mind was still somewhere else; it replayed every smile and every word from last night until the ache in my chest hollowed deeper.


“Josie Walker!” Sierra’s voice filled the shop with her cheerfulness as always. She strode in, her boots clicking against the wooden floor with a wide grin on her face. “You didn’t tell me Wade Blackwood was back in town already! Mrs. Thompson saw him at the diner yesterday, and you know she can’t keep her mouth shut for more than five seconds. So? Spill it. Did you see him? Did you talk to him?”


I forced a smile. “Yeah. I saw him.”


Sierra’s grin grew and then faltered when she saw the expression on my face. Her eyes narrowed as she really looked at me and saw the puffy redness around my eyes and the slump of my shoulders. “Josie…” Her voice dropped to a concerned whisper. “Oh, dear… You’ve been crying. What happened?”


I set down the vase with my trembling hands. “They’re engaged, Sierra. Wade and Morgan.” The words came out like shards of glass in my mouth, tearing my throat on their way out. “They’re getting married.”


For a long second, there was only silence inside my shop. Then Sierra’s jaw dropped. “What?!”


I nodded miserably.


Her shock turned to fury in an instant. “That witch!” Sierra’s hands planted firmly on her hips, her whole body was bristling like a cat about to strike. “Of course she would. Of course, Morgan couldn’t stand to let you have anything that might’ve been yours. Even if it was only in your heart. She just had to swoop in and take it, didn’t she?”


Tears welled again as I shook my head. “They didn’t even tell anyone they were dating. We only found out when they made the announcement last night.”


Sierra threw up her hands. “Unbelievable! That’s low, even for her. Parading him into your parents’ house, smiling like she’s the queen of the world, isn’t she? She knew what she was doing, Josie. She wanted you to sit there and watch her victory like always.”


Sierra’s words hit hard because they rang too true. I wrapped my arms around myself, wishing I could shrink smaller or disappear. “I told them I was happy for them,” I whispered. “But I lied.”


My friend’s eyes softened with sympathy, but the fire didn’t leave her voice. She grabbed my hands and squeezed them tight. “Listen to me, Josie Walker. You are worth ten of Morgan, and don’t you dare forget it. She might be the golden girl in everyone else’s eyes, but I see you. You’re kind, you’re strong, and you have more heart than she ever will.”


Her fierce loyalty cracked something inside me. I leaned into her hug, letting her hold me together when all I wanted to do was fall apart.


Sierra pulled back from our hug, her eyes narrowing with the kind of fire that always made me feel both protected and a little nervous. Without a word, she marched to the front door and flipped the wooden sign to CLOSED. She locked the door with a decisive click.


I blinked at her. “Sierra, what are you doing? I still have deliveries to—”


“Forget the flowers for a minute.” She strode back toward me, planting her hands firmly on the counter. “We need to talk about her.”


I frowned. “Who? Morgan?”


“Who else?” Sierra’s eyes flashed with chagrin. “I’ll bet you anything the only reason she went after Wade in the first place was because she knew how you felt about him. Your sister is a cunning little witch.”


My stomach twisted. “You don’t know that.”


Sierra pointed a finger at me like she was laying down the law. “Oh, yes, I do. Your sister has always needed to be the center of attention, Josie. Always. If you liked a dress, she suddenly had to have it. If you made a new friend, she’d swoop in and claim them for herself. Don’t think for one second she didn’t notice the way you looked at Wade growing up.”


I shook my head as heat rose to my cheeks. “I never told anyone—”


“You didn’t have to,” Sierra interrupted. “I saw it, and if I saw it, Morgan did too. This—” She waved her hands toward the door, as if the engagement announcement itself still hovered in the air. “This isn’t about love. This is about her winning. About her reminding you that she always gets what she wants, no matter the cost.”


The truth of her words pricked at something deep inside me, a painful echo of all the times I’d stood in Morgan’s shadow. From dresses, friends, attention, and yes, now Wade too.


I pressed a hand to my chest, trying to ease the ache there. “Even if that’s true. It doesn’t matter now. They’re engaged. I can’t stop that.”


“No, you can’t.” Sierra’s voice softened, but her jaw stayed tight. “But don’t you dare start believing it’s because you weren’t good enough. This is about Morgan, not you. She knows how to hurt you, Josie, and she aimed straight for the heart.”


I swallowed hard, her words lodging deep in my heart. Part of me wanted to fight her reasoning, to defend Morgan just out of habit. But another part of me, the one that had lived in her shadow all my life, couldn’t ignore how much sense it made.


And the sad part is, I couldn’t do anything about it.
