CHAPTER 3


Josie’s POV


The house finally became quiet again. For the first time in days, I could walk down the stairs without bracing myself for the sound of Morgan’s laughter ringing through the living room or Wade’s easy voice while talking to my father.


They had gone back to the life they’d built together in the city, and I was left here, in Oakridge, where I belong. It broke my heart after I learned that they were already living together in New York.


I told myself I should be grateful. Grateful that I no longer had to hide in my room to avoid seeing them together. Grateful I didn’t have to plaster a smile at the dinner table while Morgan spoke in detail about their plans together for the future or the endless list of wedding ideas she had tucked into that perfectly polished mind of hers.


But gratitude was only part of it.


The truth was, the last two days had been some of the hardest of my life. Every glance between them had been a reminder of the dream I’d held close since childhood. It now slipped through my fingers like fine sand. Every casual touch Morgan gave him, every affectionate grin Wade returned, felt like watching someone else live out a story I’d written in my own heart for years.


It had been exhausting while trying to pretend. Pretend I wasn’t hurting inside. Pretend I wasn’t wishing selfishly and desperately that he’d look at me the way he looked at Morgan.


But that was over now.


I stood by my bedroom window, staring out at the empty driveway where their car had been this morning. I breathed a sigh of relief in the silence. No more rehearsed smiles. No more forced small talk. No more biting back tears when Morgan slipped her hand into Wade with a triumphant smile.


They were finally gone.


I pressed my forehead against the cool glass of the window, watching the morning sun spread across the fields. The Blackwood property stretched beyond the fence line. It was once a place of our happy childhood, but now it's just another reminder of the dreams that would never belong to me.


Enough.


For as long as I could remember, I had lived in Morgan’s shadow.


And Wade… Wade had always been in my heart. But he wasn’t mine. He had never been mine. He’d chosen her, and no amount of wishing or aching could change that.


I pulled myself away from the window. My reflection in the glass looked tired, worn down from sleepless nights and silent heartbreak. But beneath the fatigue, I caught a glimmer of something else. Something I hadn’t seen in myself for a long time.


Resolve.


“I can’t keep doing this,” I whispered to the empty room. “I can’t keep holding onto someone who was never meant to be mine.”


The words tasted bitter in my mouth. But they also carried a strange relief. Saying them out loud felt like cutting the first thread in a knot that had bound me to Wade for years.


I would move on from this heartache. Not all at once, of course. My heart wasn’t foolish enough to believe in miracles. But step by step, I would learn to let Wade Blackwood go. I would stop coming toward the fences that separated our land. I would stop imagining him as someone who could be my life’s partner. He’s to be my brother-in-law. He’s going to be part of our family.


I could recover from this. I was certain of it.


I decided to ride my horse this beautiful morning before going to work to clear my head. The morning air was crisp against my cheeks as I guided Daisy out of the stable. My sweet mare’s ears twitched forward as she was eager for our morning ride.


Riding had always been my way of clearing my head, and after the storm of the last few days. I needed the peace more than ever.


“Come on, girl,” I murmured, stroking her mane before nudging her forward. “Let’s go for a ride.”


The steady pace of Daisy’s hooves on the grassy road began to soothe me. The scent of dew and wild grass made me feel at peace after the troubling two days in the Walker household. I tried to focus on the simple things. The sway of the reins in my hands. The whisper of the wind against my skin and the wide sky stretching endlessly above us. Nothing beats the peaceful life here in Oakridge.


But thoughts of Wade crept back inside my head again. He told us that the other reason he’d come back, apart from the engagement announcement, was to finalize the selling of Blackwood Ranch. The land that once thrived like the Walker Ranch with several livestock, where Wade and I spent our days riding horses between our properties, would soon belong to strangers. A chapter of one of the most successful ranches in Oakridge was closing, and with it, every last thread of hope I had tied to him. I had to accept that there was no future for us anymore.


I told myself I wouldn’t ride near the border ever again. It had been too full of memories, too full of wishes that would never be fulfilled. I’d promised myself I’d keep to the other trails, the ones that didn’t hurt to travel.


But as my mind wandered, so did Daisy's. Without thinking, I loosened the reins and let her choose the path as my thoughts drifted like clouds across the past. Before I knew it, the landscape shifted into the familiar one I knew too well. The fence line. Where our property met the Blackwoods.


My breath caught. There it was. The border. The place where Wade and I had spent countless hours as kids, trading secrets, daring each other into mischief, and dreaming of futures. The place where, just days ago, I’d seen him step out of his truck for the first time in years, and his smile knocked the breath right out of my lungs.


And here I was again, as if my heart had dragged me back here against my will. I tightened my grip on the reins, staring at the fence like it might reach back and accuse me of being weak with my resolve. I should have turned away and chosen another trail.


As I tugged Daisy’s reins, ready to turn her back toward home before old memories began to swallow me whole again. Just as I shifted in the saddle, the low growl of an engine made me pause in my tracks.


A shiny new pickup truck, gleaming under the morning sun, rolled into the driveway of the Blackwood ranch. My brows drew together. Wade had mentioned the sale. Could this be the new owner?


Curiosity anchored me in place. I sat straight in my saddle with one hand absently stroking Daisy’s mane as the truck door opened.


My heart gave a sudden and sharp thud. The man who stepped out of the pickup truck was unlike anyone I’d ever seen in Oakridge. He was tall and broad-shouldered with the easy confidence of someone who knew the world belonged to him. His dark blond hair, when caught in the sunlight, gleamed. It was a little tousled, as though styled without effort. And even from a distance, I could tell he wasn’t from around here.


He was dressed finely too. Too polished to be a cowboy. His button-down shirt looked expensive, his jeans were tailored, and his boots looked like they’d seen more sidewalks than pastures. A man from the city, I concluded. He stood at the edge of ranch land as if he’d stepped straight out of another life.


I swallowed hard as my pulse quickened.


He shielded his eyes against the sun, scanning the property as if he were sizing it up. Then his gaze drifted as if sensing my presence. He caught sight of me on Daisy just beyond the fence line.


Our eyes met. And in that instant, something in my chest fluttered to life. It was startling and impossible to ignore.


The stranger’s eyes lingered on me a moment longer than I would like. His mouth curved into a devilish smirk that made my stomach flip. He closed the truck door with a smooth motion before he began walking toward the fence line with the kind of unhurried confidence that only came from a man who’d never once doubted himself.


“Well now,” he drawled, his voice warm but edged with amusement. He was definitely from the city with that accent. “I didn’t expect to find an audience this early in the morning.”


I tightened my grip on Daisy’s reins, wishing my cheeks didn’t flush so quickly. “Sorry. I—I didn’t mean to intrude. I was just riding.”


He stopped a few feet from the fence, resting one hand casually on the weathered wood while his smirk deepened. “Intruding? As far as I can tell, you were here first. Guess that makes me the trespasser.”


The way he said it was smooth and teasing, which made me unsteady on Daisy’s back. I swallowed hard, trying to gather my composure. “Are you… the new owner?”


His eyes gleamed. He caught my eyes and held them with unsettling intensity. “That depends. Are you going to welcome me to town or warn me off?”


The question caught me off guard. For a second, all I could do was stare at him. He laughed softly. The sound seemed to ripple through the morning air.


“Aidan Callahan,” he said finally, tilting his head with a mocked formal nod. “Looks like we’re going to be neighbors, Miss.”


Neighbors.


The word sank deep into me, stirring a shiver I couldn’t quite explain. Because in that moment, this devilishly handsome stranger watched me with eyes far too sharp for my liking. I knew one thing for certain: nothing about my quiet little world was ever going to be the same again.


“Now that I’ve gone and introduced myself,” the stranger said, dragging me out of my reverie as he leaned a little heavier on the fence rail. “It seems only fair you do the same.”


I stiffened in the saddle. Daisy shifted beneath me as if sensing my nerves. This stranger’s gaze was steady and piercing, with a startling shade of blue that unsettled me the longer I held it.


“I…don’t think that’s necessary,” I managed to say, forcing my voice to stay even. “I’m no one important.”


His brows lifted, and that smirk returned knowingly. “No one important?” he repeated, like he couldn’t quite believe the words. “Funny thing to say when you’re the first person I’ve met in this town. That makes you pretty important, at least to me.”


I looked away, tugging gently on the reins. My pulse drummed in my ears. The last thing I needed was attention from this stranger, especially the one who seemed far too charming for his own good. “I should be going. I’ve got work to do. Good day, sir.”


“Work, huh?” His tone was teasing, but there was something sharper beneath it, as if he wasn’t used to people brushing him off. “I guess I’ll have to figure out your name the hard way, then.”


I gave Daisy a soft click, urging her to turn. “Don’t bother,” I said quietly. “It will only be a waste of time.”


I urged Daisy to go and end this conversation as we started down the trail. I still could feel the weight of his eyes following me. Those unsettling blue eyes, full of curiosity and of something I couldn’t name. They pressed into my back until the ranch faded out of sight.


And no matter how much I told myself I wanted nothing to do with Aidan Callahan, a part of me already knew it wasn’t going to be that simple. He was our new neighbor now.


After my disturbing encounter with our new neighbor, I shoved him out of my head as I went to work that morning. I hope I don’t have to see him again. Maybe from now on, it would be a smart move if I stopped coming to the border.


The bell above the flower shop door jingled as Sierra waltzed in with her usual whirlwind energy trailing behind her. She carried a tote bag and a coffee cup in her hand, her eyes practically sparkling with mischief.


“You will not believe what I just heard at Ruby’s diner,” she announced without as much as a hello. She leaned dramatically across the counter, lowering her voice like she was about to share government secrets. “The whole town is buzzing about the new owner of the Blackwood Ranch. Apparently, he’s… wait for it…” She paused, grinning widely. “Drop-dead handsome man.”


My hands stilled where I was arranging a bouquet of sunflowers. My heart gave a nervous skip. I forced a dismissive smile. “People in this town buzz about everything. Give them a week, and they’ll find something else to talk about. Have they already moved on from Amber’s unexpected pregnancy?”


Sierra narrowed her eyes, clearly not satisfied with my lack of interest. “Uh-huh. But this time it’s different. Ruthie Jenkins swore he walked into the diner this morning, looking like he belonged on the cover of some city magazine. Tall, broad shoulders, ridiculously good hair.” She sighed dramatically. “I mean, Josie, if half of what I’m hearing is true, this guy’s going to have every woman in town lined up with casseroles by Friday.”


I busied myself with trimming flower stems. I kept my gaze firmly on the flowers. “Sounds like the town got itself a new distraction, then.”


Sierra studied me a moment, and then her grin turned sly. “So, have you seen him yet? The mystery rancher? He’s your neighbor, you know.”


I tightened my grip on the scissors. Images flashed in my head: his devilish smirk and those unsettling blue eyes that had followed me down the trail. I swallowed hard and shook my head. “Nope. Haven’t seen him.”


Sierra groaned. “Ugh, Josie, how do you miss things like this? You live practically next door to that ranch!”


I forced a little laugh and shrugged. “Guess I’m too busy with the shop to notice.”


It wasn’t entirely a lie. But the words tasted bitter anyway. Because the truth was, I had noticed him. And I haven’t been able to stop thinking about him since, even though I keep telling myself to forget about our encounter.


Sierra leaned against the counter, sipping her coffee with narrowed eyes. She was waiting for me to crack. “You’re telling me you haven’t so much as caught a glimpse of him? Not even from a distance? You used to ride Daisy near the border every morning, right?”


I shook my head, finishing the bouquet. “Nope. I haven’t had the pleasure. I didn’t ride this morning.”


It was the truth wrapped in a lie. I’d had the pleasure of seeing him, if you could call it that. But I wasn’t about to admit it to her. Not when his smirk had unsettled me or the way his blue eyes made me anxious for some reason.


Sierra let out an exaggerated sigh. “That’s so typical of you, Jo. The most exciting thing to happen in this town in ages, and you managed to miss it. Sometimes I swear you live under a rock.”


I smiled faintly, keeping my attention on the flowers. “Better under a rock than in the middle of town gossip.”


She groaned. “You’re impossible. Fine, don’t swoon with the rest of us mortals. Not that I can blame you. I know your eyes only see one man and one man alone. But mark my words, if this man is half as good-looking as people say. He’s going to stir up a whole lot of trouble around here. Men like that always do.”


Her words lingered long after she skipped out of the shop, leaving behind the faint smell of coffee and perfume.


Trouble. That was the exact word I would use to describe Aidan Callahan. And I wasn’t going to let myself get tangled up with the likes of him.


At least, that was what I told myself.


Later that night, I sat by my bedroom window with Daisy grazing in the pasture outside and the lamp at my desk the only light in my room.


I tried to read, but the words on the page blurred. I tried to sketch out a few floral arrangements for an upcoming wedding, but my pencil stopped halfway through.


All I could see was those eyes. Blue like a clear summer sky. Direct and unsettling, as though Aidan Callahan had stripped away every shield I had carefully built over the years. He had looked at me, not past me, not around me, not through me, the way so many others did. No. He had looked at me like he wanted to know exactly who I was.


And I hated how much it rattled me.


I hugged my knees to my chest, resting my chin there. This wasn’t supposed to happen. My heart was already bruised and broken and barely held together after Wade and Morgan’s engagement announcement. I had promised myself no more daydreams. No more pining after things I could never have.


So why couldn’t I shake him? That smirk. That confidence. That city-polished presence that had no business belonging to a man buying up a ranch.

CHAPTER 4


Josie’s POV


By morning, the gossip about the new owner of the Blackwood Ranch had already spread like wildfire in our little town.


I couldn’t so much as fetch a coffee from my favorite coffee shop without overhearing someone swoon over ‘the new Callahan man at the Blackwood Ranch.’ According to Mrs. Bailey, he drove a shiny pickup that looked more at home in a fancy city than down a dirt road like ours. According to the waitress at the diner, he tipped generously and had a smile that could melt glass.


Well, that’s a very dramatic description.


By the time I returned to my flower shop, the chatter was lodged in my head, irritating as a splinter.


Sierra was no help either. She swept in mid-morning with her usual dramatic skill, grinning ear to ear. “So the new rancher is already the most eligible bachelor in town. The ladies at the diner were practically swooning over him over their pancakes.”


I rolled my eyes, busying myself with daisies. “Sounds like people of Oakridge need a new hobby.”


Sierra plopped onto a stool. “Don’t tell me you’re not curious about him, Josie. Everyone’s talking about him. And if you aren’t careful, some other girl’s going to snap him up before you even peek over that rock you like to hide under.”


I forced a laugh, keeping my eyes on the flowers. If only she knew I’d already met him. If only she knew how those blue eyes had invaded my dreams last night.


Sierra propped her chin in her hands, watching me far too closely as I fussed with a bunch of wildflowers that didn’t really need fussing. “You know,” she said slyly, “I bet he’s the type who’ll come sniffing around town, looking for someone to keep him company. And if half of what I’ve heard is true, he won’t be short of volunteers.”


I sighed, tying off the bouquet with more force than necessary. “Then I’m sure he’ll have plenty of options. Problem solved.”


Her eyes narrowed. They were as sharp as a hawk’s. “You say that like you don’t care. But I know you, Josie Walker. That little flick in your voice? That’s your tell.”


“My tell?” I laughed lightly, hoping it sounded convincing. “Sierra, the only thing I care about is whether the Jenkins wedding flowers will come in on time. That’s it. Otherwise, I’m in trouble.”


Sierra wasn’t buying it. I could see it in the way her eyes gleamed with a slow smile tugging at her lips. “Mm-hmm. Sure. Keep telling yourself that.”


I busied myself sweeping stems off the counter, but her words got under my skin. Because no matter how much I denied it out loud, deep down, I knew the truth: Aidan Callahan had unsettled me in more ways I couldn’t explain. And if Sierra or anyone else caught even the slightest sign of that, I’d never hear the end of it.


Sierra hopped off the stool and wandered toward the shop window, peering out like she was half-expecting Aidan Callahan to stroll by right then and there. “You know what you should do?” she said, turning back to me with a mischievous glint in her eyes.


I raised a brow. “Do I want to know?”


“Obviously.” She grinned. “You should sneak over to the border and take a peek at him. Just for… you know, research. To confirm if the rumors are true. Strictly scientific purposes, of course.”


I snorted, shaking my head as I gathered the day’s receipts. “Research? Sierra, I don’t have time to go spying on the new ranch owner.”


“Oh, come on.” She leaned against the counter, grinning wider. “It’s not spying if you happen to be riding Daisy near the fence. Totally innocent. And who knows, you might catch him without a shirt on. These city types always look like they belong in perfume ads.”


A blush crept into my cheeks before I could stop it. “You are ridiculous. Do you even hear yourself?”


“Ridiculously right.” Sierra winked and grabbed her tote bag. “Mark my words, Josie Walker. Your curiosity’s going to get the better of you. And when it does, I expect every juicy detail.”


She swept out the door with her usual dramatic flair and the bell jingling overhead.


I stood in the middle of my quiet shop with my cheeks still warm before muttering under my breath. “Trust me. Curiosity isn’t going to get the better of me.”


But the words sounded hollow, even to my ears. By late afternoon, I was still replaying Sierra’s teasing in my head. Sneak over to the border. Catch him without a shirt on. I groaned aloud just thinking about it.


There was no way. Absolutely not. I was not the type to go snooping around like some lovesick teenager.


So when I saddled Daisy after closing the shop, I told myself it was just for a quiet ride. Like I always do. Fresh air, open fields, and nothing more. I even promised myself I wouldn’t go anywhere near the Blackwood property.


But old habits die hard. My hands guided Daisy along the familiar trail, the one I had ridden for years, the one that curved like muscle memory toward the fence line where Walker land met the Blackwoods’. Before I realized it, the trees thinned, and the weathered fence appeared in the distance. My stomach dropped.


“Perfect,” I muttered. “Exactly what I wasn’t going to do. Sierra would laugh like a maniac if she learned about this.”


I tugged on the reins, ready to turn Daisy around before temptation made a fool out of me, when the low rumble of an engine reached my ears.


A shiny pickup rolled into the drive on the Blackwood side, the sunlight gleaming off its polished surface.


My pulse quickened. And then I saw him. Aidan Callahan stepped out, his broad shoulders outlined against the setting sun, his jacket slung casually over his arm, and his blond hair caught the light like fire.


Daisy shifted beneath me as I froze, caught between retreat and curiosity. Of course, he saw me. And of course, that damn smirk curved his mouth as he leaned against his truck like he’d been expecting me all along.


“Well, well,” Aidan drawled, his voice carrying easily across the fence that separates us. “If it isn’t my mysterious neighbor. I could’ve sworn you said I shouldn’t bother trying to figure out your name.”


My cheeks reddened. So much for not snooping, and I was caught just doing that now. I straightened in the saddle, gripping the reins tighter than necessary. “I wasn’t snooping, if that’s what you’re thinking.”


Aidan tilted his head, his smirk widening just a little. “Funny. Didn’t say you were. But, interestingly, that’s where your mind went.”


I huffed as heat crawled into my cheeks. Damn it. “I was just out for a ride. I take this trail all the time.”


“Mm.” He crossed his arms, the late sunlight cutting sharp lines across his broad frame. “So I should just believe it’s a coincidence you keep ending up here? At this exact fence line?”


Daisy pawed the ground beneath me, as if she shared my flustered energy. “It’s not—” I stopped myself, shaking my head. “You’re impossible. Talking to you is just a waste of time.”


He laughed quietly, carrying across the open space. “So I’ve been told.” He gave me a long and lingering look as his blue eyes were bright with mischief. “I guess that makes you my impossible neighbor.”


I rolled my eyes but couldn’t quite smother the flutter rising in my chest. “Neighbor implies some kind of friendship. We’re just strangers who happen to share a fence.”


Aidan grinned, pushing off the truck and tipping his chin toward me. “Strangers today, maybe. But I’ve got a feeling that won’t last long.”


I opened my mouth to retort, but the words tangled on my tongue. Instead, I tugged the reins gently, turning Daisy back toward home.


“Goodbye, Mr. Callahan,” I said, hoping my voice sounded firmer than I felt.


“See you around, Miss Cowgirl,” he called after me. “Be careful on your way home.”


I didn’t look back. I couldn’t. Because if I did, he might see just how much his teasing was already getting under my skin.


By the time Daisy and I made it back to the stable, the sky was streaked with orange and lavender, the kind of sunset that always made the Oakridge look softer and prettier than it was. I brushed Daisy’s mane down while whispering thanks into her ear before heading up to the house.


I took the quickest shower I could manage, scrubbing away the dust of the ride and the lingering echo of Aidan Callahan’s voice inside my head. By the time I pulled on a simple dress and came down the stairs, the scent of roasted chicken and fresh bread filled the air.


I paused halfway down, looking at the home I had grown up in. It was nothing like it used to be. When I was a child, the Walker house was modest, a little drafty in winter and creaky in summer. But ever since my father struck gold, or rather luck, with his land deal years ago. Everything had changed from then on.


Each year brought a new renovation. First, the wraparound porch with its gleaming white columns. Then there was the kitchen redone with granite counters and fancy appliances, which my mother barely used. Last year, it was the dining room, complete with a chandelier so big it nearly touched the table.


My father loved showing it all off. He loved reminding everyone, from family and friends to everyone, that he was no longer just another rancher in town. He was one of the wealthiest. And he wore that truth like a badge of honor. He was never shy about flashing it in conversation or hosting gatherings where he could bask in the envy of others.


I took a deep breath before stepping into the dining room, where the table gleamed beneath the chandelier’s golden light. My parents were already seated. Their conversation was still about Morgan’s upcoming wedding.


Sometimes, I wondered if they even noticed me at all. Our dinner would always fall into a familiar pattern of my parents talking over each other and the clinking of cutlery against the fine china. They didn’t notice how quiet I was, and I didn’t mind. It was easier this way, slipping into the background where no one expected me to fill the silence.


I ate slowly, pushing roasted chicken around my plate. I nodded at the right moments when my father launched into another story about the new thoroughbreds he bought to be a good addition to his stable, or when he bragged about how my sister made him proud once again.


The chandelier sparkled overhead, casting everything in a golden glow that felt too bright and too polished for three people having dinner.


Then, between bites, my mother turned to me as if remembering I was at the table. “Oh, Josie,” she said; her tone was light and casual. “Morgan called earlier. She told me she wants you to be one of her bridesmaids. She forgot to tell you personally when they visit because you’re always busy at your flower shop.”


The fork nearly slipped from my hand. My throat tightened as I took a sip of water to cover the pause.


“Really?” I managed to say.


“Yes. She said it would mean a lot to her,” my mother added warmly, smiling like she was sharing good news. “Isn’t that wonderful? You and your sister, standing side by side on her big day.”


Wonderful. That word echoed in my head. It was hollow and heavy all at once.


I pressed a smile to my lips, even as my chest ached. “Yes,” I said softly. “Wonderful.”


Neither of them noticed how forced my voice sounded. They were already off again, chattering about guest lists and venues, the wedding shaping itself in their minds like a fairy tale.


After dinner, I excused myself quietly. I slipped out onto the porch, where the night air was cool against my skin. The crickets had started their chorus, and the scent of fresh grass was rising from the fields. Out here, away from the chandelier’s glare and my parents’ endless chatter, I could finally breathe.


I leaned against the railing, staring out at the stretch of land that had always been my refuge. The image of Morgan in her wedding gown flashed in my mind. Her radiant smile, her hand clasped in Wade’s, and the vows spoken while I stood just behind her. I imagine myself standing beside my sister while she marries the only man I have ever loved.


The thought sliced through me sharply. How was I supposed to endure that? How was I supposed to smile, to stand in my bridesmaid’s dress and pretend it didn’t feel like the end of me?


I squeezed the porch rail until my knuckles went white. I blinked hard against the sting in my eyes.


And then, another image surfaced. With the devil-may-care smirk on Aidan Callahan’s face, his blue eyes stared at me.


The memory caught me off guard. It lingered there at the edges of my thoughts. As if my heart, bruised and battered as it was, was already cautioning me about this troublesome newcomer.

CHAPTER 5


Josie’s POV


The morning sunlight streamed through the front windows of my flower shop. It warmed the scent of roses and lilies that filled the air.


The bell over the door chimed, breaking through my peaceful morning. I was kneeling behind the counter, trimming the stems of freshly cut dahlias, their vivid orange petals fanning like tiny bursts of flame.


“Now this,” a familiar smooth and amused voice drawled behind me, “isn’t what I pictured when I heard the town’s famous flower shop.”


My hand jerked, nearly snapping a stem in half. I froze. Every nerve in my body stood on edge at the voice I already knew too well. Slowly, I lifted my gaze.


And there he was. 


Aidan Callahan.


He filled the doorway as if he owned it. His broad shoulders were framed by that dark, tailored jacket, and his sandy-blond hair caught the sunlight that streamed in behind him. His signature grin was there, but his eyes, those sharp and unsettling blue eyes, swept across the shop and landed squarely on me.


“Good morning, neighbor,” he said with that infuriating smirk I hate so much.


I straightened, smoothing my apron as if it could cover how my pulse had quickened. “You shouldn’t sneak up on people like that. What are you doing here?”


“I didn’t sneak.” He strolled farther inside, glancing at the rows of flowers and the soft pastel arrangements lining the windowsill. “The bell gave me away. You just look distracted, that’s all.”


I busied myself while tying twine around the stems, refusing to meet his gaze. “Most people come here for flowers, Mr. Callahan. Not conversation.”


“Well,” he said, leaning an elbow casually on the counter, “lucky for you, I’m not most people.”


I finally looked up, only to find him watching me too closely. He looked as if he were peeling back layers I’d worked hard to keep hidden.


“What do you want?” I asked severely.


“Maybe some flowers,” he replied, his smirk widening. “Or maybe just a name. You still haven’t given me yours.”


My stomach fluttered, though I crushed the feeling down quickly. “Like I said the other day, I’m not someone important.”


Aidan’s brow arched. His expression was unreadable. “Funny. You don’t strike me as someone unimportant.”


I felt my face heat up. I turned away, pretending to rearrange a vase of lilies behind me. He doesn’t know me. He can’t know me.


Behind me, I heard him chuckle under his breath. “I’ll take a bouquet,” he said at last. “The flower shop owner’s choice. Surprise me. I trust your judgment.”


I eyed him guardedly but was still grateful for the task because then, I didn’t have to look at his eyes anymore. Though my hands trembled faintly as I reached for blooms. I chose a mix of wild daisies, pale roses, and sprigs of lavender, the kind of bouquet that felt simple yet elegant. My hands moved with practiced ease, though I was all too aware of Aidan still leaning on the counter with his gaze fixed squarely on me.


“Do you always stare like that?” I asked without looking up. “You know that’s a bit rude, right?”


“Only when I’m trying to figure someone out,” he answered. “You’re hard to read.”


“I’m not a book.”


“Everyone’s a book,” he countered, his smirk tugging at the corner of his mouth, making him more handsome than he already was. “Some just try harder to keep their pages closed.”


I tied the twine a little tighter than necessary, refusing to let him see the effect his words had on me. “And you think you can read me?”


“Not yet.” He tilted his head, studying me like I was a puzzle. “But I’m good with puzzles.”


I finally looked up, meeting those sharp blue eyes head-on. “Maybe you should find another one to solve. Trust me, you will find me boring.”


Aidan chuckled, unbothered by my attempt to push him away. He tapped his fingers lightly against the wooden counter. “You know, for someone who claims she isn’t important, you sure put a lot of fire in your words. It makes me wonder what you’re hiding behind.”


My cheeks warmed. I thrust the finished bouquet toward him. “Here. Your flowers. You should get going.”


He accepted them, holding the bouquet in one hand while spinning a daisy between his fingers with the other. “Beautiful,” he murmured, though I had the strangest feeling he wasn’t talking about the flowers at all.


I cleared my throat. “That’ll be twelve fifty. Are you going to pay cash or card?”


Instead of pulling out his wallet immediately, he leaned closer across the counter, lowering his voice. “Tell me one thing, and I’ll pay double.”


I narrowed my eyes. “What kind of thing?”


“Your name,” he said simply.


My stomach twisted. He was one damned stubborn lout. “We’re already neighbors, and you already know where I work. Isn’t that enough?”


“No,” he replied smoothly. “I like to know you more. I’m curious about you.”


“I don’t think that’s necessary,” I answered. “Let’s keep being strangers, shall we?”


I was glad when he finally gave up. Just as Aidan slid the bill across the counter, the bell over the door jingled again.


“Josie. You won’t believe what I just—” Sierra’s voice trailed off when she spotted him. Her eyes widened, flicking from the bouquet in his hand to me standing stiffly behind the counter. “Oh.”


Aidan didn’t seem fazed by the interruption. In fact, he turned slightly, giving Sierra one of his easy smiles that probably melted girls left and right. “Good afternoon, Miss.”


Sierra blinked, clearly taken aback, before offering a faint reply, “Uh, hi.”


“Keep the change,” Aidan said, dropping a crispy twenty bucks on the counter. His tone was casual and almost amused. He tipped his head at me with that maddening grin tugging at his lips once more. “See you around.”


With that, he pushed the door open and strolled out onto the sidewalk with a bouquet in hand.


Sierra stood frozen for a moment before rounding on me, her eyes burning with curiosity. “Okay. Who. Was. That?”


I busied myself rearranging the ribbons by the counter, hoping she’d let it slide. “A customer.”


“A customer?” Sierra echoed, her tone incredulous. She leaned closer, lowering her voice as though we were conspiring. “That was him, wasn’t it? The new owner of Blackwood Ranch. The one everyone’s talking about. And he was here. Buying flowers. From you.”


My shoulders stiffened. “I don’t know. People buy flowers all the time.”


“Do not play coy with me, Jo.” Sierra pointed toward the door, as she could still see him walking down the street. “That man is a walking headline. Tall, broad-shouldered, and ridiculously handsome. He could have walked right off a movie poster, I swear. And he was flirting with you.”


I nearly choked. “He wasn’t—”


“Oh, he was,” Sierra cut in with a knowing grin. “Don’t think I caught that little wink on his way out. He winked, Josie. At you. I think I just saw the first scandal our sleepy town’s had in years.”


I gave her a flat look, but my cheeks betrayed me. They heated under her intense scrutiny. Sierra gasped dramatically. “You’re blushing!” she accused. “You are flustered. This is so much better than I imagined. Forget Wade Blackwood. Your life just got a whole lot more interesting, bestie.”


I turned away, trying to busy myself with the register. “He just happened to buy flowers. Don’t think too much of it.”


Sierra folded her arms, her grin spreading even wider. “Mmhmm. Men who aren’t interested don’t smile like that, Josie. And they don’t wink either.” She nudged me playfully with her elbow. “Looks like there’s a new love story in the making in Oakridge.”


I didn’t answer since I knew that my friend was being absurd with her romantic ideas.


Sierra leaned her elbows on the counter, her eyes narrowing like a detective zeroing in on a guilty suspect. “So, are you going to tell me why your face looks like you’ve been standing too close to the oven? Or do I have to drag it out of you?”


“I’m fine,” I said quickly, reaching for the receipt pad as though it needed reorganizing. My hands fumbled with the pages slipping from my grasp.


Sierra snorted. “Fine, my foot. I’ve known you since second grade. You only get that pink in the cheeks when you’re either embarrassed or thinking about something you shouldn’t be thinking about.”


I shot her a warning look. “Drop it, Sierra.”


“Uh-huh.” She leaned in closer. “You know, when I walked in, I swear I could’ve cut the tension between you and Mr. Tall-Dark-and-Dangerous with a knife. He was looking at you, Jo. Not in a casual way either. He was looking at you like he was memorizing your freckles.”


My heart gave a nervous stutter as I busied myself stacking ribbon spools. “You’re imagining things, Sierra.”


“Am I?” she said sing-song, then gasped. “Oh my gosh! Do you like him?”


I spun around so fast I nearly knocked the ribbon basket to the floor. “I do not!”


Sierra clapped her hands while laughing. “You’re protesting way too hard. That’s it! I’m calling it. Josie Walker has a crush on the mysterious rancher.”


“I just met him,” I hissed, lowering my voice as if Aidan could still be lurking outside. “I don’t even know his name. Besides, I am not interested in him at all.”


“Doesn’t matter what you say. Your face says enough.” Sierra tilted her head, her eyes twinkling with mischief. “And honestly, after Wade? You deserve someone who looks at you like that. Someone who looks at you like you’re the only person in the room.”


Her words stung, though they were meant to be comforting. I hated that Wade’s name could still make my chest tighten. And I hated even more that Sierra wasn’t wrong. Aidan had looked at me differently. Unsettlingly, so to speak.


I swallowed hard and forced a laugh. “You’re reading way too much into a bouquet purchase.”


Sierra arched a brow. “We’ll see. I’ve got a feeling this isn’t the last time he’s going to show up in here.”


I turned back to the counter, pretending to fuss with the ribbon again. But in truth, my mind was already betraying me. I replayed the way Aidan’s smirk tugged at his lips and the way his blue eyes lingered on me as though he knew I was hiding something.


For the first time in a long while, the thought of someone seeing me that way made my pulse race.


I didn’t go straight home after closing the shop. I lingered in town long after most of the shops had gone dark, letting the glow of the diner signs and the bustle of the evening traffic distract me. Anything to keep from being alone with my thoughts.


I told myself I was just enjoying the crisp night air and that I needed to clear my head after Sierra’s relentless teasing. But in truth, I wasn’t ready to go home. The space where the walls had grown heavy with memories I’d rather not relive.


By the time I finally pulled toward the ranch, the stars were out in full in the night sky. They were scattered like diamonds across the sky.


Tonight, something felt off.


It hit me the moment the house came into view.


There sat a truck I’d already come to recognize in our driveway. The polished chrome and that paint caught what little light the moon offered. A city man’s truck, it was so out of place against the farm vehicles that usually cluttered our yard.


Aidan Callahan’s truck.


My stomach dropped.


What was he doing here?


The porch light burned bright, spilling its glow over the front steps. Through the curtains, shadows moved inside the living room. I could see my father’s broad frame, my mother’s animated gestures, and another silhouette in the room that made my chest tighten with dread.


A low rumble of laughter drifted out of the house while my father’s booming voice carried easily into the night. He was entertaining a visitor, and I didn’t need to guess who his guest was.


I stood frozen as the knot in my stomach pulled tighter. I had a bad feeling I couldn’t quite name crawling through me.


Aidan Callahan. He was sitting in my parents’ living room. I had no idea why, but the thought unsettled me more than anything else had all week.


I forced my feet toward the porch. Each step felt heavier than the last as my chest tightened with the kind of dread that made me want to turn and run the other way. But there was no avoiding it.


The warmth of the living room hit me as soon as I stepped through the door, along with the sight I’d been bracing for. My parents sat in their favorite chairs. They were positively glowing with enthusiasm. Across from them, sitting with ease as if he belonged there, was Aidan Callahan.


His posture was relaxed, with one arm draped casually across the back of the sofa. When his eyes landed on me, he smiled. It was not polite nor innocent, but it was the same devil-may-care smirk that had already managed to wedge itself into my thoughts.


“Josie!” My mother beamed, patting the seat beside her like I was still a little girl. “Come here, sweetheart. Look who came to visit! Our new neighbor.”


My father’s chest puffed with pride as though he’d been the one to invite Aidan to town. “Mr. Callahan here just bought the Blackwood ranch. Fine young man. You should’ve seen the deal he struck.”


I swallowed hard, forcing a tight smile as my eyes flicked from my parents’ glowing faces back to Aidan. He hadn’t looked away, his gaze holding mine like he was in on some joke I didn’t understand.


My mother’s voice broke through the tension. “Isn’t it wonderful, Josie? To have such a nice neighbor move in right next door.”


I nodded stiffly, though my face felt pale and my palms were sweaty. Wonderful wasn’t the word I’d use. With every second that Aidan’s smirk lingered, it felt more like the ground beneath me was shifting.


Aidan leaned back into the sofa as though it were made for him. “It’s a fine town you’ve got here,” he said easily, his voice rich with that confident drawl. “Not quite like the city, but I think I could get used to it.”


My father chuckled. “A smart man knows when to slow down. That ranch will treat you well if you put your back into it. Fine land, plenty of good grazing.”


“Exactly why I bought it,” Aidan agreed smoothly. “It’s got potential, and I like a challenge,” he said while his eyes flicked toward me. The corner of his mouth quirked ever so slightly.


I looked away quickly, pretending to straighten the hem of my sleeve. But my cheeks betrayed me; they heated all the same.


“And he’s single, too,” my mother added with a laugh. “Imagine that, Josie. Our neighbor’s not some retired old rancher this time.”


My throat tightened. “Mom—”


But Aidan only chuckled, his gaze still angled toward me even as he addressed my mother. “I’m afraid your daughter already made it clear she wants nothing to do with me.”


My parents blinked at him, then they turned their attention to me with a surprised expression.


My stomach twisted. I would kill him right here, given a chance. He should be thankful that my parents were here with us.


“Did she now?” my father asked with an amused look. “Well, don’t take it personally, son. Josie’s shy.”


“That she is,” Aidan murmured, his smirk deepening as his eyes lingered on me a beat too long.


I wanted the floor to open up and swallow me whole. My parents laughed, oblivious to the tension between their guest as they chattered on about ranching and renovations. Aidan leaned back again, looking every bit the picture of confidence.


But every so often, his gaze would slide back to me. They were curious and far too knowing, making it impossible to ignore the storm brewing silently between us that was unseen by anyone else in the room.

CHAPTER 6


Aidan’s POV


Oakridge. The name alone sounded dull, something out of a postcard no one actually wanted to send. And the reality wasn’t any better. One main street. A handful of shops that all looked like they hadn’t changed since the town was established. Folks who walked too slowly, smiled too much, and talked as if the world began and ended in this boring little town.


I hated it already.


Still, I played my part to perfection. The charming new neighbor. The polite young man buying fruits, smiling at shopkeepers, shaking hands as if I gave a damn about belonging here.


Let them whisper about me over pie at the diner. Let them gawk at my truck like it was some kind of alien craft. That was all part of the show.


I hadn’t come to Oakridge to make friends.


I’d come for something else.


Buying the Blackwood Ranch wasn’t some whim or business venture. It was a move that I had been preparing for years. Every step brought me closer to the moment I had been waiting for, the one where debts would be repaid and old scores would need to be settled.


I ran a hand along the arm of the Walker family’s sofa, listening to Josie’s father as he bragged about their own ranch and newfound wealth.


The man had no idea. None of them did. They smiled, they welcomed me, and they treated me like I was just another neighbor. When in reality, I was a storm slowly rolling in on their perfect little world.


But then there was her.


Josie Walker.


The quiet and soft-spoken girl was hiding behind her demure self as though she could disappear into the background. She wasn’t like the rest of them. Her eyes had betrayed her earlier this day. They were wide and wary when she thought I wasn’t looking. She didn’t know what to do, and I found that amusing.


Amusing. Yes, that was the word I would use to describe her. Because for the first time since I set my plan into motion, something in this town had managed to catch my interest.


The Walkers laughed easily, sipping coffee and speaking of cattle markets, thoroughbreds, and land values as though any of it mattered. I nodded when expected. I smiled when it suited me, but I kept my real thoughts carefully veiled.


They didn’t need to know what the ranch truly meant to me. Oh, no. Not yet.


The Blackwood property was only the beginning of my meticulous plan. It would take time before I could put everything into motion. But I could wait. I already waited this long to achieve this.


In time, Oakridge would stop looking so safe. By then, no one would remember the charming stranger who walked into town with a polite smile and a shiny truck.


Except maybe Josie.


My gaze flicked to her. She sat perched rigidly on the edge of her chair. She probably wished she were invisible. Pale as porcelain, her lips pressed tightly, and her eyes cast anywhere but at me. But when she finally looked in my direction, her expression faltered.


There it was again. That spark of unease. She probably sensed it, even if she couldn’t name it. That alone set her apart from the rest of them.


And for reasons I hadn’t quite decided. I found myself unwilling to dismiss her entirely. She was innocent in all of this, but she was part of my carefully curated plan. The girl I need for my revenge.


But still, she lingered in my mind long after I should’ve stopped thinking about her. I told myself that because she was the tool I needed to make my plan succeed. I need her to trust me.


I leaned back in my chair, a smirk tugging at my mouth as the Walkers continued to chatter, oblivious to what awaited them. Let them savor this happiness more. Soon enough, the smiles would fade from their faces.


For now, all I needed to do was wait as I played my part as the charming new neighbor.


***


I tossed my jacket over the arm of the chair after I visited the Walkers’ home. I crossed the empty living room of the Blackwood ranch house. It still smelled of dust, even after the cleaning crew had been through. It was the kind of smell that clings to old wood and memories better left to rot.


God, what a forsaken place.


I poured myself a glass of bourbon from the decanter I’d brought because this house certainly didn’t have anything worth drinking. I stood by the window, staring out at the dark stretch of fields. This place was nothing but miles of grass, fence posts, and fucking silence.


Back in the city, I could’ve been on a rooftop terrace right now. The skyline at my feet, crystal glasses, laughter, and the kind of company that knew how to make the night interesting would make my evening better. Not this stillness. Not this suffocating silence.


I told myself I could endure it.


At least, for a while.


This ranch wasn’t meant to be permanent. Just another step in a plan I’d been setting in motion for years. Every decision, every deal, and every mile that brought me here all had a purpose.  With every single thread I was pulling, I was patient enough to watch the rest unravel in its time.


Still, patience had never been my strong suit. I took a long swallow of bourbon, letting the burn remind me what was waiting for me when this was over.


My life. My world. One built of glass towers and the finest things, not the dirt roads and cattle fences like this hellish place.


I had no use for Oakridge. No use for its people, either. And yet, my mind flicked back to the girl at the Walkers’ house.


Josie. I found myself smiling just thinking about her. She’d looked like she wanted the ground to swallow her whole the moment she walked into the room and found me there chatting with her parents.


That look in her eyes was as if she already knew I didn’t belong here, as though she wanted to run before I got a chance to get too close to her.


I set my glass down with a clink against the wooden table. It didn’t matter what she thought. I needed her for my plan. She couldn’t escape me.


For now, I would not scare her. I would try to gain her trust. And the sooner I finished my business here, the sooner I could go back to my life in the city and never look back.


But for now, Oakridge would have to endure me as I would endure this forsaken place.


***


Something roused me from my dreamless sleep. For a moment, I thought someone was about to break the front door. The pounding came sharp and insistent in these early morning hours. I groaned, dragging a hand over my face.


Morning. The damn country morning. Out here, everything woke up early—the birds, the air, even the damned silence.


I’d barely gotten three hours of decent sleep on this rock masquerading as a mattress.


Another knock came. Louder this time.


“Yeah, yeah,” I muttered, rolling off the bed. I tugged my bedroom door open with one hand, still half-asleep, as I made my way to the living room and found myself staring at this Southern beauty when I finally opened the damned door.


Josie Walker.


She stood at the porch, stiff as a board. She was clutching a basket wrapped in a cloth. Her eyes darted from my bare chest to anywhere else, as if the very sight of me half-naked might burn her. Well, that woke me up faster than any coffee could.


“What a surprise,” I drawled, leaning against the doorframe. I enjoyed seeing the flustered look on her innocent face. “I didn’t take you for the type to bring men breakfast in bed.”


Her pretty face became flushed instantly, a shade that almost made me laugh out loud. Almost.


“It’s not—” she swallowed hard, searching for composure. “It’s from my mom. She thought you might like something homemade since you’re new in town. She heard that you’re always having breakfast at the diner.”


I let my eyes linger on her just long enough to make her shift uncomfortably. I looked at those beautiful brown eyes. I knew she hated how unsettled I made her feel when I was staring at her intently. She was truly an interesting woman.


“And she sent you to deliver breakfast for me?” I asked, smirking at the thought. “What an honor.”


Josie gripped the basket tightly. “I don’t have a choice,” she said quickly. Too quickly. “I’m the only one who is available to bring this to you since I take my horse for a ride every morning. Also, the maids are busy at this time of day.”


I chuckled. I noticed that she was a chatter when she was nervous. “Is that so? Do I need to think that I’m special then?”


Her jaw clenched. I could see that she wanted to retort but changed her mind. She shoved the basket toward me like it might explode in her hands if she held it any longer. “Here. Enjoy. I should get going, Mr. Callahan. Good day.”


I didn’t take it right away. I let the silence stretch between us. When I plucked the basket from her hands, I intentionally brushed my fingers against hers just enough to see her breath hitch.


“Tell your mother I’m grateful for the food,” I replied smoothly. “As for you, Josie, I’ll take this as a neighborly welcome. Though I’d be lying if I said I didn’t enjoy the delivery more than the meal.”


Her lips parted as if she might argue. Then she shook her head, muttering something I couldn’t catch. She turned sharply on her heel and left with her head held up high after our morning encounter.


I watched her retreat and mount her horse swiftly like a woman who was born to ride those majestic beasts.


A smile tugged at my mouth. Oakridge might be dull and predictable. But Josie Walker was the one surprise that was worth keeping me entertained in this boring little town.


***


Josie’s POV


I practically ran down the steps of the Blackwood porch. My heart was still hammering like Daisy’s hooves against hard soil. I wouldn’t be surprised if my face were in a deep shade of red at the moment.


What in the world had I just walked into? I asked myself furiously.


Breakfast. That was all it was, just a simple neighborly gesture from my mother. Nothing scandalous about it, except he had to open the door looking like that.


Half-naked. His hair was mussed from sleep. His chest was bare with solid muscles in the right places, not the kind of softness you’d expect from a man who didn’t work on a ranch. He looked like one of those men you only saw on glossy magazine covers, smug and entirely too aware of his magnificent body.


And that smirked. Lord, that smirk! He knew exactly what kind of mess he’d made of my poor brain when he smiled like that. He enjoyed every second watching my discomfort.


I concluded that he was the very devil. I clutched my hands together as I hurried down the path, while my face was burning so hot I was sure the whole town could see it. I’d never seen a man like him up close, not here in Oakridge.


Wade was handsome, yes, but he was someone familiar and safe. While Aidan Callahan was something else entirely.


He was dangerous. Unpredictable. Out of place in our quaint little town in every way possible. I hated that my pulse still hadn’t calmed, even if I had already gone back to the Walker Ranch.


What must he have thought? That I’d gone there to what? Flirt? Throw myself at him with a basket of freshly made breakfast? He must have thought that my mother was pushing me toward him since he was single. The thought made me groan. I covered my face even though I was all alone.


I should’ve refused when Mom asked me to deliver the food. I should’ve let someone bring it after their chores were done or left it on the porch like a proper stranger would. Instead, I had to stand there, fumbling like a fool while he looked at me with those piercing blue eyes so sharp it felt like he could see straight through my soul.


I shook my head, forcing my steps to go faster as if distance would erase this morning's encounter. It meant nothing, just a man who was too smug for his own good.


Deep down, I knew I’d remember the sight of that smirk, that careless lean against the doorframe, far longer than I wanted to admit.


By the time I reached home, I’d nearly convinced myself to forget the whole thing and move on. But the moment I walked through the door, Mom looked up from where she was sitting on the sofa reading a glossy magazine. “Did he like it? The breakfast?”


I halted. “I—I suppose. He said to thank you.”


Mom smiled. “Good. It’s only a neighborly thing to do to make him feel welcome. The poor man must be lonesome, rattling around that big old ranch by himself. The ladies at the church mentioned to me that they keep seeing him having breakfast at the diner. He probably doesn’t know how to cook. That’s why I decided to bring him some freshly made home-cooked meals.”


My parents were clearly blinded by his nice guy persona, but I knew better. That man shouldn’t be trusted.


“I don’t think he’s lonely, Mom. He seemed happy on his own at the Blackwood Ranch.”


My mom scowled at me. “Now, don’t be rude to the man, Josie. Just because he is smiling doesn’t mean he’s not lonely. We don’t know what his reason was for choosing to buy the Blackwood Ranch, but we need to make him feel welcome in our town.”


I nodded at her, keeping my eyes down so she wouldn’t see the heat rising in my cheeks. Lonesome wasn’t the word I’d use to describe Aidan Callahan.


Infuriating. Unsettling. Even impossible, maybe. But never lonesome.


And if Mom knew how he’d answered the door. Half-dressed and smirking like the very devil himself. She probably would never have sent me in the first place…


I went back to my room and took a quick shower before going to work. I slipped into a pale blue dress, one of the simpler ones I saved for work, and brushed my hair back quickly. I was about to go to work after my encounter with that man this morning. The mirror in front of me reflected a face that still looked flushed, even though an hour had passed since my errand.


It was ridiculous.


He was just a man, I thought grimly. A neighbor. Someone passing through Oakridge with money to burn and an attitude far too big for our little town and nothing more.


And yet, my hands trembled as I tied the ribbon around my waist. The memory played on repeat inside my head. The door swung open, and there was the sight of him standing there, bare-chested in the early morning light with his knowing eyes. The way his smirk cut through me like a blade and lingered long after I’d turned away.


But what struck me was when our hands briefly brushed against each other. It was a brief contact of skin, and yet, it sent a ripple effect through my entire body.


Get a grip, Josie.


I pulled on my boots while shaking my head. I didn’t want him in my thoughts. I didn’t want to see how he made me feel unsettled. But no matter how I scolded myself, the unease clung to me like dust after a long ride.


By the time I gathered my things, ready to leave for the flower shop, I had only one thing certain. Aidan Callahan was trouble.


Trouble I wanted nothing to do with.


So why did I feel as if no matter how hard I tried, he’d already taken root in my mind?

cNT



CHAPTER 7


Josie’s POV


The bell above my shop door jingled. The sound was enough to jolt me from my thoughts. I nearly dropped the bundle of peonies I’d been holding, as my hands were still clumsy from the memory that had happened to me this morning.


That annoying smirk. The way my pulse had stumbled out of beat at the sight of his half-naked body and the way he was entirely too amused with the whole situation.


“Josie,” Sierra’s voice carried before I even looked up. She breezed in like she owned the place. She looked stunning in her yellow sundress. “Have you heard?”


I didn’t need to ask what she meant. Her grin said everything.


“If this is about the new Blackwood Ranch owner, I am not interested,” I said sharply.


“Of course it’s about him!” She leaned across the counter with her gleaming eyes. “Aidan Callahan. That’s his name. He’s been the talk of Main Street since last night. Half the ladies at the diner nearly fainted when he walked in for supper. Handsome as sin, they said. Different, too. Not like the ranch boys we’ve got here.”


I scoffed. “Oh, please…”


“If the man who bought flowers for you the other day was Aidan Callahan, then the rumors about him were not exaggerated. Also…” Sierra added, lowering her voice. “Rumor has it that he’s a very wealthy man. A billionaire. They say that he has a lot of businesses across the country.”


I rolled my eyes, busying myself with wrapping the bouquet in brown paper. To be honest, I didn’t want to listen to how the ladies in our town kept on singing phrases for that mysterious ma. Can’t they see that he was just pretending beneath that charming smile?  


“Is that so?” I asked dryly.


“Don’t give that uninterested voice, Jo,” Sierra reprimanded, her eyes narrowing at me. “Are you telling me that you’re not even the least bit curious about him?”


The paper crinkled too loudly under my fingers. My stomach twisted while heat climbed into my face. I couldn’t let my friend see how much just thinking about that guy would summon such a reaction from me. I shook my head. “Nope. Not in the least.”


“Excuse me?” Sierra laughed, flipping her hair dramatically. “Tell me the truth? Are you blind or something? You sure don’t have the same eyesight as the rest of us in Oakridge.”


I turned to the shelves, fussing with a flower that didn’t need fussing. “Well, that’s me. I am not interested in the new guy in our town. I bet he will not last long in Oakridge before he realizes how much our town doesn’t suit him.”


“What a harsh thing to say, Josie Walker.” Sierra gasped, clutching her chest in mock horror. “You sure do hate his very existence, do you? And why is that? Because he’s the guy who bought the Blackwood Ranch? The last piece of land that ties you to the love of your life?”


I glared at her, but Sierra merely smiled at my violent reaction.


“Don’t give me that look,” she said, smirking. “I thought you were already in the process of getting over Wade? Trust me, you don’t want people to know that you’re harboring feelings for your future brother-in-law.”


I shook my head. “How can I forget him when you keep on reminding me of him?”


Sierra grinned as a mischievous thought got inside her head again. “Maybe Aidan Callahan is the person you need to help you move on quickly from Wade.”


“I told you, I am not remotely interested in that man.” But even as I said that, his image flashed in my mind again. I pressed my lips together and prayed Sierra wouldn’t notice the way my hands trembled just thinking about Aidan Callahan and the way he looked at me.


Thankfully, for the rest of the day, it was peaceful and uneventful as my regular customers came to buy flowers. I had the chance to chat with them while preparing their orders. Of course, I couldn’t miss that the subject of our light talks kept drifting to the new guy in town.


As much as I want to change the subject immediately, I can’t. I didn’t want them, especially the matron ladies, who were notorious gossips, to think that I had some hidden animosity toward the Walkers’ new neighbor. I didn’t want to be the center of the town gossip for openly hating the ‘nice and handsome man’ who arrived in Oakridge.


In the end, I smiled and agreed with their stories and gossip about Aidan Callahan. By the time I closed the shop and decided to go home, I was thoroughly exhausted.


The sun still hung warm over the hills. Too early for dinner, but too late to start anything new. The hours between work and supper always felt restless, so I decided to do what always calmed me down when there was so much on my mind. A took Daisy for a late afternoon ride.


The air smelled of hay and dust as I crossed the yard toward the stables. I found Hank, one of the hired ranch hands, brushing down one of my father’s geldings. His face broke into a smile when he saw me. “Afternoon, Miss Josie.”


“Good afternoon, Hank,” I said, smiling back. “Is Daisy saddled up yet?”


“She’s right in her stall, waiting on you,” he answered, then added with a chuckle. “Though she’s been giving me the look all day, like she knew you’d come for her this afternoon.”


I laughed softly. Before I could reply, Hank went on like he was simply making light conversation.


“Your daddy went by earlier,” he informed me. “Told me to get Red Hawk ready since your new neighbor might be wanting a ride with him soon. He said Mr. Callahan ought to have a horse, at least for the back trails up in Blackwood. Truck won’t take him far out there.”


I blinked. My hand froze on the stable door of Daisy. “Wait—you mean… Dad lent Red Hawk to the new neighbor?”


“Sure did,” Hank nodded. “Your daddy’s proud of his thoroughbreds. He wants to show off, I reckon. And besides, Mr. Callahan’s going to need a steady mount if he plans on exploring that land properly.”


The words landed heavily in my chest. Red Hawk wasn’t just any horse. He was one of Dad’s finest thoroughbreds, the kind of animal Dad never trusted to just anyone.


And now, he lent it to Aidan Callahan.


I turned away before Hank could notice the heat rising in my cheeks. It was silly to feel anything about it. Of course, Dad would extend generosity to a wealthy new landowner. It was good business and good neighborliness.


Still, the thought of Aidan Callahan sitting tall in one of our saddles, riding the hills I’d grown up on, made my stomach twist.


It felt like he wasn’t just invading Oakridge. He was seeping into every corner of my life, piece by piece.


“You all right, Miss Josie?” Hank’s voice dragged me out of my reverie.


I looked up at him as I finished saddling Daisy. “Yes, I’m fine, Hank.”


He tipped his Stetson hat before I led my mare out of the stable. I still couldn’t believe that my father lent Aidan Callahan one of his prized studs.


I swung into Daisy’s saddle with one thought in my head. Anywhere but the border.


I knew these trails like the back of my hand. There were plenty of ways to ride, plenty of paths that would keep me far from Blackwood’s fence line and the man who’d been messing with my peace since the moment he arrived.


I told myself I’d take the creek path where Wade and I used to play when we were still kids. Or maybe the ridge trail that leads past the cottonwood trees.


Anywhere but the border.


As Daisy’s hooves carried me farther out, my chest tightened with a nagging thought I couldn’t shake. Red Hawk. Dad’s pride and joy. What if Callahan didn’t know how to ride? He was a city folk, after all. What if he injured Red Hawk because of his carelessness? That man belonged in the city, not on a saddle.


Before I knew it, my reins had tugged Daisy in the very direction I swore I wouldn’t go. Toward the border. Toward the Blackwood ranch.


I’m not going to snoop, I told myself firmly. I’m just checking on Red Hawk. That’s all.


The excuse rang heavy even in my ear, but I clung to it anyway. Because I had no other excuse for coming to the property of that man.


The fence line came into view, the familiar stretch where the Walker land bled into Blackwood’s. My eyes scanned the field, searching for a glimpse of Red Hawk’s copper coat. Just a peek at the horse, and then I would go back with my ride.


That was when I spotted him. The horse stood not far from the trail, its reins dragging in the dirt and its ears flicking nervously. But it wasn’t Red Hawk that made my breath catch. It was the man beside him. Aidan Callahan lay sprawled in the grass, motionless.


My heart lurched as my blood ran cold.


“Aidan?” The name slipped out before I even realized I’d spoken it.


He didn’t move. Panic surged through me. I dug my heels into Daisy’s sides, urging her closer. My pulse was pounding in my ears. All my unease, all my stubborn vows to keep my distance, melted into a terrifying thought.


Did he fall off Red Hawk and have a bad concussion? What if he’s hurt? What if he’s not breathing?


I slid off Daisy’s saddle before she even came to a full stop. My boots sank into the ground as I ran toward him. I jumped off the fence that separates our lands. My heart was in my throat. My breath was shallow, as panic tightened around me like a vise.


“Aidan…” I dropped to my knees in the grass, reaching for his shoulder as dread clawed at me. I gently tapped his cheek to wake him up. “Are you all right—”


Then, his eyes opened. Blue as a summer sky stared back at me. He smirked like a lazy cat in the sun. “Well, good afternoon, sweetheart.”


I stared at him, halfway between relief and outrage. My hands were still on his shoulder and cheek. “You—” I sputtered angrily. “You scared me half to death! I thought you were—”


“Dead?” He stretched, folding his arms behind his head as if this were the most comfortable bed in the world. “Now that’s dramatic, Josie. I was only resting.”


“Resting?” My voice pitch got higher. I scoffed. I couldn’t believe this guy! “On the ground? In the middle of a field? Do you have any idea how it looked from a distance when there’s a horse beside you?”


His grin widened. “Judging by the way you came running, I’d say it looked effective.”


I sucked in a sharp breath. My whole body flared with embarrassment. Of course, he’d twist this into some kind of game. Of course, he’d enjoy watching me fluster like a fool.


I shot to my feet, brushing the dirt from my denim jeans while still trying to get the flush out of my face. “You’re impossible! You’re infuriating!”


“Guilty.” He tilted his head, his eyes glinting with amusement as he finally sat up. “But admit it, you were worried about me for a moment.”


“I—” My words caught as my tongue tied itself in knots. I wanted to deny it, to throw his arrogance right back in his face. But the memory of that first jolt of fear and the way my heart had staggered when I saw him still on the ground betrayed me.


I pushed him back to the ground as hard as I could. Before I turned sharply, striding back toward Daisy with the dignity left in me.


Behind me, I heard the devilish man laugh wickedly, carrying his laughter easily across the grass. It only made my cheeks burn hotter.


Damn it. Damn him!


Even if I kept on cursing him inside my head, I couldn’t ignore that I was the one who jumped to a conclusion. I was the one who ran after him after seeing him motionless on the grass. I thought…


I shook my head. It was a fault for getting nosy about Red Hawk. I should have just gone to the trail I first intended to go to. I should have saved myself from such embarrassment.


I yanked Daisy’s reins and swung back into the saddle, refusing to glance over my shoulder. My cheeks were still burning, and my pulse was still tripping over itself.


Aidan’s laugh echoed faintly from the distance. I knew that his blue eyes were still on me. I could feel them burning in my back. His amusement infuriated me more than anything in this world, as if my anger were just another one of his amusements.


I gritted my teeth, focusing on the trail ahead. I should’ve stayed away. I told myself I’d ride the creek path and avoid the border. But I didn’t listen. I was stubborn. I just had to check on Red Hawk, and now here I am. I made a fool of myself again.


I hated that he’d caught me off guard like that. I hated how easily he unsettled me, like he’d been in Oakridge for years instead of days. But what I hated the most of all things was how my heart had leapt when I thought something had happened to him.


I shouldn’t care. I didn’t want to care. I pressed Daisy into a quicker pace, letting the wind whip against my face as if it could blow the memory away. No matter how fast I rode, the image clung stubbornly to me. Aidan Callahan was lying in the grass with a smirk tugging at his lips, and his blue eyes were lit with mischief.


By the time the Walker house came into view, I’d convinced myself of one thing: I had to stay away from him as far as possible.


For my own sanity.


For my own heart.


Whatever game he was playing, I didn’t want to be part of it.


And yet, deep down, a restless little voice whispered at the back of my head that this wasn’t the last time our paths would cross.


Not by a long shot.
