CHAPTER 8


Josie’s POV


For the next several days, I kept myself in a small but familiar routine. Flower shop in the morning. Home in the evening. Dinner with my parents, where I became more withdrawn than ever, since the topic at the dinner table was always about Wade and Morgan’s wedding. My parents, especially my dad, couldn’t hide their glee at the upcoming wedding.


I told myself I didn’t care. The sooner I stopped caring for Wade, the faster I could get over my love for him.


So far, everything was working according to my plan. Also, I made a new lifelong goal: avoid Aidan Callahan at all costs.


If I saw even a glimpse of that ostentatious truck parked in front of the general store, I turned on the corner. If I heard people mentioning that he was at the café having lunch, I would skip lunch altogether. Even on horseback, I mapped new paths through the Walker land, steering Daisy as far away from the border as possible.


It was easier that way. I never felt such harmony in my life when this new routine proved to be effective. I began to feel everything went back to my normal and monotonous life.


But this afternoon, I let my guard down. The shop had been slow, but that’s okay. It was not every day people would buy flowers. There were certain days when you were not getting your target sales.


The weight of the week pressed on my chest until I couldn’t stand hiding in my room before dinner. I decided to go for a ride like I usually do. I saddled Daisy and headed out. I told myself I just needed some fresh air.


Instead of taking the long ridge trail or circling the meadows, my hands guided Daisy toward the creek for a change. It was the place where Wade and I had accidentally stumbled across years ago. Half-hidden by a bend of trees, where the water pooled into a quiet flow before rushing downstream. We’d claimed it as ours, a secret we never spoke of to anyone else.


The moment the sparkle of water came into view, a pang struck me low in my chest. I hadn’t been here in so long, not since… well, not since I lost the right to still think of it as ours.


I slid off Daisy and let her drink at the water’s edge while I stood there with my arms folded tight against the ache threatening to break me.


The sunlight filtered through the leaves. I remembered Wade’s laughter echoing here and his warmth when it was just the two of us.


I squeezed my eyes shut. I wasn’t supposed to come here. This place was too full of memories and too full of what-ifs.


Maybe that’s why I had come. Because it was easier to drown in old memories than to risk being unsettled by new ones, by those blue eyes and those devilish smirks that had no business haunting me.


I was still caught in my reverie while helplessly clinging to the memories of the past. I knew I told myself that I should already start the process of moving on, but…


A sharp sound of another horse’s sneer sliced through the quiet creek, making me aware of how my thoughts were starting to make me feel melancholy.


Daisy lifted her head, her ears flicking at the sound of another horse nearby, while I went still.


This creek was supposed to be mine—ours. Nobody ever came here except for me and Wade and me.


My gaze swept the clearing, and my breath hitched when I spotted a familiar chestnut coat and black mane tied loosely to a tree not far from me.


Red Hawk.


My pulse stumbled as my blood ran cold. If Red Hawk was here, then that meant…


I darted my gaze around the banks, through the shadows of the trees. My heart pounded hard enough to shake me. Still, no rider in sight.


Of course. Of course, it had to be him. My father lent him that thoroughbred. He was the only one who would dare to use one of my father’s prized possessions.


I should’ve left right then. I should have slipped back onto Daisy and pretended I’d never seen the horse. Never took a trip to memory lane, as I had no right to them anymore.


I started to turn, but then I saw his clothes. They were carelessly spread across the fallen trunk of an old oak, and his boots tipped on their sides like a man had kicked them off without a second thought.


My stomach dropped. No. He couldn’t possibly…


Before I could piece everything together, a splash in the water shattered the silence.


My head snapped toward the creek. What I saw nearly made me choke on air. And there he was, gliding lazily in the water as if the whole world belonged to him. Aidan Callahan was as naked as the day he was born!


He floated on his back with his eyes closed, and his face tilted toward the late afternoon sun. He looked utterly and maddeningly unbothered.


Oh Lord.


Heat burned up my neck. The heat engulfed my face too as my traitorous eyes went wide, as I caught far more detail than I had any business catching.


I slapped a hand over my mouth to keep from making a sound. But my heart thundered loud enough that I swore he’d hear it.


Every inch of me screamed to run and never look back. Get out before he sees you, Josie.


My legs stay rooted. They were frozen between mortification and something far more dangerous I refused to name.


This creek wasn’t haunted by Wade’s memory anymore. It was now owned by Aidan Callahan, and he didn’t even know he had an audience for his reckless adventure.


I should’ve turned around. I should’ve bolted the second I realized what I was looking at. But I acted like a fool. I found myself staring at him, at the water shimmering against his skin, at the kind of body only a city man like him with too much time on his hands could have.


Then the inevitable thing happened. He tilted his head, just ever so slightly. Those piercing blue eyes opened and stared right at me.


I felt the world stop spinning. I gasped and stumbled back. I nearly tripped over a root, as my face burned so hot I thought I might ignite on the spot.


Aidan’s lips curved into that devil’s smirk I was beginning to know too well. He pushed up in the water unhurriedly. There wasn’t a hint of shame in him. He didn’t even bother to cover his manhood for modesty’s sake, as it was still visible in my vision.


The water was still slicking down his body as he tried to make conversation with me. “Well, well. Who do we have here? I didn’t take you for the peeping type, sweetheart.”


My jaw dropped. I was thankful that I still knew how to speak. “I—I wasn’t… I wasn’t peeping!”


“Oh?” He swam closer to the bank, utterly unashamed. He grinned wickedly in my direction. “So you just happened to be standing there watching me all wide-eyed and red as a tomato?”


“Don’t you dare come closer to me,” I threatened as he was about to get out of the water. “I swear, I will shout, and you’ll be in big trouble.”


He smirked. “I don’t think someone will hear you, sweetheart. This place is pretty far.”


I grimaced. He was right. This place was pretty far from the ranch, which was why it remained untouched even after all these years.


Mortification clawed through me. I spun on my heel when I realized that I was still gawking at him. I tried to march back to Daisy, but his voice followed me like a noose tightening around my wounded pride.


“You’re running again. You caught me in my most natural state. The last thing you could do is say hello like a good neighbor.”


“Hello?!” I sputtered, not daring to glance back. I knew when I turned around to confront him. He would see how much this unfortunate encounter had rattled me. “How can I bother with pleasantries when you’re naked?”


He chuckled, as if he found this whole thing amusing instead of humiliating. “Relax, sweetheart. It’s just skin. It was nothing you haven’t seen before, right?”


I nearly tripped again. “You’re insufferable.”


“Maybe,” he admitted with laughter. “But I noticed you didn’t look away right away. You like what you see?”


I scoffed. “As if!”


He chuckled again. I could hear the water flowing behind me. The unmistakable sound of his movement in the water and the splash of water falling off his skin. I shut my eyes tightly as my chest squeezed even tighter with every step I imagined him taking toward the shore.


I heard the scrape of fabric. The rustle of clothes sliding over his damp skin. My breath hitched while my hands curled into fists at my sides. Every muscle in my body screamed at me to bolt. To run to Daisy and never look back. I wanted to forget about this place and the memories with it. But my legs… They rooted me to the spot.


“Well,” his voice drawled from a much closer distance now. “Are you going to keep standing there with your back turned on me, sweetheart? You know it is rude to talk to someone with your back to them, right?”


I kept my back firmly on him. “You are highly mistaken if you think I will not look at you while you’re still naked.”


“Mm-hmm.” I could hear the smirk in his tone. It sent a shiver racing down my spine. “Funny, because I could’ve sworn those pretty eyes of yours were glued to me a while ago while I was still naked.”


“Stop it,” I demanded, covering my ears as if it would stop my embarrassment.


“Fine, fine.” Another rustle, and he was fully dressed now, thank God. “But answer me this, sweetheart. How’d you know I was here? It seems too perfect to be a coincidence. Are you looking for me? Perhaps you already miss me.”


I spun around, unable to take his smugness for another second more. He stood a few feet away from me. He was now fully clothed, thank goodness, while his hair was still dripping with water and his grin was far too satisfied.


“This is our—my spot,” I corrected myself. “My secret place. It’s always been mine.”


For a moment, something flickered in his eyes. Then he leaned back casually against the old tree, folding his arms with his grin widening. “Well, not anymore.”


The words hit me like a slap, my mouth hanging open. He gestured at the water lazily. “The creek flows right between Walker land and Blackwood, doesn’t it? Looks to me like this place belongs to both of us from now on.”


My cheeks blushed again. It was part fury and part something I couldn’t name. “You can’t just claim it like that. You have no right—”


“I beg to differ.” His gaze locked on mine. “You already found me here, sweetheart. It seems like fate had other plans for your little secret place.”


I swallowed hard, wishing desperately I could come up with something clever. Something that would wipe that smug look off his face. Instead, all I could do was turn sharply toward Daisy. My heart was still galloping as fast as her hooves would soon carry me.


But even as I mounted, even as I told myself to never come back to this creek, I knew the truth.


He’d already staked his claim on the place. On my peace. And maybe even on me.


My pulse still thundered in my ears while my whole body vibrated with heat and humiliation. I nudged Daisy forward. I was desperate to put space between us when his voice carried lazily across the creek’s open space. “Don’t stay away too long, sweetheart. It will be a shame if your little secret spot starts to feel more like mine than yours. Because I intend to come here very often from now on. I like it. And I intend to make this mine.”


“Yours?” I scoffed, looking at him over my shoulder.


The smirk was back on his face. “Yes. Mine.”


I stiffened, biting the inside of my cheek so hard it almost bled. There was something in his eyes that made my heart skip a beat.


I was scared. Not of him. But of the possibility that lies behind his meaningful words. I moved forward, refusing to fall for his bait. I didn’t look back. I didn’t give him the satisfaction. But his words followed me all the way home, echoing louder than Daisy’s hoofbeats.


No matter how fast I rode. I couldn’t shake the truth. He’d already burrowed under my skin, and I couldn’t brush his image from my mind.


And that smirk…


Lord help me. That smirk was starting to have an effect on me.

CHAPTER 9


Aidan’s POV


I couldn’t get the sight out of my head when I went out for a ride with my borrowed horse, and I came across this secluded creek on my new ranch.


It wasn’t part of my plan, but when I saw the stream running calmly, I suddenly had the urge to swim since I was already all sweaty from my ride. I didn’t have a problem with my nakedness. Being in the wild and a secluded place didn’t bother me.


Then the unexpected happened: Josie Walker stood there, rooted to the spot like a deer caught in headlights. Her face turned a dozen shades of red the second she realized what she’d stumbled across.


Hell, the way she went from pale to scarlet in two seconds flat nearly had me laughing out loud.


I’d never seen anyone blush that hard in my life. When she spun away from me, stiff as a board, her shoulders up to her ears as if she were terrified I’d breathe in her direction. I swear, I had to bite back the urge to tease her even more.


I had teased her anyway. I couldn’t help myself. I watched her fluster, stumble over her words, and practically run from me like I was the devil himself. It was the kind of entertainment that didn’t come often in a place as dry and dull as this town.


The way she reacted to seeing my naked body was a comical sight. The look on her face seemed to indicate she had just seen something blasphemous. And then it hit me, as clear as the sunlight glinting off that creek.


She wasn’t just shy.


She wasn’t just timid.


She was innocent.


A virgin.


The realization settled in my chest. That explained everything. The way she avoided my gaze. The way her voice trembled whenever I leaned too close. The way she couldn’t even handle the sight of a bare chest without turning into a blushing mess.


And damn if that didn’t make her even more interesting. A slow grin tugged at my mouth. I already expected that Oakridge was as boring, but Josie Walker proved to be a delightful surprise in this godforsaken town.


She was sweet. Innocent. And most definitely off-limits. The way she looked at me was as if she wanted to run as far away from me as possible. I couldn’t help but wonder just how long her resistance would last with me living next door.


And I had time.


Plenty of it.


I leaned back against the porch rail of the ranch house I now owned, my boots crossed at the ankle as the sun dipped lower with each passing minute. The smile curling at my lips wasn’t just amusement. It was pure satisfaction.


She was curious whether she admitted it or not. I saw it in the way her eyes lingered on me just a heartbeat too long before she bolted.


She wanted to run, but I wanted her to pull. The more she wanted to resist the attraction, the more I wanted to get her attention. That kind of tug-of-war was a man’s playground. And I had nothing but time to enjoy pulling her rope. I knew without a doubt that she’d fall for my trap eventually.


Josie Walker's innocence might be amusing to watch, but she was now the key to my agenda, why I came to this boring town.


I hadn’t planned on her. Hell, I hadn’t planned on anyone except the Walkers as a whole. This land, this deal, and this town. They were all part of a bigger picture, one I’d been planning for years with patience most men didn’t have.


But Josie… she was different. Untouched. Naïve. The kind of girl who’d never seen the wolf until his teeth were already at her throat.


I almost pitied her. Almost.


Because if I played this right, Josie wouldn’t just be a distraction from this godforsaken place. She’d be the perfect piece in my plan, whether she realized it or not. And when the time comes, the Walkers won’t even see it coming.

Josie’s POV


I unlocked my flower shop door. The crisp morning air still clings to the sleepy street. I was halfway through flipping the sign to open when a voice that was infuriating and impossible to ignore cut across the quiet morning. “Good morning, Peeping Tom.”


My blood ran cold. My eyes went wide as my head snapped toward him. Aidan Callahan leaned casually against his shiny truck that was parked across the street. His arms were folded on his chest while his legs were crossed at the ankles. He was having a photoshoot for those glossy magazine covers. And that damn smirk tugging at his mouth again.


My heart stopped. Then, I realized what he just said. Oh, he had a death wish! And I was more than happy to be the one to bring him to death’s door.


There were people out here on the streets, too. We were not alone, curse him! Mrs. Carter, who just bought her muffins and bagels from the bakery. Old Man Henry shuffled past with his cane.


Anyone could’ve heard him.


Mortification burned so hot in my cheeks, I thought I’d pass out on the spot. Before I could stop myself, I stormed across the little distance between us. I grabbed his wrist and dragged him inside my shop before slamming the door behind us.


“What is wrong with you?!” I hissed, my grip was tight before I realized I was still holding his wrist. I let go of him as if I had been burned and stumbled a few steps back.


His grin went wide. “What? Can’t I greet my neighbor now? What’s wrong with that?”


“What’s wrong?” I echoed with disbelief, lowering my voice to a harsh whisper. “You know exactly what’s wrong, Mr. Callahan. Don’t give that innocent look. We both know you are far from innocent. You want me to humiliate the townspeople from yesterday!”


He tilted his head, feigning the look of innocence. But those eyes, those sharp blue ones, were far too amused and gave him away. “What a harsh thing to say, sweetheart? If I remember correctly, you were watching me swim. Nothing shameful about a little curiosity.”


A red stain spread through my cheeks and spilled into my ears, too. “I wasn’t curious!”


“Sure,” he drawled, leaning against one of my display tables. His eyes trailed lazily over the buckets of roses and wildflowers. “That’s why you nearly fell over your own feet when I caught you.”


I pressed a hand to my face, groaning in disbelief. I didn’t understand why I even bothered to have this conversation with him. “You’re a terrible human being.”


“And you,” he said, lowering his voice in a dangerous way that made my knees want to buckle. “You are adorable when you’re flustered.”


I dropped my hand. I was about to glare at him, but my pulse rose when I realized that he had already crossed the distance between us. He was just now a foot away. I automatically took a step backward until I hit the counter.


“If you’re just here to torment me, you can leave now,” I snapped, though my voice lacked the weight I wanted.


Aidan didn’t even flinch. Instead, he leaned farther over the counter, and I had to lean back until my back nearly pressed against the table.


For a moment, I thought he was going to kiss me, but he just reached for the buckets of flowers behind me, plucking a single stem of rose from the bunch with infuriating casualness.


“Careful with that!” I darted forward, snatching it from his fingers before he could ruin the stem. “Don’t waste a beautiful bloom just to play with it.”


He chuckled lazily. “Touchy. Do you treat all your customers this way, or just the ones you catch swimming naked?”


My face burned hot enough that I thought I could light the whole shop. “You are not a customer. You’re a—” I bit down on the rest before I could call him every name I had lined up in my head.


He grinned, the kind of grin that said he knew exactly what I was thinking. “I’m your neighbor, sweetheart. You might as well get used to me.”


I pushed him away. I busied myself by putting the rose back in the bucket. I tried desperately to ignore the way his presence filled every inch of my shop. I could feel him watching me and the weight of those blue eyes tracking my every move.


“You really are easy to rile up,” he said, his voice laced with amusement. “One little word and you’re red from head to toe.”


“I am not—” I spun around, ready to deny it. Only to catch him smirking again. My voice faltered. “—that easy.”


“Mmm.” He tilted his head, looking unconvinced. “Keep telling yourself that, sweetheart.”


I opened my mouth, scrambling my mind for a snarky comeback when the door jingled open, and Sierra’s voice rang out.


“Josie! Can you make me a—”


She stood still at the doorway. Her eyes landed on Aidan first, then flicked to me. Her brows shot up, and the sly grin spreading across her face was nearly as infuriating as this man’s annoying smirk.


“Oh. Sorry! Am I interrupting something?” she drawled. She was about to shut the door behind her. “I’m sorry. Maybe I should come back when you’re not occupied anymore, Jo.”


“W-wait!” I stammered, heat rushing to my cheeks again as I tried to stop Sierra from leaving. “It’s not—it’s nothing… It’s not what you think. He was just—”


“Tormenting her,” Aidan supplied smoothly, crossing his arms with a smile. “Apparently, that’s my role around here.”


Sierra’s grin widened as she looked between us, her eyes gleaming positively. “Oh, I see. Tormenting her. Right.”


I groaned. “Sierra—”


But she was already stepping closer, practically vibrating with curiosity. “You didn’t tell me you and the new ranch owner were on speaking terms already.”


Aidan’s gaze flicked to mine, that devilish grin tugging at his mouth again as though daring me to deny it.


And for the first time, I realized just how dangerous he really was, not because of what he said but because of how easily he could tangle me up without lifting a finger.


Aidan glanced at Sierra before settling his eyes back at me, his smile tilting into that familiar curve that made my stomach flip against my will. “Ladies,” he said smoothly, dipping his head in a mock farewell. “I think it’s time for me to go. I have tormented Josie enough for the day.”


And just like that, he strode out the door. The little bell above it was chiming cheerfully, completely at odds with the chaos he left behind in his wake.


I stood frozen for a moment, staring at the empty doorway as his smile lingered in my mind like a tattoo. I shook my head hard, as if I could shake him loose. I was thankful that he finally left. My day just barely began, but I was already exhausted from dealing with Aidan Callahan.


“Oh. My. God.” Sierra’s sing-song voice floated beside me. “Do not even think you’re wiggling your way out of explaining what that was all about.”


I turned on her as I exhaled deeply. “I don’t know what you mean?”


Sierra crossed her arms, her eyes sparkling as she’d just uncovered the juiciest gossip in all of Oakridge. “The way Mr. Tall, dark, and devilishly handsome was looking at you like you were the only flower in this whole shop worth picking. And the way you—” She jabbed a finger at me. “—were turning every shade of pink under those gazes.”


My face went hot once again. “You’re mistaken. It’s nothing. He’s nothing.”


She arched a brow. “Nothing doesn’t stroll into your shop first thing in the morning and call you Peeping Tom.”


I looked at her sharply. “Where did you hear that?”


“Mrs. Carter,” my friend supplied with a grin. “She’s telling people that she heard the handsome newcomer call our dear Josie a Peeping Tom.”


I cursed under my breath as I avoided Sierra’s knowing gaze. “Mrs. Carter misheard it.”


“Uh-huh…” She wiggled her finger. “Mrs. Carter’s eyes might be poor, but her ears work perfectly fine. Why else do you think that woman is a notorious gossip?”


I groaned, burying my face in my hands. “I knew it. They heard him. I hate him!”


“No,” Sierra corrected with a wicked grin. “You don’t hate him. And that’s the problem.”


I dropped my hands, glaring at her. “Please, for the love of all things holy, do not start spinning wild stories about this. He’s just an obnoxious man. That’s all.”


Sierra only smirked, humming to herself like she didn’t believe me for a second. “Obnoxious. Handsome. Rich. Did I mention handsome? Yeah, I already did. Now tell me what you did do, and he’s accusing you of being a Peeping Tom?”


I threw the nearest rag at her, which she caught with a laugh. “I am not telling you. What do you want, anyway? Why are you here? Don’t you have work to do?”


“Alright, alright, I’ll stop teasing you now.” She raised her hands in mock surrender. “But I didn’t just come here to torment you like the handsome Mr. Callahan.”


I narrowed my eyes. “Really?”


She laughed, then tapped her fingers against the counter. “Actually, I came because I need a bouquet. Something cheerful. Sunflowers, maybe? You know that Amber just got back into town, and I thought I’d stop by her place with a little welcome-home gift.”


Amber was our friend in high school. The three of us were inseparable back in the day. But after graduation, she left town and never came back. Sierra and I also lost contact with her.


“Life must have been hard for her to come back to our town after swearing she’ll never set foot in Oakridge again.” Her smile was a mix of fondness and sympathy. “The moment she came back, she became the target of cruel gossip about her pregnancy. Five months along, maybe? It doesn’t look like the father’s in the picture.”


I sighed. “I know. She’s the only reason why I’m glad Aidan Callahan strutted into town and stole the gossip spotlight from Amber.”


“Yep. Amber was the subject of whispers. Folks said all sorts of cruel things. You know how Oakridge can be. But I thought it’d be nice to actually show up for her. I heard she rarely goes out of her house.”


Sierra’s words stirred something inside me. For all her craziness, she had a heart of gold. “Let’s visit her together,” I said, already turning toward the cooler. “I’ll put something together she’ll love.”


As I clipped stems and arranged blooms, I couldn’t help but feel a pang of admiration. Sierra had once been targeted by cruel gossip, but she held her head high. While I couldn’t even admit to her that Aidan Callahan had already barged into my life more than I realized.


By the time I tied the bouquet with a soft ribbon, Sierra was watching me closely with her unreadable expression.


“What?” I asked.


“Nothing.” She took the bouquet carefully, then smiled faintly. “Just glad some things in this town never change. You and your flowers, me and my big mouth.”


I forced a laugh. If only she knew how much had already changed and how much I was trying to keep buried.


“Silly,” I told her, grabbing my purse on the counter. “Let’s go and visit Amber. I know she’ll be happy to see us. I’ll just open the shop again this afternoon.”

CHAPTER 10


Josie’s POV


I had never been so determined to avoid anyone in my life. After Aidan Callahan started calling me a peeping tom, I doubled down on keeping my distance from him.


I avoided the main street when I thought he might be around. I ducked into alleys like a fugitive. I even stopped riding Daisy altogether during the afternoon. My rides had always been my sanctuary, but now they felt like a risk, a chance encounter I couldn’t bear.


But a week without riding felt like a piece of myself had gone missing.


So, on a fine Sunday morning, I gave in. I saddled Daisy with trembling hands. I told myself it would just be a short ride—an hour at most. Just enough to stretch her legs, clear my head, and remind myself why I loved the open land.


I made a silent promise to myself before we left the stable: I would stay away from the border. I will not go anywhere near Aidan Callahan’s land.


And for a while, I managed it. The morning sun warmed my face, and the breeze teased at my hair. Daisy’s steady pace beneath me made me feel like I was alive again. I should’ve been content with this little freedom and gone back to the stables.


But old habits were hard to break.


I lost track of where we were heading. By the time I blinked back to the present time, my heart sank when I realized what daydreaming had led me to again. There it was. The fence line where Walker’s land met Blackwood.


The freaking border. It was the same place where I’d sworn I wouldn’t go.


I stiffened in my saddle the moment I spotted him.


Aidan Callahan.


My enemy.


He crouched near the barn on the Blackwood side, his sleeves rolled up, grease smudged on his arm, while his head was bent as he worked on something. For a wild second, I thought I could slip away before he noticed me. I would turn Daisy around and head for the creek trail instead. I wanted to salvage what was left of my morning before his annoying smirk could ruin my day.


Then I saw what he was working on.


The old tractor.


I stiffened. I’d know that rusty hunk of metal anywhere. It had once belonged to Wade’s father. I had sat on that seat more times than I could count. My hands were clutching the wheel while Wade’s laughter guided me through the basics of driving. That tractor wasn’t just machinery. It was a memory.


Now, Aidan Callahan had his hands on it.


I should’ve kept riding. I should’ve ignored him. But the words came out of me before I could stop them. “What are you doing with that?”


He looked up. His blue eyes looked amused, as if he’d been waiting for me all along. A slow smirk tugged at his lips. “Well, good morning to you too, Walker,” he drawled, wiping his hands on a rag beside him. “Done avoiding me?”


I went still on Daisy’s back. “I don’t know what you’re talking about.”


He chuckled, which sent a ripple effect through my body. “I think you do. You think I didn’t notice?”


I nodded at Wade’s old tractor. “What are you planning to do with that thing?”


Aidan followed my gaze at the tractor. “I’m bored. I think I will try to fix it. Unless you’ve got some claim on this old beast?”


I tightened my grip on Daisy’s reins, wishing I’d never opened my mouth. “Well, it used to belong to the Blackwoods. Wade used to…”


Aidan cocked his head, studying me with an unsettling sharpness. “Wade, huh? If I remember correctly, Wade is the only child born to the Blackwood couple. Wade used to what, sweetheart?”


I pressed my lips together, regretting every second of this encounter. Why did I have a loose mouth? “Nothing.”


He rose to his full height, tossing the rag over the hood of the tractor. There was that look again. The one that made my stomach twist, as if he could see right through me even when I desperately wanted to be invisible.


“Wade used to what, Josie?” He kept on pressing. His expression was teasing, but there was a sharpness underneath it as if he already knew the answer.


I felt the heat spreading through my cheeks. “It’s none of your business.”


He leaned against the tractor, crossing his arms over his chest. “Let me guess. He let you drive this thing around? Show you the ropes like some small-town Prince Charming?”


The words hit too close to home. I told myself not to take his bait, but the expression on my face seemed to be betraying me.


Aidan’s brows lifted when he caught my silence and the look on my face. “Ah. Nailed it.”


My hands tightened on Daisy’s reins. “You don’t know anything, Mr. Callahan. Stop jumping to conclusions.”


“I know enough,” he answered smoothly, tilting his head as though he could put it together like a puzzle. “You’ve got that look on your face, sweetheart. The one a girl gets when the boy next door is her whole world. Let me guess, you like him.”


My throat went dry, as the air around me suddenly felt too heavy. “You’re wrong.”


“Am I? I don’t think so. Wade Blackwood.” He said Wade’s name like it was a weapon, watching me flinch. “He’s the one you couldn’t take your eyes off of. And now…” He gave a mocking shrug. “I heard now he’s marrying your sister.”


The words were a knife to my gut, even though I’d already bled them a hundred times over in my head. I swallowed hard, fighting the urge not to cry in front of him.


“Go to hell, Callahan,” I muttered, yanking Daisy’s reins to turn her away. I never cursed anyone in my life, and he was the first person who received it.


I didn’t regret it.


I tried to leave, but I could still feel his gaze burning into my back, that infuriating smirk that was carved into his face.


“Running away again?” His voice rang out behind me, slicing right through the calm morning.


I froze, and Daisy shifted beneath me as though she could feel my hesitation.


I looked back over my shoulder. Aidan was still leaning against the tractor, his arms crossed, and that devil’s grin plastered on his face like he knew exactly how to get under my skin.


“I’m not running!” I snapped.


“Hmm,” he drawled, pushing off the tractor with ease. “You could’ve fooled me. You’ve got this look like you’d rather be anywhere but standing here with me. Which usually means one thing: running. Josie Walker likes running when the situation is not in her favor.”


My jaw clenched. Every bone in my body screamed at me to leave. To prove him right by galloping Daisy far away from this man, but if I did, I’d never hear the end of it.


I tugged Daisy to a halt and forced myself to stay, even though my pulse was already pounding hard in my ears. “I told you, I’m not running.”


“Good,” he said, stepping closer. His eyes were piercing, and they were locked on me in a way that made it impossible to breathe. “Because you don’t strike me as the cowardly type, Josie Walker. Shy, sure. Quiet, definitely. But a coward?” His head tilted, and there was a spark of challenge in his gaze. “I don’t buy it.”


My cheeks warmed again. “You don’t know anything about me.”


Aidan’s smile softened just enough to be dangerous. “That’s where you’re wrong, sweetheart. I know more than you think. And the rest… The rest, I’ll figure out in time.”


I hated how his words made my stomach twist. I hated even more that I stayed there, stubbornly refusing to turn away, even as everything in me screamed that I should.


Aidan’s eyes glittered with mischief as he leaned a shoulder against the tractor. “Since this rusty heap means so much to you. How about you lend me a hand? Who better to help me fix it than the girl who had a lot of fond memories from it?”

But then my gaze drifted back to the tractor, to its peeling, and to the countless afternoons Wade and I had spent with it rumbling beneath us. My chest tightened.


This machine held pieces of my heart, pieces of a past I’d never get back. Before I could think twice, I found myself nodding at his invitation. “Fine.”


I slid down from Daisy. I tied her reins loosely to the fence post. She snorted softly, as if questioning my decision as much as I was.


Aidan pushed off the tractor, taking a few unhurried steps toward the fence line. “Need a hand getting over here?”


“Not a chance,” I muttered under my breath.


Before he could blink, I swung myself smoothly over the fence. One clean jump and my boots landed squarely on the Blackwood side. Dust kicked up beneath me as I straightened and brushed off my palms.


When I glanced up, his eyebrows were raised in genuine surprise. “Impressive.”


I couldn’t help the triumphant smile that curved my lips. “I told you I’m not weak or clumsy. I’m more than just a flower shop owner. I’m a cowgirl.”


For the first time since I’d met him, Aidan didn’t answer right away. His smile shifted, not the devilish smirk I’d come to expect but something darker. Something that made my breath catch in my throat.


“I see.”


I looked away quickly, pretending to focus on the tractor as my heartbeat was hammering far too loudly in my ears. That smile, whatever it meant, was far more dangerous than all his teasing combined.


I brushed past him toward the tractor. I was determined to ignore the strange weight of that smile still hanging in the air.


“So what’s wrong with it?” I asked, crouching down to inspect the wheels and gears like I actually knew what I was doing. “It had been abandoned in the barn since the Blackwoods left.”


Aidan chuckled low in his throat. He crouched beside me. “That’s what I’m trying to figure out. I know how to mechanic cars, but I don’t have experience with farm machinery. Thought maybe the tractor expert could enlighten me.”


I shot him a look. “I never said I was an expert.”


“No,” he agreed. The teasing lilt was back in his voice. “But you looked at it like it held all your childhood secrets. I’d bet you know every dent, every sound this beast makes.”


I ignored him and focused instead on the rusty bolts near the wheel. “It doesn’t start, does it?”


“Doesn’t even cough,” he admitted.


I reached for a stubborn lever and tugged it as hard as I could. My fingers brushed with grease, but I ignored it. He leaned closer, watching me with an amused curiosity that prickled against my skin.


“Careful,” he said softly. “That lever sticks.”


I pulled anyway, and sure enough, it refused to budge. I wiped my hands on my jeans while muttering under my breath. “Some things never change.”


Aidan tilted his head. “Funny. You say that like you’ve got a history with it.”


I hesitated. “Wade taught me how to drive it.”


Silence stretched between us. I regretted saying it immediately. Aidan’s expression shifted, just slightly. “Ah. The infamous Wade Blackwood. Figures.”


I stood abruptly, brushing my hands on my jeans again. “Forget it. You probably don’t care about fixing it anyway. Why waste your time? You’ll just buy a new one.”


To my surprise, he stood too. He closed the space between us. “What makes you think I’ll just buy a new one? Maybe I like working with what’s already here.”


I felt a huge lump in my throat as I refused to meet his eyes. “You don’t seem like the type to waste your time on things that have no value to your life.”


He chuckled. “That’s because you don’t know me yet, Josie Walker.”


I fought the urge to step. I could almost feel the current sparking in the air between us. My heart pounded so loudly I was sure he could hear it too. I was desperate to break the tension, so I decided to change the subject. “So… once you fix the tractor, what do you plan to do with it?”


His smirk returned. “Use it, of course. What else?”


I forced a shaky laugh. “Of course. Silly me.”


Aidan’s voice dropped tauntingly. “Unless you plan on forbidding me to use it. Since it once belonged to the boy you like.”


I flushed. “I don’t—”


“Yes, you do.” He cut right through my denial. “It’s written all over your face, Josie. Wade Blackwood. This tractor. That look in your eyes. You don’t even have to say it. I know. I can tell.”


My chest constricted as panic bubbled up inside me. “You don’t know what you’re talking about.”


He chuckled as he started closing the distance between us.


I stumbled back instinctively as my spine brushed against the cold metal. The tractor loomed behind me while Aidan caged me with his body. Suddenly, there was nowhere left to go.


My hands curled into fists at my sides. “W-What are you doing?”


He didn’t answer. He simply stared down at me with those hungry blue eyes, tracing every inch of my face as though he were memorizing it. Slowly, he reached up, and my breath hitched. His fingertips brush the side of my temple before tucking a stray lock of hair behind my ear.


The touch was featherlight, but it sent my pulse racing. My whole body trembled with an emotion I didn’t want to name.


He slowly lowered his head, closing the space between us with his breath ghosting just over my lips.


My heart felt like it might explode. At the very last second, before his lips could claim mine, I shoved my hands against his chest. I stumbled sideways and broke free from the magnetic pull that terrified me more than anything.


“I-I… have to go,” I stammered, pushing past him. My boots crunched against the dirt as I scrambled toward Daisy. My hands were shaking so badly that it took me two tries to untie her reins.


I swung onto her back, desperate to put as much distance between us as possible. Before I could get away, his voice cut through the air.


“You can run all you want, Josie,” he called after me, “but sooner or later, you won’t want to.”


My whole body went rigid. I didn’t dare look back. I couldn’t. But his words followed me anyway, settling around my chest like a snare. No matter how fast Daisy carried me away, I knew they would haunt me long after I was gone.
